# Discrepancy Theory: An Algorithmic and Geometric Perspective

**arXiv ID**: 2608.00140v1
**Authors**: Nikhil Bansal, Aleksandar Nikolov
**Published**: 2026-07-31
**Categories**: math.HO, cs.DM, cs.DS, math.CO, math.MG
**Comments**: Manuscript for an unpublished monograph
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.00140v1

## Abstract

Combinatorial discrepancy theory is a subject with roots in combinatorics, geometry, and number theory, and with numerous applications to mathematics and computer science. At its core, discrepancy theory is about dividing a collection of objects into two parts that are as balanced as possible. For example, given a collection of subsets of a finite universe, we may wish to color the elements with two colors so that each set is approximately evenly split. Other problems in discrepancy are more geometric in flavor, and ask for example, to assign signs to a collection of vectors, so that the sum of the signed vectors is as small as possible. Classical results, such as the Beck-Fiala theorem and Spencer's "six deviations" result, show that it is often possible to attain remarkably small discrepancy, often far smaller than what naive random colorings achieve.   In recent years, discrepancy theory has undergone a transformation, driven by new algorithmic techniques and a rich interplay between probability, optimization, and convex geometry. These developments have led not only to new constructive proofs of foundational theorems, but also to several new results and research directions. This monograph aims to provide an accessible and unified introduction to these modern developments, with a focus on the core algorithmic and convex geometric ideas that have driven them. For several results, we provide new simpler analyses, while highlighting the intuition behind the proofs.

## Discrepancy Theory: An Algorithmic and Geometric PerspectiveNikhil Bansal111Supported in part by the NWO VICI award 639.023.812 and the NSF awards CCF-2327011 and CCF-2504995.Aleksandar Nikolov222Supported in part by an NSERC Discovery Grant (RGPIN-2021-03206), and the Canada Research Chairs program (CRC-2020-00004).

## Abstract

Combinatorial discrepancy theoryis a subject with roots in combinatorics, geometry, and number theory, and with numerous applications to mathematics and computer science.
At its core, discrepancy theory is about dividing a collection of objects into two parts that are as balanced as possible.
For example, given a collection of subsets of a finite universe, we may wish to color the elements with two colors so that each set is approximately evenly split.
Other problems in discrepancy are more geometric in flavor, and ask for example, to assign signs to a collection of vectors, so that the sum of the signed vectors is as small as possible.
Classical results, such as the Beck–Fiala theorem and Spencer’s “six deviations” result, show that it is often possible to attain remarkably small discrepancy — often far smaller than what naive random colorings achieve.

In recent years, discrepancy theory has undergone a transformation, driven by new algorithmic techniques and a rich interplay between probability, optimization, and convex geometry.
These developments have led not only to new constructive proofs of foundational theorems, but also to several new results and research directions.
This monograph aims to provide an accessible and unified introduction to these modern developments, with a focus on the core algorithmic and convex geometric ideas that have driven them. For several results, we provide
new simpler analyses, while highlighting the intuition behind the proofs.

We begin by surveying classical techniques for bounding discrepancy, including iterated rounding, partial colorings, and Banaszczyk’s method, emphasizing the underlying tools from high-dimensional geometry. As most of these methods are non-constructive, they do not yield efficient algorithms for computing the colorings that achieve the discrepancy upper bounds.
We then describe several algorithmic techniques
that match and even improve upon the classical methods.
We also discuss related topics such as the computational complexity of approximating discrepancy, and discrepancy minimization in the online model.

## Chapter 1Introduction

This is a book aboutcombinatorial discrepancy theory— a
subject with roots in combinatorics, geometry, and number theory, and
numerous applications to other areas of mathematics and computer
science. In many of the applications, discrepancy provides a way to
“simplify” a “complex” object. In this introductory chapter, we define the basic concepts in discrepancy theory, and illustrate the ways it can be applied with several examples.

## 1.1Discrepancy of Sets and Sparsification

Consider the following balancing problem. We have a collection of subsetsSS\SSof some finite universe𝒰\mathcal{U}, which we will call aset system. We want to color the universe𝒰\mathcal{U}with two colors, say red and blue, so that the red elementsRR, and the blue elementsBB“look similar” to all the sets inSS\SS. I.e., each set inSS\SSshould have, as much as possible, a similar number of elements fromRRand fromBB. Then thediscrepancyof the coloring ismaxS∈SS⁡||S∩R|−|S∩B||,\max_{S\in\SS}\Big|\,|S\cap R|-|S\cap B|\,\Big|,

and the discrepancy of the set systemSS\SSis the minimum discrepancy over all colorings, i.e., all partitions of𝒰\mathcal{U}into disjoint setsRRandBB. It is convenient to encode a coloring as a functionx:𝒰→{−1,+1}x:\mathcal{U}\to\{-1,+1\},
wherex​(u)=+1x(u)=+1corresponds to placinguuinRR, andx​(u)=−1x(u)=-1corresponds to placing it inBB. We further definex​(S):=∑u∈Sx​(u)x(S):=\sum_{u\in S}x(u), which is just|S∩R|−|S∩B||S\cap R|-|S\cap B|.
We can now write the discrepancy of a coloringxxand the discrepancy ofSS\SS, respectively, asdisc⁡(SS,x)\displaystyle\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x):=maxS∈SS⁡|x​(S)|,\displaystyle:=\max_{S\in\SS}|x(S)|,disc⁡(SS)\displaystyle\operatorname{disc}(\SS):=minx:𝒰→{−1,+1}⁡disc⁡(SS,x).\displaystyle:=\min_{x:\mathcal{U}\to\{-1,+1\}}\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x).

Using Hoeffding’s inequality, and the union bound, it is easy to see that, with probability at least12\frac{1}{2}, a uniformly random coloringx:𝒰→{−1,+1}x:\mathcal{U}\to\{-1,+1\}achievesdisc⁡(SS,x)≲|𝒰|​log⁡(2​|SS|)\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x)\lesssim\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|\log(2|\SS|)}.111Here and in the
rest of the book we use the notationA≲BA\lesssim Bto mean thatA≤C​BA\leq CBfor an absolute constantC>0C>0, andA≳BA\gtrsim Bto
meanB≲AB\lesssim A.A major theme of this book, and of discrepancy theory in general, is the study of methods to construct colorings that achieve smaller discrepancy than random colorings.

## Application: Epsilon Approximations.

Most applications of the discrepancy of set systems rely on the observation that, because each setSSinSS\SSis approximately equally split between the two color classes, restrictingSSto one of the color classes reduces its size by approximately half. Thus, restricting all sets to the smaller color class gives another set system on a smaller universe, in which the relative sizes of the sets are preserved.

Let us illustrate this idea with a geometric application. Let us takePPto be a finite point set in the plane. Letℛ2\mathcal{R}_{2}be the family of all axis-aligned rectangles,i.e., all sets of the
formR=[a,b]×[c,d]R=[a,b]\times[c,d]. A subsetQQofPPis anε\varepsilon-approximation ofPPwith respect toℛ2\mathcal{R}_{2}, if for every axis-aligned rectangleRRwe have that||Q∩R||Q|−|P∩R||P||≤ε.\left|\frac{|Q\cap R|}{|Q|}-\frac{|P\cap R|}{|P|}\right|\leq\varepsilon.

I.e.,QQmust be such that the fraction of points in it that land in any axis-aligned rectangle is equal, up to an additive errorε\varepsilon, to the fraction of points ofPPthat land in the same rectangle. Typically, we are interested inε\varepsilon-approximations of small size. Such anε\varepsilon-approximation is a concise approximation of the set systemℛ2|P:={R∩P:R∈ℛ2}\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P}:=\{R\cap P:R\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\}of subsets ofPPinduced by axis-aligned rectangles. Naturally, there is nothing special here about axis-aligned rectangles, andε\varepsilon-approximations can be defined analogously for other families of geometric sets, e.g., halfspaces, or Euclidean balls, and in dimensions higher than 2. They are a useful concept in computational geometry. For example, a smallε\varepsilon-approximation immediately gives a small-space data structure for approximate range counting: the data structure is theε\varepsilon-approximationQQ, and given a query rectangleRR, the number of points ofPPinRRcan be approximated by|Q∩R|⋅|P|/|Q||Q\cap R|\cdot|P|/|Q|, which gives an additive approximation ofε​|P|\varepsilon|P|.

Discrepancy gives us a way to construct anε\varepsilon-approximationQQof half the size ofPP. Let us denoten:=|P|n:=|P|. Suppose that, for the set systemSS:=ℛ2|P\SS:=\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P}induced by axis-aligned rectangles onPP, the coloringxxachievesD:=disc⁡(SS)D:=\operatorname{disc}(\SS), and letQQbe the points inPPcolored+1+1. The discrepancy constraint implies that for every rectangleR∈ℛ2R\in\mathcal{R}_{2},||Q∩R|−|(P∖Q)∩R||≤D.\Big||Q\cap R|-|(P\setminus Q)\cap R|\Big|\leq D.

On the other hand,|P∩R|=|Q∩R|+|(P∖Q)∩R||P\cap R|=|Q\cap R|+|(P\setminus Q)\cap R|, so‖Q∩R​|−12|​P∩R‖=12​||Q∩R|−|(P∖Q)∩R||≤D2.\Big||Q\cap R|-\frac{1}{2}|P\cap R|\Big|=\frac{1}{2}\Big||Q\cap R|-|(P\setminus Q)\cap R|\Big|\leq\frac{D}{2}.

If the size ofQQwere exactlyn/2n/2, then dividing both sides by|Q||Q|would give us thatQQis aD/nD/n-approximation toPPwith respect to axis-aligned rectangles. But now we can observe that some axis-aligned rectangle contains all the points inPP, so the discrepancy bound also implies that||Q|−n/2|≤D/2\left||Q|-n/2\right|\leq D/2. Then, we can make sure that|Q|=n/2|Q|=n/2by adding or removing at mostD/2D/2points from it, and this modifiedQQis a2​D/n2D/n-approximation.

Letdisc⁡(ℛ2,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)be the maximum ofdisc⁡(ℛ2|P)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P})over allnn-point setsPP. The argument above gives us the following lemma.

## Lemma 1.1.

For anynn-point setP⊆ℝ2P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}, there is anε\varepsilon-approximationQQofPPwith respect to axis-aligned rectangles such that|Q|=n/2|Q|=n/2andε≤2​disc⁡(ℛ2,n)/n\varepsilon\leq 2\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)/n.

We will see later in Chapter7thatdisc⁡(ℛ2,n)≲log1.5⁡(2​n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)\lesssim\log^{1.5}(2n).
Thus theε\varepsilonin Lemma1.1is very close to0whennnis large. Typically, however, we can settle for a constantε\varepsilon, but would like|Q||Q|to only depend onε\varepsilon. We can achieve this by applying Lemma1.1repeatedly, together with the following simple composition lemma, whose proof is left as an exercise.

## Lemma 1.2.

IfQ1Q_{1}is anε1\varepsilon_{1}-approximation ofPP, and alsoQ2Q_{2}is anε2\varepsilon_{2}-approximation ofQ1Q_{1}, thenQ2Q_{2}is an(ε1+ε2)(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})-approximation ofPP.

We can now apply Lemma1.1repeatedly, each time halving the size ofQQ, and Lemma1.2tells us that the approximations add up. If we stop at a setQQofsspoints, we have thatQQis anε\varepsilon-approximation forε=2​disc⁡(ℛ2,n)n+4​disc⁡(ℛ2,n/2)n+…+disc⁡(ℛ2,2​s)s≲disc⁡(ℛ2,n)s,\varepsilon=\frac{2\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)}{n}+\frac{4\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n/2)}{n}+\ldots+\frac{\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},2s)}{s}\lesssim\frac{\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)}{s},

where the last bound follows sincedisc⁡(ℛ2,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)grows polylogarithmically innn. Plugging indisc⁡(ℛ2,n)≲log1.5⁡(2​n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)\lesssim\log^{1.5}(2n)and solving forss, we get the following theorem.

## Theorem 1.3.

For anyε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1), and anynn-point setP⊆ℝ2P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}, there is anε\varepsilon-approximationQQofPPwith respect to axis-aligned rectangles such that|Q|≲(log1.5⁡(1/ε))/ε|Q|\lesssim(\log^{1.5}(1/\varepsilon))/\varepsilon.

Note that, as long as we can efficiently find a coloringxxof anynnpointsPPachievingdisc⁡(ℛ2|P,x)≲log1.5⁡(2​n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P},x)\lesssim\log^{1.5}(2n), we can also perform the construction in Theorem1.3efficiently. Unfortunately, no such efficient algorithm is known for this bound, but the slightly weaker bounddisc⁡(ℛ2|P,x)≲log2⁡(2​n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P},x)\lesssim\log^{2}(2n)can be achieved in polynomial time using the methods of Chapter8.

The size bound in Theorem1.3would be immediately improved by improving the bound on the discrepancydisc⁡(ℛ2,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n). This motivates the following problem, to which we return several times in this book.

## Problem 1.

Determine the maximum discrepancy of a set system induced by the familyℛd\mathcal{R}_{d}ofdd-dimensional axis-aligned boxes (cross
products ofddintervals) on annn-point set,i.e.,
give tight bounds ondisc⁡(ℛd,n):=max⁡{disc⁡(ℛd|P):P⊆ℝd,|P|=n}.\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n):=\max\{\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d}|_{P}):P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},|P|=n\}.

Later in the book we will see upper and lower bounds ondisc⁡(ℛd,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)that are tight within aO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n})factor.

Bounds onε\varepsilon-approximations with respect to other families of geometric sets similar to Theorem1.3can be proved using the same argument and bounds on the discrepancy of the appropriate set system.

## 1.2Discrepancy of Matrices and Rounding

The discrepancy of a set system can be written in a linear algebraic way, which also suggests a natural way to define discrepancy of a matrix. LetSS\SSbe a set system on a universe𝒰\mathcal{U}, and let us takeMMto be the incidence matrix ofSS\SS,i.e., the matrix with rows indexed bySS\SS, columns indexed by𝒰\mathcal{U}, and entries equal toM​(S,u):={1if​u∈S0if​u∉SM(S,u):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }u\in S\\
0&\text{if }u\not\in S\end{cases}

for anyS∈SSS\in\SSandu∈𝒰u\in\mathcal{U}.222We identifynn-dimensional vectorsxxwith functionsx:[n]→ℝx:[n]\to\mathbb{R}, wherex​(i)x(i)is theii-th coordinate ofxx, andm×nm\times nmatricesMMwith functionsM:[m]×[n]→ℝM:[m]\times[n]\to\mathbb{R}, whereM​(i,j)M(i,j)is the entry in rowiicolumnjjofMM.Then it is easy to check thatdisc⁡(SS,x)\displaystyle\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x)=maxS∈SS⁡|(M​x)S|=‖M​x‖∞,\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\SS}|(Mx)_{S}|=\|Mx\|_{\infty},disc⁡(SS)\displaystyle\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=minx∈{−1,1}𝒰⁡‖M​x‖∞.\displaystyle=\min_{x\in\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal{U}}}\|Mx\|_{\infty}.

Inspired by these observations, we can then define the discrepancy of anym×nm\times nmatrixMMand a coloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}asdisc⁡(M,x):=‖M​x‖∞\operatorname{disc}(M,x):=\|Mx\|_{\infty}, and the discrepancy of the matrixMMasdisc⁡(M):=minx∈{−1,+1}n⁡‖M​x‖∞\operatorname{disc}(M):=\min_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}\|Mx\|_{\infty}.

## Application: Rounding

Suppose thaty∈[0,1]ny\in[0,1]^{n}satisfies the constraintsM​y=bMy=bfor somem×nm\times nmatrixMMandmm-dimensional vectorbb. For example,yycan be the solution to a linear programming relaxation of a combinatorial optimization problem. Matrix discrepancy allows us to roundyyto a binary vectorz∈{0,1}nz\in\{0,1\}^{n}so that the constraints are approximately satisfied.

The simplest case is rounding a vectoryyall of whose coordinates are equal to12\frac{1}{2}, i.e.,y=12​1→y=\frac{1}{2}\vec{1}, where1→\vec{1}is the all-ones vector. Then, ifx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}achieves‖M​x‖∞=disc⁡(M)\|Mx\|_{\infty}=\operatorname{disc}(M), for the binary vectorz:=y+12​xz:=y+\frac{1}{2}xwe have‖M​z−b‖∞=‖M​(z−y)‖∞=12​‖M​x‖∞=disc⁡(M)2.\left\|Mz-b\right\|_{\infty}=\|M(z-y)\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\|Mx\|_{\infty}=\frac{\operatorname{disc}(M)}{2}.

This is, in fact, essentially the same observation as the one used in Lemma1.1. Ifyyis instead half-integral, i.e., its coordinates are in{0,12,1}\left\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\right\}, then we can apply the same construction, but only to the coordinates equal to12\frac{1}{2}. The resulting error in our rounding will be half the discrepancy of the submatrix ofMMcorresponding to these coordinates. In general, to round arbitrary vectorsyyusing this method, we need a bound on the maximum discrepancy of any submatrix ofMM. This motivates the definition ofhereditary discrepancy:herdisc⁡(M):=maxJ⊆[n]⁡disc⁡(M​(∗,J)),\operatorname{herdisc}(M):=\max_{J\subseteq[n]}\operatorname{disc}(M(*,J)),

whereM​(∗,J)M(*,J)is the submatrix ofMMconsisting of the columns indexed byJJ. By applying the idea above to the bit representation ofyy, we get the following rounding result.

## Theorem 1.4.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMM,
and anyy∈[0,1]ny\in[0,1]^{n},
there exists somez∈{0,1}nz\in\{0,1\}^{n}such that‖M​(z−y)‖∞≤herdisc⁡(M).\left\|M(z-y)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(M).

## Proof.

Let us assume that there is some finitekksuch that, for eachj∈[n]j\in[n],y​(j)∈2−k​ℤy(j)\in 2^{-k}\mathbb{Z},i.e., that each coordinatey​(j)y(j)can be written using at mostkkbits after the radix point. By standard compactness arguments, it is
enough to show that the theorem holds under this assumption. Suppose that, for everyjj,bk​(j)b_{k}(j)is thekk-th bit after the radix point ofy​(j)y(j), andy′​(j)y^{\prime}(j)is the real number given by the firstk−1k-1bits after the radix point. Theny​(j)=y′​(j)+2−k​bk​(j)y(j)=y^{\prime}(j)+2^{-k}b_{k}(j). LetJ={j:bk​(j)=1}J=\{j:b_{k}(j)=1\}, and find a
coloringx∈{−1,+1}Jx\in\{-1,+1\}^{J}which achieves‖M​(∗,J)​x‖∞=disc⁡(M​(∗,J))≤herdisc⁡(M).\|M(*,J)x\|_{\infty}=\operatorname{disc}(M(*,J))\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(M).

We set, for allj∈Jj\in J,y′′​(j):=y​(j)+x​(j)​2−k=y′​(j)+2−k+x​(j)​2−k,y^{\prime\prime}(j):=y(j)+x(j)2^{-k}=y^{\prime}(j)+2^{-k}+x(j)2^{-k},

andy′′​(j):=y′​(j)=y​(j)y^{\prime\prime}(j):=y^{\prime}(j)=y(j)forj∉Jj\not\in J. I.e., for every coordinatey​(j)y(j)whosekk-th bit after the radix point is11, we round that bit up ifx​(j)=+1x(j)=+1, or down otherwise.
Clearly eachy′′​(j)y^{\prime\prime}(j)can be written with at mostk−1k-1bits after the radix point,
and‖M​(y′′−y)‖∞=2−k​‖M​(∗,J)​x‖∞≤2−k​herdisc⁡(M).\|M(y^{\prime\prime}-y)\|_{\infty}=2^{-k}\|M(*,J)x\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{-k}\operatorname{herdisc}(M).

We then replaceyywithy′′y^{\prime\prime}andkkwithk−1k-1, and inductively apply the same bit-rounding process untilk=0k=0, at which point we have our vectorz∈{0,1}nz\in\{0,1\}^{n}. Summing the errors from rounding each bit, we get‖M​(z−y)‖∞≤(2−1+2−2+…+2−k)​herdisc⁡(M)≤herdisc⁡(M).∎\|M(z-y)\|_{\infty}\leq(2^{-1}+2^{-2}+\ldots+2^{-k})\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(M).\qed

Theorem1.4can be used to derive a result similar to Theorem1.3. We letMMbe the incidence matrix ofℛ2|P\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P}, andy=sn​1→y=\frac{s}{n}\vec{1}. Applying Theorem1.4and lettingQ:={p∈P:z​(p)=1}Q:=\{p\in P:z(p)=1\}, we have that, for any rectangleR∈ℛ2R\in\mathcal{R}_{2},‖Q∩R​|−ns|​P∩R‖≤herdisc⁡(ℛ2|P)≤disc⁡(ℛ2,n)≲log1.5⁡(2​n).\left||Q\cap R|-\frac{n}{s}|P\cap R|\right|\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P})\leq\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)\lesssim\log^{1.5}(2n).

Moreover, since some rectangle contains all ofPP, we also have||Q|−s|≲log1.5⁡(2​n)||Q|-s|\lesssim\log^{1.5}(2n), so up to a constant factor increase in the bound above, we can assume that|Q|=s|Q|=s. Thus, dividing the inequality above byssgives us thatQQis an(log1.5⁡n)/s(\log^{1.5}n)/s-approximation. Unfortunately, this is not quite enough to get the bound in Theorem1.3or any bound that depends entirely onε\varepsilon. We can address this with a slight strengthening of Theorem1.4. Let us define a parametrized version of hereditary discrepancy byherdisc⁡(M,s):=maxJ⊆[n]:|J|≤s⁡disc⁡(M​(∗,J)).\operatorname{herdisc}(M,s):=\max_{J\subseteq[n]:|J|\leq s}\operatorname{disc}(M(*,J)).

We then have the stronger rounding result stated next, which follows by essentially the same proof.

## Theorem 1.5.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMM, and anyy∈[0,1]ny\in[0,1]^{n}, there exists somez∈{0,1}nz\in\{0,1\}^{n}such that‖M​(z−y)‖∞≤∑k=1∞2−k​herdisc⁡(M,2k​∑j=1nyj).\left\|M(z-y)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}\operatorname{herdisc}\left(M,2^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}\right).

## Proof.

We constructzzin the same way as in Theorem1.4, and simply observe that the setJ={j:bk​(j)=1}J=\{j:b_{k}(j)=1\}of coordinates ofyywithkk-th bit after the radix point equal to11is of size at most2k​∑j=1nyj2^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}.
∎

Theorem1.5now allows us to recover Theorem1.3sinceherdisc⁡(ℛ2|P,s)≤disc⁡(ℛ2,s)≲log1.5⁡(2​s)\operatorname{herdisc}(\mathcal{R}_{2}|_{P},s)\leq\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},s)\lesssim\log^{1.5}(2s).

Moreover, as with Theorem1.3, the roundings in Theorems1.4and1.5can found efficiently as long as we can find colorings with discrepancy bounded by the hereditary discrepancy. We return to this question in Chapter4.

## 1.3Vector Balancing and Approximate Carathéodory

While the infinity norm is natural for the discrepancy of set systems, and for measuring how much a rounded solution to a system of linear equations violates the equations, in other applications of matrix discrepancy we may need to consider other norms. The definitions we made above can be generalized easily to such a setting. Suppose∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}is some norm onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, and letXXbe the corresponding normed space. Then, the discrepancy of a coloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}with respect to anm×nm\times nmatrixMMandXXisdiscX⁡(M,x):=‖M​x‖X\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M,x):=\|Mx\|_{X}. The discrepancy ofMMwith respect toXXisdiscX⁡(M):=minx∈{−1,+1}n⁡‖M​x‖X\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M):=\min_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}\|Mx\|_{X}. Hereditary discrepancy is again defined by taking a maximum over submatrices:herdiscX⁡(M,s)\displaystyle\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M,s):=maxJ⊆[n]:|J|≤s⁡discX⁡(M​(∗,J)),\displaystyle:=\max_{J\subseteq[n]:|J|\leq s}\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M(*,J)),herdiscX⁡(M)\displaystyle\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M):=maxJ⊆[n]⁡discX⁡(M​(∗,J)).\displaystyle:=\max_{J\subseteq[n]}\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M(*,J)).

It is easy to check that, since the proofs only use the triangle inequality and homogeneity properties of theℓ∞\ell_{\infty}norm, Theorems1.4and1.5hold under any norm. Next we consider theℓ2\ell_{2}norm, and give another geometric application of these rounding theorems.

## Approximate Carathéodory Theorem.

Letu0,u1,…,unu_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}be vectors inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, and letvvbe a point in their convex hull. Carathéodory’s theorem shows that there exists a subsetS⊆{0}∪[n]S\subseteq\{0\}\cup[n]of cardinality at mostm+1m+1so thatvvis
also in the convex hull of{ui:i∈S}\{u_{i}:i\in S\}. This is generally
tight, for example whenu0=0u_{0}=0,uiu_{i}is theii-th standard basis
vector ofℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, andvvis the vector with every coordinate equal to1m+1\frac{1}{m+1}. However, if we only askvvto becloseto the
convex hull of{ui:i∈S}\{u_{i}:i\in S\}, then perhaps we can hope to improve the
bound on the size ofSS, and, moreover, we can hope for bounds
that are independent of the dimensionmm.

Once we allow for approximation, we need to fix a way to measure distances, and a scale. We will, for now, pick the Euclidean, i.e.,ℓ2\ell_{2}distance. As for fixing a scale, a
natural way to do so is to ask that the diametermaxi=0n⁡maxj=0n⁡‖ui−uj‖2\max_{i=0}^{n}\max_{j=0}^{n}\|u_{i}-u_{j}\|_{2}be bounded by11.
Since everything in our set up is invariant under translation, we can
assume thatu0=0u_{0}=0, and, by the diameter condition,‖ui‖2≤1\|u_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1for alli∈[n]i\in[n].

We will use discrepancy to approximatevvby the average ofssvectors, for a parameterss, so that the approximation error scales likemin⁡{ms,1s}\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{m}}{s},\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\right\}.
To this end, let us explicitly writevvas a convex combination ofu0,…​unu_{0},\ldots u_{n}asv=α0​u0+α1​u1+…+αn​un=α1​u1+…+αn​un\displaystyle v=\alpha_{0}u_{0}+\alpha_{1}u_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}u_{n}=\alpha_{1}u_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}u_{n}

for someα1,…,αn≥0\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\geq 0such that∑i=1nαi≤1\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\leq 1. By Carathéodory’s theorem we can assume thatn≤mn\leq m. Inspired by the proof of Theorem1.3via Theorem1.4, let us definey∈ℝny\in\mathbb{R}^{n}byy​(i):=s​αiy(i):=s\alpha_{i}. Takez∈{0,1}nz\in\{0,1\}^{n}to be the rounding ofyyguaranteed by Theorem1.4when used with theℓ2\ell_{2}norm and the matrixMMwith columnsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}. LetS:={i:z​(i)=1}S:=\{i:z(i)=1\}.333Technically, we use Theorem1.4to round the vector with coordinatesy​(i)−⌊y​(i)⌋y(i)-\lfloor y(i)\rfloor, and we first add⌊y​(i)⌋\lfloor y(i)\rfloorcopies ofiitoSSbefore adding also thoseiifor whichz​(i)=1z(i)=1.We then have that‖s​v−∑i∈Sui‖2≤herdiscℓ2⁡(M).\left\|sv-\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}_{\ell_{2}}(M).(1.1)

Moreover, for a uniformly randomx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}, and a matrixMMwithnncolumns each ofℓ2\ell_{2}norm at most11,𝔼​‖M​x‖22≤n\mathbb{E}\|Mx\|_{2}^{2}\leq n. Therefore,discℓ2⁡(M)≤n\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{2}}(M)\leq\sqrt{n}, and, since this reasoning applies to any submatrix as well,herdiscℓ2⁡(M)≤n\operatorname{herdisc}_{\ell_{2}}(M)\leq\sqrt{n}. We have‖s​v−∑i∈Sui‖2≤n≤m.\left\|sv-\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}\leq\sqrt{m}.

If we could guarantee that|S|=s|S|=s, then dividing both sides of the inequality bysswould guarantee that‖v−1|S|​∑i∈Sui‖2≤ms.\left\|v-\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{m}}{s}.

Unfortunately, we cannot automatically deduce that|S||S|is close tossfrom (1.1). Nevertheless, there is a simple workaround, given in the following corollary of Theorems1.4and1.5.

## Corollary 1.6.

In both Theorem1.4and Theorem1.5, we can assume that∑j=1nz​(j)≤∑j=1ny​(j)\sum_{j=1}^{n}z(j)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}y(j).

## Proof.

Follows from the same proof we gave for the theorems, after noticing that we can always take the coloringx∈{−1,+1}Jx\in\{-1,+1\}^{J}to be such that∑j∈Jx​(j)≤0\sum_{j\in J}x(j)\leq 0since if this is not the case, we can just flip the colors without changing the discrepancy.
∎

We can now show the following result.

## Theorem 1.7.

Suppose thatu0,…,unu_{0},\ldots,u_{n}are vectors inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, and their diameter is at most11. For any vectorvvin their convex hull, and any positive integerss, there exists a (multi-)setS⊆{0}∪[n]S\subseteq\{0\}\cup[n]of sizesssuch that‖v−1s​∑i∈Sui‖2≲min⁡{ms,1s}.\left\|v-\frac{1}{s}\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\right\|_{2}\lesssim\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{m}}{s},\frac{1}{\sqrt{s}}\right\}.

## Proof.

As above, we may assumen≤mn\leq m, shift the vectors so thatu0=0u_{0}=0, and writev=α1​u1+…+αn​unv=\alpha_{1}u_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}u_{n}for non-negativeα1,…,αn\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}that sum to at most11. Definey∈ℝny\in\mathbb{R}^{n}byy​(i):=s​αiy(i):=s\alpha_{i}. If somey​(i)y(i)is larger than11, then we add⌊y​(i)⌋\lfloor y(i)\rfloorcopies ofiitoSS, and replacey​(i)y(i)byy​(i)−⌊y​(i)⌋y(i)-\lfloor y(i)\rfloor. We can now apply Corollary1.6toyyto get a vectorz∈{0,1}nz\in\{0,1\}^{n}, and add alliisuch thatz​(i)=1z(i)=1toSS. By the guarantee of the corollary, we have that|S|≤s​∑i=1nαi=s|S|\leq s\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}=s. To make sureSShas size exactlyss, we add sufficiently many copies of0toSS.

Recall the observation above thatdiscℓ2⁡(M,x)≤n\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{2}}(M,x)\leq\sqrt{n}for annn-column matrixMMwith columns whoseℓ2\ell_{2}norms are at most11. Thereforeherdisc⁡(M,t)≤t\operatorname{herdisc}(M,t)\leq\sqrt{t}for such a matrix, and the theorem follows from the inequality (1.1) and the more precise bound‖s​v−∑i∈Sui‖2≤∑k=1∞2−k​herdiscℓ2⁡(M,2k​s)≲s​∑k=1∞2−k/2≲s.∎\left\|sv-\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}\operatorname{herdisc}_{\ell_{2}}(M,2^{k}s)\lesssim\sqrt{s}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k/2}\lesssim\sqrt{s}.\qed

The key quantity in the proof of Theorem1.7is a vector balancing constant. SupposeKKis some subset ofℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, and∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}is, as above, a norm onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}. The vector balancing constants forKKandXXare defined byvb⁡(K,X,n)\displaystyle\operatorname{vb}(K,X,n):=sup{discX⁡((ui)i=1n):u1,…,un∈K},\displaystyle:=\sup\{\operatorname{disc}_{X}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}):u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in K\},vb⁡(K,X)\displaystyle\operatorname{vb}(K,X):=supnvb⁡(K,X,n).\displaystyle:=\sup_{n}\operatorname{vb}(K,X,n).

In our proof of Theorem1.7we used the simple observation thatvb⁡(Bℓ2,ℓ2,n)≤n\operatorname{vb}(B_{\ell_{2}},\ell_{2},n)\leq\sqrt{n}, whereBℓ2:={u:‖u‖2≤1}B_{\ell_{2}}:=\{u:\|u\|_{2}\leq 1\}is the unit ball. The same proof shows that if the vectorsu0,…,unu_{0},\ldots,u_{n}have diameter bounded by11in the norm∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}, then we can guarantee‖v−1|S|​∑i∈Sui‖X≤1s​∑k=1∞2−k​vb⁡(BX,X,2k​s),\left\|v-\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}u_{i}\right\|_{X}\leq\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}\operatorname{vb}(B_{X},X,2^{k}s),

whereBX:={u∈ℝm:‖u‖X≤1}B_{X}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|u\|_{X}\leq 1\}.

There is also no reason why
the diameter ofu0,…​unu_{0},\ldots u_{n}has to be bounded in the same norm in
which we measure the approximation. For example, in the next chapter,
we will see thatvb⁡(Bℓ1,ℓ∞)≤2\operatorname{vb}(B_{\ell_{1}},\ell_{\infty})\leq 2(this is a
result of Beck and Fiala[31]). Then, using analogous reasoning to the above, we have that wheneveru0,…,unu_{0},\ldots,u_{n}haveℓ1\ell_{1}diameter at most11, for any pointvvin their
convex hull we can find an averagev′v^{\prime}ofssvectors fromu0,…,unu_{0},\ldots,u_{n}so that‖v−v′‖∞≤2s\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{s}. Remarkably, it is an open problem whether this‖v−v′‖∞≲1s\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{1}{s}error bound can be achieved under the weaker assumption thatu0,…,unu_{0},\ldots,u_{n}have Euclidean diameter at most11. Such an improvement would
follow from a positive solution to the following problem, due to
Komlós.

## Problem 2.

Prove or disprove the boundvb⁡(Bℓ2m,ℓ∞m)≲1\operatorname{vb}(B_{\ell_{2}^{m}},\ell_{\infty}^{m})\lesssim 1.

Later in the book, we will see partial progress towards
Problem2, and, in particular, a bound ofO​(log⁡m)O(\sqrt{\log m})due to Banaszczyk[10]. We will see both
Banaszczyk’s result in Chapters5and6, and also two different proofs in Chapter8and9that gives an efficient
algorithm to construct the coloring achieving this bound.444Very recently, Bansal and Jiang[17]have obtained anO​(log1/4⁡m)O(\log^{1/4}m)discrepancy bound for the Komlós problem, building on the techniques we will see in Chapter8. However, we will not discuss their result in this book.

## 1.4Prefix Discrepancy and the Steinitz Problem

Suppose anm×nm\times nmatrixMMhas columnsu1,…,un∈ℝmu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}. A useful strengthening of matrix discrepancy is prefix discrepancy, which requires that the same coloring simultaneously balances each prefix of columnsu1,…,uiu_{1},\ldots,u_{i}. Formally, we define theprefix discrepancyof a matrixMMwith columnsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}, and a coloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}aspdisc⁡(M,x):=maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1jx​(i)​ui‖∞.\operatorname{pdisc}(M,x):=\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}x(i)u_{i}\right\|_{\infty}.

The prefix discrepancy ofMMis the minimum ofpdisc⁡(M,x)\operatorname{pdisc}(M,x)over all coloringsx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}. Similarly to matrix discrepancy, we can also define a variantpdiscX⁡(M)\operatorname{pdisc}_{X}(M)in which theℓ∞\ell_{\infty}norm is replaced by some other norm∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}.

This is a natural generalization of discrepancy when we decide on the colorsx​(1),…,x​(n)x(1),\ldots,x(n)one at a time, e.g., because the column vectorsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}are specified online. We will also see that prefix discrepancy can be used to prove bounds on standard discrepancy in problems with some implicit ordering: this will be the approach we take for Problem1in Chapter7. Here we will see another application to a classical geometric problem.

## The Steinitz Problem.

Suppose we have vectorsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, so that each vector is “short”,i.e.,ui∈[−1,+1]mu_{i}\in[-1,+1]^{m}for allii. Ifu1+…+un=0u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n}=0, then the partial sumsu1+…+uju_{1}+\ldots+u_{j}trace a path in space from the origin back to itself. At intermediate
steps, the path can be arbitrarily far from0, but it always takes
short steps. Can we re-arrange the vectors, so that the path always
stays close to0, and, in particular, the norm‖∑i=1jui‖∞\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{i}\right\|_{\infty}of each partial sum is
always bounded by a function depending only on the dimensionmm? See
Figure1.1for an illustration.Figure 1.1:A path of short steps returning to
the origin, and a rearrangement of the steps so that the path
stays much closer to the origin.

This question was first asked (and answered affirmatively) by
Steinitz[118]. Note that the requirement that the vectors
sum to0is not essential. In general, ifv=u1+⋯+unv=u_{1}+\cdots+u_{n}is the sum of the vectors; then we can make sure that the path
traced by the partial sums stays close to the line from0tovv,i.e., that‖jn​v−∑i=1jui‖∞\left\|\frac{j}{n}v-\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{i}\right\|_{\infty}is as small as possible for allj∈[n]j\in[n]. This question is, however, essentially equivalent to the version where the vectors sum to0, which we can see by replacing eachuiu_{i}withui−1n​vu_{i}-\frac{1}{n}v.

The Steinitz problem arises
naturally in many contexts, e.g. in designing algorithms for integer
programming[48], and approximation algorithms for
scheduling problems[111]. Let us use the notationst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})for how close we can keep the path to the line from0tovv, i.e.,st⁡((ui)i=1n):=minπ∈Sn⁡maxj=1n⁡‖jn​v−∑i=1juπ​(i)‖∞,\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}):=\min_{\pi\in S_{n}}\max_{j=1}^{n}\Bigg\|\frac{j}{n}v-\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi(i)}\Bigg\|_{\infty},

whereSnS_{n}is the set of permutations of[n][n], andv:=∑i=1nuiv:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}. Once again, this question also makes sense for any norm∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}in place of theℓ∞\ell_{\infty}norm, and we denote the corresponding quantity bystX⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}_{X}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}).

Prefix discrepancy is a very useful tool in boundingst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}). From now on, let us return to the assumptionu1+…+un=0u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n}=0. If some prefix sums are too large, then prefix discrepancy allows us to rearrange the vectors so that the prefix sum norms improve. The rearrangement is simple: we take a coloringxx, and place the vectors colored+1+1first, followed by the vectors colored−1-1in reverse order. For some intuition, let us take some prefix sumu1+…+uiu_{1}+\ldots+u_{i}. After the rearrangement, this prefix gets split into a prefix in the first half of the rearranged sequence (the vectors amongu1,…​uiu_{1},\ldots u_{i}colored+1+1), and a suffix in the second half (the vectors colored−1-1). If the prefix discrepancy is small, then the new prefix and suffix will have sums approximately equal to12​∑j=1iui\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{i}u_{i}, which has smaller norm than∑j=1iui\sum_{j=1}^{i}u_{i}. Since the vectors add up to0, the norms of the suffix sums equal the norms of their complementary prefix sums, so we have improved the norms of all prefix sums by this rearrangement.

The main theorem and its proof follow.

## Theorem 1.8.

For any integern≥1n\geq 1, and any
vectorsu1​…,un∈ℝmu_{1}\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}such thatu1+…+un=0u_{1}+\ldots+u_{n}=0,st⁡((ui)i=1n)≤maxσ∈Sn⁡pdisc⁡((uσ​(i))i=1n).\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})\leq\max_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\operatorname{pdisc}((u_{\sigma(i)})_{i=1}^{n}).(1.2)

whereSnS_{n}is the set of permutations of[n][n].

## Proof.

Letπ\pibe the permutation of[n][n]that minimizesmaxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1juπ​(i)‖∞\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{\infty}, i.e., achievesst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}). Letx:[n]→{−1,+1}x:[n]\to\{-1,+1\}achievepdisc⁡((uπ​(i))i=1n)\operatorname{pdisc}((u_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n}). We
will show that if (1.2) doesnothold, then we can choose
another permutationπ′\pi^{\prime}for whichmaxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1juπ′​(i)‖∞<maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1juπ​(i)‖∞=st⁡((ui)i=1n),\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi^{\prime}(i)}\right\|_{\infty}<\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{\infty}=\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}),(1.3)

which contradicts the definition ofst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}). We chooseπ′\pi^{\prime}using the
swapping operation alluded to above. We setS={i:x​(i)=+1}S=\{i:x(i)=+1\}, we place
the vectorsuiu_{i}indexed byi∈Si\in Sfirst, ordered according toπ\pi, followed by the vectors indexed byi∉Si\not\in S, ordered in
reverse order ofπ\pi. Using1S​(⋅)1_{S}(\cdot)for the indicator function ofSS, we havemaxj=1|S|⁡‖∑i=1juπ′​(i)‖∞\displaystyle\max_{j=1}^{|S|}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi^{\prime}(i)}\right\|_{\infty}=maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1j1S​(i)​uπ​(i)‖∞=maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1j1+x​(i)2​uπ​(i)‖∞\displaystyle=\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}1_{S}(i)u_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{\infty}=\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}\frac{1+x(i)}{2}u_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{\infty}≤12​maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1juπ​(i)‖∞+12​maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1jx​(i)​uπ​(i)‖∞\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}x(i)u_{\pi(i)}\right\|_{\infty}≤12​st⁡((ui)i=1n)+12​pdisc⁡((uπ​(i))i=1n).\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{pdisc}((u_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n}).

A similar calculation shows thatmaxj=|S|+1n⁡‖∑i=1juπ′​(i)‖∞≤12​maxj=1n⁡st⁡((ui)i=1n)+12​pdisc⁡((uπ​(i))i=1n).\max_{j=|S|+1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{\pi^{\prime}(i)}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}\max_{j=1}^{n}\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{pdisc}((u_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n}).

Thus, ifpdisc⁡((uπ​(i))i=1n)<st⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{pdisc}((u_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n})<\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}), then (1.3) holds.
∎

Theorem1.8is due to Chobanyan[41],
and is known as Chobanyan’s transference theorem. The proof above only uses the triangle inequality forℓ∞m\ell_{\infty}^{m}, so the transference theorem in fact holds forstX⁡(⋅)\operatorname{st}_{X}(\cdot)andpdiscX⁡(⋅)\operatorname{pdisc}_{X}(\cdot)for any normed spaceXX. As with our other applications of discrepancy, it is not hard to see that if we can efficiently find the colorings achieving the prefix discrepancy bound, we can also efficiently find the re-arrangement in the theorem.

In order to use Theorem1.8to boundst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})foru1,…,un∈[−1,1]mu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in[-1,1]^{m}, we need to bound the partial coloring discrepancypdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{pdisc}(M)of anym×nm\times nmatrixMMwith entries in[−1,1][-1,1]. A simple way to do so is to take a uniformly random coloringx:[n]→{−1,+1}x:[n]\to\{-1,+1\}.
Hoeffding’s inequality and the union bound then give uspdisc⁡(M,x)≲n​log⁡(m+n),\operatorname{pdisc}(M,x)\lesssim\sqrt{n\log(m+n)},with positive probability. This argument and Theorem1.8then imply that this bound also holds forst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})wheneveru1,…,un∈[−1,1]mu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in[-1,1]^{m}.

This is much less than the trivial boundnn, unlessmmis
exponential innn. But what about bounds that depend only onmm?
In
the next chapter, we will show thatst⁡((ui)i=1n)≤2​m\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})\leq 2mholds under the same assumptions, and, moreover, it holds foranynorm, and vectorsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}in its unit ball. Thus, the path of partial sums in the Steinitz
problem can be made to stay within a bounded distance from the origin, no matter how many steps we take!

The next tantalizing problem is open.

## Problem 3.

Give tight bounds (in terms ofmmandnn) onst⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})whenu1,…,un∈[−1,1]mu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in[-1,1]^{m}, and onpdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{pdisc}(M)form×nm\times nmatricesMMwith entries in[−1,1][-1,1].

Similarly, give tight bounds (in terms ofmmandnn) onstℓ2m⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}_{\ell_{2}^{m}}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})whenu1,…,un∈Bℓ2m:={x∈ℝm:‖x‖2≤1}u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in B_{\ell_{2}^{m}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|x\|_{2}\leq 1\}. Also give tight bounds onpdiscℓ2m⁡(M)\operatorname{pdisc}_{\ell_{2}^{m}}(M)for anm×nm\times nmatrixMMwith columns bounded by11inℓ2\ell_{2}norm.

It is conjectured that the right bound in terms ofmmfor both problems isO​(m)O(\sqrt{m}). However, the best known bound for both isO​(m)O(m),
which also holds for everymm-dimensional
norm[111,53,21]. Partial progress towards the
conjecture forℓ2m\ell_{2}^{m}was made by Banaszczyk[11], who
showed that, whenu1,…,um∈Bℓ2mu_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in B_{\ell_{2}^{m}},pdiscℓ2m⁡((ui)i=1n)\displaystyle\operatorname{pdisc}_{\ell_{2}^{m}}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})≲m+log⁡n,\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{\log n},s​tℓ2m​((ui)i=1n)\displaystyle st_{\ell_{2}^{m}}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})≲m+log⁡n,\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{\log n},

where the second bound follows from the first via Theorem1.8.

## 1.5Organization of the Book

In this book we survey methods of proving upper bounds on the notions
of discrepancy introduced above: discrepancy of set systems and matrices, vector balancing in different normed spaces, prefix discrepancy. We emphasize
methods from high dimensional probability and convex geometry. We also
spend considerable attention on methods of bounding discrepancy that
also give efficient algorithms for finding a coloring. This
is an important consideration for applications of discrepancy theory
to computational problems.

There are several other book-length surveys of discrepancy theory. The monographs of Back and Chen[30], and of Drmota and Tichy[46]are primarily about continuous discrepancy questions, and irregularities / uniformity of distribution. The books of Chazelle[39]and Matoušek[85]cover a mix of combinatorial discrepancy and continuous discrepancy, and the connection between the two. We differ from these books in our focus on combinatorial discrepancy, and our emphasis on geometric techniques and efficient algorithms. Most of the results and algorithms we describe in the later chapters of this book were discovered after Chazelle and Matoušek’s books were published.

We begin with linear algebraic arguments for bounding discrepancy in
Chapter2. These techniques are flexible, and we will
apply them to all problems in this introductory chapter, as well as
some new ones. They also give efficient algorithms. The bounds proved
with linear algebra, however, are often not tight. To address this, in
Chapter3we introduce Beck’s partial coloring method,
and a more refined geometric variant of it due to Giannopoulos.

While the classical
partial coloring method often (but not always!) gives tight bounds on
discrepancy, its classic variant does not give efficient constructions
of low discrepancy colorings. In Chapter4, we introduce two recent
algorithmic variants of the partial coloring method: one is based on
randomizing the linear algebraic method due to Lovett and Meka, and another is an algorithmic
version of Giannopoulos’s partial coloring theorem, due to Rothvoss. We also explain how semidefinite programming can be used to efficiently construct colorings whose discrepancy is competitive against the hereditary discrepancy.

Then, in
Chapter5, we introduce a method of Banaszczyk to prove discrepancy upper bounds that goes beyond partial colorings. We
describe Banaszczyk’s original proof and the powerful geometric ideas
it utilizes in Chapter6. Banaszczyk’s proof does not
give an efficient coloring algorithm, but we give another,
algorithmic, proof of his main result in
Chapter9based on the so-called Gram-
Schmidt Walk. Before this, we also give a different algorithmic proof of the bound it implies for the Komlós conjecture (Problem2) in Chapter8, based on using semidefinite programming.

In Chapter7, we turn to
the computational complexity of approximating discrepancy and
hereditary discrepancy, based onγ2\gamma_{2}-norms from convex geometry. We show that an optimal application of
Banaszczyk’s theorem allows approximating hereditary discrepancy of
arbitrary set systems and matrices up to polylogarithmic
factors.

Finally, in Chapter10we consider a model in
which the elements to be colored arrive online and must be colored as
they come. We describe the Self-Balancing Walk algorithm, which shows that almost optimal discrepancy upper bounds hold even in
this online setting.
