## 1.7Bibliographic Notes

Epsilon approximations were defined by Vapnik and Chervonenkis[125]. Discrepancy theory was used by Matoušek, Welzl, and Wernisch to construct smallε\varepsilon-approximations of families of sets of bounded VC dimension[92]. Their argument is analogous to our proof of Theorem1.3. A general version of this argument can be found in Matoušek’s book[85].

Problem1was first asked by Tusnády in the early
1980s. In particular, he asked ifdisc⁡(ℛ2,n)≲1\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)\lesssim 1. Beck
answered Tusnády’s question negatively[27], by proving thatdisc⁡(ℛ2,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)is at least a universal constant times the minimum
Lebesgue measure discrepancy of annn-point set in the plane. Here,
the Lebesgue measure discrepancy of a setP⊆[0,1]dP\subseteq[0,1]^{d}of
sizennequalssupR∈ℛd||R∩P|−n​λd​(R)|\sup_{R\in\mathcal{R}_{d}}||R\cap P|-n\lambda^{d}(R)|,
whereλd\lambda^{d}is the Lebesgue measure inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}. Beck’s proof that Lebesgue measure discrepancy is bounded byO​(disc⁡(ℛ2,n))O(\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n))uses an argument similar to the proof of Theorem1.3.
Schmidt[110]proved the Lebesgue measure
discrepancy of anynn-point setPPin[0,1]2[0,1]^{2}is at leastΩ​(log⁡n)\Omega(\log n), which is tight. Together with Beck’s result, Schmidt’s lower bound shows thatdisc⁡(ℛ2,n)=Ω​(log⁡n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n)=\Omega(\log n). This remains the best known lower
bound ondisc⁡(ℛ2,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{2},n). In higher dimensions, Matoušek,
Nikolov, and Talwar proved thatdisc(ℛd,n)≳log(n)d−1\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\gtrsim\log(n)^{d-1}in any constant dimensiondd[91]. We will cover this lower
bound in Chapter7. The strongest known upper bound for Tusnády’s
problem isdisc(ℛd,n)≲log(2n)d−0.5\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\lesssim\log(2n)^{d-0.5}, and is due to
Nikolov[98]. We cover it in Chapter5.

It is worth noting also that determining tight bounds on the best
possible Lebesgue measure discrepancy (with respect to axis-aligned
boxes) of annn-point set in[0,1]d[0,1]^{d}is a notorious open problem ford>2d>2,
and the gaps in high dimensions are much bigger than they are fordisc⁡(ℛd,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n). The
best upper bounds are inO(log(n)d−1)O(\log(n)^{d-1})and the best known lower
bounds are inΩ(log(n)(d−1)/2+ηd)\Omega(\log(n)^{(d-1)/2+\eta_{d}})for someηd>0\eta_{d}>0that goes to0withdd[33]. We refer to
the books of Beck and Chen[26], and
Matoušek[85]for further discussion of
this fascinating problem.

Theorem1.4is due to
Lovász, Spencer, and Vesztergombi, who also defined the notion of
hereditary discrepancy[82].

The vector
balancing question can be traced back to work by Anatole Beck, who
showed an equivalence between the strong law of large numbers holding
in a Banach spaceXX, and the condition thatminx∈{−1,+1}n⁡‖x​(1)​v1+…+x​(n)​vn‖X≤c​n\min_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}\|x(1)v_{1}+\ldots+x(n)v_{n}\|_{X}\leq cnfor
some fixed constantc<1c<1, allnn, and allv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}in
the unit ball ofXX[25]. Around the same time, Dvoretzky asked what can be said in
general about the maximum of the quantityminx∈{−1,1}n⁡‖x​(1)​v1+…+x​(n)​vn‖X\min_{x\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}\|x(1)v_{1}+\ldots+x(n)v_{n}\|_{X}over allv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}in the unit ball ofXX(see the Unsolved Problems chapter of[1]).
Vector balancing problems were
investigated by Gluskin[52], Giannopoulos[51],
Banaszczyk[9,10], Dadush, Nikolov, Talwar,
Tomczak-Jaegermann[45], Reis and Rothvoss[104], among others.

The approximate Carathéodory theorem (proved with a different
argument) seems to be due to Maurey, see, e.g.,[101]. Maurey’s proof, covered, for example, by
Vershynin[126], is based on randomly sampling from the
distribution given by the coefficients of a convex
combination. Similar results can be shown also using optimization
techniques, either using the Frank-Wolfe algorithm[42], or using mirror
descent[93]. Theorem1.5is due to Dadush, Nikolov, Talwar,
Tomczak-Jaegermann[45], who also first observed the
connection to approximate Carathéodory theorems. This connection was
further pursued by Reis and Rothvoss[103].

A bound independent ofnnon the Steinitz constantstX⁡((ui)i=1n)\operatorname{st}_{X}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n})for any finite
dimensional normXXand vectorsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}in unit ball,
was first proved by Steinitz[118]. He needed such a
bound as a lemma in his higher-dimensional generalization of Riemann’s theorem that any
conditionally convergent series inℝ\mathbb{R}can be rearranged to converge
to any number inℝ\mathbb{R}. Steinitz used his lemma to show that the set of
values to which the rearrangements of a series of vectors inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}converges is an affine subspace ofℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}. This result, and the lemma,
were rediscovered later by Kadec[64]. The best bound for
the Steinitz constant in terms of the dimensionmmof the normed spaceXXismm, and is due to Sevastjanov
and Grinberg[111,53]. Theorem1.8is due to Chobanyan[41], and an algorithmic version of it was described by Harvey and Samadi[59].

## 1.8Recent Work not Covered Here

Several results closely related to the topics covered in this book have been made public since the writing of the book was completed. Aden-Ali[2]presented a computationally efficient (in fact, linear time) online discrepancy minimization algorithm that satisfies the same discrepancy guarantees as the inefficient algorithm of Kulkarni, Reis, and Rothvoss[67], and significantly improves on the algorithm presented in Chapter10. The algorithm of Aden-Ali (discovered by ChatGPT 5.5-Pro) gives a new constructive proof of Theorem5.15, and resolves several open problems in this book.

Shortly after the paper by Aden-Ali became available online, Altschuler and Tikhomirov[5]used similar techniques to give improved bounds for the Beck-Fiala problem, showing that the Beck-Fiala Conjecture (see Chapter2) holds for set systems onnnelements of degree at leastΩ(log(n)1+η)\Omega(\log(n)^{1+\eta})for any fixedη>0\eta>0.

## Chapter 2Linear Algebraic Methods

In this chapter we see the first example of a general and flexible
method for bounding the discrepancy of set systems and matrices. The
method is based on linear algebraic arguments, and happens to also
give efficient deterministic algorithms to compute low discrepancy
colorings. It is very versatile, and allows proving non-trivial, and
sometimes even tight bounds in many different settings: we will use it
to bound the discrepancy of set systems with bounded degree, and for systems of
axis-aligned boxes, and of set systems induced by permutations, and to
give bounds for vector balancing and for the Steinitz problem in
arbitrary norms. Some of the ideas developed here will form the basis of
more powerful randomized algorithms presented in later chapters.

## 2.1The Beck-Fiala Theorem

Recall that a set system is a pair(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U}), whereSS\SSis a
collection of subsets of the (finite) universe𝒰\mathcal{U}. Let us use the notationdeg⁡(u)\operatorname{deg}(u)for the degree ofu∈𝒰u\in\mathcal{U}, i.e., the number
of sets inSS\SSthatuuappears in. The maximum degreedeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)ofSS\SSis the maximum degreedeg⁡(u)\operatorname{deg}(u)over allu∈𝒰u\in\mathcal{U}. The maximum degree ofSS\SSis a natural measure
of how sparse it is, and we will see that small degree set systems
have a low discrepancy. The next exercise is the easiest interesting
case of this phenomenon.

## Exercise 2.1.

Given an undirected graphG=(V,E)G=(V,E), defineSS\SSwith universe𝒰=E\mathcal{U}=Eand setsSuS_{u},
for each vertexu∈Vu\in V, consisting of all edges with endpointuu. Thendeg⁡(SS)=2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)=2. Show that ifGGis
bipartite, thendisc⁡(SS)≤1\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\leq 1. Show furthermore thatdisc⁡(SS)≤2\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\leq 2for all graphsGG.

More generally we have the following fundamental question in
discrepancy theory.

## Problem 4.

Determine the best possible bound on the discrepancydisc⁡(SS)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)of a set systemSS\SSas a function ofdeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS).

In this section, we prove the Beck-Fiala theorem, stated next.

## Theorem 2.2.

For any set system(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U})there exists a coloringxx,
computable in polynomial time, such thatdisc⁡(SS,x)≤2​deg⁡(SS)−1\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x)\leq 2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)-1.

It should be surprising thatanydiscrepancy bound can be shown purely in terms ofdeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS). A key
insight is that a set system with small maximum degree only has few “large” sets, i.e., sets of size≫deg⁡(SS)\gg\operatorname{deg}(\SS), as we show next.

## Lemma 2.3.

For any set systemSS\SSover a universe𝒰\mathcal{U}of sizenn, and
anys>0s>0,|{S∈SS:|S|>s}|<n​deg⁡(SS)/s.|\{S\in\SS:|S|>s\}|<n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/s.

## Proof.

LetI:={(S,u):u∈S}I:=\{(S,u):u\in S\}be the set of
set-element incidences inSS\SS. We will count|I||I|in two
different ways. On the one hand,|I|≤n​deg⁡(SS)|I|\leq n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)as each
element of𝒰\mathcal{U}contributes at mostdeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)incidences.
On the other hand, denoting bykkthe number of sets inSS\SSof size greater thanss, we have|I|>k​s|I|>ks.
Combining the two inequalities givesk​s<n​deg⁡(SS)ks<n\operatorname{deg}(\SS), as desired.
∎

The proof of the Beck-Fiala theorem relies on two observations. The
first is that a set system consisting only of small sets has small
discrepancy. Indeed,anycoloring of a set of sizesshas
discrepancy at mostss. The second observation is that requiring that a few sets have small discrepancy still leaves many degrees of freedom for the coloring. In this chapter we formalize this second observation using linear algebra. In Chapter3, we will see a different formalization through geometry and Gaussian measure, and in Chapter4we combine the two approaches.

## 2.1.1The Beck-Fiala Algorithm

We now prove Theorem2.2, using a linear algebraic approach.

The key idea that enables using linear algebra is to relax the concept of a coloring tofractional coloringx∈[−1,+1]𝒰x\in[-1,+1]^{\mathcal{U}}. The algorithm will compute a sequence of fractional coloringsx0,…,xTx_{0},\ldots,x_{T},
where initially,x0x_{0}assigns color0to every element, and at each steptt,xtx_{t}colors at least one more element with±1\pm 1thanxt−1x_{t-1}. So by stepT≤nT\leq n,xT∈{−1,+1}𝒰x_{T}\in\{-1,+1\}^{\mathcal{U}}.

Whenever some colorxt​(u)x_{t}(u)reaches{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}, we fixxt​(u)x_{t}(u)forever, and we also maintain the invariant thatxt​(S)=xt−1​(S)x_{t}(S)=x_{t-1}(S)for all setsS∈SSS\in\SSthat have more thandeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)elements that are not yet colored±1\pm 1. These are the “dangerous sets” – with potential to incur large discrepancy. The existence of suchxtx_{t}will follow from Lemma2.3and a simple linear algebraic argument.

## The Formal Algorithm.

The coloringxxis computed by an iterative algorithm. At each steptt, the algorithm maintains a fractional coloringxt∈[−1,1]𝒰x_{t}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{U}}, a set of fixed elementsFt:={u∈𝒰:|xt−1​(u)|=1}F_{t}:=\{u\in\mathcal{U}:|x_{t-1}(u)|=1\}, and a set of “dangerous” sets𝒟t:={S∈SS:|S∖Ft|>deg⁡(SS)}\mathcal{D}_{t}:=\{S\in\SS:|S\setminus F_{t}|>\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\}.
Initially, we setx0:=0x_{0}:=0, and the algorithm terminates whenFt+1=𝒰F_{t+1}=\mathcal{U}, at which point we outputx:=xtx:=x_{t}.

To computextx_{t}fromxt−1x_{t-1}, in stept≥1t\geq 1, we find a nonzero update vectorΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}such that
- 1.

Δ​xt​(S):=∑u∈SΔ​xt​(u)=0\Delta x_{t}(S):=\sum_{u\in S}\Delta x_{t}(u)=0for all dangerous setsS∈𝒟tS\in\mathcal{D}_{t};
- 2.

Δ​xt​(u)=0\Delta x_{t}(u)=0for all fixed elementsu∈Ftu\in F_{t}.

We argue why such aΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}exists below. To compute the coloringxtx_{t}, we setxt=xt−1+γ​Δ​xtx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\gamma\Delta x_{t}, where we chooseγ>0\gamma>0to be the largest possible value such thatxt∈[−1,1]𝒰x_{t}\in[-1,1]^{\mathcal{U}}. See Figure2.1.Figure 2.1:We findxtx_{t}by starting fromxt−1x_{t-1}and going in the direction ofΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}until we hit a boundary of the cube[−1,+1]𝒰[-1,+1]^{\mathcal{U}}.

## Analysis.

Let us use the notationAt:=𝒰∖FtA_{t}:=\mathcal{U}\setminus F_{t}for the “active” elements, i.e., the ones not yet fixed. First we verify that, as long asFt≠𝒰F_{t}\neq\mathcal{U}(equivalentlyAt≠∅A_{t}\neq\emptyset), there exists aΔ​xt≠0\Delta x_{t}\neq 0satisfying1. and2. The two conditions form a system of linear equations, and to show that the system has a nonzero solutionΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}, we will argue that it is under-constrained. By Lemma2.3, applied to the set systemSS|At\SS|_{A_{t}}withs=deg⁡(SS)≥deg⁡(SS|At)s=\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\geq\operatorname{deg}(\SS|_{A_{t}}), we have that|𝒟t|<|At||\mathcal{D}_{t}|<|A_{t}|. Thus,Δ​xt\Delta x_{t}must satisfy at most|𝒟t|+|Ft|=|𝒟t|+(n−|At|)<n|\mathcal{D}_{t}|+|F_{t}|=|\mathcal{D}_{t}|+(n-|A_{t}|)<nlinear equations, and the subspace of solutions to these equations has dimension at least11. This shows that we can find someΔ​xt≠0\Delta x_{t}\neq 0satisfying1. and2.

Next, the algorithm terminates in at mostnnsteps asFt+1⊋FtF_{t+1}\supsetneq F_{t}, by the choice ofγ\gammaand thus|Ft||F_{t}|increases by at least11in each step.

Finally, we analyze the discrepancy of the final coloringx=xTx=x_{T}. Notice that, asFtF_{t}is increasing intt,𝒟t⊆𝒟t−1\mathcal{D}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{t-1}, i.e., a set can only stop being dangerous once, and it never becomes dangerous again. Since initiallyx0​(S)=0x_{0}(S)=0for allS∈SSS\in\SS, we have thatxt​(S)=0x_{t}(S)=0as long asS∈𝒟tS\in\mathcal{D}_{t}.
To bound the final discrepancy, consider an arbitrary setS∈SSS\in\SS, and suppose that it was last dangerous at time stept=t​(S)t=t(S), i.e.,S∈𝒟t∖𝒟t+1S\in\mathcal{D}_{t}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{t+1}. Then,|x​(S)|≤|xt​(S)|+|x​(S)−xt​(S)|=|x​(S)−xt​(S)|.|x(S)|\leq|x_{t}(S)|+|x(S)-x_{t}(S)|=|x(S)-x_{t}(S)|.

As the color stays unchanged for any elementu∈Ft+1u\in F_{t+1}after timett, we havex​(u)=xt​(u)x(u)=x_{t}(u)and thus|x​(S)−xt​(S)|=|∑u∈S∖Ft+1(x​(u)−xt​(u))|\displaystyle|x(S)-x_{t}(S)|=\Big|\sum_{u\in S\setminus F_{t+1}}(x(u)-x_{t}(u))\Big|≤∑u∈S∖Ft+1|x​(u)−xt​(u)|<2​|S∖Ft+1|≤2​deg⁡(SS).\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u\in S\setminus F_{t+1}}|x(u)-x_{t}(u)|<2|S\setminus F_{t+1}|\leq 2\operatorname{deg}(\SS).

The strict inequality follows sincex​(u)∈{−1,+1}x(u)\in\{-1,+1\}, andxt​(u)∈(−1,+1)x_{t}(u)\in(-1,+1)for anyu∉Ft+1u\not\in F_{t+1}, thusx​(u)−xt​(u)∈(−2,2)x(u)-x_{t}(u)\in(-2,2). The final inequality follows asS∉𝒟t+1S\not\in\mathcal{D}_{t+1}.

We have now shown that|x​(S)|<2​deg⁡(SS)|x(S)|<2\operatorname{deg}(\SS), and, as|x​(S)||x(S)|is an integer, we must have|x​(S)|≤2​deg⁡(SS)−1|x(S)|\leq 2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)-1. AsSSwas arbitrary, the result follows.

## Improvements.

Theorem2.2is not tight, and Beck and Fiala
conjectured that the tight bound isdisc⁡(SS)≲deg⁡(SS)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}. The best known bounds (for generaldeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)) that only depend ondeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)are linear indeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS), and, in fact, even the leading
constant22has not been improved.111Very recently, Bansal and Jiang[17]proved the conjecturedO​(deg⁡(SS))O(\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)})bound whendeg⁡(SS)=Ω​(log2⁡n)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)=\Omega(\log^{2}n).Later in this book we will see
bounds that have only mild dependence on the number of elementsnn.

For now, the next exercise gives a small improvement to
Theorem2.2.

## Exercise 2.4.

Modify the proof of Theorem2.2to show the improved
bounddisc⁡(SS)≤max⁡{2​deg⁡(SS)−3,deg⁡(SS)}\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\leq\max\{2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)-3,\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\}.

Hint:By an argument analogous to
Lemma2.3, a set system onnnelements with maximum degreesshas at mostnnsets with size at leastss. What happens when the
set system has exactlynnsuch sets?

Note that, unlike Theorem2.2,
Exercise2.4recovers the (tight) bounddisc⁡(SS)≤2\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\leq 2whendeg⁡(SS)≤2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\leq 2.

## 2.2Template of a Linear Algebraic Algorithm

In this section we generalize the approach taken in the proof of the Beck-Fiala theorem to formulate a template for an algorithm computing low discrepancy colorings using linear algebra. We will use it later for various applications. We keep the notation from the proof of Theorem2.2whenever possible.

## The Generic Algorithm.

We start with
some fractional coloringx0∈[−1,1]nx_{0}\in[-1,1]^{n}, usuallyx0:=0.x_{0}:=0.Then we construct a coloring inphases, where in thett-th phase we computextx_{t}.

In phasett, we define a matrixMtM_{t}ofdangerous directions, and a set ofactiveelementsAt⊆[n]A_{t}\subseteq[n]. The set offixedelements is alwaysFt:={i∈[n]:|xt−1​(i)|=1}F_{t}:=\{i\in[n]:|x_{t-1}(i)|=1\}, and we requireAt∩Ft=∅A_{t}\cap F_{t}=\emptyset. We choose the next coloringxtx_{t}so that
- 1.

Mt​xt=Mt​xt−1M_{t}x_{t}=M_{t}x_{t-1};
- 2.

xt​(i)=xt−1​(i)x_{t}(i)=x_{t-1}(i)for eachi∉Ati\not\in A_{t}.

We also aim to fix as many elements as possible, i.e., to maximize|Ft+1|−|Ft||F_{t+1}|-|F_{t}|. At the firstTTfor whichFT+1=[n]F_{T+1}=[n], we outputxTx_{T}.

Intuitively, each row ofMtM_{t}defines a “dangerous direction”, and moving the coloring in such a direction may incur too much discrepancy. For this reason we ask the coloring updateΔ​xt=xt−xt−1\Delta x_{t}=x_{t}-x_{t-1}to lie in the kernel ofMtM_{t}, which is our “safe subspace”.

The proof of the Beck-Fiala theorem follows the template above. In it, each phase fixes the color of at least one element, leading up tonnphases. In other applications, we may want to fix more elements in a single phase so that the number of phases becomes much smaller. To computextx_{t}fromxt−1x_{t-1}we shall use the following lemma.

Recall thatM​(∗,J)M(*,J)denotes the submatrix ofMMconsisting of the columns indexed by the setJJ.

## Lemma 2.5.

LetMMbe anm×nm\times nmatrix, letx∈[−1,+1]nx\in[-1,+1]^{n}, and letA⊆[n]A\subseteq[n]be such that for anyi∈Ai\in A,|x​(i)|<1|x(i)|<1. Ifrank⁡M​(∗,A)<|A|\operatorname{rank}M(*,A)<|A|, then there exists ax′∈[−1,+1]nx^{\prime}\in[-1,+1]^{n}such that
- 1.

M​x′=M​xMx^{\prime}=Mx;
- 2.

for anyi∈[n]∖Ai\in[n]\setminus A,x′​(i)=x​(i)x^{\prime}(i)=x(i)
- 3.

for at least|A|−rank⁡M​(∗,A)|A|-\operatorname{rank}M(*,A)elementsi∈Ai\in A, we havex′​(i)∈{−1,+1}x^{\prime}(i)\in\{-1,+1\}.

Moreover, givenMMandxx,x′x^{\prime}can be computed in
polynomial time.

The proof of Lemma2.5is simple and left as an exercise.

Similar to the proof of the Beck-Fiala theorem, we start initially withx0=0nx_{0}=0^{n}and iteratively update the coloringxtx_{t}toxt+1=xt+Δ​xtx_{t+1}=x_{t}+\Delta x_{t}at each step using Lemma2.5withx=xtx=x_{t}andx′=xt+1x^{\prime}=x_{t+1}.

One may wonder if we can gain something by picking the updateΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}more carefully. In Chapter4we will see an example of this, where the directions are picked randomly from some appropriate distribution.

Applying Lemma2.5to the incidence matrix of
a set system gives the following immediate corollary that we record for ease of reference.

## Lemma 2.6.

Let(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U})be a set system, letx∈[−1,+1]𝒰x\in[-1,+1]^{\mathcal{U}}be a
fractional coloring, and letA⊆𝒰A\subseteq\mathcal{U}be the set of elements with|x​(u)|<1|x(u)|<1. If|SS|A|<|A||\SS|_{A}|<|A|, then there is ax′∈[−1,+1]𝒰x^{\prime}\in[-1,+1]^{\mathcal{U}}such that
- 1.

for allS∈SSS\in\SS,x′​(S)=x​(S)x^{\prime}(S)=x(S);
- 2.

for allu∈𝒰∖Au\in\mathcal{U}\setminus A,x′​(u)=x​(u)x^{\prime}(u)=x(u);
- 3.

for at least|A|−|SS|A||A|-|\SS_{|A}|elementsu∈Au\in Awe havex′​(u)∈{−1,+1}x^{\prime}(u)\in\{-1,+1\}.

Moreover, givenSS\SSandxx,x′x^{\prime}can be computed in
polynomial time.

## Simple Application.

As an application of Lemma2.5, we first show
a useful structural result about discrepancy, that the
hereditary discrepancy of anym×nm\times nmatrix is always achieved, up to a
factor of22, on a submatrix consisting of at mostmmcolumns
fromMM. For this reason, discrepancy bounds in terms ofnncan often be automatically strengthened to hold in terms ofmin⁡{m,n}\min\{m,n\}instead.

## Theorem 2.7.

Let∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}be a norm onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, and letMMbe anm×nm\times nmatrix. Then there exists a setJ⊆[n]J\subseteq[n]of
size|J|≤m|J|\leq msuch thatdiscX⁡(M)≤2​herdiscX⁡(M​(∗,J))≤2​herdiscX⁡(M,m)\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M)\leq 2\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M(*,J))\leq 2\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M,m)

## Proof.

We apply Lemma2.5with the matrixMM,A=[n]A=[n], andx=0x=0. This gives us a fractional
coloringx′x^{\prime}for whichM​x′=0Mx^{\prime}=0, and the setJ:={i:|x′​(i)|<1}J:=\{i:|x^{\prime}(i)|<1\}has size at mostrank⁡M≤m\operatorname{rank}M\leq m.
Lety∈[−1,+1]Jy\in[-1,+1]^{J}bex′x^{\prime}restricted toJJ.
By Theorem1.4, there
exists some coloringz∈{−1,+1}Jz\in\{-1,+1\}^{J}such that‖M​(∗,J)​(z−y)‖X≤2​herdiscX⁡(M​(∗,J))\|M(*,J)(z-y)\|_{X}\leq 2\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M(*,J)).

We can then
extendx′x^{\prime}to a coloringx′′∈{−1,+1}nx^{\prime\prime}\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}by settingx′′​(i):={z​(i)i∈Jx′​(i)i∉J.x^{\prime\prime}(i):=\begin{cases}z(i)&i\in J\\
x^{\prime}(i)&i\not\in J.\end{cases}

We now havediscX⁡(M,x′′)≤‖M​x′‖X+‖M​(∗,J)​(z−y)‖X=2​herdiscX⁡(M​(∗,J)),\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M,x^{\prime\prime})\leq\|Mx^{\prime}\|_{X}+\|M(*,J)(z-y)\|_{X}=2\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M(*,J)),

as we needed to show.
∎

## 2.3Discrepancy of Permutations

For our next application of the linear algebraic method, we study the
discrepancy of set systems consisting of prefixes ofkkpermutations. We are givenkkpermutationsπ1,…,πk\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}of[n][n], and the set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])consists of prefixes of
the permutations, i.e., all sets of
the form{πi​(1),…,πi​(j)}\{\pi_{i}(1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(j)\}whereiiandjjrange over[k][k]and[n][n], respectively. We say thatSS\SSis the set system induced byπ1,…,πk\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}. One of the
problems we will return to later in the book is determining the
discrepancy of such set systems.

## Problem 5.

For a set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])induced bykkpermutations of[n][n], determine the best possible bound ondisc⁡(SS)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)as a
function ofnnandkk.

In addition to being
natural set systems in their own right,kkpermutation systems are
useful when studying the discrepancy of rectangles, and they have
applications to approximation algorithms. We will see the first connection later in this chapter.

The discrepancy of set systems induced bykkpermutations exhibits
an interesting transition: it is equal to11whenkkis11or22, and can be as large asΩ​(log⁡n)\Omega(\log n)for anyk≥3k\geq 3. Whenk=1k=1, we can just colorπ​(1),…,π​(n)\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(n)with alternating colors, in this order, and the resulting
coloring clearly has discrepancy11.

The case of22permutations
is more interesting, and we briefly sketch the construction of a
coloringxxwith discrepancy11. The idea is to ensure, for
everyi∈[2]i\in[2], andj∈[⌊n/2⌋]j\in[\lfloor n/2\rfloor]thatx​(πi​(2​j−1))≠x​(πi​(2​j))x(\pi_{i}(2j-1))\neq x(\pi_{i}(2j)). Then, any prefixSSof either permutation can be partitioned into the pairs{πi​(2​j−1),πi​(2​j)}\{\pi_{i}(2j-1),\pi_{i}(2j)\}, and at most one additional element, and each pair
contributes discrepancy0tox​(S)x(S).

To find such a coloringxxwe construct a graphG=([n],E)G=([n],E), where the
edge setEEconsists of all such pairs{πi​(2​j−1),πi​(2​j)}\{\pi_{i}(2j-1),\pi_{i}(2j)\}.
Then, the coloringxxthat we
need corresponds to a proper 2-coloring of the vertices ofGG,
which exists asGGis a union of two
matchings, and hence a disjoint union of paths and even length
cycles.

## kkPermutations.

The combinatorial argument above does not extend to33or more
permutations, but the linear algebraic method allows us to prove anO​(k​log⁡n)O(k\log n)bound on the discrepancy ofkkpermutations.

The idea is to divide the permutations into intervals, and
make sure that each interval receives zero total discrepancy, but with respect to a fractional coloring, rather than a full±1\pm 1coloring. In order to find such a fractional coloring using
Lemma2.6, we need that the total number of intervals whose discrepancy we keep at0is small. In particular, we make sure that the number of intervals is at most half the number of active elements, and thus we can fix half of them in each phase. This guarantees we can find a proper coloring inO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)phases, giving the logarithmic discrepancy bound.

Formally, we show the following.

## Theorem 2.8.

For any integersk≥1k\geq 1andn≥1n\geq 1, and any permutationsπ1,…,πk\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}of[n][n], there exists a coloringxx,
computable in polynomial time, such that for the set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])induced byπ1,…,πk\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}we havedisc⁡(SS)≲k​log⁡2​n.\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim k\log 2n.

## The Algorithm.

We apply our template. The initial coloring is set tox0:=0x_{0}:=0, and recall that the fixed elements areFt:={i:|xt−1​(i)|=1}F_{t}:=\{i:|x_{t-1}(i)|=1\}. We will always setAt:=[n]∖FtA_{t}:=[n]\setminus F_{t}. We computextx_{t}fromxt−1x_{t-1}using Lemma2.6, applied to the coloringxt−1x_{t-1}, the active setAtA_{t}, and a set systemℐt\mathcal{I}_{t}of at most|At|/2|A_{t}|/2intervals that we describe next.

To defineℐt\mathcal{I}_{t}, we construct a set systemℐi,t\mathcal{I}_{i,t}of size|ℐi,t|≤|At|/2​k|\mathcal{I}_{i,t}|\leq|A_{t}|/2kfor each permutationπi\pi_{i}, and setℐt=ℐ1,t∪…∪ℐk,t\mathcal{I}_{t}=\mathcal{I}_{1,t}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{I}_{k,t}. To constructℐi,t\mathcal{I}_{i,t}, take the interval{πi​(1),…,πi​(b1)}\{\pi_{i}(1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(b_{1})\}that contains exactly2​k2kelements ofAtA_{t}; then the next interval{πi​(b1+1),…,πi​(b2)}\{\pi_{i}(b_{1}+1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(b_{2})\}that contains2​k2kelements ofAtA_{t}, and so on, until no such interval remains.

We run our generic algorithm with these set systems until the first timeTTwhenFT+1=[n]F_{T+1}=[n], and at that point we outputx:=xTx:=x_{T}.

## Analysis.

Lemma2.6guarantees that, after phasett, at least|At|/2|A_{t}|/2new elements become fixed, i.e.,|At+1|≤|At|/2|A_{t+1}|\leq|A_{t}|/2. SinceTTis the smallest integer for whichAT+1<1A_{T+1}<1, we haveT≤log2⁡(2​n)T\leq\log_{2}(2n).

Next we bound how the discrepancy changes from one fractional coloring to the next. Let us fix some prefixS∈SSS\in\SS, whereS={πi​(1),…,πi​(j)}S=\{\pi_{i}(1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(j)\}. We can partitionS∩AtS\cap A_{t}into disjoint setsI1,…,Iℓ∈ℐtI_{1},\ldots,I_{\ell}\in\mathcal{I}_{t}and a “remainder” setRRof size at most|R|≤2​k−1|R|\leq 2k-1, whereI1,…,IℓI_{1},\ldots,I_{\ell}are all the setsℐi,t⊆ℐt\mathcal{I}_{i,t}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{t}fully contained inS∩AtS\cap A_{t}. So we must haveI1∪…∪Iℓ={πi​(1),…,πi​(b)}∩AtI_{1}\cup\ldots\cup I_{\ell}=\{\pi_{i}(1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(b)\}\cap A_{t}for someb≤jb\leq j, and also|{πi​(b+1),…,πi​(j)}∩At|<2​k|\{\pi_{i}(b+1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(j)\}\cap A_{t}|<2k.
Therefore,R={πi​(b+1),…,πi​(j)}∩AtR=\{\pi_{i}(b+1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(j)\}\cap A_{t}has size at most2​k−12k-1.

Now, using thatxt​(Ij)=xt−1​(Ij)x_{t}(I_{j})=x_{t-1}(I_{j})for allI1,…,IℓI_{1},\ldots,I_{\ell}, we have|xt​(S)−xt−1​(S)|\displaystyle\left|x_{t}(S)-x_{t-1}(S)\right|≤∑j=1ℓ|xt​(Ij)−xt−1​(Ij)|+|xt​(R)−xt−1​(R)|\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|x_{t}(I_{j})-x_{t-1}(I_{j})|+|x_{t}(R)-x_{t-1}(R)|=|xt​(R)−xt−1​(R)|≤4​k−2.\displaystyle=|x_{t}(R)-x_{t-1}(R)|\leq 4k-2.(2.1)

As there areT=log2⁡(2​n)T=\log_{2}(2n)phases,
andx0​(S)=0x_{0}(S)=0,
the
final discrepancy ofSSis at most|xT​(S)|≤∑t=1T|xt​(S)−xt−1​(S)|=O​(k​log⁡n).|x_{T}(S)|\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|x_{t}(S)-x_{t-1}(S)\right|=O(k\log n).

## 2.4Discrepancy of Boxes

In this section we apply the Beck-Fiala theorem (Theorem2.2), as well as Theorem2.8, to give two simple bounds on the
discrepancy of axis-aligned boxes inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}. Let us recall some
notation from the Introduction. We defineℛd\mathcal{R}_{d}to be the set of
all axis-aligned boxes inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, i.e., all sets of the typeRa,b:={u∈ℝd:a≤u≤b},R_{a,b}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:a\leq u\leq b\},wherea,b∈ℝda,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, anda≤u≤ba\leq u\leq bis understood to hold
coordinate by coordinate, i.e.,a​(i)≤u​(i)≤b​(i)a(i)\leq u(i)\leq b(i)for alli∈[d]i\in[d]. A setPPofnnpoints inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}induces the set
systemℛd|P={Ra,b∩P:a,b∈ℝd}\mathcal{R}_{d}|_{P}=\{R_{a,b}\cap P:a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}of subsets
ofPPinduced by boxes. Recall Problem1, asking
us to determine tight bounds ondisc(ℛd,n):=sup{disc(ℛd|P):P⊆ℝd,|P|=n}.\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n):=\sup\{\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d}|_{P}):P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},|P|=n\}.

To bounddisc⁡(ℛd,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n), it is slightly easier to restrict the sets to𝒜d|P:={R0,b∩P:b∈ℝd}\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P}:=\{R_{0,b}\cap P:b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}of anchored boxes, i.e., boxes with one corner fixed at the origin, and bounddisc(𝒜d,n):=sup{disc(𝒜d|P):P⊆ℝd,|P|=n}.\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d},n):=\sup\{\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P}):P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},|P|=n\}.This suffices, as each axis-aligned rectangle can be expressed as a±\pmcombination of2d2^{d}anchored boxes (we leave the simple proof as an exercise), and thus
we have the inequalitiesdisc⁡(𝒜d,n)≤disc⁡(ℛd,n)≤2d​disc⁡(𝒜d,n).\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d},n)\leq\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\leq 2^{d}\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d},n).

Moreover, we can always modify the points inPPso thatPPis contained in the grid[n]d[n]^{d}, without changing the set system𝒜d|P\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P}. This also gives usdisc(𝒜d,n)=sup{disc(𝒜d|P):P⊆[n]d,|P|=n}.\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d},n)=\sup\{\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P}):P\subseteq[n]^{d},|P|=n\}.

We will make use of the method ofcanonical intervals. For a non-negative
integerkk, we define𝒞k\mathcal{C}_{k}to consist of consecutive disjoint intervals (restricted to the integers) of size2k2^{k}. For a givennn, we can take the set
system𝒞\mathcal{C}to consist of all the intervals in⋃k=0⌊log2⁡n⌋𝒞k\bigcup_{k=0}^{\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor}\mathcal{C}_{k}that are entirely contained in[n][n].𝒞\mathcal{C}has small degree:
because eachi∈[n]i\in[n]is contained in a unique canonical interval
in𝒞k\mathcal{C}_{k}, the degree of𝒞\mathcal{C}is bounded asdeg⁡(𝒞)≤1+log2⁡n\operatorname{deg}(\mathcal{C})\leq 1+\log_{2}n. It is an easy exercise to show that every anchored intervalI={1,…,i}⊆[n]I=\{1,\ldots,i\}\subseteq[n]can be decomposed into the union of at most1+log2⁡n1+\log_{2}ndisjoint intervals from𝒞\mathcal{C}, each from a different𝒞k\mathcal{C}_{k}.

A typical application of canonical intervals is given in the next theorem, which applies the Beck-Fiala theorem to bound the discrepancy of boxes. We only give a proof sketch and leave the details as an exercise.

## Theorem 2.9.

For any integersn≥1n\geq 1andd≥2d\geq 2,disc(ℛd,n)≲log(2n)2​d\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\lesssim\log(2n)^{2d}, and, moreover, for any
fixeddd, a coloring of any setPPofnnpoints inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}achieving this discrepancy can be computed in time polynomial innn.

## Proof Sketch.

For simplicity, we sketch the proof in the cased=2d=2, which already captures the key ideas.
As we saw above it is enough to bound the discrepancy of the set system𝒜2|P\mathcal{A}_{2}|_{P}induced by anchored boxes on arbitrarynn-point subsetPPof[n]2[n]^{2}. In order to use the Beck-Fiala theorem, we instead consider a set system with low degree. To this end, let𝒞​ℛ2:=𝒞×𝒞\mathcal{CR}_{2}:=\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}be the set system of canonical rectangles, i.e., rectangles whose sides are given by canonical intervals, and letSS:=𝒞​ℛ2|P\SS:=\mathcal{CR}_{2}|_{P}be its restriction toPP. Then, since𝒞\mathcal{C}has degree at most1+log2⁡n1+\log_{2}n,𝒞​ℛ2\mathcal{CR}_{2}, and therefore alsoSS\SS, has degree at most(1+log2⁡n)2(1+\log_{2}n)^{2}. The Beck-Fiala theorem (Theorem2.2) then implies thatdisc(SS)≲log(2n)2\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\log(2n)^{2}.

Moreover, since any anchored interval in[n][n]can be written as the disjoint union of at most1+log2⁡n1+\log_{2}ncanonical intervals, we have that any anchored rectangleA∈𝒜2A\in\mathcal{A}_{2}in[n]2[n]^{2}can be written as the disjoint union of at most(1+log2⁡n)2(1+\log_{2}n)^{2}canonical rectangles from𝒞​ℛ2\mathcal{CR}_{2}. Restricting toPP, this means that any set in𝒜2|P\mathcal{A}_{2}|_{P}is the disjoint union of at most(1+log2⁡n)2(1+\log_{2}n)^{2}sets fromSS\SS. Therefore, the coloring that achieves discrepancydisc(SS)≲log(2n)2\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\log(2n)^{2}also givesdisc(𝒜2|P)≲log(2n)4\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{2}|_{P})\lesssim\log(2n)^{4}.
∎

We can get a better bound by encoding one dimension of the boxes as permutations, only using canonical intervals on the other dimensions, and using Theorem2.8in place of the Beck-Fiala theorem.

## Theorem 2.10.

For any integersn≥1n\geq 1andd≥2d\geq 2,disc(ℛd,n)≲log(2n)2​d−1\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\lesssim\log(2n)^{2d-1}, and, moreover, for any
fixeddd, a coloring of any setPPofnnpoints inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}achieving this discrepancy can be computed in time polynomial innn.

## Proof Sketch.

Once again, we focus on the cased=2d=2, leaving the extension to higher dimensions as an exercise. As before, we only need to bound the discrepancy of𝒜d|P\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P}for an arbitrarynn-point setP⊆[n]2P\subseteq[n]^{2}. To reduce this
problem to the discrepancy of permutations, we constructm≤1+log2⁡nm\leq 1+\log_{2}npermutationsπ1,…,πm\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{m}ofPP, one for each integerkkbetween0and⌊log2⁡n⌋\lfloor\log_{2}n\rfloor. We will think of each permutation as an
ordering ofPP. The permutationπk\pi_{k}is defined so that all points ofPPwhoseyy-coordinates are in the same canonical interval in𝒞k\mathcal{C}_{k}are listed contiguously, and in increasing order of thexx-coordinate (see Figure2.2for an example).Figure 2.2:The permutationπ2\pi_{2}for a pointset in[8]2[8]^{2}, listing the
points withyy-coordinate in{1,2,3,4}\{1,2,3,4\}first, in
increasing order of theirxx-coordinate, and then listing the
points withyy-coordinate in{5,6,7,8}\{5,6,7,8\}. The bottom-left
corner is(1,1)(1,1).

IfSS\SSis the set system induced byπ1,…,πm\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{m}onPP, then, by Theorem2.8,disc(SS)≲mlog(2n)≲log(2n)2.\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim m\log(2n)\lesssim\log(2n)^{2}.

The theorem is now implied by the bounddisc⁡(𝒜d|P,x)≤2​(1+log2⁡n)​disc⁡(SS,x),\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P},x)\leq 2(1+\log_{2}n)\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x),(2.2)

valid for any coloringxxofPP.
Notice that, since we can express any interval of a permutation (i.e., a set
of the type{π​(i),…,π​(j)}\{\pi(i),\ldots,\pi(j)\}fori≤ji\leq j) as the
difference of two prefixes, the discrepancy of any interval of any
of the permutationsπk\pi_{k}is bounded by2​disc⁡(SS,x)2\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x).
To prove (2.2), it is then enough show that every anchored box in𝒜2|P\mathcal{A}_{2}|_{P}can be written as the disjoint union of1+log2⁡n1+\log_{2}nintervals of the permutationsπ1,…,πm\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{m}. Take an arbitrary anchored rectangleR0,b∩PR_{0,b}\cap P. Decomposing its vertical side[0,b​(2)][0,b(2)]into a disjoint union of canonical intervals allows us to writeR0,bR_{0,b}as a disjoint union(R1′∩P)∪…∪(Rℓ′∩P)(R^{\prime}_{1}\cap P)\cup\ldots\cup(R^{\prime}_{\ell}\cap P)ofℓ≤1+log2⁡n\ell\leq 1+\log_{2}nrectangles, where the vertical side of eachRi′R^{\prime}_{i}is a canonical interval, and the horizontal side is[0,b​(1)][0,b(1)]. The vertical side ofRi′R^{\prime}_{i}being a canonical interval implies that all points inRi′∩PR^{\prime}_{i}\cap Pare listed contiguously in one of the permutationsπk\pi_{k}, i.e., they form an interval ofπk\pi_{k}. This proves our claim, and therefore inequality (2.2) and the theorem.
∎

## 2.5Vector Balancing

Suppose that∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}is a norm onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, and recall our
notation for the discrepancy of anm×nm\times nmatrixMMmeasured in terms of∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}:discX⁡(M):=minx∈{−1,+1}n⁡‖M​x‖X.\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M):=\min_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}\|Mx\|_{X}.

Recall further that the vector balancing constant of a setK⊆ℝmK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}with respect to the normed spaceXXisvb⁡(K,X):=sup{discX⁡((ui)i=1n):u1,…,un∈K,n∈ℕ}.\operatorname{vb}(K,X):=\sup\{\operatorname{disc}_{X}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}):u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in K,n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

It is natural to setKKto be the unit ballBX={u∈ℝm:‖u‖X≤1}B_{X}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|u\|_{X}\leq 1\}ofXX, and ask howvb⁡(BX,X)\operatorname{vb}(B_{X},X)can be bounded
in terms ofmmand of geometric properties ofBXB_{X}. The triangle
inequality shows thatdiscX⁡(M)≤∑i=1n‖ui‖X≤n\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{i}\|_{X}\leq nfor any matrixM=(ui)i=1nM=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}with columns inBXB_{X},
and this bound holds foranycoloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}. This bound is weak whennnis much bigger thanmm, but
the linear algebraic method
allows us to reduce to then≤mn\leq mcase, and show a bound for an arbitrary normed spaceXXthat only depends on the dimension.

## Theorem 2.11.

Let∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}be a norm onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}with unit ballBXB_{X}.
Thenvb⁡(BX,X)≤2​m\operatorname{vb}(B_{X},X)\leq 2m, and moreover, for any matrixMMwith columnsu1,…,un∈BXu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in B_{X}, a
coloringxxachievingdiscX⁡(M)≤2​m\operatorname{disc}_{X}(M)\leq 2mcan
be computed in polynomial time.

LetBpm={u∈ℝm:‖u‖p≤1}B_{p}^{m}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|u\|_{p}\leq 1\}be the unit ball ofℓpm\ell_{p}^{m}.
Then,vb⁡(Bpm,ℓpm)≲m1/p,\operatorname{vb}(B_{p}^{m},\ell_{p}^{m})\lesssim m^{1/p},for1≤p≤21\leq p\leq 2,
andvb⁡(Bpm,ℓpm)≲min⁡{p,log⁡2​m}​m\operatorname{vb}(B_{p}^{m},\ell_{p}^{m})\lesssim\min\{\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\log 2m}\}\sqrt{m}forp≥2p\geq 2.

## Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem2.11, letu1,…,un∈Bpu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in B_{p}, and define the matrixM:=(ui)i=1nM:=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}.
By Theorem2.7, it is enough to boundherdiscX⁡(M​(∗,J))\operatorname{herdisc}_{X}(M(*,J))for anyJ⊆[n]J\subseteq[n]of size at mostmm, i.e., it suffices to show the discrepancy bounds
withnnin place ofmm:1≤p≤2⟹discℓpm⁡(M)≲n1/p,\displaystyle 1\leq p\leq 2\implies\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{p}^{m}}(M)\lesssim n^{1/p},(2.3)p≥2⟹discℓpm⁡(M)≲min⁡{p,log⁡2​m}​n.\displaystyle p\geq 2\implies\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{p}^{m}}(M)\lesssim\min\{\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\log 2m}\}\sqrt{n}.(2.4)

Let us start with the Euclidean casep=2p=2. Then, forx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}chosen uniformly at random, we have the parallelogram law𝔼​‖M​x‖22=𝔼​‖∑i=1nx​(i)​ui‖22=∑i=1n‖ui‖22≤n.\mathbb{E}\|Mx\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}x(i)u_{i}\Big\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n.(2.5)

So there must then exist at least one coloringxxsuch that‖M​x‖22≤n\|Mx\|_{2}^{2}\leq n, and thusdiscℓ2m⁡(M)≤n\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{2}^{m}}(M)\leq\sqrt{n}.

Interestingly, an approximate version of the upper bound in
(2.5) holds for anyp≥2p\geq 2by the
type-22inequality forℓp\ell_{p}[75, Chapter
9]. The inequality states that, for a uniformly
randomx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n},𝔼​‖∑i=1nx​(i)​ui‖p2≲p​∑i=1n‖ui‖p2≤p​n.\mathbb{E}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}x(i)u_{i}\Big\|_{p}^{2}\lesssim{p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{i}\|_{p}^{2}\leq pn.(2.6)

This implies that, forp≥2p\geq 2,discℓpm⁡(M)≲p​n.\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{p}^{m}}(M)\lesssim\sqrt{pn}.

By the inequalities‖x‖q≤‖x‖p≤m1p−1q​‖x‖q\|x\|_{q}\leq\|x\|_{p}\leq m^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\|x\|_{q}, which hold for any1≤p≤q≤∞1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty, allℓpm\ell_{p}^{m}norms are equivalent up to a constant
forp≥log⁡mp\geq\log m. Therefore, forp≥max⁡{2,log⁡m}p\geq\max\{2,\log m\}, we also have the stronger bounddiscℓpm⁡(M)≲log⁡(2​m)​n.\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{p}^{m}}(M)\lesssim\sqrt{\log(2m)n}.This takes care of the casep≥2p\geq 2.

The appropriate analog of the type-2 inequality (2.6) for1≤p<21\leq p<2is the type-ppinequality, which states
that for a uniformly randomx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n},𝔼​‖∑i=1nx​(i)​ui‖pp≲∑i=1n‖ui‖pp≤n.\mathbb{E}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}x(i)u_{i}\Big\|_{p}^{p}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{i}\|_{p}^{p}\leq n.

This implies that, for1≤p≤21\leq p\leq 2, we havediscℓpm⁡(M)≲n1/p.\operatorname{disc}_{\ell_{p}^{m}}(M)\lesssim n^{1/p}.∎

The example of them×mm\times midentity matrix again shows that the
bounds in Theorem2.13are tight up to constants for1≤p≤21\leq p\leq 2. The example of an appropriately scaled Hadamard matrix (i.e., anm×mm\times mmatrixMMwith entries in{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}and pairwise orthogonal rows and columns) shows that the bounds are also tight up to themax⁡{p,log⁡2​m}\max\{\sqrt{p},\sqrt{\log 2m}\}term forp≥2p\geq 2: we leave the details of the argument as an exercise.

As we will see later however, a better bound holds forp=∞p=\infty(equivalently, forp≥log⁡mp\geq\log m): this is a landmark result of Spencer (Theorem3.5), which we prove in Chapter3.

Let us finally remark that the proof of the Beck-Fiala theorem
(Theorem2.2) can be generalized to show thatvb⁡(B1m,ℓ∞m)≤2\operatorname{vb}(B_{1}^{m},\ell_{\infty}^{m})\leq 2. We leave this generalization as an
exercise.

We recall here the Komlós problem
(Problem2from the Introduction), asking to determine
ifvb⁡(B2m,ℓ∞m)≲1\operatorname{vb}(B_{2}^{m},\ell_{\infty}^{m})\lesssim 1. A positive resolution of this problem also implies a
positive resolution of the Beck-Fiala conjecture mentioned earlier.
In general, it is an interesting problem to determine how the vector
balancing constantvb⁡(Bpm,ℓqm)\operatorname{vb}(B_{p}^{m},\ell_{q}^{m})depends onmmwhenppis much smaller thanqq.

## 2.6Prefix Discrepancy and Signed Series

Next, we recall the Steinitz problem, where givennn“short”
vectorsu1,…,un∈ℝmu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}whose sum is0, we ask
if the vectors can be rearranged so that all partial sums are
bounded independently ofnn. As we saw in the Introduction, the Steinitz problem is closely related to the
prefix discrepancy of a matrixM=(ui)i=1nM=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}, defined aspdiscX⁡(M):=minx∈{−1,+1}n⁡maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1jx​(i)​ui‖X,\operatorname{pdisc}_{X}(M):=\min_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}\max_{j=1}^{n}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}x(i)u_{i}\Big\|_{X},

where∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}is a norm. Namely, by
Theorem1.8(Chobanyan’s transference theorem),
the Steinitz
constant ofM=(ui)i=1nM=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{n},stX⁡(M)=minπ∈Sn⁡maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1jui−jn​∑k=1nuk‖X,\operatorname{st}_{X}(M)=\min_{\pi\in S_{n}}\max_{j=1}^{n}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}u_{i}-\frac{j}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{k}\Big\|_{X},

satisfies the boundstX⁡(M)≤maxπ∈Sn⁡pdiscX⁡(π∘M),\operatorname{st}_{X}(M)\leq\max_{\pi\in S_{n}}\operatorname{pdisc}_{X}(\pi\circ M),

whereπ∘M:=(uπ​(i))i=1n\pi\circ M:=(u_{\pi(i)})_{i=1}^{n}is the rearrangement of
the columns ofMMaccording toπ\pi. In other words, the
worst-case prefix discrepancy over re-arrangements of the columns ofMMbounds the Steinitz constant.

Let us recall the notation for the worst-case Steinitz constant
and the worst-case prefix discrepancy: for a setK⊆ℝmK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}, and a normed spaceX=(ℝm,∥⋅∥X)X=(\mathbb{R}^{m},\|\cdot\|_{X}), we havest⁡(K,X):=sup{stX⁡((ui)i=1n):u1,…,un∈K,n∈ℕ},\displaystyle\operatorname{st}(K,X):=\sup\{\operatorname{st}_{X}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}):u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in K,n\in\mathbb{N}\},ss⁡(K,X):=sup{pdiscX⁡((ui)i=1n):u1,…,un∈K,n∈ℕ}.\displaystyle\operatorname{ss}(K,X):=\sup\{\operatorname{pdisc}_{X}((u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}):u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in K,n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Chobanyan’s lemma then implies thatst⁡(K,X)≤ss⁡(K,X)\operatorname{st}(K,X)\leq\operatorname{ss}(K,X)for anyKKandXX. The next theorem bounds these by2​m2m.
This significantly strengthens Theorem2.11, by extending the bound there for discrepancy to that for prefix discrepancy.

## Theorem 2.14.

Let∥⋅∥X\|\cdot\|_{X}be a norm onℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}with unit ballBXB_{X}.
Thenst⁡(BX,X)≤ss⁡(BX,X)≤2​m.\operatorname{st}(B_{X},X)\leq\operatorname{ss}(B_{X},X)\leq 2m.

Moreover, for any matrixMMwith columnsu1,…,un∈BXu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in B_{X}, a
coloringxxachievingpdiscX⁡(M)≤2​m\operatorname{pdisc}_{X}(M)\leq 2mcan
be computed in polynomial time.

## The Algorithm.

Also, we can assume thatm<nm<n,

As usual, letxt−1x_{t-1}be the coloring at the beginning of steptt, and
initially,x0:=0x_{0}:=0. At each stept≥1,t\geq 1,we chooseAtA_{t}to consist of the firstm+1m+1indicesi1,…,im+1i_{1},\ldots,i_{m+1}satisfying|xt−1​(ij)|<1|x_{t-1}(i_{j})|<1for allj∈[m+1]j\in[m+1]. In particular, att=1t=1,A1:=[m+1]A_{1}:=[m+1].
In other words,AtA_{t}can be viewed as a sliding window of the firstm+1m+1strictly fractional coordinates.
At steptt, applying Lemma2.5toMM,xt−1,Atx_{t-1},A_{t}gives a fractional coloringxtx_{t}such that
- 1.

M​xt=M​xt−1Mx_{t}=Mx_{t-1};
- 2.

for alli∈[n]∖Ati\in[n]\setminus A_{t},xt​(i)=xt−1​(i)x_{t}(i)=x_{t-1}(i);
- 3.

We terminate whenever fewer
thanm+1m+1such indices are left – once that happens,

As the rank ofM​(∗,At)M(*,A_{t})is at mostm<|At|=m+1m<|A_{t}|=m+1, at each steptt, at least one variable inAtA_{t}reaches±1\pm 1, and the algorithm terminates in at mostnnsteps.

The key observation is that as long asAt⊆[j]A_{t}\subseteq[j], i.e., the sliding windowAtA_{t}is contained in the firstjjcoordinates, the discrepancydtd_{t}of this prefix stays zero.

To finish the analysis, notice that the final coloringxxcan differ
from the coloringxtjx_{t_{j}}in at mostmmcoordinates in[j][j]. This is because for any coordinatei≤ji\leq j,x​(i)x(i)can differ fromxtj​(i)x_{t_{j}}(i)only ifi∈Atj+1∩[j]i\in A_{t_{j}+1}\cap[j], and there are onlymmsuchiiby our choice oftjt_{j}.

## 2.7Bibliographic Notes

Theorem2.2is due to Beck and Fiala[31], who
also pioneered the linear algebraic method presented in this
chapter. Exercise2.4is due to Bednarchak and
Helm[32]. The best known upper bound on discrepancy purely in terms of
the degree is2​deg⁡(SS)−log∗⁡(deg⁡(SS))2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)-\log^{*}(\operatorname{deg}(\SS))and is due to
Bukh[37]. Very recently, Bansal and Jiang showed anO​(deg⁡(SS))O(\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)})bound wheneverdeg⁡(SS)=Ω​(log2⁡n)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)=\Omega(\log^{2}n). For generaldeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS), they obtain anO~​(deg⁡(SS)+log⁡n)\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}+\sqrt{\log n})bound, whereO~​(⋅)\widetilde{O}(\cdot)hides factors polynomial inlog⁡log⁡n\log\log n.
Their techniques also give anO​(log1/4⁡m)O(\log^{1/4}m)discrepancy bound for the Komlós problem.

The application of the Beck-Fiala theorem to the discrepancy of
axis-aligned boxes in Theorem2.9is due
to Beck[27]. Theorems2.8and2.10are due to
Bohus[34]. Beck asked whether thelog⁡n\log nterm can be
removed in Theorem2.8for33(or more)
permutations. He also proved that the discrepancy of22permutations
is at most11: see[115]. Newman, Neiman, and Nikolov
answered Beck’s question negatively and showed that theO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)bound is
tight for33permutations[96]. Franks gave a simplified proof of this
result[50]. While the dependence onnnin
Theorem2.8is tight, it is possible to improve the
dependence onkk[116], as we will see in the next
chapter. An upper bound on the discrepancy ofkkpermutations that is
simultaneously tight in terms of bothkkandnnis not known.

Theorems2.11and2.14are due to Bárány and Grinberg[21].
Sevastjanov, and Sevastjanov and Grinberg used linear algebraic
arguments to give better bounds for the Steinitz constant, showing
that it is bounded byddin anydd-dimensional normed
space[111,53]. Their proof develops a linear algebraic
argument directly for the Steinitz problem, rather than going through
discrepancy, and gives a better bound than
Theorem2.14. This argument, and more applications of
linear algebraic methods to vector balancing and related problems can
be found in Bárány’s survey[23].

Linear algebraic arguments similar to the ones in this chapter have
also found applications in the design of approximation algorithms for
computationally hard problems. An early example is the work of
Karmarkar and Karp on the bin packing
problem[66]. Many more algorithms using these
techniques are surveyed by Lau, Ravi, and
Singh[72].

## Chapter 3Partial Coloring Methods

The linear algebraic method from Chapter2is a powerful
tool for taking advantage of the structure of a set system when
proving discrepancy upper bounds. We saw that it can be used to give significantly improved
discrepancy upper bounds
over those
achieved by random colorings for many natural set systems, e.g.,
set systems of bounded degree, and set systems induced by rectangles
or by permutations.

However, it is not at all clear how to use this method to match, let alone improve the upper bounddisc⁡(SS)≲n​log⁡(2​n)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{n\log(2n)}for a set systemSS\SSofO​(n)O(n)subsets of a
universe ofnnelements, achieved by a random coloring.
In this
chapter, we develop the method of partial colorings, which combines the
power of randomization with the ability of the linear algebraic method
to take advantage of weak dependencies between sets in a set
system.
We give a number of applications of the method, including a
proof of Spencer’s Six Standard Deviations Suffice Theorem showing the discrepancy upper bound
above can be improved todisc⁡(SS)≲n\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{n}.

## 3.1Beck’s Partial Coloring Lemma

To build some intuition for partial coloring methods, we start with
the simplest of them: the partial coloring lemma of Beck.

A general
theme of discrepancy upper bounds is decomposing a set system into
a potentially large number of small sets, and a small number of
potentially large sets. We saw this in the proof of the Beck-Fiala
theorem in Chapter2, where we showed that a set systemSS\SSof maximum degreedeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)on a universe of sizennhas at mostnc\frac{n}{c}sets of size greater thanc​deg⁡(SS)c\operatorname{deg}(\SS). This is useful, since it is easier to control the
discrepancy of small sets, and when the large sets are not too many,
keeping their discrepancy low imposes a small number of constraints on
our colorings. In Chapter2we used linear algebra to
formalize this intuition. Beck’s lemma below gives another
formalization, based on a beautiful combination of combinatorial and
probabilistic reasoning.

In preparation, we prove a simple combinatorial lemma, showing that in
any large enough set of colorings, at least two colorings must
disagree on many elements. Stronger statements are known, but we state
one with an elementary proof, as it suffices for our needs.

## Lemma 3.1.

Suppose𝒞⊆{−1,+1}n\mathcal{C}\subseteq\{-1,+1\}^{n}is a set of colorings
of size|𝒞|≥2n/2|\mathcal{C}|\geq 2^{n/2}. Then there exist two coloringsx,x′∈𝒞x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}such that|{i:x​(i)≠x′​(i)}|≥n/10.|\{i:x(i)\neq x^{\prime}(i)\}|\geq n/10.

## Proof.

Let us fix anyx∈𝒞x\in\mathcal{C}. The
number of coloringsx′∈{−1,+1}nx^{\prime}\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}for which|{i:x​(i)≠x′​(i)}|≤n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq x^{\prime}(i)\}|\leq n/10is∑i=0⌊n/10⌋(ni)≤2n​H​(1/10)<2n/2≤|𝒞|.\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor n/10\rfloor}{n\choose i}\leq 2^{nH(1/10)}<2^{n/2}\leq|\mathcal{C}|.

whereH​(p):=−p​log2⁡p−(1−p)​log2⁡(1−p)H(p):=-p\log_{2}p-(1-p)\log_{2}(1-p)is the binary
entropy function, and we used standard estimates on the volumes of
Hamming balls (e.g. Proposition 3.3.3 in[56]).
Then there must be at least onex′∈𝒞x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}for which|{i:x​(i)≠x′​(i)}|≥n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq x^{\prime}(i)\}|\geq n/10.
∎

We are now ready to prove Beck’s partial coloring lemma. The lemma applies to set systemsSS\SSpartitioned into a set systemSS1\SS_{1}, typically consisting of the large sets, which should be relatively few, and a set systemSS2\SS_{2}, typically consisting of the small sets. The lemma guarantees that one can find a partial coloring (i.e., a coloring that’s allowed to assign color0to a constant fraction of elements), which has discrepancy0on sets inSS1\SS_{1}, and has discrepancy matching the one achieved by a random coloring onSS2\SS_{2}.

## Lemma 3.2.

LetSS1\SS_{1}andSS2\SS_{2}be set systems on a set𝒰\mathcal{U}of sizen≥3n\geq 3such that|S|≤s|S|\leq sfor allS∈SS2S\in\SS_{2}, and∏S∈SS1(2​|S|+1)≤2(n−1)/5.\prod_{S\in\SS_{1}}{(2|S|+1)}\leq 2^{(n-1)/5}.

Then there exists a partial coloringx:𝒰→{−1,0,+1}x:\mathcal{U}\to\{-1,0,+1\}such that
- 1.

x​(S)=0x(S)=0for allS∈SS1S\in\SS_{1};
- 2.

|x​(S)|≲s​log⁡(2​|SS2|)|x(S)|\lesssim\sqrt{s\log(2|\SS_{2}|)}for
allS∈SS2S\in\SS_{2};
- 3.

|{u∈𝒰:x​(u)≠0}|≥n/10.|\{u\in\mathcal{U}:x(u)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

## Proof.

Letx′x^{\prime}be chosen uniformly at random from{−1,+1}𝒰\{-1,+1\}^{\mathcal{U}}. By the union bound and Hoeffding’s inequality,ℙ​[maxS∈SS2⁡|x′​(S)|>t]≤2​|SS2|​e−t2/2​s.\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{S\in\SS_{2}}|x^{\prime}(S)|>t\right]\leq 2|\SS_{2}|e^{-t^{2}/2s}.

Whent:=2​s​ln⁡(4​|SS2|),t:=\sqrt{2s\ln(4|\SS_{2}|)},this is at most1/21/2, and therefore the set𝒞:={x′∈{−1,+1}𝒰:disc⁡(SS2,x′)≤2​s​ln⁡(4​|SS2|)}\mathcal{C}:=\left\{x^{\prime}\in\{-1,+1\}^{\mathcal{U}}:\operatorname{disc}(\SS_{2},x^{\prime})\leq\sqrt{2s\ln(4|\SS_{2}|)}\right\}

has size|𝒞|≥2n−1|\mathcal{C}|\geq 2^{n-1}.

On the other hand, the number of different values that the vector(x′​(S))S∈SS1(x^{\prime}(S))_{S\in\SS_{1}}can take asx′x^{\prime}ranges over{−1,+1}𝒰\{-1,+1\}^{\mathcal{U}}is at most∏S∈SS1(2​|S|+1)\prod_{S\in\SS_{1}}{(2|S|+1)}, which is bounded by2(n−1)/52^{(n-1)/5}by
assumption.
By the pigeonhole
principle, there must be a set𝒞′⊆𝒞\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}of colorings of𝒰\mathcal{U}of size|𝒞′|≥|𝒞|/2(n−1)/5≥24​(n−1)/5|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}|\geq|\mathcal{C}|/2^{(n-1)/5}\geq 2^{4(n-1)/5}such that any twox′,x′′∈𝒞′x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}satisfyx′​(S)=x′′​(S)x^{\prime}(S)=x^{\prime\prime}(S)for
allS∈SS1S\in\SS_{1}. As4​(n−1)/5≥n/24(n-1)/5\geq n/2forn≥3n\geq 3, applying Lemma3.1to𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}shows that there exist two coloringsx′,x′′∈𝒞′x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}that differ on at leastn/10n/10elements of𝒰\mathcal{U}.
We can now takex:=12​(x′−x′′)x:=\frac{1}{2}(x^{\prime}-x^{\prime\prime})as our
partial coloring. Asx​(u)∈{−1,1}x(u)\in\{-1,1\}wheneverx′​(u)≠x′′​(u)x^{\prime}(u)\neq x^{\prime\prime}(u),xxcolors properly at leastn/10n/10elements of𝒰\mathcal{U}, proving the third property ofxx. The
first property holds becausex′​(S)=x′′​(S)x^{\prime}(S)=x^{\prime\prime}(S)for allS∈SS1S\in\SS_{1}, sox​(S)=12​(x′​(S)−x′′​(S))=0x(S)=\frac{1}{2}(x^{\prime}(S)-x^{\prime\prime}(S))=0. The
second property holds sincex′,x′′∈𝒞x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}, so for
anyS∈SS2S\in\SS_{2}we have|x​(S)|≤12​(|x′​(S)|+|x′′​(S)|)≤2​s​ln⁡(4​|SS2|).∎|x(S)|\leq\frac{1}{2}(|x^{\prime}(S)|+|x^{\prime\prime}(S)|)\leq\sqrt{2s\ln(4|\SS_{2}|)}.\qed

Note that the proof of
Lemma3.2, unlike the linear algebraic arguments in
Chapter2, does not suggest an efficient algorithm to
find the partial coloring. The main issue is the use of the pigeonhole
argument on the exponentially large set of colorings𝒞\mathcal{C}to construct𝒞′\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Lemma3.2is a refinement of the discrepancy bounds we can
get from a uniformly random coloring. In particular, ifSS1\SS_{1}is
empty, then we can, with high probability, achieve the same
discrepancy onSS2\SS_{2}as in the lemma with a full uniformly random
coloring. Being able to set the discrepancy of some sets to0,
however, allows us to prove discrepancy bounds that are not achieved
by a random coloring (except with exponentially small probability). As an illustration, we
prove a bound for the Beck-Fiala problem. Later in the chapter
we will see more advanced techniques that allow proving sharper
bounds.

## Theorem 3.3.

For any set system(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U})with maximum degreedeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)and|𝒰|=n>1|\mathcal{U}|=n>1,
we havedisc(SS)≲deg⁡(SS)log(n)2\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}\log(n)^{2}.

## Proof.

Let us assume thatn≥3n\geq 3, as otherwise the theorem is
trivially true. Similarly, we can assume
thatdeg⁡(SS)≤n2\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\leq n^{2}, since the trivial upper
bound ofdisc⁡(SS)≤n\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\leq nholds for any coloring.
As in the proof of Lemma2.3,
the number of sets inSS\SSsatisfies|SS|≤n​deg⁡(SS)≤n3|\SS|\leq n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\leq n^{3}.

We defineSS1:={S∈SS:|S|>s}\SS_{1}:=\{S\in\SS:|S|>s\}andSS2:={S∈SS:|S|≤s}\SS_{2}:=\{S\in\SS:|S|\leq s\}for a parameterssthat we choose later. By Lemma2.3,|SS1|<n​deg⁡(SS)/s,|\SS_{1}|<n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/s,and, therefore,∏S∈SS1(2​|S|+1)≤(2​n+1)n​deg⁡(SS)/s=2n​log2⁡(2​n+1)​deg⁡(SS)/s.\prod_{S\in\SS_{1}}{(2|S|+1)}\leq(2n+1)^{n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/s}=2^{n\log_{2}(2n+1)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/s}.

In order to apply Lemma3.2, we need that the exponent
on the right is at most(n−1)/5(n-1)/5, so we sets:=C​log2⁡(2​n+1)​deg⁡(SS),s:=C\log_{2}(2n+1)\operatorname{deg}(\SS),for a sufficiently large constantC>0C>0.
Then the lemma gives us a partial coloringx:𝒰→{−1,0,+1}x:\mathcal{U}\to\{-1,0,+1\}with discrepancydisc⁡(SS,x)≲deg⁡(SS)​log2⁡(n)​log⁡(2​|SS2|)≲deg⁡(SS)​log⁡(2​n),\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x)\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)\log_{2}(n)\log(2|\SS_{2}|)}\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}\log(2n),

where we used the trivial
bound|SS2|≤|SS|≤n3|\SS_{2}|\leq|\SS|\leq n^{3}. Moreover,
Lemma3.2guarantees that the setA:={u∈𝒰:x​(u)=0}A:=\{u\in\mathcal{U}:x(u)=0\}satisfies|A|≤9​n/10|A|\leq 9n/10.

To obtain a full coloring, we can iterate this process. In
particular, asdeg⁡(SS|A)≤deg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS|_{A})\leq\operatorname{deg}(\SS), we can
inductively
find a coloringx′x^{\prime}of the restrictionSS|A\SS|_{A}, and assign the color inx′x^{\prime}to the elements that are given color0byxx. As there areO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)iterations, the overall discrepancy can increase by a factor at mostO​(log⁡n)O(\log n), giving the boundO(deg⁡(SS)log(n)2)O(\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}\log(n)^{2}), as desired.
∎

## 3.2A Stronger Partial Coloring Lemma, and Applications

While Beck’s partial coloring lemma is a powerful tool, it often gives
suboptimal bounds with large poly-logarithmic factors, as we saw in
the case of the Beck-Fiala problem. Intuitively, one reason for this
is that the lemma only allows setting two types of discrepancy bounds
for the partial coloring: a set either receives0discrepancy, or
the discrepancy it would get under a random coloring. More precise
bounds can be derived by allowing more freedom in setting discrepancy
bounds. In this section, we state a stronger partial coloring lemma
that follows this approach, and present some applications of it. We
prove the lemma later in the chapter, as a corollary of a more general
geometric result.

## 3.2.1Statement

Next we state this stronger partial coloring lemma. Rather than
restrict ourselves to set systems, we adopt a more general linear
algebraic formalism. The proof of Lemma3.4is deferred to Section3.3.

## Lemma 3.4.

Letv1,…,vm∈ℝnv_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}be such that‖vi‖2≤1\|v_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1for eachii, and letλ1,…,λm\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}be
positive reals. For anys>0s>0, define the functionh​(s):={1.5​exp⁡(−s2/8)s≥4ln⁡(20/s2)s<4.h(s):=\begin{cases}1.5\exp(-s^{2}/8)&s\geq 4\\
\ln\left(20/s^{2}\right)&s<{4}\end{cases}.(3.1)

If∑i=1mh​(λi)≤n/8\sum_{i=1}^{m}h(\lambda_{i})\leq n/8, then there exists a partial coloringx∈{−1,0,+1}nx\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}such that, for alli∈[m]i\in[m],|⟨vi,x⟩|≤λi​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}, and|{j:x​(j)≠0}|≥n/10|\{j:x(j)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

To apply Lemma3.4to a set systemSS:={S1,…,Sm}\SS:=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\}, we can choose eachviv_{i}to be the indicator vector of the setSiS_{i}, and, if we want the bound|x​(Si)|≤di|x(S_{i})|\leq d_{i},
we can setλi:=di/|Si|\lambda_{i}:=d_{i}/\sqrt{|S_{i}|}.

We can now compare
Lemmas3.2and3.4. Suppose thatSS1={S1,…​Sk}\SS_{1}=\{S_{1},\ldots S_{k}\}, andSS2={Sk+1,…,Sm}\SS_{2}=\{S_{k+1},\ldots,S_{m}\},
where any set inSS2\SS_{2}has size at mostss, and definev1,…,vmv_{1},\ldots,v_{m}as above. If we setλi=1/2​|Si|\lambda_{i}=1/\sqrt{2|S_{i}|}fori∈[k]i\in[k], andλi=8​ln⁡(12​|SS2|)\lambda_{i}=\sqrt{8\ln(12|\SS_{2}|)}for eachi∈{k+1,…,m}i\in\{k+1,\ldots,m\}, then a partial
coloringxxsuch that|⟨vi,x⟩|≤λi|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq\lambda_{i}for alli∈[m]i\in[m]would satisfy|x​(S)|<1|x(S)|<1, and thusx​(S)=0x(S)=0forS∈SS1S\in\SS_{1}, and also|x​(S)|≲s​log⁡(2​|SS2|)|x(S)|\lesssim\sqrt{s\log(2|\SS_{2}|)}forS∈SS2S\in\SS_{2}, as
in Lemma3.2. Lemma3.4guarantees such
a coloring if∑S∈SS1ln⁡(40​|Si|)≤(n−1)/8.\sum_{S\in\SS_{1}}\ln(40|S_{i}|)\leq(n-1)/8.

For comparison, the requirement in Lemma3.2can be re-written as∑S∈SS1log2⁡(2​|S|+1)≤(n−1)/5,\sum_{S\in\SS_{1}}\log_{2}(2|S|+1)\leq(n-1)/5,

which is of the same form. Thus, Lemma3.4allows us
to recover an analogous statement to
Lemma3.2. However, Lemma3.4gives
us much more freedom in setting theλi\lambda_{i}parameters. We
illustrate how this freedom translates to better discrepancy bounds
with the next two applications.

## 3.2.2Applications

## Spencer’s Theorem.

For our first application we consider an arbitrary set system(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U})ofm:=|SS|m:=|\SS|sets on a universe of sizen:=|𝒰|n:=|\mathcal{U}|. Recall that a random coloring achieves a discrepancy
ofO​(n​log⁡m)O(\sqrt{n\log m}), and, in the absence of additional structure,
it is reasonable to conjecture that this is tight. Surprisingly, this
is not the case, and a celebrated result of Spencer shows that, for
anym≥nm\geq n,disc⁡(SS)≲n​log⁡(2​m/n)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{n\log(2m/n)}. In particular, whenm=O​(n)m=O(n), we get a discrepancy bound ofO​(n)O(\sqrt{n}),
showing that the logarithmic term incurred by a random coloring is
unnecessary. In fact, Spencer showed that whenm=nm=nthe
discrepancy is at most6​n6\sqrt{n}, and his result is often
referred to as the Six Standard Deviations Suffice theorem.

Using Lemma3.4, we prove the following slightly stronger statement for matrices with bounded entries.

## Theorem 3.5.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixM∈[−1,+1]m×nM\in[-1,+1]^{m\times n},
wherem≥nm\geq n, we havedisc⁡(M)≲n​log⁡(2​m/n)\operatorname{disc}(M)\lesssim\sqrt{n\log(2m/n)}.

Spencer’s result follows withMMas the incidence matrix ofSS\SS.

The core of the proof of Theorem3.5is the
following partial coloring result.

## Lemma 3.6.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixM∈[−1,+1]m×nM\in[-1,+1]^{m\times n},
wherem≥nm\geq n, there exists a partial coloringx∈{−1,0,+1}nx\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}such that‖M​x‖∞≲n​log⁡(2​m/n)\|Mx\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{n\log(2m/n)}and|{i:x​(i)≠0}|≥n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

## Proof.

We use Lemma3.4, where vectorviv_{i}is theii-th
row ofMM, andλi:=8​ln⁡(12​m/n).\lambda_{i}:=\sqrt{8\ln(12m/n)}.Then the statement follows from Lemma3.4.
∎

We can show
Theorem3.5by iteratively applying
Lemma3.6to obtain
a full coloring. The key observation in the case of Spencer’s theorem
is that the discrepancy of partial colorings decreases rapidly
withnnand thus the discrepancy of the full coloring only increases by a constant factor.

## Proof of Theorem3.5.

Letxxbe as in Lemma3.6Then the setA:={u∈𝒰:x​(u)=0}A:=\{u\in\mathcal{U}:x(u)=0\}has size bounded by|A|≤9​n/10|A|\leq 9n/10. We inductively find a coloringx′x^{\prime}ofM​(∗,A)M(*,A), and assign colorx​(i)x(i)to anyi∉Ai\not\in A, and colorx′​(i)x^{\prime}(i)to anyi∈Ai\in A.
The overall discrepancy is then
bounded bydisc⁡(M)≲∑k=0⌊log10⁡n⌋n​(0.9)k​log⁡(2​m(0.9)k​n)≲n​log⁡(2​mn),\operatorname{disc}(M)\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\log_{10}n\rfloor}\sqrt{n(0.9)^{k}\log\left(\frac{2m}{(0.9)^{k}n}\right)}\lesssim\sqrt{n\log\left(\frac{2m}{n}\right)},

which proves the theorem.
∎

## The Beck-Fiala Problem.

Next, we see how to use Lemma3.4to give an
improvement to Theorem3.3.

## Theorem 3.7.

For any set system(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U})with maximum degreedeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS)and|𝒰|=n|\mathcal{U}|=n,
we havedisc⁡(SS)≲deg⁡(SS)​log⁡(n)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}\log(n).

Again, we will iteratively apply the following partial coloring bound.

## Lemma 3.8.

For any set system(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U})with maximum degreedeg⁡(SS)\operatorname{deg}(\SS), there
exists a partial coloringx∈{−1,0,+1}nx\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}such thatdisc⁡(SS,x)≲deg⁡(SS),\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x)\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{deg}(\SS)},and|{i:x​(i)≠0}|≥n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

## Proof.

Recall that, by Lemma2.3, for anys>0s>0we
have that the number of sets inSS\SSof size larger thanssis at
mostn​deg⁡(SS)/sn\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/s. We use this observation together with
Lemma3.4to find a partial coloring.

LetSS:={S1,…,Sm}\SS:=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\}, and, as usual, letviv_{i}be the indicator vector ofSiS_{i}. Letλi:=C​deg⁡(SS)/|Si|\lambda_{i}:=\sqrt{C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/|S_{i}|}for a large enough
constantC>0C>0to be decided later.
For any integerttbetween0and⌈log2⁡(n)⌉\lceil\log_{2}(n)\rceil, letI​(t)I(t)consist of thosei∈[m]i\in[m]for which|Si|∈(2t−1,2t]|S_{i}|\in(2^{t-1},2^{t}]. By
Lemma2.3,|I​(t)|<2​n​deg⁡(SS)/2t|I(t)|<2n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/2^{t}. We
can then write the sum in Lemma3.4as∑i=1mh​(λi)=∑t=0⌈log2⁡(n)⌉∑i∈I​(t)h​(λi)\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}h(\lambda_{i})=\sum_{t=0}^{\lceil\log_{2}(n)\rceil}\sum_{i\in I(t)}h(\lambda_{i})<∑t=0⌈log2⁡(n)⌉2​n​deg⁡(SS)2t​h​(C​deg⁡(SS)/2t),\displaystyle<\sum_{t=0}^{\lceil\log_{2}(n)\rceil}\frac{2n\operatorname{deg}(\SS)}{2^{t}}h\left(\sqrt{C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/2^{t}}\right),

where, for the inequality, we used that the functionh​(s)h(s)defined in (3.1) is non-increasing withss.
It is therefore enough to verify this∑t=0⌈log2⁡(n)⌉deg⁡(SS)​2−t​h​(C​deg⁡(SS)/2t)≤116\sum_{t=0}^{\lceil\log_{2}(n)\rceil}\operatorname{deg}(\SS)2^{-t}h\left(\sqrt{C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/2^{t}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{16}(3.2)

for a large enough constantCC. Consider the valuet∗:=log2⁡(C​deg⁡(SS)/16)t^{*}:=\log_{2}\left(C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/16\right), which is the value ofttfor whichC​deg⁡(SS)/2t=4.\sqrt{C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/2^{t}}=4.For anyt>t∗t>t^{*}, we havedeg⁡(SS)​2−t​h​(C​deg⁡(SS)/2t)=16C⋅2−(t−t∗)​ln⁡(5⋅2t−t∗/4).\operatorname{deg}(\SS)2^{-t}h\left(\sqrt{C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/2^{t}}\right)=\frac{16}{C}\cdot 2^{-(t-t^{*})}\ln\left(5\cdot 2^{t-t^{*}}/4\right).

It is clear that the sum over termst>t∗t>t^{*}isO​(1/C)O\left(1/C\right).

For
anyt≤t∗t\leq t^{*}, we havedeg⁡(SS)​2−t​h​(C​deg⁡(SS)/2t)=24C⋅2t∗−t​e−2t∗−t+1.\operatorname{deg}(\SS)2^{-t}h\left(\sqrt{C\operatorname{deg}(\SS)/2^{t}}\right)=\frac{24}{C}\cdot 2^{t^{*}-t}e^{-2^{t^{*}-t+1}}.

Again, it is easily seen that the sum over termst<t∗t<t^{*}isO​(1/C)O(1/C). Thus (3.2) holds for a large enoughCC.
∎

The next exercise extends Theorem3.7to a bound for
the Komlós problem.

## Exercise 3.9.

Modify the proof of Lemma3.8and
Theorem3.7to show that for anym×nm\times nmatrixMMwith columnsu1,…,unu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}such that‖uj‖2≤1\|u_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1for eachjj, we havedisc⁡(M)≲log⁡(2​n).\operatorname{disc}(M)\lesssim\log(2n).

Matoušek’s book[85]gives a number of other applications of
Lemma3.4.

## 3.3Proof of the Partial Coloring Lemma

Our next goal is to prove Lemma3.4. The original
proofs of the lemma, due to a sequence of works by Spencer, Boppana, and Matoušek, are
similar to the proof of Beck’s partial coloring lemma
(Lemma3.2), and relied on a combination of probabilistic, combinatorial,
and information theoretic arguments. Here we describe a different
geometric approach, due to Giannopoulos[51], that
allows us to prove essentially equivalent results, while being more
flexible. In Section3.4, we will see that using the geometric
arguments directly can also sometimes give easier proofs of existence of
good partial colorings than applying Lemma3.4.
Moreover, we will see later in the book that the geometric viewpoint is
helpful in developing efficient algorithms.

## 3.3.1A Geometric Partial Coloring Lemma

The first step we take is to encode discrepancy constraints as a
centrally symmetric convex bodyK⊆ℝnK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n},i.e., a convex
setKKwith non-empty interior such thatK=−KK=-K. For example,
ifMMis anm×nm\times nmatrix, the constraintdisc⁡(M,x)≤t\operatorname{disc}(M,x)\leq tcan be encoded asx∈t​Kx\in tKwhereK:={x∈ℝn:‖M​x‖∞≤1}K:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|Mx\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}. The
convexity and symmetry ofKKfollow from the triangle inequality,
homogeneity, and symmetry of theℓ∞m\ell_{\infty}^{m}norm. This encoding
of the problem is the key link between discrepancy and geometry
exploited in the arguments below.

Finding a good
partial coloring with discrepancy at mostttis now equivalent to
finding some point of{−1,0,+1}n\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}with many nonzero
coordinates inside ofKK. Intuitively, this task should be easier
whenKKis large, and the measure of largeness that will be
fruitful for us is Gaussian measure: the probability that a standard
Gaussian random vectorgglies inKK. We use the notationγn​(K):=ℙ​[g∈K]=(2​π)−n/2​∫Ke−‖x‖22/2​𝑑x\gamma^{n}(K):=\mathbb{P}[g\in K]={(2\pi)^{-n/2}}\int_{K}e^{-\|x\|_{2}^{2}/2}dx

for thenn-dimensional Gaussian measure.

We first need a standard lemma about how
Gaussian measure changes when we shiftKK.

## Lemma 3.10.

LetKKbe a centrally symmetric set inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}. Then,
for anyt∈ℝnt\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, we haveγn​(K+t)≥e−‖t‖22/2​γn​(K).\gamma^{n}(K+t)\geq e^{-\|t\|_{2}^{2}/2}\gamma^{n}(K).

## Proof.

By the symmetry ofKK, we haveK+t=−K+tK+t=-K+t, soγn​(K+t)\displaystyle\gamma^{n}(K+t)=12​(γn​(−K+t)+γn​(K+t))\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^{n}(-K+t)+\gamma^{n}(K+t))=(2​π)−n/2​∫K12​(e−‖t+x‖22/2+e−‖t−x‖22/2)​𝑑x\displaystyle={(2\pi)^{-n/2}}\int_{K}\frac{1}{2}(e^{-\|t+x\|_{2}^{2}/2}+e^{-\|t-x\|_{2}^{2}/2})\,dx≥(2​π)−n/2​∫K(e−(‖t+x‖22+‖t−x‖22)/4)​𝑑x\displaystyle\geq{(2\pi)^{-n/2}}\int_{K}(e^{-(\|t+x\|_{2}^{2}+\|t-x\|_{2}^{2})/4})\,dx(A.M.≥\geqG.M.)=(2​π)−n/2​∫K(e−‖t‖22/2−‖x‖22/2)​𝑑x=e−‖t‖22/2​γn​(K).∎\displaystyle={(2\pi)^{-n/2}}\int_{K}(e^{-\|t\|^{2}_{2}/2-\|x\|_{2}^{2}/2})\,dx=e^{-\|t\|_{2}^{2}/2}\gamma^{n}(K).\ \ \ \ \qed

We are now ready to prove a partial coloring lemma due to
Giannopoulos’s, that will in turn imply Lemma3.4.

## Lemma 3.11.

Suppose thatK⊆ℝnK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}is a centrally symmetric convex
body such thatγn​(K)≥2−n/4\gamma^{n}(K)\geq 2^{-n/4}.
Then there exists a partial coloringx∈{−1,0,+1}nx\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}such thatx∈2​Kx\in 2Kand|{i:x​(i)≠0}|≥n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

## Proof.

The idea will be to find a set𝒞⊆{−1,+1}n\mathcal{C}\subseteq\{-1,+1\}^{n}of size at
least2n/22^{n/2}such that any two coloringsx′,x′′∈𝒞x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}satisfyx′−x′′∈O​(1)⋅Kx^{\prime}-x^{\prime\prime}\in O(1)\cdot K. Thenx=(x′−x′′)/2x=(x^{\prime}-x^{\prime\prime})/2for anyx′,x′′∈𝒞x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}that
disagree in at leastn/10n/10coordinates gives the desired partial coloring.

Letggbe a standard Gaussian inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, and defineC​(g):=|{x∈{−1,+1}n:g∈x/2+K}|.C(g):=|\{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}:g\in x/2+K\}|.

We have𝔼​[C​(g)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[C(g)]=∑x∈{−1,+1}nγn​(x/2+K).\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}\gamma^{n}\left(x/2+K\right).

By Lemma3.10, each term on the right
is at leaste−n/8​γn​(K)≥e−n/8​2−n/4e^{-n/8}\gamma^{n}(K)\geq e^{-n/8}2^{-n/4}, and thus𝔼​[C​(g)]≥23​n/4​e−n/8≥2n/2.\mathbb{E}[C(g)]\geq 2^{3n/4}e^{-n/8}\geq 2^{n/2}.

Pick someggfor whichC​(g)≥2n/2C(g)\geq 2^{n/2}and let𝒞\mathcal{C}be the set{x∈{−1,+1}n:g∈x/2+K}\{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}:g\in x/2+K\}, i.e., the set whose size isC​(g)C(g). By Lemma3.1,
there must exist twox′,x′′∈𝒞x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}for which|{i:x′​(i)≠x′′​(i)}|≥n/10.|\{i:x^{\prime}(i)\neq x^{\prime\prime}(i)\}|\geq n/10.Then we take our partial coloring asx:=12​(x′−x′′)x:=\frac{1}{2}(x^{\prime}-x^{\prime\prime}). Note thatg−x′/2∈Kg-x^{\prime}/2\in K, or equivalently,x′/2−g∈Kx^{\prime}/2-g\in K, andg−x′′/2∈Kg-x^{\prime\prime}/2\in Kby the definition of𝒞\mathcal{C}and the symmetry ofKK. This
impliesx=(12​x′−g)+(g−12​x′′)∈2​K.x=\left(\frac{1}{2}x^{\prime}-g\right)+\left(g-\frac{1}{2}x^{\prime\prime}\right)\in 2K.

Moreover,x​(i)≠0x(i)\neq 0wheneverx′​(i)≠x′′​(i)x^{\prime}(i)\neq x^{\prime\prime}(i),
which happens for at leastn/10n/10choices ofii. This
proves the lemma.
∎

## Remark 3.12.

The constants in the statement of Lemma3.11, and also Lemma3.1,
were not optimized and are somewhat arbitrary. In general, for anyc<1c<1andγn​(K)≥2−c​n\gamma^{n}(K)\geq 2^{-cn}we can conclude
that there exists a partial coloringx∈Oc​(1)⋅Kx\in O_{c}(1)\cdot KwithΩc​(n)\Omega_{c}(n)nonzero coordinates.
The proof method, however, does
not extend toKKsuch thatγn​(K)≤2−n\gamma^{n}(K)\leq 2^{-n}.

The following exercise suggests a strengthening of
Lemma3.11.

LetK⊆ℝmK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}be a symmetric convex body such thatγm​(K)≥2−n/4\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 2^{-n/4}, and letM:=(ui)i=1nM:=(u_{i})_{i=1}^{n}be anm×nm\times nmatrix whose columns satisfy‖ui‖2≤1\|u_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1for allii. Prove that there exists a
partial coloringx∈{−1,0,+1}nx\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}such thatM​x∈2​KMx\in 2Kand|{i:x​(i)≠0}|≥n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

Lemma3.11to show that𝔼​[C​(g)]≥2−n/4​∑x∈{−1,+1}ne−‖M​x‖22/8.\mathbb{E}[C(g)]\geq 2^{-n/4}\sum_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}e^{-\|Mx\|_{2}^{2}/8}.

Now use the A.M.-G.M. inequality to lower bound the sum on

Exercise3.13allows us to defineKKas a set of
good discrepancy vectors as opposed to a set of good fractional

problem, we have anm×nm\times nmatrixMMall of whose columns
haveℓ2\ell_{2}norm at most11. Then settingK=[−t,+t]mK=[-t,+t]^{m}fort≥2​ln⁡(2​m)t\geq\sqrt{2\ln(2m)}givesγm​(K)≥12≥2−n/4\gamma^{m}(K)\geq\frac{1}{2}\geq 2^{-n/4}(forn≥4n\geq 4) and shows that there exists a partial
coloringxxsuch that‖M​x‖∞≲log⁡(2​m)\|Mx\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(2m)}. In Chapter5we will see a
