## Comparison with Random Coloring.

It is worth comparing Lemma3.11to what we can get from
a random coloring.
Suppose thatKKis a symmetric
convex set inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, andx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}is
chosen uniformly at random,i.e., every coordinate is an independent
and uniform sample from{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}. Let us denote by∥⋅∥K:=inf{t≥0:x∈tK}\|\cdot\|_{K}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:x\in tK\}the norm with unit ballKK.
Because a uniformly randomx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}is more concentrated
than a standard Gaussiang∈ℝng\in\mathbb{R}^{n},𝔼​‖x‖K\mathbb{E}\|x\|_{K}is dominated
by𝔼​‖g‖K\mathbb{E}\|g\|_{K}. More precisely, we have the inequality (inequality
(4.8) in Ledoux and Talagrand’s book[75])𝔼​‖x‖K≤π2​𝔼​‖g‖K.\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|x\|_{K}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathbb{E}\|g\|_{K}.

Therefore, there always exists afullcoloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}that lies inπ2​𝔼​[‖g‖K]⋅K\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathbb{E}[\|g\|_{K}]\cdot K. Since it is a Lipschitz function of a Gaussian, the random
variable‖g‖K\|g\|_{K}is tightly concentrated around its median
(see, e.g., Theorem 5.2.2 in[126]). From this, it is not
hard to show that whenγn​(K)≥12\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\frac{1}{2},𝔼​[‖g‖K]≲1\mathbb{E}[\|g\|_{K}]\lesssim 1, and, therefore, we can find a full
coloring in a constant scaling ofKK. (A similar argument is
carried out in detail in Chapter5.)

Thus, a random
coloring works well in the large measure setting ofγn​(K)≥12\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\frac{1}{2}. The power of Lemma3.11, and
the reason why it allows us to prove tighter bounds than possible by a
random coloring, is that it allows us to work in the “small ball”
setting, in whichγn​(K)\gamma^{n}(K)is allowed to be exponentially
small.

As a simple illustration, consider the incidence matrixMMof the set system of all prefix intervals{1,…,i}\{1,\ldots,i\},i∈[n]i\in[n], on the universe[n][n], andK:={x∈ℝn:‖M​x‖∞≤1}.K:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|Mx\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}.Then‖x‖K=maxi=1n⁡|x​(1)+…+x​(i)|,\|x\|_{K}=\max_{i=1}^{n}|x(1)+\ldots+x(i)|,

and‖x‖K≳n\|x\|_{K}\gtrsim\sqrt{n}with high probability for a
uniformly random coloringxx. This is much worse than the
discrepancy ofMM, which is just11forxxthat alternates
between−1-1and+1+1. On the other hand, classical small ball
inequalities imply thatγn​(t​K)≳e−c​n/t2\gamma^{n}(tK)\gtrsim e^{-cn/t^{2}}for a
constantc>0c>0, which is enough to getγn​(t​K)≥2−n/4\gamma^{n}(tK)\geq 2^{-n/4}for a constanttt. Thus, Lemma3.11is strong enough to
give a partial coloring with constant discrepancy.
Later, we expand on this example, and use the small ball inequality to show that any set system induced bykkpermutations has
a partial coloring of discrepancyO​(k)O(\sqrt{k}). Thekkpermutations
example will also show that Lemma3.11cannot be
strengthened to give a full coloring, since there are set systems
induced by33permutations on[n][n]that have discrepancyΩ​(log⁡n)\Omega(\log n).

## 3.3.2Gaussian Correlation

To derive Lemma3.4from Lemma3.11we
need a way to prove lower bounds on the Gaussian measureγn​(K)\gamma^{n}(K)of the convex bodyK:={x∈ℝn:|⟨vi,x⟩|≤0.5​λi​‖vi‖2∀i∈[m]}K:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq 0.5\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}\ \ \forall i\in[m]\}

under the assumptions of Lemma3.4. Then anyx∈2​Kx\in 2Kwould satisfy|⟨vi,x⟩|≤λi​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}. The only
property ofKKwe will use is that it is the intersection of slabs
of bounded width: we haveK=⋂i=1mSi,K=\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}S_{i},whereSi:={x∈ℝn:|⟨vi,x⟩|≤0.5​λi​‖vi‖2}S_{i}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq 0.5\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}\}is a symmetric slab,i.e., the region of widthλi​‖vi‖2\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}between two hyperplanes orthogonal toviv_{i}. The following key lemma, due to Sidak[127], shows
why this property is useful.

## Lemma 3.14(Sidak’s Lemma).

Suppose thatK=−KK=-Kis a centrally symmetric closed convex set inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, and thatS:={x∈ℝn:|⟨u,x⟩|≤t}S:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle u,x\rangle|\leq t\}is a
symmetric slab defined by the vectoru∈ℝnu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}. Then,γn​(K∩S)≥γn​(K)​γn​(S).\gamma^{n}(K\cap S)\geq\gamma^{n}(K)\gamma^{n}(S).(3.3)

In particular, ifK=⋂i=1mSiK=\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}S_{i}andS1,…,SmS_{1},\ldots,S_{m}are symmetric slabs defined asSi:={x∈ℝn:|⟨ui,x⟩|≤ti}S_{i}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle u_{i},x\rangle|\leq t_{i}\}for some vectorsu1,…,um∈ℝnu_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}s.t.‖ui‖2=1\|u_{i}\|_{2}=1for allii, andt1,…,tm>0t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}>0, thenγn​(K)≥∏i=1nγn​(Si)=∏i=1nγ1​([−ti,ti]).\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\prod_{i=1}^{n}\gamma^{n}(S_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\gamma^{1}\left(\left[-t_{i},t_{i}\right]\right).

Lemma3.14allows us to reduce estimating the Gaussian
measure ofKKto estimating Gaussian measures
of slabs, and it is easy to see that the Gaussian measure of a slab can be computed as the Gaussian measure of a one-dimensional interval. We can get a pretty good handle on the
latter using standard concentration inequalities, for example.

We first derive Lemma3.4from
Lemmas3.11and3.14. Then, later in this
section, we give a self contained short proof of
Lemma3.14.

## Proof of Lemma3.4.

As mentioned above, we work with the convex bodyK:=⋂i=1mSi,K:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}S_{i},defined by the slabsSi:={x∈ℝn:|⟨vi,x⟩|≤0.5​λi​‖vi‖2}S_{i}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq 0.5\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}\}. Equivalently, we can defineSi={x∈ℝn:|⟨ui,x⟩|≤0.5​λi}S_{i}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle u_{i},x\rangle|\leq 0.5\lambda_{i}\}

forui:=vi‖vi‖u_{i}:=\frac{v_{i}}{\|v_{i}\|}, and
Lemma3.14gives usγn​(K)≥∏i=1mγn​(Si)≥∏i=1mγ1​([−0.5​λi,0.5​λi])\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\prod_{i=1}^{m}\gamma^{n}(S_{i})\geq\prod_{i=1}^{m}\gamma^{1}\left([-0.5\lambda_{i},0.5\lambda_{i}]\right)

Using the inequalityℙ​[|g|≥s]≤e−s2/2\mathbb{P}[|g|\geq s]\leq e^{-s^{2}/2}for a
standard one-dimensional Gaussiangg, we haveγ1​([−0.5​λi,0.5​λi])≥1−e−λi2/8\gamma^{1}([-0.5\lambda_{i},0.5\lambda_{i}])\geq 1-e^{-\lambda_{i}^{2}/8}, and thusγn​(K)≥∏i=1m(1−e−λi2/8).\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\prod_{i=1}^{m}(1-e^{-\lambda_{i}^{2}/8}).(3.4)

We now have the inequalities1−e−s2/8≥{e−1.5​e−s2/8s≥4s2/20s≤4,1-e^{-s^{2}/8}\geq\begin{cases}e^{-1.5e^{-s^{2}/8}}&s\geq{4}\\
s^{2}/20&s\leq{4}\end{cases},(3.5)

where the right hand side ise−h​(s)e^{-h(s)}, for the functionh​(s)h(s)defined in (3.1).
Both inequalities can be
verified by observing that the functionfL,c​(t):=e−L​t−(1−c​t)f_{L,c}(t):=e^{-Lt}-(1-ct)is convex inttand satisfiesfL,c​(0)=0f_{L,c}(0)=0for allLLandcc. Then,fL,c​(t)≤0f_{L,c}(t)\leq 0for allt∈[0,a]t\in[0,a]if and only iffL,c​(a)≤0f_{L,c}(a)\leq 0. The first inequality then follows from
substitutinge−s2/8e^{-s^{2}/8}forttand settingL:=1.5L:=1.5,c:=1c:=1,a:=e−2a:=e^{-2}. The second inequality follows from substitutings2/8s^{2}/8fortt, and settingL:=1L:=1,c:=2/5c:=2/5,a:=2a:=2.

Plugging in (3.5) into (3.4), we getγn​(K)≥exp⁡(−∑i=1mh​(λi))≥e−n/8≥2−n/4,\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{m}h(\lambda_{i})\right)\geq e^{-n/8}\geq 2^{-n/4},

where the penultimate inequality is by assumption. The lemma now

∎

## Remark 3.15.

Lemma3.4clearly holds withh​(s):=−ln⁡(1−e−s2/2)h(s):=-\ln\left(1-e^{-s^{2}/2}\right)in place ofh​(s)h(s), and the functionh​(s)h(s)is simply an upper bound onh​(s)h(s). In applications, however,h​(s)h(s)is usually easier to use.

## Proof of Sidak’s Lemma.

We now take a slight detour and give a short proof of
Lemma3.14, following an argument of Giannopoulos (see
the proof and discussion in[120]). Let us first introduce one of the central
theorems in geometric analysis: the Prekopa-Leindler inequality.

## Lemma 3.16.

Letλ∈(0,1)\lambda\in(0,1), and letf,g,h:ℝn→ℝ≥0f,g,h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}be
three non-negative measurable functions satisfyingh​(λ​x+(1−λ)​y)≥f​(x)λ​g​(y)1−λh(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)\geq f(x)^{\lambda}g(y)^{1-\lambda}(3.6)

for allx,y∈ℝnx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}. Then we have∫ℝnh​(x)​𝑑x≥(∫ℝnf​(x)​𝑑x)λ​(∫ℝng​(x)​𝑑x)1−λ.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h(x)dx\geq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(x)dx\right)^{\lambda}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}g(x)dx\right)^{1-\lambda}.

We do not prove this inequality here, but refer the reader to Lecture 5 in Ball’s
survey[8], or Barthe’s survey[24]for short proofs. A classical
application of Lemma3.16is to prove the Brunn-Minkowski
inequality which states that, under
appropriate measurability assumptions, for any setsA,B⊆ℝnA,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}, and anyλ∈(0,1)\lambda\in(0,1),voln(λA+(1−λ)B)≥voln(A)λvoln(B)1−λ.\operatorname{vol}^{n}(\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B)\geq\operatorname{vol}^{n}(A)^{\lambda}\operatorname{vol}^{n}(B)^{1-\lambda}.(3.7)

Here,voln⁡(⋅)\operatorname{vol}^{n}(\cdot)denotesnn-dimensional volume,i.e.,
Lebesgue measure, andλ​A+(1−λ)​B\lambda A+(1-\lambda)Bis the Minkowski
sum{λ​x+(1−λ)​y:x∈A,y∈B}\{\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y:x\in A,y\in B\}.
To prove Sidak’s lemma, we, instead, need a version of the
Brunn-Minkowski inequality for Gaussian measure.

## Lemma 3.17.

For any Borel setsA,B⊆ℝnA,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}, and anyλ∈(0,1)\lambda\in(0,1), we haveγn​(λ​A+(1−λ)​B)≥γn​(A)λ​γn​(B)1−λ.\gamma^{n}(\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B)\geq\gamma^{n}(A)^{\lambda}\gamma^{n}(B)^{1-\lambda}.

## Proof.

Letp​(x):=(2​π)−n/2​e−‖x‖22/2p(x):=(2\pi)^{-n/2}e^{-\|x\|_{2}^{2}/2}be the
Gaussian density function.
Aiming to use Lemma3.16, we definef,g,h:ℝn→ℝ≥0f,g,h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}byf​(x)\displaystyle f(x):=p​(x)​1​{x∈A};\displaystyle:=p(x)1\{x\in A\};g​(y)\displaystyle g(y):=p​(y)​1​{y∈B};\displaystyle:=p(y)1\{y\in B\};h​(z)\displaystyle h(z):=p​(z)​1​{z∈λ​A+(1−λ)​B}.\displaystyle:=p(z)1\{z\in\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B\}.

Measurability off,g,hf,g,hfollows from the assumption thatAAandBBare Borel (and, therefore, so isA+BA+B), and from the
continuity ofpp.
We need to then verify that (3.6) holds for theseff,gg, andhh. Let us fix somex,y∈ℝnx,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}. It is easy to check thatp​(x)p(x)is log-concave,i.e.,p​(λ​x+(1−λ)​y)≥p​(x)λ​p​(y)1−λ.\displaystyle p(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)\geq p(x)^{\lambda}p(y)^{1-\lambda}.(3.8)

Moreover, ifx∈Ax\in Aandy∈By\in B, then, by definition,λ​x+(1−λ)​y∈λ​A+(1−λ)​B\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B, which implies that1​{λ​x+(1−λ)​y∈λ​A+(1−λ)​B}≥1​{x∈A}λ​1​{y∈B}1−λ.1\{\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\in\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B\}\geq 1\{x\in A\}^{\lambda}1\{y\in B\}^{1-\lambda}.(3.9)

Multiplying (3.8) and (3.9)
together, we get (3.6). The lemma is now implied by
Lemma3.16.
∎

Equipped with the log-concavity of slice measures (inequality
(3.10)), we are ready to prove Sidak’s lemma.

## Proof of Lemma3.14.

We first note that the lemma is trivial forn=1n=1. In that case,KKandSSare both closed intervals symmetric around0, andK∩SK\cap Sis the smaller of the two intervals. We haveγ1​(K∩S)=min⁡{γ1​(K),γ1​(S)}≥γ1​(K)​γ1​(S).\gamma^{1}(K\cap S)=\min\{\gamma^{1}(K),\gamma^{1}(S)\}\geq\gamma^{1}(K)\gamma^{1}(S).

Inequality (3.10) and a simple computation let us
reduce the higher dimensional case to the one dimensional one. We can assume, by rescalinguu, thatS={x∈ℝn:|⟨u,x⟩|≤t}S=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|\langle u,x\rangle|\leq t\}and‖u‖2=1\|u\|_{2}=1. By
the rotation invariance of the standard Gaussian measure, we may also
assume thatu=enu=e_{n}, thenn-th standard basis vector. Therefore,S={x∈ℝn:xn∈[−t,t]}.S=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x_{n}\in\left[-t,t\right]\right\}.Note that, usingggfor a standard Gaussian random variable inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n},γn​(S)=ℙ​[|g​(n)|≤t]=γ1​([−t,t]).\displaystyle\gamma^{n}(S)=\mathbb{P}\left[|g(n)|\leq t\right]=\gamma^{1}\left(\left[-t,t\right]\right).

Applying (3.11) toK∩SK\cap S, and noticing thatfK∩S​(s)f_{K\cap S}(s)equalsfK​(s)f_{K}(s)ifs∈[−t,t]s\in[-t,t], and0otherwise, we haveγn​(K∩S)=12​π​∫−tte−s2/2​fK​(s)​𝑑s.\gamma^{n}(K\cap S)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-t}^{t}e^{-s^{2}/2}f_{K}(s)ds.(3.12)

The right hand side is just the expected value offK​(g)​1​{g∈[−t,t]}f_{K}(g)1\{g\in[-t,t]\}for a standard11-dimensional Gaussian random variablegg. Using integration by parts, this expectation equals∫0∞ℙ​[fK​(g)​1​{g∈[−t,t]}≥r]​𝑑r=∫0∞γ1​(IK​(r)∩[−t,t])​𝑑r,\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[f_{K}(g)1\{g\in[-t,t]\}\geq r]dr=\int_{0}^{\infty}\gamma^{1}\left(I_{K}(r)\cap\left[-t,t\right]\right)dr,(3.13)

whereIK​(r):={s∈ℝ:fK​(s)≥r}I_{K}(r):=\{s\in\mathbb{R}:f_{K}(s)\geq r\}.
By the log-concavity offKf_{K}(inequality (3.10)), the setIK​(r)I_{K}(r)is convex,i.e., an
interval. Moreover,fKf_{K}is clearly continuous, soIK​(r)I_{K}(r)is
closed, and, forKKsymmetric around the origin,fKf_{K}is even, andIK​(r)I_{K}(r)is also
symmetric around the origin. Thus, by the one-dimensional case of
the lemma,γ1​(IK​(r)∩[−t,t])≥γ1​(IK​(r))​γ1​([−t,t])=γ1​(IK​(r))​γn​(S).\gamma^{1}\left(I_{K}(r)\cap\left[-t,t\right]\right)\geq\gamma^{1}(I_{K}(r))\gamma^{1}\left(\left[-t,t\right]\right)=\gamma^{1}(I_{K}(r))\gamma^{n}(S).

Plugging this inequality back into (3.12) and (3.13)gives usγn​(K∩S)\displaystyle\gamma^{n}(K\cap S)≥γn​(S)​∫0∞γ1​(IK​(r))​𝑑r\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{n}(S)\int_{0}^{\infty}\gamma^{1}(I_{K}(r))dr=γn​(S)​∫0∞ℙ​[fK​(g)≥r]​𝑑r\displaystyle=\gamma^{n}(S)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[f_{K}(g)\geq r]dr=γn​(S)​𝔼​[fK​(g)]\displaystyle=\gamma^{n}(S)\mathbb{E}[f_{K}(g)]=γn​(S)​∫−∞∞e−s2/2​fK​(s)​𝑑s\displaystyle=\gamma^{n}(S)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-s^{2}/2}f_{K}(s)ds=γn​(S)​γn​(K).\displaystyle=\gamma^{n}(S)\gamma^{n}(K).

Above, the first equality uses the definition ofIK​(r)I_{K}(r), the second uses integration by parts again, withgga standard 1-dimensional Gaussian, and the last equality uses (3.11).
∎

Let us now state a significant generalization
of Lemma3.14: Royen’s Gaussian correlation inequality.

## Theorem 3.19.

For any two closed convex setsK,LK,Lsymmetric around the origin
(i.e.,K=−KK=-KandL=−LL=-L), we haveγn​(K∩L)≥γn​(K)​γn​(L).\gamma^{n}(K\cap L)\geq\gamma^{n}(K)\gamma^{n}(L).(3.14)

Theorem3.19, whose proof we omit, was known as the
Gaussian correlation conjecture and was open for many years until it
was proved by Royen[109](see also the
exposition[70]). We will use it next to bound the discrepancy of set systems induced by
permutations.

## 3.4Discrepancy of Permutations and Gaussian Correlation

Recall that the set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])induced bykkpermutationsπ1,…,πk\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}of[n][n]consists of prefix sets of the type{πi​(1),…,πi​(j)}\{\pi_{i}(1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(j)\}. In Chapter2we used
the linear algebraic method to show that the discrepancy of such a set
system is bounded byO​(k​log⁡(n))O(k\log(n)). While the dependence onnnin
this bound is optimal for a constantk≥nk\geq n, the dependence onkkcan be improved using the partial coloring method. For example,
a careful application of Lemma3.4shows that there
exists a partial coloring of discrepancyO​(k)O(\sqrt{k}), as indicated
in the following exercise. In this exercise we use the notion of
canonical interval defined in Chapter2.

## Exercise 3.20.

To apply Lemma3.11to the

andSSi\SS_{i}consists of all prefix sets of the form{πi​(1),…,πi​(j)}\{\pi_{i}(1),\ldots,\pi_{i}(j)\}for one fixed permutationπi\pi_{i}. Clearly,K=K1∩…∩KkK=K_{1}\cap\ldots\cap K_{k}.

We will show that, for somet≲kt\lesssim\sqrt{k},γn​(t​Ki)≥2−n/(4​k)\gamma^{n}(tK_{i})\geq 2^{-n/(4k)}, and thenγn​(t​K)≥2−n/4\gamma^{n}(tK)\geq 2^{-n/4}follows from Theorem3.19.
Notice thatγn​(t​Ki)\gamma^{n}(tK_{i})is simplyℙ​[maxj=1n⁡|g​(πi​(1))+…+g​(πi​(j))|≤t],\mathbb{P}[\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|g(\pi_{i}(1))+\ldots+g(\pi_{i}(j))\right|\leq t],

for a standard Gaussian
vectorg∈ℝng\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Since the distribution ofggis
invariant under permuting coordinates, this is equivalent to
estimatingℙ[maxj=1n|g(1)+…+g(j))|≤t].\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|g(1)+\ldots+g(j))\right|\leq t\right].

That is, the probability that annnstep
random walk on the real line with standard Gaussian steps starting at0stays between−t-tand+t+t. This is a question we
can answer using standard results from probability theory. It is
convenient to work instead with a continuous random process, in
particular, Brownian motion. Standard Brownian motion is a random
process{B​(s):s≥0}\{B(s):s\geq 0\}such that
- •

B​(0)=0B(0)=0with probability 1;
- •

for all integersk>1k>1, and all times0≤s1≤…≤sm0\leq s_{1}\leq\ldots\leq s_{m}, the incrementsB​(s1)−B​(0)B(s_{1})-B(0),B​(s2)−B​(s1)B(s_{2})-B(s_{1}),…\ldots,B​(sm)−B​(sm−1)B(s_{m})-B(s_{m-1})are jointly independent;
- •

for alls≥0s\geq 0andΔ≥0\Delta\geq 0,B​(s+Δ)−B​(s)B(s+\Delta)-B(s)is
distributed as a Gaussian with mean0and varianceΔ\Delta;
- •

B​(s)B(s)is almost surely continuous as a function ofss.

For a proof of the non-trivial fact that Brownian motion exists,
see[94]. It is well-known that Brownian motion
satisfies the following small ball inequality: for allt>0t>0,ℙ​[sups∈[0,1]|B​(s)|≤t]≳exp⁡(−π2/8​t2).\mathbb{P}\Big[\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|B(s)|\leq t\Big]\gtrsim\exp(-\pi^{2}/8t^{2}).(3.17)

For a derivation, see,e.g., equation (7.1) of Ledoux’s lecture notes[74]. We
claim that (3.17) implies a similar bound formaxj=1n⁡|g​(1)+…+g​(j)|\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|g(1)+\ldots+g(j)\right|.
To see this, note that the vectors(g​(1),…,g​(n))(g(1),\ldots,g(n))andn​(B​(1/n)−B​(0),…,B​(1)−B​((n−1)/n))\sqrt{n}(B(1/n)-B(0),\ldots,B(1)-B((n-1)/n))are identically
distributed. Therefore,ℙ​[maxj=1n⁡|g​(1)+⋯+g​(j)|≤t]\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|g(1)+\cdots+g(j)\right|\leq t\right]=ℙ​[maxj=1n⁡|B​(j/n)|≤t/n]\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|B(j/n)\right|\leq t/\sqrt{n}\right]≥ℙ​[sups∈[0,1]|B​(s)|≤t/n]\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\Big[\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|B(s)|\leq t/\sqrt{n}\Big]≳exp⁡(−π2​n/8​t2).\displaystyle\gtrsim\exp(-\pi^{2}n/8t^{2}).(3.18)

We can now prove the existence of a partial
coloring of discrepancyO​(k)O(\sqrt{k})for any set system induced bykkpermutations.

## Lemma 3.21.

For any set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])induced byk≥1k\geq 1permutations of[n][n], there exists a partial
coloringx∈{−1,0,+1}nx\in\{-1,0,+1\}^{n}such that for everyS∈SSS\in\SS,|x​(S)|≲k|x(S)|\lesssim\sqrt{k}, and|{i:x​(i)≠0}|≥n/10|\{i:x(i)\neq 0\}|\geq n/10.

## Proof.

As discussed above, it is enough to show that, for eachi∈[k]i\in[k],γn​(t​Ki)≥2−n/4​k\gamma^{n}(tK_{i})\geq 2^{-n/4k}for somet≲kt\lesssim\sqrt{k}. Let us first handle the casen≥C​kn\geq Ckfor
a large enough constantC>0C>0. By (3.18), we
haveγn​(t​Ki)=ℙ​[maxj=1n⁡|g​(1)+…+g​(j)|≤t]≥c​e−π2​n/8​t2,\gamma^{n}(tK_{i})=\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|g(1)+\ldots+g(j)\right|\leq t\right]\geq ce^{-\pi^{2}n/8t^{2}},

for a constantc>0c>0. IfC≥8​ln⁡(1/c)ln⁡(2)C\geq\frac{8\ln(1/c)}{\ln(2)}, then
it is enough to sett:=π2​kln⁡(2)≲kt:=\sqrt{\frac{\pi^{2}k}{\ln(2)}}\lesssim\sqrt{k}for the right hand side to be at least2−n/4​k2^{-n/4k}.

The proof in the casen≤C​kn\leq Ckis analogous, but, instead of
(3.18), we use the boundℙ​[maxj=1n⁡|g​(1)+…+g​(j)|≥t]≤2​e−t2/2​n.\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{j=1}^{n}\left|g(1)+\ldots+g(j)\right|\geq t\right]\leq 2e^{-t^{2}/2n}.(3.19)

This inequality can be derived from an analogous one for Brownian
motion (see, e.g., Theorem 2.18 in Mörters and
Peres’s book[94]) as we did for
(3.18). Therefore,γn​(t​Ki)≥1−2​e−t22​n\gamma^{n}(tK_{i})\geq 1-2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2n}}for everyi∈[k]i\in[k], and it suffices to make sure that1−2​e−t22​n≥2−n4​k.1-2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2n}}\geq 2^{-\frac{n}{4k}}.Analogously to inequality (3.5), we can argue that this inequality holds
for somet≲n​log⁡(C​k/n)t\lesssim\sqrt{n\log\left({Ck}/{n}\right)}. We can
now check that, becausen≤C​kn\leq Ck, the right hand side is bounded
byO​(k)O(\sqrt{k}).
∎

The following theorem follows from Lemma3.21by
completing the partial coloring to a full coloring in the usual way.

## Theorem 3.22.

For any integerk≥1k\geq 1, and any set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])induced bykkpermutations of[n][n], we havedisc⁡(SS)≲k​log⁡(2​n).\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{k}\log(2n).

As mentioned in Chapter2,
the dependence onnnin Theorem3.22is tight for anyk≥3k\geq 3[96]. It is open, however, whether the boundk​log⁡(2​n)\sqrt{k}\log(2n)is asymptotically tight both inkkandnn. It is consistent with current knowledge that this bound can be improved toO​(k+log⁡n)O(\sqrt{k}+\log n), for example.
Note that
Lemma3.21implies that the assumptions in
Lemma3.11are not sufficient to guarantee the existence
of a full, rather than partial, coloring. If they were sufficient,
then we would be able prove a constant upper bound on the discrepancy
of any set system induced by33permutations, contradicting the lower
bound of Neiman, Newman, and Nikolov[96].

An interesting question we close with is whether other classical small
ball inequalities for Gaussian processes, as well as Royen’s
correlation inequality, can be useful in proving discrepancy upper
bounds. For example, the small ball inequality for the Brownian sheet
(see again Chapter 7 of Ledoux’s notes[74]) appears to be
related to Tusnády’s problem (Problem1) in22dimensions.

## 3.5Bibliographic Notes

The partial coloring method was first developed by Beck, who proved
Lemma3.2[28,29]. He used his method to
show that the discrepancy of arithmetic progressions restricted to[n][n]is bounded byn1/4n^{1/4}up to a factor polynomial inlog⁡(n)\log(n). This nearly matches a lower bound of Roth[106].

The partial coloring method of Beck was
further refined by Spencer[113]in order to prove
Theorem3.5. Spencer’s techniques are combinatorial
and do not use the geometric ideas presented in
Section3.3. The method was further refined by
Matoušek[86]and Matoušek and
Spencer[88]. The latter paper formulated a
general partial coloring lemma similar to
Lemma3.4. Matoušek and Spencer proved their
partial coloring lemma using an information theoretic argument due to
Boppana, initially proposed as a simplification of Spencer’s proof of
Theorem3.5[4]. Their
paper also removed the logarithmic terms in Beck’s upper bound for the
discrepancy of arithmetic progressions, matching Roth’s lower bound
up to a universal constant. The Matoušek and Spencer
partial coloring lemma is sometimes also known as the entropy method.

In our presentation of the partial coloring method we depart from the
combinatorial approach and instead adopt geometric techniques
developed in the study of the vector balancing problem. The geometric
approach presented here is originally due to Gluskin[52], who independently
proved the same discrepancy upper bound as
Spencer[113]. Lemma3.11is due to
Giannopoulos[51], who strengthened and simplified Gluskin’s
results.

Theorem3.7and Exercise3.9are due to Giannopoulos[51], who
used Lemma3.11, and a geometric estimate of the Gaussian
measure of slices of a cube, and, independently, to
Srinivasan[117]who used Lemma3.4. As already mentioned, a stronger upper bound ofO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n})for the Komlós problem was given by
Banaszczyk[10]using a completely different argument not
based on partial colorings. We explain Banaszczyk’s argument and its
various applications in
Chapters5and6.

Lemma3.21and Theorem3.22are
due to unpublished work of Spencer, Srinivasan, and
Tetali[116]. Their proof used Lemma3.4,
along the lines of Exercise3.20. The proof we present
here using Royen’s correlation inequality and the small ball
inequality for Brownian motion has not been published before. We note
that some proofs of the small ball inequality (3.17)
use arguments similar to Spencer, Srinivasan, and Tetali’s
argument. Other proofs rely on special properties of Brownian motion,e.g., its connection to the Dirichlet problem.

Many other applications of the partial coloring method – to
Tusnády’s problem, to giving tight bounds on the discrepancy of
halfspaces and other geometric sets, and others – are surveyed in
Matoušek’s book[85].

## Chapter 4Algorithms for Partial Coloring

In Chapter3, we saw the partial coloring method, and how it can give substantially improved bounds for various problems.
For a long time however, no efficient algorithms were known for these methods, which prevented the use of discrepancy in many algorithmic applications. Interestingly, in recent years, several algorithmic approaches have been developed for these, based on ideas from probability, linear algebra, geometry and optimization.

In this chapter, we describe two such elegant results. First, the Edge-Walk algorithm due to Lovett and Meka, that gives an algorithmic version of Theorem3.4. Second, an algorithm due to Rothvoss for the more general setting of arbitrary symmetric convex bodies in Theorem3.11.
In Section4.3, we describe the notion of vector discrepancy and an algorithm for finding low discrepancy colorings based on semidefinite programming.

## 4.1Lovett-Meka Algorithm

Recall the setting of Theorem3.4, where we are givenmmvectorsv1,…,vm∈ℝnv_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, and real parametersλ1,…,λm≥0\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\geq 0, and the goal is to find a partial coloringxxsuch that|⟨vi,x⟩|≤λi​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}.

We will show the following result.

## Theorem 4.1.

Letv1,…,vm∈ℝnv_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, and letx0∈[−1,1]nx_{0}\in[-1,1]^{n}be some arbitrary starting point, and letλ1,…,λm≥0\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\geq 0be such that∑iexp⁡(−λi2/16)≤n/16.\sum_{i}\exp(-\lambda_{i}^{2}/16)\leq n/16.(4.1)

Letδ>0\delta>0be an arbitrarily small parameter.
Then there is a randomized algorithm that runs in time polynomial inn,mn,mand1/δ1/\delta, and, with constant probability, findsx∈[−1,1]nx\in[-1,1]^{n}such that

(i)|{i:|x​(i)|≥1−δ}|≥n2|\{i:|x(i)|\geq 1-\delta\}|\geq\frac{n}{2}, and

(ii)|⟨vi,x−x0⟩|≤λi​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x-x_{0}\rangle|\leq\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}for eachi∈[m]i\in[m].

## Remark 4.2.

We setδ=1/poly​(n,m)\delta=1/\text{poly}(n,m)to keep the running time polynomial,
but it is convenient to think ofδ\deltaas0. So (i) says that the colors of at leastn/2n/2elements reach±1\pm 1, and (ii) controls the discrepancy incurred for each vector.

The “colors”x​(i)x(i)produced by the algorithm in Theorem4.1lie in[−1,1][-1,1], in contrast to{−1,0,1}\{-1,0,1\}in Theorem3.4.
But this makes no difference in applications as the starting pointx0x_{0}is allowed to be arbitrary, and we can iterate the algorithm forO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)rounds to obtain a full coloring.
In fact, this flexibility in colors allows the condition in (4.1) to be more relaxed than in (3.1). In particular, the contribution of anyλi\lambda_{i}to the left side in (4.1) never exceeds11, whileh​(λi)h(\lambda_{i})in (3.1) gets arbitrarily large asλi\lambda_{i}approaches0.

## 4.1.1The Algorithm

At a high level, the algorithm is based on combining the linear algebraic approach from Chapter2with probabilistic techniques.

The idea.The algorithm starts at the pointx0x_{0}, and performs a standard Brownian motion (approximated by a random walk with tiny Gaussian increments). See Figure4.1below. Letxtx_{t}denote the coloring at timett. If some coordinatext​(j)x_{t}(j)reaches±1\pm 1, its value is fixed and no longer updated (i.e., all subsequent steps of the walk are orthogonal toeje_{j}, thejj-th standard basis vector).
Moreover, if any discrepancy constraint becomes tight, i.e.,|⟨vi,xt−x0⟩|=λi​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x_{t}-x_{0}\rangle|=\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}, all the subsequent steps of the walk are orthogonal toviv_{i}.

Clearly, by design, the coloringxtx_{t}stays inside the cube[−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}, and no discrepancy constraint is violated.
Notice that every time some coordinate reaches±1\pm 1, or a discrepancy constraint becomes tight, the dimension of the subspace where the walk is performed may reduce by11.
The key idea of the analysis is to show that the condition (4.1) ensures that many coordinates are likely to reach±1\pm 1, before the walk gets “stuck”.
To see why this might be the case, consider the case when allλi\lambda_{i}are equal, and, therefore, equal to a large enough constantCC. Suppose also for simplicity, thatx0=0x_{0}=0. Then the boundary of the strip{x:|⟨vi,x⟩|≤C​‖vi‖2}\{x:|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\leq C\|v_{i}\|_{2}\}, which encodes a discrepancy constraint, is at Euclidean distanceCCfrom the origin, while the boundary of the cube[−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}is at distance11from the origin. So, intuitively, the Brownian motion is more likely to hit the boundary of the cube before a discrepancy constraint becomes tight.Figure 4.1:Lovett-Meka Algorithm. The random walk starting fromx0x_{0}, and hitting a discrepancy constraint.

## The Formal Algorithm.

Fix a small step sizeγ>0\gamma>0, such thatδ≥c​γ​log1/2⁡(2​n​m/γ)\delta\geq c\gamma\log^{1/2}(2nm/\gamma)for a sufficiently large constantcc, to be determined later.
The algorithm proceeds in discrete time stepst=1,2,…,ℓ=4/γ2t=1,2,\ldots,\ell=4/\gamma^{2}.
Letxt−1x_{t-1}denote the coloring at the beginning of time steptt(equivalently, at the end of time stept−1t-1).
At eacht=1,…,ℓt=1,\ldots,\ell, the coloringxt−1x_{t-1}is updated toxtx_{t}as follows:

(i) LetFt:={j∈[n]:|xt−1​(j)|>1−δ}F_{t}:=\{j\in[n]:|x_{t-1}(j)|>1-\delta\}denote the set of tight (i.e.,fixed) elements thus far, and letDt:={i∈[m]:|⟨vi,xt−1−x0⟩|≥(λi−δ)​‖vi‖2}D_{t}:=\{i\in[m]:|\langle v_{i},x_{t-1}-x_{0}\rangle|\geq(\lambda_{i}-\delta)\|v_{i}\|_{2}\}

denote the set of tight discrepancy constraints thus far (i.e., thedangerousdirections).

(ii) LetWt:={u∈ℝn:u​(j)=0​for all​j∈Ft,⟨vi,u⟩=0​for all​i∈Dt}W_{t}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:u(j)=0\text{ for all }j\in F_{t},\,\langle v_{i},u\rangle=0\text{ for all }i\in D_{t}\}be the subspace orthogonal to the tight constraints.

(iii)
Sample a random standard Gaussian vectorgt∼N​(Wt)g_{t}\sim N(W_{t})in the subspaceWtW_{t}, and setxt=xt−1+γ​gtx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\gamma g_{t}.

Above, for a linear subspaceWWofℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, we use the notationN​(W)N(W)to denote the standard Gaussian distribution restricted toWW, andg∼N​(V)g\sim N(V)to mean thatggis a random variable distributed according toN​(V)N(V).

## 4.1.2Analysis

We show that the algorithm satisfies the guarantee in Theorem4.1.

Notice that at any timett, we have thatxt−x0=∑s=1tγ​gsx_{t}-x_{0}=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\gamma g_{s}, where eachgs∼N​(Ws)g_{s}\sim N(W_{s}). As the subspaceWsW_{s}depends onxs−1x_{s-1}, which in turn depends ong1,…,gs−1g_{1},\ldots,g_{s-1}, the coloringxtx_{t}evolves as a martingale with Gaussian increments.

So let us begin by recalling some basic properties of Gaussians and martingales. The analysis is elementary, and follows directly from these properties.

Some properties of Gaussians.Let us use the notationN​(μ,σ2)N(\mu,\sigma^{2})for the one-dimensional Gaussian distribution with meanμ\muand varianceσ2\sigma^{2}, andg∼N​(μ,σ2)g\sim N(\mu,\sigma^{2})to denote thatggis a random variable distributed according toN​(μ,σ2)N(\mu,\sigma^{2}). Ifg1∼N​(μ1,σ12)g_{1}\sim N(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})andg2∼N​(μ2,σ22)g_{2}\sim N(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2})are independent Gaussians, their sum is also Gaussian withg1+g2∼N​(μ1+μ2,σ12+σ22)g_{1}+g_{2}\sim N(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}).
This implies that ifg=(g​(1),…,g​(n))g=(g(1),\ldots,g(n))is the standard gaussian random vector inℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, withg​(1),…,g​(n)g(1),\ldots,g(n)independent samples fromN​(0,1)N(0,1), then for anyu∈ℝnu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, we have⟨g,u⟩=∑ig​(i)​u​(i)∼N​(0,‖u‖22).\langle g,u\rangle=\sum_{i}g(i)u(i)\sim N(0,\|u\|_{2}^{2}).

For a linear subspaceWW, andN​(W)N(W), as above, the standard Gaussian distribution restricted toWW, rotational invariance implies that a random vectorg∼N​(W)g\sim N(W)can be written asg=g​(1)​w1+⋯+g​(d)​wdg=g(1)w_{1}+\cdots+g(d)w_{d}for any orthonormal basis{w1,…,wd}\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{d}\}forWWandg​(1),…,g​(d)g(1),\ldots,g(d)independent samples fromN​(0,1)N(0,1). This implies the following useful facts.

## Lemma 4.3.

LetWWbe a subspace ofℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}of dimensionddandg∼N​(W)g\sim N(W). Then for allu∈ℝnu\in\mathbb{R}^{n},⟨g,u⟩∼N​(0,σ2)\langle g,u\rangle\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})withσ2≤‖u‖22\sigma^{2}\leq\|u\|_{2}^{2}. Moreover,∑i=1n𝔼​[g​(i)2]=d\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[g(i)^{2}]=d.

## Proof.

LetPPbe the orthogonal projection ontoWW.
Asg∈Wg\in W, for anyu∈ℝnu\in\mathbb{R}^{n},⟨g,u⟩=⟨g,P​u⟩\langle g,u\rangle=\langle g,Pu\rangleand hence⟨g,u⟩∼N​(0,‖P​u‖2)\langle g,u\rangle\sim N(0,\|Pu\|^{2}). Clearly,‖P​u‖2≤‖u‖2\|Pu\|_{2}\leq\|u\|_{2}.

For the second part, ifw1,…,wdw_{1},\ldots,w_{d}is an orthonormal basis forWW, ande1,…,ene_{1},\ldots,e_{n}is the standard basis ofℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}, then by the above observation,𝔼​[g​(i)2]=𝔼​[⟨g,ei⟩2]=‖P​ei‖22=∑j=1d⟨ei,wj⟩2.\mathbb{E}[g(i)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\langle g,e_{i}\rangle^{2}]=\|Pe_{i}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\langle e_{i},w_{j}\rangle^{2}.

We thus have∑i=1n𝔼​[g​(i)2]=∑i=1n∑j=1d⟨ei,wj⟩2=∑j=1d∑i=1n⟨ei,wj⟩2=∑j=1d‖wj‖22=d,\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[g(i)^{2}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\langle e_{i},w_{j}\rangle^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle e_{i},w_{j}\rangle^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|w_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=d,

where the third equality follows as{e1,…,en}\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}is an orthonormal basis forℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}.
∎

Finally, we need some concentration properties for martingales with Gaussian increments.
Recall that forg∼N​(0,1)g\sim N(0,1), for anyλ≥0\lambda\geq 0,ℙ​[|g|≥λ]≤2​exp⁡(−λ2/2).\mathbb{P}[|g|\geq\lambda]\leq 2\exp(-\lambda^{2}/2).

(In fact, the factor22is not necessary.) More generally, we have the following.

## Lemma 4.4.

Letg1,…,gℓg_{1},\ldots,g_{\ell}be random variables overℝ\mathbb{R}such that, for all1≤t≤ℓ1\leq t\leq\ell,gtg_{t}only depends ong1,…,gt−1g_{1},\ldots,g_{t-1}, and the law ofgtg_{t}conditioned ong1,…​gt−1g_{1},\ldots g_{t-1}is, with probability11, equal to that of a Gaussian with mean zero and variance at mostσ2\sigma^{2}.
Then for anyλ>0\lambda>0,ℙ​[|g1+⋯+gℓ|≥λ​ℓ​σ]≤2​exp⁡(−λ2/2).\mathbb{P}[|g_{1}+\cdots+g_{\ell}|\geq\lambda\sqrt{\ell}\sigma]\leq 2\exp(-\lambda^{2}/2).

## Proof.

Recall that forg∼N​(0,σ2)g\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}), its moment generating function is𝔼​[exp⁡(β​g)]=exp⁡(β2​σ2/2)\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta g)]=\exp(\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}/2)for allβ≥0\beta\geq 0.
LetSt=g1+⋯+gtS_{t}=g_{1}+\cdots+g_{t}. Then𝔼​[exp⁡(β​St)]=\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta S_{t})]=𝔼​[𝔼​[exp⁡(β​(St−1+gt))|g1,…,gt−1]]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta(S_{t-1}+g_{t}))|g_{1},\ldots,g_{t-1}]]=\displaystyle=𝔼​[exp⁡(β​St−1)​𝔼​[exp⁡(β​gt)|g1,…,gt−1]]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta S_{t-1})\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta g_{t})|g_{1},\ldots,g_{t-1}]]≤\displaystyle\leq𝔼​[exp⁡(β​St−1)]​exp⁡(β2​σ2/2),\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta S_{t-1})]\exp(\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}/2),

and hence by induction𝔼​[exp⁡(β​Sℓ)]≤exp⁡(β2​ℓ​σ2/2)\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta S_{\ell})]\leq\exp(\beta^{2}\ell\sigma^{2}/2).
For anyβ≥0\beta\geq 0,ℙ​[Sℓ≥λ​ℓ​σ]=ℙ​[exp⁡(β​Sℓ)≥exp⁡β​λ​ℓ​σ]≤exp⁡(β2​ℓ​σ2/2−β​λ​ℓ​σ)\mathbb{P}[S_{\ell}\geq\lambda\sqrt{\ell}\sigma]=\mathbb{P}[\exp(\beta S_{\ell})\geq\exp{\beta\lambda\sqrt{\ell}\sigma}]\leq\exp(\beta^{2}\ell\sigma^{2}/2-\beta\lambda\sqrt{\ell}\sigma)

by Markov’s inequality, and making the choiceβ=λ/ℓ\beta=\lambda/\sqrt{\ell}that minimizes the left hand side gives the result.
∎

Basic properties of the algorithm.Let us note some simple observations about the algorithm.
- 1.

Once an element is fixed, its value does not change and it remains fixed. Similarly, once. Once a discrepancy constraint becomes tight, it stays tight since subsequent coloring updates are orthogonal toviv_{i}. SoFtF_{t},DtD_{t}can only increase with time, and, in particular,dim​(Wt)\text{dim}(W_{t})is non-increasing.
- 2.

The final coloringxℓx_{\ell}returned by the algorithm satisfiesxℓ∈[−1,1]nx_{\ell}\in[-1,1]^{n}and⟨vi,xℓ−x0⟩≤λi​‖vi‖2\langle v_{i},x_{\ell}-x_{0}\rangle\leq\lambda_{i}\|v_{i}\|_{2}for alli∈[m]i\in[m]with high probability.
Indeed, consider the first timettwhenxt​(j)>1x_{t}(j)>1. Then it must be thatxt−1​(j)<1−δx_{t-1}(j)<1-\delta(otherwisext−1​(j)x_{t-1}(j)would not have been updated at timett), and thus it must be thatgt​(j)≥δ/γg_{t}(j)\geq\delta/\gamma.
Asgt​(j)=⟨gt,ej⟩∼N​(0,σ2)g_{t}(j)=\langle g_{t},e_{j}\rangle\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})withσ2≤1\sigma^{2}\leq 1(recall Lemma4.3),
this probability is at most2​exp⁡(−(δ/γ)2/2)≤(m​n/γ)−c2\exp(-(\delta/\gamma)^{2}/2)\leq(mn/\gamma)^{-c}, forccarbitrarily large, by the choice ofγ\gamma.
An identical argument works for discrepancy constraints, and the claim follows by a union bound over theO​(m​n/γ2)O(mn/\gamma^{2})choices fori,ji,jandtt.

The main argument.It remains to show that|Fℓ|≥n/2|F_{\ell}|\geq n/2(that is,|xℓ​(i)|≥1−δ|x_{\ell}(i)|\geq 1-\deltafor at leastn/2n/2elements) with some constant probability.
As|Fℓ|≤n|F_{\ell}|\leq n,
by Markov’s inequality applied ton−|Fℓ|n-|F_{\ell}|,
it suffices to show that𝔼​[|Fℓ|]≥0.6​n\mathbb{E}[|F_{\ell}|]\geq 0.6n.

This will follow from the following two claims.
First, many constraints must become tight on average. In particular,

## Lemma 4.5.

𝔼​[|Fℓ|+|Dℓ|]≥3​n/4\mathbb{E}[|F_{\ell}|+|D_{\ell}|]\geq 3n/4.

Second, not many discrepancy constraints can become tight. In particular,

## Lemma 4.6.

Assumingδ≤0.1\delta\leq 0.1,𝔼​[|Dℓ|]≤(1.1)​n/8\mathbb{E}[|D_{\ell}|]\leq(1.1)n/8.

We now prove Lemmas4.5and4.6.

## Proof of Lemma4.5.

Asdim(Wℓ)≥n−|Fℓ|−|Dℓ|\dim(W_{\ell})\geq n-|F_{\ell}|-|D_{\ell}|, it suffices to show that𝔼​[dim(Wℓ)]≤n/4\mathbb{E}[\dim(W_{\ell})]\leq n/4.
To do this, we track how‖xt‖22\|x_{t}\|_{2}^{2}evolves.

To this end, notice that sincext=xt−1+γ​gtx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\gamma g_{t}, we have𝔼​[‖xt‖22−‖xt−1‖22|xt−1]=𝔼​[2​γ​⟨xt−1,gt⟩+‖gt‖2|xt−1]=γ2​dim(Wt),\mathbb{E}[\|x_{t}\|_{2}^{2}-\|x_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}|x_{t-1}]=\mathbb{E}[2\gamma\langle x_{t-1},g_{t}\rangle+\|g_{t}\|^{2}|x_{t-1}]=\gamma^{2}\dim(W_{t}),

where we use that𝔼​[⟨xt−1,gt⟩|xt−1]=0\mathbb{E}[\langle x_{t-1},g_{t}\rangle|x_{t-1}]=0and𝔼​[‖gt‖2]=dim(Wt)\mathbb{E}[\|g_{t}\|^{2}]=\dim(W_{t})by Lemma4.3.
Summing up over all time steps and averaging, and observing thatdim(Wt)\dim(W_{t})is non-increasing, this gives us𝔼​[‖xℓ‖22]=‖x0‖22+∑1≤t≤ℓγ2​𝔼​[dim(Wt)]≥γ2​ℓ​𝔼​[dim(Wℓ)]\mathbb{E}[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}]=\|x_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq t\leq\ell}\gamma^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\dim(W_{t})]\geq\gamma^{2}\ell\,\mathbb{E}[\dim(W_{\ell})]

Asℓ=4/γ2\ell=4/\gamma^{2}, this gives𝔼​[dim(Wℓ)]≤𝔼​[‖xℓ‖22]/4\mathbb{E}[\dim(W_{\ell})]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}]/4.
The result then follows, as‖xℓ‖22≤n\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}\leq ntrivially for anyxℓ∈[−1,1]nx_{\ell}\in[-1,1]^{n}.111Actually, we need some care asxℓx_{\ell}could end up outside[−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}. However, the argument in Observation2implies that for anyj∈[n]j\in[n],𝔼​[xℓ​(j)2]≤(1−δ)2+γ2<1\mathbb{E}[x_{\ell}(j)^{2}]\leq(1-\delta)^{2}+\gamma^{2}<1. So we have𝔼​[‖xℓ‖22]<n\mathbb{E}[\|x_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}]<n, which suffices for the proof of the Lemma.∎

## Proof of Lemma4.6.

Consider some vectorviv_{i}. The corresponding discrepancy constraint becomes tight if|⟨vi,xt−x0⟩|≥(λi−δ)​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x_{t}-x_{0}\rangle|\geq(\lambda_{i}-\delta)\|v_{i}\|_{2}at some timett. As the subsequent coloring updates are orthogonal toviv_{i}, this is same as|⟨vi,xℓ−x0⟩|≥(λi−δ)​‖vi‖2|\langle v_{i},x_{\ell}-x_{0}\rangle|\geq(\lambda_{i}-\delta)\|v_{i}\|_{2}(i.e., the constraint is tight at timeℓ\ell). Let us call this eventEiE_{i}.

As⟨vi,xℓ−x0⟩\langle v_{i},x_{\ell}-x_{0}\rangleis a martingale with Gaussian increments, with variance at mostγ2​‖vi‖22\gamma^{2}\|v_{i}\|_{2}^{2}per step andℓ=4/γ2\ell=4/\gamma^{2}, by Lemma4.4we haveℙ​[Ei]≤2​exp⁡(−(λi−δ)+2/8),\mathbb{P}[E_{i}]\leq 2\exp(-(\lambda_{i}-\delta)_{+}^{2}/8),

where we denote(λi−δ)+=max⁡(0,λi−δ)(\lambda_{i}-\delta)_{+}=\max(0,\lambda_{i}-\delta). So,𝔼​[|Dℓ|]=∑iℙ​[Ei]≤∑i2​exp⁡(−(λi−δ)+2/8).\mathbb{E}[|D_{\ell}|]=\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}[E_{i}]\leq\sum_{i}2\exp(-(\lambda_{i}-\delta)_{+}^{2}/8).

To relate this to condition (4.1), we can use that, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,λi2=((λi−δ)+δ)2≤2​(λi−δ)2+2​δ2.\lambda_{i}^{2}=((\lambda_{i}-\delta)+\delta)^{2}\leq 2(\lambda_{i}-\delta)^{2}+2\delta^{2}.Therefore, sinceδ≤0.1\delta\leq 0.1,(λi−δ)+2≥λi2/2−δ2≥λi2/2−0.01(\lambda_{i}-\delta)^{2}_{+}\geq\lambda_{i}^{2}/2-\delta^{2}\geq\lambda_{i}^{2}/2-0.01.
Thenℙ​[Ei]≤2​exp⁡(−λi2/16+0.01/8)≤(2.2)​exp⁡(−λi2/16)\mathbb{P}[E_{i}]\leq 2\exp(-\lambda_{i}^{2}/16+0.01/8)\leq(2.2)\exp(-\lambda_{i}^{2}/16), and (4.1) gives𝔼​[|Dℓ|]=∑iℙ​[Ei]≤∑i(2.2)​exp⁡(−λi2/16)≤(1.1)​n/8.∎\mathbb{E}[|D_{\ell}|]=\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}[E_{i}]\leq\sum_{i}(2.2)\exp(-\lambda_{i}^{2}/16)\leq(1.1)n/8.\qed

This completes the proof of Theorem4.1.

## 4.2Rothvoss’ Algorithm

While the Lovett-Meka algorithm suffices for most applications of the partial coloring method, it does not easily generalize to general convex bodies, so it does not give a constructive proof of Giannopoulos’s partial coloring Lemma (Lemma3.11). Recall that Lemma3.11guarantees that any symmetric convex bodyKKinℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}of Gaussian measure at least2−n/42^{-n/4}contains a partial coloringxxwith at leastn10\frac{n}{10}colored coordinates. Attempting to apply the Lovett-Meka algorithm to compute such a coloring, we are faced with the problem that the bodyKKcan be defined by infinitely many inequalities, making it impossible to apply the condition (4.1).

Using an entirely different approach, Rothvoss was able to give an algorithmic version of Lemma3.11, stated in the next theorem.

## Theorem 4.7.

Letϵ\epsilonbe a sufficiently small constant, letδ>0\delta>0be sufficiently small compared toϵ\epsilon,222The constantsϵ,δ\epsilon,\deltacan be computed easily from the proof below, but we avoid plugging specific values to avoid clutter.and letK⊆ℝnK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}be a symmetric convex body with Gaussian measureγn​(K)≥exp⁡(−ϵ​n)\gamma^{n}(K)\geq\exp(-\epsilon n). Then there is a randomized polynomial time algorithm that, with probability1−exp⁡(−Ω​(n))1-\exp(-\Omega(n))(with the implied constant depending onϵ\epsilonandδ\delta), finds a pointx∈K∩[−1,1]nx\in K\cap[-1,1]^{n}with|{i:x​(i)∈{−1,1}}|≥δ​n|\{i:x(i)\in\{-1,1\}\}|\geq\delta n.

## 4.2.1Algorithm

The algorithm can be described in a single line!

Sample a random Gaussiang∼N​(0,In)g\sim N(0,I_{n}), and output the pointx∗x^{*}inK∩[−1,1]nK\cap[-1,1]^{n}closest togg.
That is, outputx∗=arg⁡min⁡{‖x−g‖2:x∈K∩[−1,1]n}.x^{*}=\arg\min\,\{\|x-g\|_{2}:x\in K\cap[-1,1]^{n}\}.(4.2)

That’s it! See Figure4.2for an illustration.Figure 4.2:Rothvoss’ Algorithm. The shaded area indicatesK∩[−1,1]nK\cap[-1,1]^{n}, andx∗x^{*}is the closest point inK∩[−1,1]nK\cap[-1,1]^{n}to the random Gaussiangg.

Notice thatx∗x^{*}can be determined efficiently, to any desired accuracy, by solving a convex program, asf​(x):=‖x−g‖2f(x):=\|x-g\|_{2}is a convex function for any fixedgg, andK∩[−1,1]nK\cap[-1,1]^{n}is a convex set.
All one needs is a membership oracle forKK.
In fact, the objective function is strictly convex333i.e.,f​(λ​x+(1−λ)​y)<λ​f​(x)+(1−λ)​f​(y)f(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)<\lambda f(x)+(1-\lambda)f(y)wheneverx≠yx\neq y,λ∈(0,1)\lambda\in(0,1). Indeed, we can equivalently consider the objective‖x−g‖22\|x-g\|^{2}_{2}and here,‖λ​x+(1−λ)​y−g‖22−λ​‖x−g‖22−(1−λ)​‖y−g‖22=−λ​(1−λ)​‖x−y‖22\|\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y-g\|_{2}^{2}-\lambda\|x-g\|_{2}^{2}-(1-\lambda)\|y-g\|_{2}^{2}=-\lambda(1-\lambda)\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}for allgg.and thus the optimumx∗x^{*}is always unique.

## 4.2.2Analysis

We now show that the algorithm satisfies Theorem4.7. The analysis is very elegant, and uses two key observations about how
the objective valuev∗:=‖x∗−g‖2v^{*}:=\|x^{*}-g\|_{2}of the program (4.2) behaves. Note that bothx∗x^{*}andv∗v^{*}are functions ofgg.

(i) With high probability,v∗≥c​nv^{*}\geq c\sqrt{n}, wherec>0c>0is a universal constant independent ofϵ\epsilon.

(ii) Ifx∗x^{*}has fewer thanδ​n\delta ncoordinatesx∗​(i)∈{−1,1}x^{*}(i)\in\{-1,1\}, then with high probability,v∗v^{*}must be small, roughlyO​((ϵ+δ​ln⁡1/δ)​n)O(\sqrt{(\epsilon+\delta\ln 1/\delta)n}).

The result then follows directly from these observations.

In light of this, let us take a brief detour to understand how the distanced​(g,A):=min⁡{‖x−g‖2:x∈A}d(g,A):=\min\{\|x-g\|_{2}:x\in A\}of a pointggto a setA⊂ℝnA\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}behaves, wheng∼N​(0,In)g\sim N(0,I_{n})is chosen randomly.

Fattenings and Gaussian Isoperimetric Inequality.Forδ>0\delta>0, letAδ={x:d​(x,A)≤δ}A_{\delta}=\{x:d(x,A)\leq\delta\}, theδ\delta-fattening ofAA, denote the set consisting of points within distanceδ\deltafromAA.
Clearly, for anygg,
asd​(g,A)≤δd(g,A)\leq\deltaif and only ifg∈Aδg\in A_{\delta}, we have that, for a standard Gaussiangginℝn\mathbb{R}^{n},ℙ​[d​(g,A)≤δ]=γn​(Aδ).\mathbb{P}[d(g,A)\leq\delta]=\gamma^{n}(A_{\delta}).

In other words, we can understand the distribution of the distanced​(g,A)d(g,A)by looking at the Gaussian measure of fattenings ofAA.

The key tool to get a handle onγn​(Aδ)\gamma^{n}(A_{\delta})is the classical Gaussian isoperimetric (GI) inequality[35], which states that among all sets with equal Gaussian measure, fattenings increase the measure of halfspaces the least. This is a Gaussian analog of the more classical isoperimetric inequality for volume (Lebesgue measure), which states that among all sets of equal volume, the Euclidean sphere has the smallest surface area. While the classical isoperimetric inequality is easily derived from the Brunn-Minkowski inequality (3.7), the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality is a deeper fact.

Formally, a halfspace is a set of the formH:={x∈ℝn:⟨u,x⟩≤t}H:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\langle u,x\rangle\leq t\}for some unit vectoru∈ℝnu\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, and scalart∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}. Theδ\delta-fattening ofHHisHδ:={x∈ℝn:⟨u,x⟩≤t+δ}H_{\delta}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\langle u,x\rangle\leq t+\delta\}. By the rotational symmetry of Gaussians, we may assume thatu=e1u=e_{1}and thus,γn​(H)=Φ​(t)=γ1​((−∞,t])=∫−∞t(2​π)−1/2​exp⁡(−t2/2)​𝑑t.\gamma^{n}(H)=\Phi(t)=\gamma^{1}((-\infty,t])=\int_{-\infty}^{t}(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp(-t^{2}/2)\,dt.

## Theorem 4.8(GI Inequality).

LetA⊂ℝnA\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}be any measurable set andHHbe any halfspace so thatγn​(A)=γn​(H)\gamma^{n}(A)=\gamma^{n}(H). Then for everyδ>0\delta>0, we have thatγn​(Aδ)≥γn​(Hδ)\gamma^{n}(A_{\delta})\geq\gamma^{n}(H_{\delta}).
Equivalently,
for a setAA, define the Gaussian isoperimetric functiont​(A):=Φ−1​(γn​(A))t(A):=\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{n}(A)).
Then,t​(Aδ)≥t​(Hδ)=t​(H)+δ=t​(A)+δ.t(A_{\delta})\geq t(H_{\delta})=t(H)+\delta=t(A)+\delta.

This gives the following key fact — ifγn​(A)\gamma^{n}(A)is not too small, then the measure concentrates close toAA.
More quantitatively,

## Lemma 4.9.

LetA⊂ℝnA\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}be a set withγn​(A)=exp⁡(−β​n)≤1/2\gamma^{n}(A)=\exp(-\beta n)\leq 1/2. Then,ℙg∼N​(0,In)​[d​(g,A)≥(8​β​n)1/2]<exp⁡(−β​n)\mathbb{P}_{g\sim N(0,I_{n})}[d(g,A)\geq(8\beta n)^{1/2}]<\exp(-\beta n).

Let us first recall thatΦ​(0)=1/2\Phi(0)=1/2, and thatΦ​(t)≤exp⁡(−t2/2)\Phi(t)\leq\exp(-t^{2}/2)fort≤0t\leq 0, and by symmetry,Φ​(t)≥1−exp⁡(−t2/2)\Phi(t)\geq 1-\exp(-t^{2}/2)fort≥0t\geq 0.

## Proof.

Letδ:=(8​β​n)1/2\delta:=(8\beta n)^{1/2}.
We will show thatγn​(Aδ)≥1−exp⁡(−β​n)\gamma^{n}(A_{\delta})\geq 1-\exp(-\beta n).
We use the notation in Theorem4.8.

AsΦ​(t​(A))=γn​(A)=exp⁡(−β​n)≤1/2\Phi(t(A))=\gamma^{n}(A)=\exp(-\beta n)\leq 1/2, we havet​(A)≤0t(A)\leq 0(asΦ​(0)=1/2\Phi(0)=1/2). UsingΦ​(t)≤exp⁡(−t2/2)\Phi(t)\leq\exp(-t^{2}/2)fort≤0t\leq 0, this givesexp⁡(−β​n)≤exp⁡(−t​(A)2/2)\exp(-\beta n)\leq\exp(-t(A)^{2}/2), and hencet​(A)≥−(2​β​n)1/2t(A)\geq-(2\beta n)^{1/2}.

By Theorem4.8,t​(Aδ)≥−(2​β​n)1/2+δ=(2​β​n)1/2t(A_{\delta})\geq-(2\beta n)^{1/2}+\delta=(2\beta n)^{1/2}, and asΦ​(t)≥1−exp⁡(−t2/2)\Phi(t)\geq 1-\exp(-t^{2}/2)fort>0t>0, this givesγn​(Aδ)≥1−exp⁡(−β​n)\gamma^{n}(A_{\delta})\geq 1-\exp(-\beta n).
∎

We now get back to proving Theorem4.7.
We first show that the objective valuev∗=‖x∗−g‖2v^{*}=\|x^{*}-g\|_{2}in(4.2)is typically quite large.

## Lemma 4.10.

ℙg∼N​(0,In)​[v∗≤n/6]=exp⁡(−Ω​(n))\mathbb{P}_{g\sim N(0,I_{n})}[v^{*}\leq\sqrt{n}/6]=\exp(-\Omega(n)).

## Proof.

As the domainK∩[−1,1]nK\cap[-1,1]^{n}ofxxin (4.2) is contained in[−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}, it suffices to show thatℙ​[d​(g,[−1,1]n)≤n/6]=exp⁡(−Ω​(n))\mathbb{P}[d(g,[-1,1]^{n})\leq\sqrt{n}/6]=\exp(-\Omega(n)).

Consider the squared distanced2​(g,[−1,1]n)d^{2}(g,[-1,1]^{n}).
Then each coordinateiiwith|g​(i)|≥2|g(i)|\geq 2contributes at least11, and this happens independently with probability2​γ1​([2,∞))≥1/252\gamma^{1}([2,\infty))\geq 1/25for eachii. Thus by standard Chernoff boundsℙ​[d2​(g,[−1,1]n)<n/36]=exp⁡(−Ω​(n))\mathbb{P}[d^{2}(g,[-1,1]^{n})<n/36]=\exp(-\Omega(n)).
∎

## Lower bound on the number of±1\pm 1coordinates inx∗x^{*}.

To get a handle on whenx∗x^{*}has few±1\pm 1coordinates, we need a basic observation about convex programs.

For a subset of coordinatesI⊆[n]I\subseteq[n],
define the setS​(I):=[−1,1]I×ℝ[n]∖IS(I):=[-1,1]^{I}\times\mathbb{R}^{[n]\setminus I}. In particular,S​([n])=[−1,1]nS([n])=[-1,1]^{n}. We have the following.

## Lemma 4.11.

Fix someg∈ℝng\in\mathbb{R}^{n}. Letxg∗x_{g}^{*}be the (unique) solution to (4.2),444We put the subscriptggto emphasize the dependence ongg.and letIg={i∈[n]:xg∗​(i)∈{−1,1}}I_{g}=\{i\in[n]:x_{g}^{*}(i)\in\{-1,1\}\}be the set of coordinates for which the constraintsx​(i)∈[−1,1]x(i)\in[-1,1]are tight atxg∗x_{g}^{*}. Thenarg⁡min⁡{‖x−g‖2:x∈K∩S​(Ig)}=xg∗.\arg\min\{\|x-g\|_{2}:x\in K\cap S(I_{g})\}=x_{g}^{*}.

In other words, the optimizer to the convex program (4.2) does not change upon dropping the constraintsx​(i)∈[−1,1]x(i)\in[-1,1]fori∉Igi\notin I_{g}.

## Proof.

Let us denoteyg∗=arg⁡min⁡{‖x−g‖2:x∈K∩S​(Ig)},y_{g}^{*}=\arg\min\{\|x-g\|_{2}:x\in K\cap S(I_{g})\},

and
suppose for the sake of contradiction thatyg∗≠xg∗y_{g}^{*}\neq x_{g}^{*}.
Then, it must be that‖yg∗−g‖2<‖xg∗−g‖\|y_{g}^{*}-g\|_{2}<\|x_{g}^{*}-g\|, by strong convexity and as there are fewer constraints onyg∗y_{g}^{*}(as[−1,1]n⊂S​(Ig∗)[-1,1]^{n}\subset S(I_{g}^{*})).
Also, clearly,yg∗​(i),xg∗​(i)∈[−1,1]y_{g}^{*}(i),x_{g}^{*}(i)\in[-1,1]for eachi∈Ig∗i\in I_{g}^{*}(as these coordinate constraints are enforced).

Next, crucially, by the definition ofIg∗I_{g}^{*}we have thatxg∗​(i)∈(0,1)x_{g}^{*}(i)\in(0,1)fori∉Ig∗i\notin I_{g}^{*}.
So even thoughyg∗​(i)y_{g}^{*}(i)may not lie in[−1,1][-1,1]fori∉Ig∗i\notin I_{g}^{*}, there exists someλ∈(0,1)\lambda\in(0,1)such that the pointx~=λ​xg∗+(1−λ)​yg∗\tilde{x}=\lambda x_{g}^{*}+(1-\lambda)y_{g}^{*}

lies in[−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}. As bothxg∗,yg∗∈Kx_{g}^{*},y_{g}^{*}\in KandKKis convex, we also have thatx~∈K∩[−1,1]n\tilde{x}\in K\cap[-1,1]^{n}. This implies thatx~\tilde{x}is also a feasible solution to (4.2). However, by strong convexity,‖x~−g‖22​<λ∥​xg∗−g∥22+(1−λ)​‖yg∗−g‖22<‖xg∗−g‖22,\|\tilde{x}-g\|^{2}_{2}<\lambda\|x_{g}^{*}-g\|_{2}^{2}+(1-\lambda)\|y_{g}^{*}-g\|_{2}^{2}<\|x_{g}^{*}-g\|^{2}_{2},

which contradicts the optimality ofxg∗x_{g}^{*}.
∎

For a subset of coordinatesI⊂[n]I\subset[n], consider the objective valuevg∗(I):=min{∥x−g∥2:x∈K∩S(I)},v_{g}^{*}(I):=\text{min}\{\|x-g\|_{2}:x\in K\cap S(I)\},

in (4.2) when the constraintsx​(i)∈[−1,1]x(i)\in[-1,1]are enforced only fori∈Ii\in I.

Clearly, the objective to (4.2) satisfiesvg∗=vg∗​([n])≥vg∗​(I)v^{*}_{g}=v^{*}_{g}([n])\geq v^{*}_{g}(I)for allI⊂[n]I\subset[n](as the domainK∩[−1,1]n⊂K∩S​(I)K\cap[-1,1]^{n}\subset K\cap S(I)).
On the other hand,
Lemma4.11gives thatvg∗=vg∗​(Ig)​for everyg∈ℝn.v^{*}_{g}=v^{*}_{g}(I_{g})\text{ for every $g\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.(4.3)

## Completing the proof.

We now show that, with high probability over the choice ofgg, the valuevg∗​(I)v^{*}_{g}(I)is small foreveryIIsuch that|I|≤δ​n|I|\leq\delta n.

## Lemma 4.12.

For any fixed subsetI⊂[n]I\subset[n]with|I|≤δ​n|I|\leq\delta n,ℙg​[vg∗​(I)≥8​(ϵ+δ)​n]≤exp⁡(−(ϵ+δ)​n).\mathbb{P}_{g}[v_{g}^{*}(I)\geq\sqrt{8(\epsilon+\delta)n}]\leq\exp(-(\epsilon+\delta)n).

Asvg∗​(I)=d​(g,K∩S​(I))v_{g}^{*}(I)=d(g,K\cap S(I)), we apply Lemma4.9withA=K∩S​(I)A=K\cap S(I).
To boundγn​(A)\gamma^{n}(A)from below, note thatS​(I)=∩i∈ISiS(I)=\cap_{i\in I}S_{i}is an intersection of stripsSi={x:|x​(i)|≤1}S_{i}=\{x:|x(i)|\leq 1\}, and thus by the Sidak’s Lemma3.14,γn​(K∩S​(I))≥exp⁡(−ϵ​n)⋅(γ1​([−1,1]))|I|≥exp⁡(−(ϵ+δ)​n),\gamma^{n}(K\cap S(I))\geq\exp(-\epsilon n)\cdot(\gamma^{1}([-1,1]))^{|I|}\geq\exp(-(\epsilon+\delta)n),

where we use thatγ1​([−1,1])≥0.682≥1/e\gamma^{1}([-1,1])\geq 0.682\geq 1/eand|I|≤δ​n|I|\leq\delta n.
∎

Applying a union bound over all such subsets, we have that, with probability at least1−2H​(δ)​n​exp⁡(−(ϵ+δ)​n)1-2^{H(\delta)n}\exp(-(\epsilon+\delta)n),vg∗​(I)≤8​(ϵ+δ)​n​for allIwith​|I|≤δ​n.v^{*}_{g}(I)\leq\sqrt{8(\epsilon+\delta)n}\,\,\,\,\text{ for all $I$ with }|I|\leq\delta n.

By (4.3) this means thatℙ​[vg∗≤8​(ϵ+δ)​n]≥1−2H​(δ)​n​exp⁡(−(ϵ+δ)​n)−ℙ​[|Ig|≥δ​n].\mathbb{P}[v_{g}^{*}\leq\sqrt{8(\epsilon+\delta)n}]\geq 1-2^{H(\delta)n}\exp(-(\epsilon+\delta)n)-\mathbb{P}[|I_{g}|\geq\delta n].

Choosingϵ\epsilonto be a sufficiently small constant, andδ≤c​ε/ln⁡(1/ε)\delta\leq c\varepsilon/\ln(1/\varepsilon)for sufficiently smallcc, and using Lemma4.10, we have that|Ig|≥δ​n|I_{g}|\geq\delta nwith probability exponentially close to11. This completes the proof of Theorem4.7.

## 4.3Vector Discrepancy and SDPs

The partial coloring method can often give close to tight bounds for various general classes of matrices. E.g. any matrixMMwithm=nm=nrows and entries in[−1,1][-1,1]hasO​(n)O(\sqrt{n})discrepancy.
However, one can ask a stronger question —
givenanyspecific matrixMM, can we efficiently find a coloring with discrepancy close todisc⁡(M)\operatorname{disc}(M)?

Unfortunately, this is too much to hope for and discrepancy is very hard to approximate in general. For example, we will prove the following result in Chapter5.

## Theorem 4.13.

For a set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])with|SS|=O​(n)|\SS|=O(n)sets, it is𝖭𝖯\mathsf{NP}-hard to distinguish whetherdisc⁡(SS)=0\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=0ordisc⁡(SS)=Ω​(n)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=\Omega(\sqrt{n}).

This hardness result also suggests that LP or SDP based convex programming relaxations for discrepancy are unlikely to give useful information about the actual discrepancydisc⁡(M)\operatorname{disc}(M).

While this is true in a sense, it turns out that SDPs can be very useful. In fact, the first algorithmic results for partial colorings were based on SDPs and random walks. In general, SDPs gives a flexible and powerful way to “design” random walks. Let us begin by exploring relaxations for discrepancy more closely.

LP relaxation.Recall that a linear program (LP) consists of variablesx​(1),…,x​(n)∈ℝx(1),\ldots,x(n)\in\mathbb{R}, viewed as a vectorx=(x​(1),…,x​(n))∈ℝnx=(x(1),\ldots,x(n))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, and the goal is to optimize some linear objective subject to some linear constraints. LPs can be solved optimally in polynomial time.

Given anm×nm\times nmatrixMMwith rowsv1,…,vm∈ℝnv_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, the following is a natural LP relaxation for discrepancy,min⁡t​s.t.−t≤⟨vi,x⟩≤t,∀i∈[m]​and−1≤x​(j)≤1,∀j∈[n].\min t\,\,\,\,\text{ s.t. }\,\,-t\leq\langle v_{i},x\rangle\leq t,\,\,\,\forall i\in[m]\,\,\,\text{ and }\,-1\leq x(j)\leq 1,\,\,\,\forall j\in[n].

However this LP always has the trivial solutionx=𝟎x=\mathbf{0}with objectivet=0t=0, and hence is not at all useful.

## Semidefinite Programs (SDPs).

These
are a more general class of optimization problems, and an SDP can be viewed as an LP with
variables of the formX​(i,j)X(i,j)for1≤i,j≤n1\leq i,j\leq n, arranged as entries of ann×nn\times nmatrixXX, where we require thatXXbe symmetric and positive semidefinite (PSD), denoted byX⪰0X\succeq 0.

Formally, for matricesA,BA,B, let⟨A,B⟩=Tr​(AT​B)=∑i​jA​(i,j)​B​(i,j)\langle A,B\rangle=\text{Tr}(A^{T}B)=\sum_{ij}A(i,j)B(i,j)denote the usual trace inner product for matrices. A SDP has the formmax⁡⟨C,X⟩s.t.⟨Ak,X⟩≤bk,1≤k≤m,X⪰0\max\langle C,X\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\langle A_{k},X\rangle\leq b_{k},\quad 1\leq k\leq m,\quad X\succeq 0

whereC,A1,…,Am∈ℝn×nC,A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}are arbitrary, and it can be solved in polynomial time to any desired accuracy under mild assumptions.

Recall that a matrixX⪰0X\succeq 0is PSD if and only if it is the Gram matrix of some vectorsw1,…,wn∈ℝnw_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, i.e.,X​(i,j)=⟨wi,wj⟩X(i,j)=\langle w_{i},w_{j}\rangle. So we can equivalently view SDPs as vector programs — where the variables are the vectorswiw_{i}and we can impose arbitrary linear constraints on their inner products (but not on thewiw_{i}themselves).

SDP relaxation for discrepancy.Given a matrixM∈ℝm×nM\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}, and some target discrepancy boundλ\lambda, consider the following SDP, where the colorsx​(j)∈±1x(j)\in\pm 1are allowed to be arbitrary unit vectors, and the analogous quantity for the discrepancy|∑jM​(i,j)​x​(j)||\sum_{j}M(i,j)x(j)|of rowiibecomes‖∑jM​(i,j)​wj‖2\|\sum_{j}M(i,j)w_{j}\|_{2}. See Figure4.3for an example.‖∑jM​(i,j)​wj‖22≤λ2for​i∈[m],‖wj‖22=1j∈[n].\left\|\sum_{j}M(i,j)w_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}\quad\text{for }i\in[m],\qquad\|w_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=1\quad{j\in[n].}(4.4)Figure 4.3:The unit vectorswj∈ℝnw_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}in the SDP solution.

Let us define the vector discrepancy of matrix as follows.

## Definition 4.14.

We define
the vector discrepancyvdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{vdisc}(M)of a matrixMMas the smallestλ≥0\lambda\geq 0for which (4.4) is feasible.

Clearly4.4impliesvdisc⁡(M)≤disc⁡(M)\operatorname{vdisc}(M)\leq\operatorname{disc}(M).
However, at first glance, vector colorings do not seem to give useful information. E.g., for any matrixMMwith entries in[−1,1][-1,1](as in Spencer’s theorem3.5), the solutionwi=eiw_{i}=e_{i}, theii-th standard basis vector, is always feasible withλ=n1/2\lambda=n^{1/2}.

Interestingly however, this SDP becomes quite useful whenλ≪n1/2\lambda\ll n^{1/2}, as it gives non-trivial correlations between the vectorswiw_{i}that we can exploit. In particular, even thoughdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{disc}(M)is hard to approximate (as we will prove in Chapter7), we can show the following instance-wise result.

## Theorem 4.15.

Given anym×nm\times nmatrixMM, one can find a coloring with discrepancyO​((log⁡m​log⁡n)1/2​herdisc⁡(M))O((\log m\log n)^{1/2}\operatorname{herdisc}(M))in polynomial time.

We first describe the algorithm and then prove Theorem4.15.

## 4.3.1Algorithm

At a high level, the algorithm performs a random walk as in the Lovett-Meka algorithm. However, instead of taking a random step in a subspace, the random increments for coordinates are carefully correlated so that the discrepancy increment for each row ofMMis small. In particular, at each step it solves the SDP in (4.4) (restricted to coordinates with colors not yet±1\pm 1) withλ=herdisc⁡(M)\lambda=\operatorname{herdisc}(M), and generates a step of the walk by using this solution as the covariance matrix.
We now describe this formally.

## Algorithm.

Fix a small step sizeγ=maxi,j⁡|M​(i,j)|/n2\gamma=\max_{i,j}|M(i,j)|/n^{2}and letδ=4​γ​log⁡(2​m​n/γ)\delta=4\gamma\log(2mn/\gamma).
Letλ=herdisc⁡(M)\lambda=\operatorname{herdisc}(M).555While we do not knowherdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M), we can do a binary search onλ\lambda.

Letxt−1x_{t-1}denote the coloring and letFt={j:|xt−1​(j)|>1−δ}F_{t}=\{j:|x_{t-1}(j)|>1-\delta\}be the set of fixed elements at the end of time stept−1t-1.
Initializex0​(j)=0x_{0}(j)=0forj∈[n]j\in[n]andF1=∅F_{1}=\emptyset.

At each timet=1,2,…,ℓ=(4​log⁡n)/γ2t=1,2,\ldots,\ell=(4\log n)/\gamma^{2}, do the following.

(i) Solve the SDP‖∑jM​(i,j)​wjt‖22≤λ2∀i∈[m],\displaystyle\|\sum_{j}M(i,j)w^{t}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}\quad\forall i\in[m],‖wjt‖22=1​if​j∉Ft,and​‖wjt‖22=0​otherwise.\displaystyle\|w^{t}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=1\text{ if }j\not\in F_{t},\text{ and }\|w^{t}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=0\text{ otherwise}.

(ii) Samplegt∼N​(0,In)g_{t}\sim N(0,I_{n}), and setxt​(j)=xt−1​(j)+γ​⟨gt,wjt⟩​for eachj∈[n].x_{t}(j)=x_{t-1}(j)+\gamma\langle g_{t},w^{t}_{j}\rangle\text{ for each $j\in[n]$}.

SetFt+1={j:|xt​(j)|>1−δ}F_{t+1}=\{j:|x_{t}(j)|>1-\delta\}.

Output the final coloringx​(j)=sign​(xℓ​(j))x(j)=\text{sign}(x_{\ell}(j)).

## 4.3.2Analysis

The ideas are similar to those in Section4.1.

First, note that the SDP is always feasible at each timett. This is because we setλ=herdisc⁡(M)\lambda=\operatorname{herdisc}(M), and hencedisc⁡(M​(∗,[n]∖Ft))≤λ.\operatorname{disc}(M(*,[n]\setminus F_{t}))\leq\lambda.

Let us now consider how the coloringsxtx_{t}and the row discrepancies evolve.
Fix some elementjj. Its colorxt​(j)x_{t}(j)starts at0and evolves as a martingale with updatesΔ​xt​(j)=γ​⟨wjt,gt⟩,\Delta x_{t}(j)=\gamma\langle w^{t}_{j},g_{t}\rangle,which are distributed as a Gaussian with mean0and varianceγ2​‖wjt‖2\gamma^{2}\|w^{t}_{j}\|^{2}.
Similarly, for theii-th rowviv_{i}ofMM, its discrepancyxt​(vi):=∑jM​(i,j)​xt​(j)x_{t}(v_{i}):=\sum_{j}M(i,j)x_{t}(j)is0att=0t=0, and evolves as a martingale with increments∑jM​(i,j)​Δ​xt​(j)=∑jγ​⟨gt,∑jM​(i,j)​wjt⟩,\sum_{j}M(i,j)\Delta x_{t}(j)=\sum_{j}\gamma\langle g_{t},\sum_{j}M(i,j)w^{t}_{j}\rangle,

where the right hand side is a Gaussian with mean0and variance equal toγ2​‖∑jM​(i,j)​wjt‖2.\gamma^{2}\left\|\sum_{j}M(i,j)w^{t}_{j}\right\|^{2}.

The discrepancy bound.Fix some rowii. As‖∑jM​(i,j)​wjt‖2≤λ2\|\sum_{j}M(i,j)w^{t}_{j}\|^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}by the SDP constraint, the discrepancy incrementΔ​xt​(vi)\Delta x_{t}(v_{i})is a Gaussian with mean0and variance bounded above byγ2​λ2\gamma^{2}\lambda^{2}.
So
by Lemma4.4,ℙ​[|xℓ​(vi)|≥ℓ1/2​γ​λ⋅c​(log⁡m)1/2]≤2​exp⁡(−(c2​log⁡m)/2).\mathbb{P}[|x_{\ell}(v_{i})|\geq\ell^{1/2}\gamma\lambda\cdot c(\log m)^{1/2}]\leq 2\exp(-(c^{2}\log m)/2).

Pluggingℓ=(4​log⁡n)/γ2\ell=(4\log n)/\gamma^{2}and choosingcclarge enough, a union bound over themmrows gives thatdisc⁡(M,x)=O​(λ​(log⁡m​log⁡n)1/2)\operatorname{disc}(M,x)=O(\lambda\,(\log m\log n)^{1/2})whp.

All elements become fixed.As long asj∉Ftj\not\in F_{t}is alive, the SDP constraint ensures that‖wjt‖2=1\|w^{t}_{j}\|_{2}=1. So,Δ​xt​(j)\Delta x_{t}(j)is a mean0Gaussian with varianceγ2\gamma^{2}, and at each step,xt2​(j)x_{t}^{2}(j)increases on average by𝔼​[xt2​(j)|xt−1​(j)]−xt−12​(j)=𝔼​[(Δ​xt​(j))2|xt−1]=γ2.\mathbb{E}[x_{t}^{2}(j)|x_{t-1}(j)]-x^{2}_{t-1}(j)=\mathbb{E}[(\Delta x_{t}(j))^{2}|x_{t-1}]=\gamma^{2}.

A similar argument as in Lemma4.5gives thatxt​(j)x_{t}(j)will reach±1−δ\pm 1-\deltain(2/γ2)(2/\gamma^{2})steps with probability at least1/21/2(irrespective of where it started).
Dividingℓ=4​log⁡n/γ2\ell=4\log n/\gamma^{2}into2​log⁡n2\log nintervals of size2/γ22/\gamma^{2}, the probability thatjjstays active (i.e., is not fixed) till the end of the algorithm is at most1/n21/n^{2}. The result then follows by a union bound over thennelements.

Rounding Error.We condition on the event that at the end of the algorithm all elements are fixed, i.e.,Fℓ+1=[n]F_{\ell+1}=[n].
As a color is not updated once|xt​(j)|≥1−δ|x_{t}(j)|\geq 1-\delta, our choice ofδ\deltaensures that|xℓ​(j)|≤1|x_{\ell}(j)|\leq 1for alljjwith high probability.
So, rounding thexℓ​(j)x_{\ell}(j)to±1\pm 1at the end incurs error at mostn​δ=o​(maxi​j⁡|M​(i,j)|)n\delta=o(\max_{ij}|M(i,j)|)per row, which is negligible asherdisc⁡(M)≥maxi​j⁡|M​(i,j)|\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\geq\max_{ij}|M(i,j)|.

## 4.4Bibliographic Notes

The first algorithmic approach for partial coloring was given by Bansal[12], who gave efficient algorithms for various applications such as theO​(n1/2)O(n^{1/2})bound for Spencer’s problem withm=O​(n)m=O(n)sets, and theO​(t1/2​log⁡n)O(t^{1/2}\log n)bound for the Beck-Fiala problem. His approach was based on SDPs and random walks. Theorem4.15is also from[12].

Even though the approach of[12]gave efficient algorithms, the algorithms relied on the existence of partial coloring as given by Theorem3.4to argue the feasibility of the underlying SDPs, and hence was not fully constructive.
The first truly constructive algorithm for the partial coloring method was given by Lovett and Meka[83], which we described in Section4.1,

The algorithm in Section4.2, for general convex bodies, is due to Rothvoss[108].
Another elegant algorithm, that also works for general symmetric convex bodiesKK, is due to Eldan and Singh[49]. This algorithm is also very simple to state —– Pick a uniformly random directionθ∈ℝn\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}and solve the linear programmax⁡⟨θ,x⟩\max\langle\theta,x\ranglesubject tox∈K∩[−1,1]x\in K\cap[-1,1].

The Gaussian Isoperimetric inequality was proved by Sudakov and Tsirelson[119]and independently by Borell[35]. Several other proofs are now known and we refer to the note by Ledoux[73]. We will see a generalization of this inequality called Ehrhard’s inequality in Chapter6.

Interestingly Rothvoss’s algorithm enjoys a guarantee similar to Theorem4.15. Dadush, Nikolov, Talwar, and Tomczak-Jaegermann showed that, using Rothvoss’s algorithm withK={x∈ℝn:‖M​x‖∞≤C​herdisc⁡(M)}K=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|Mx\|_{\infty}\leq C\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\}for a large enough constantCC, we get a partial coloring with discrepancyO​(herdisc⁡(M))O(\operatorname{herdisc}(M)). We can then extend the partial coloring to a full coloringxxwith discrepancyO​(log⁡(n)​herdisc⁡(M))O(\log(n)\operatorname{herdisc}(M))[45]. They also showed that this holds more generally for discrepancy measured in any norm.

The SDP-based approach in Section4.3was also used in[14], with some additional constraints, to obtain the first algorithmic bound for the Komlós problem, matching Banaszczyk’s non-constructive bound[10]. We will see Banaszczyk’s method in Chapter5and the algorithm of[14]in Chapter8.

Several other algorithmic approaches have also been developed.
These are typically stated for specific problems, but often the underlying idea is more generally applicable.
Harvey, Schwartz and Singh[58]developed an algorithm based on inscribing ellipsoids and random walks.
Rothvoss and Reis[104]consider another variant that combines ideas from the algorithms in Section4.1and4.2.
More recently, deterministic algorithms based on
barrier potential functions[18]and regularized optimization[100]have also been developed. Remarkably, the approach of[100], in addition to being algorithmic, also improves the constant for Spencer’s problem form=nm=nsets from5.325.32in[113]to3.673.67.

Beyond discrepancy, these approaches have also led to several new results in areas such as approximation algorithms, computational geometry, machine learning, differential privacy and graph sparsification. We do not discuss these here due to lack of space, but would like to highlight the breakthrough result of Rothvoss[107]and Hoberg and Rothvoss[60], who used these ideas to obtain an additiveO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)approximation for the classical bin packing problem, improving on the long-standingO​(log2⁡n)O(\log^{2}n)bound by Karmarkar and Karp[66]. For a general connection between discrepancy and rounding techniques, we refer to[13].

There has also been a lot of recent interest in designing discrepancy algorithms with fast running times[61,62]. In Chapter10, we will see a beautiful algorithm due to Alweiss, Liu and Sawhney[6], that runs in time linear innnz​(M)\text{nnz}(M), the number of non-zero entries ofMM, and also works in the online setting.

Matoušek[90]used the connection between vector discrepancy and hereditary discrepancy in Section4.3, together with SDP duality, to show a remarkable result that the hereditary discrepancy of any matrixMMis tightly characterized by the so-called determinant lower bound (see Chapter7for the definition). The lower bounddetLB⁡(M)≤herdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(M)was shown by Lovász, Spencer and Vesztergombi[82], and we give an exposition of their proof in Chapter7. Matoušek showed the upper boundherdisc⁡(M)≤O​(log⁡n)3/2​detLB⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\leq O(\log n)^{3/2}\operatorname{detLB}(M).
This was further improved by Jiang and Reis[63]to a logarithmic bound, which is also the best possible[79].
We will explore some related ideas further in Chapter7.

## Chapter 5Banaszczyk’s Method

A problem with the partial coloring method is that it requiresO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)rounds to obtain a full coloring, often resulting in an extraO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)factor loss in the overall discrepancy bound. For example,
for the Komlós problem (Problem2), we incurredO​(log⁡n)O(\log n)discrepancy in
Exercise3.9, even though there exists a partial
coloring withO​(1)O(1)discrepancy.

In a remarkable result[10], Banaszczyk gave a different way
to find a full coloring directly, which often gives better
bounds111Though it does not seem to be directly comparable with
the partial coloring method. For example, we do not know how to
obtain theO​(n)O(\sqrt{n})for Spencer’s problem using Banaszczyk’s
result, and, in particular, improves the bound
toO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n}).
This was the best known bound for the Komlós problem until very recently[17].

We now state Banaszczyk’s theorem.
In addition to giving an improved bound for the Komlós problem,
it gives a powerful and general geometric result which has many other
surprising applications. We will discuss some of them in this chapter, and others later in the book.

## Theorem 5.1(Banaszczyk[10]).

LetKKbe an arbitrary closed convex set inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}with Gaussian measureγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2, and letv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}be arbitrary vectors with length‖vi‖2\|v_{i}\|_{2}at most1/51/5. Then there exist signsx​(1),…,x​(n)∈{−1,1}x(1),\ldots,x(n)\in\{-1,1\}such that∑i=1nx​(i)​vi∈K\sum_{i=1}^{n}x(i)v_{i}\in K.

## Remark 5.2.

At first glance, the constants1/21/2and1/51/5above may seem
somewhat arbitrary. However,γm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2ensures that
the origin0{0}is inKK, which is necessary if say, eachvi=0v_{i}={0}. Similarly, some upper bound on‖vi‖2\|v_{i}\|_{2}is also
necessary; e.g., ifKKis the strip{x∈ℝm:|x​(1)|≤a}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:|x(1)|\leq a\}witha≈0.6745a\approx 0.6745so thatγm​(K)=1/2\gamma^{m}(K)=1/2, then no
signs may exist if‖vi‖2>a\|v_{i}\|_{2}>a, e.g., ifn=1n=1andv1=b​e1v_{1}=be_{1}(i.e., the vector withbbin the first coordinate and0’s
everywhere else) for someb>ab>a.

For most applications, these constants do not really matter and there is a lot of slack. For example, ifKKis the unitℓ2\ell_{2}-ball of radiusm\sqrt{m}, thenγm​(K)=Θ​(1)\gamma^{m}(K)=\Theta(1), butγm​(2​K)\gamma^{m}(2K)is exponentially close to11.

## 5.1Applications

The ability to choose the bodyKKin Theorem5.1makes the result quite powerful. Let us consider some examples.

## 5.1.1Komlós Problem

Theorem5.1directly gives the following for the Komlós problem.

## Theorem 5.3.

Given vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vi‖2≤1\|v_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1, there
exists a coloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}such that‖∑ix​(i)​vi‖∞≲log⁡(2​m)\|\sum_{i}x(i)v_{i}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(2m)}.

## Proof.

We apply Theorem5.1toK:=[−t,t]mK:=[-t,t]^{m}withttlarge enough so thatγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2. Asγm​(K)=ℙ​[g∈K]=ℙ​[‖g‖∞≤t]≥1/2,\gamma^{m}(K)=\mathbb{P}[g\in K]=\mathbb{P}[\|g\|_{\infty}\leq t]\geq 1/2,

whereg=(g​(1),…,g​(m))g=(g(1),\ldots,g(m))is a standard Gaussian random vector
inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}. We haveℙ​[|gi|≥t]≤e−t2/2\mathbb{P}[|g_{i}|\geq t]\leq e^{-t^{2}/2}and settingt=2​ln⁡(2​m)t=\sqrt{2\ln(2m)}suffices by a union bound over themmcoordinates.
∎

## Remark 5.4.

This also gives anO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n})bound using a simple
trick.222Even though the span ofv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}has
dimension at mostnn, we cannot assume thatm=nm=nhere as the

each‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1, and thus there are at mostn2n^{2}rowsuiu_{i}with‖ui‖22≥1/n\|u_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\geq 1/n. For the remaining rows, by
Cauchy-Schwarz, any coloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}achieves
discrepancy at most|⟨ui,x⟩|≤‖ui‖2​‖x‖2≤1,|\langle u_{i},x\rangle|\leq\|u_{i}\|_{2}\|x\|_{2}\leq 1,as‖x‖2=n\|x\|_{2}=\sqrt{n}. Thus we can assume thatm≤n2m\leq n^{2}.

## 5.1.2Discrepancy and theγ2\gamma_{2}Norm

The above example can be substantially generalized by changing the
choice of the bodyKK. In Theorem5.3, we choseKKto be theℓ∞m\ell_{\infty}^{m}ball, sufficiently scaled up, and bounded
the discrepancy of a matrixMMwhose columns haveℓ2\ell_{2}norm at
most11. On the other hand, ifMMhas rows withℓ2\ell_{2}norm at
most11, we can chooseKKto be{x:‖M​x‖∞≤t}\{x:\|Mx\|_{\infty}\leq t\}for
a large enoughtt, and apply Theorem5.1to the standard
basis vectors. (For this case, Theorem5.1is an overkill,
and picking a uniformly random coloring gives the same result.) The
following result interpolates between these two cases.

## Lemma 5.5.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMthat can be written as the matrix
productM=U​VM=UVof two matricesU∈ℝm×kU\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}andV∈ℝk×nV\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}, where each rowuiu_{i}ofUUsatisfies‖ui‖2≤r\|u_{i}\|_{2}\leq r, and each columnvjv_{j}ofVVsatisfies‖vj‖2≤c\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq c,
we havedisc⁡(M)≲r​c​ln⁡(2​m).\operatorname{disc}(M)\lesssim rc\sqrt{\ln(2m)}.

## Proof.

Let us define the convex bodyK={y∈ℝk:‖U​y‖∞≤r​2​ln⁡(2​m)}K=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\|Uy\|_{\infty}\leq r\sqrt{2\ln{(2m)}}\}

To prove the lemma, we will apply Banaszczyk’s theorem to the bodyKKand the column vectors of the matrix15​c​V\frac{1}{5c}V. We first show that
the Gaussian measure ofKKis at least12\frac{1}{2}. For a standard
Gaussian random vectorgginℝk\mathbb{R}^{k}, by the union bound we have
that, fort:=r​2​ln⁡(2​m)t:=r\sqrt{2\ln(2m)},γk​(K)\displaystyle\gamma^{k}(K)=ℙ​[g∈K]=ℙ​[‖U​g‖∞≤t]≥1−∑i=1mℙ​[|⟨ui,g⟩|≥t]\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[g\in K]=\mathbb{P}[\|Ug\|_{\infty}\leq t]\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}[|\langle u_{i},g\rangle|\geq t]≥1−∑i=1mexp⁡(−t2/2​‖ui‖22)≥1−m​exp⁡(−t2/2​r2)≥12.\displaystyle\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\exp(-t^{2}/2\|u_{i}\|_{2}^{2})\geq 1-m\exp(-t^{2}/2r^{2})\geq\frac{1}{2}.
