one-dimensional Gaussian with mean0and variance‖ui‖22\|u_{i}\|_{2}^{2}.

Now Theorem5.1applied to the columns15​c​v1,…,15​c​vn\frac{1}{5c}v_{1},\ldots,\frac{1}{5c}v_{n}of15​c​V\frac{1}{5c}Vgives that there exists a±1\pm 1coloringxxsuch that∑j=1nx​(j)​vj5​c∈K\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)\frac{v_{j}}{5c}\in Kor equivalently thatV​x∈5​c​KVx\in 5cK.
Recalling the definition ofKK, this gives‖M​x‖∞=‖U​V​x‖∞≤5​r​c​2​ln⁡(2​m).∎\|Mx\|_{\infty}=\|UVx\|_{\infty}\leq 5rc\sqrt{2\ln{(2m)}}.\qed

Theγ2\gamma_{2}-norm of a matrixMM.The infimum of the productr​crcover all factorizationsM=U​VM=UVis
called theγ2\gamma_{2}-norm ofMM, and denotedγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M). In this notation, Lemma5.5can be
restated asdisc⁡(M)≤γ2​(M)​ln⁡(2​m)for all matricesM∈ℝm×n\operatorname{disc}(M)\leq\gamma_{2}(M)\sqrt{\ln(2m)}\quad\text{for all matrices $M\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$}(5.1)

It has several interesting
properties and is closely related to the hereditary discrepancy ofMM. In Chapter7we explore this further, and
develop a general method of proving upper and lower bounds on
hereditary discrepancy using theγ2\gamma_{2}norm.

## 5.2Vector Discrepancy for Komlós and the Cloning Trick

Recall that in vector discrepancy,
instead of asking for a±\pmcoloring,
we relax eachx​(j)x(j)to be some vector on the unit sphere inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}for an arbitrary dimensiondd. As we saw in Section4.3, vector coloring is
equivalent to a semidefinite programming relaxation of discrepancy.

We now give a surprising application of Banaszczyk’s theorem to show a constant upper bound for the vector discrepancy of instances of the Komlós problem, i.e., matrices with columns whoseℓ2\ell_{2}norm is at most11.
The proof uses what we call “the cloning trick”, which has several
other applications.

## Theorem 5.6.

For any vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1for
allj∈[n]j\in[n], there exists a positive integerddand a coloringx:[n]→ℝdx:[n]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}with‖x​(j)‖2=1\|x(j)\|_{2}=1for allj∈[n]j\in[n]such that the maximum vector discrepancy over all rowsi∈[m]i\in[m]satisfiesmaxi=1m⁡‖∑j=1nx​(j)​vj​(i)‖2≲1.\max_{i=1}^{m}\Bigg\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}(i)\Bigg\|_{2}\lesssim 1.(5.2)

## Proof.

The idea is to create several disjoint copies of the instance and
apply Banaszczyk’s theorem (with a suitable bodyKK) to obtain
several±1\pm 1colorings, such that for each rowii, the colorings
have low discrepancy onaverage. We can these combine these
colorings into a single vector coloring by associating each coloring to one coordinate of the vector
coloring. The key point is that requiring each row to have low discrepancy on average is a much more relaxed condition, and we do not need to lose thelog⁡n\sqrt{\log n}factor that we incur, if we are only allowed a single coloring. The details follow.

Letddbe a parameter that we will set later. (It will turn out thatd=O​(log⁡m)d=O(\log m)suffices.) Let, further,e1,…,ede_{1},\ldots,e_{d}be the
standard basis ofℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, and definevℓ,j:=eℓ⊗vj∈ℝd​mv_{\ell,j}:=e_{\ell}\otimes v_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{dm}, where⊗\otimesis the Kronecker product. I.e., vectorvℓ,jv_{\ell,j}consists ofddblocks ofmmcoordinates each, and each
block is all zeroes, except theℓ\ell-th block, which is equal tovjv_{j}. See Figure5.1for a picture of the block
structure of(vℓ,j)ℓ,j(v_{\ell,j})_{\ell,j}.
We have‖vℓ,j‖2=‖eℓ‖2​‖vj‖2=‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{\ell,j}\|_{2}=\|e_{\ell}\|_{2}\|v_{j}\|_{2}=\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1,
by basic properties of Kronecker products.Figure 5.1:The block structure of(vℓ,j)ℓ,j(v_{\ell,j})_{\ell,j}ford=3d=3. All unshaded entries are0’s.

Consider now the convex bodyK=∩i=1mKiK=\cap_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}inℝd​m\mathbb{R}^{dm}defined by the intersection of the bodiesKi={y∈ℝd​m:∑ℓ=1dy​(ℓ,i)2≤2​d},K_{i}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{dm}:\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}y(\ell,i)^{2}\leq 2d\},

where we usey​(ℓ,i)y(\ell,i)as shorthand fory​((ℓ−1)​m+i)y((\ell-1)m+i). In other
words, we think of vectors inℝd​m\mathbb{R}^{dm}as havingddblocks ofmmcoordinates each, andy​(ℓ,i)y(\ell,i)is theii-th coordinate in theℓ\ell-th block.
We claim thatKKhas large Gaussian measure.

## Claim 5.7.

Ford≲log⁡(2​m)d\lesssim\log(2m)large enough,γd​m​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{dm}(K)\geq 1/2.

## Proof.

AsK=∩i=1mKiK=\cap_{i=1}^{m}K_{i}, by the union bound it suffices to show
thatγd​m​(ℝd​m∖Ki)=ℙ​[∑ℓ=1dg​(ℓ,i)2>2​d]≤1/2​m\gamma^{dm}(\mathbb{R}^{dm}\setminus K_{i})=\mathbb{P}[\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}g(\ell,i)^{2}>2d]\leq 1/2m, where theg​(ℓ,i)g(\ell,i)are
independent standard Gaussian random variables.

For eachiiandℓ\ell, we have𝔼​[g​(ℓ,i)2]=1\mathbb{E}[g(\ell,i)^{2}]=1andg​(ℓ,i)2g(\ell,i)^{2}has subexponential tails, and thus by standard tail bounds,e.g.,[126, Corollary 2.8.3], we haveℙ​[∑ℓ=1dg​(i,ℓ)2≥(1+ε)​d]≤2​exp⁡(−c​d​min⁡(ε,ε2)),\mathbb{P}\Big[\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}g(i,\ell)^{2}\geq(1+\varepsilon)d\Big]\leq 2\exp(-cd\min(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{2})),

for some fixedc>0c>0. Pluggingε=1\varepsilon=1,d≥1c​ln⁡(4​m)d\geq\frac{1}{c}\ln(4m)gives the result.
∎

Consider the coloringx′∈{−1,1}d​nx^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}^{dn}obtained by applying Theorem5.1to thed​ndnvectorsvℓ,jv_{\ell,j}and the bodyKK. Let us usex′​(ℓ,j)x^{\prime}(\ell,j)for the color (i.e., value in{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}) given tovℓ,jv_{\ell,j}. Letyydenote the
resulting discrepancy vector∑ℓ=1d∑j=1nx′​(ℓ,j)​vℓ,j\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{n}x^{\prime}(\ell,j)v_{\ell,j}.
Then15​y∈K\frac{1}{5}y\in K, and, by the definition ofKK, we have
that15​y∈Ki\frac{1}{5}y\in K_{i}for eachi∈[m]i\in[m]. Thus,∑ℓ=1dy​(ℓ,i)2≤10​d\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}y(\ell,i)^{2}\leq 10d.
Also notice that, asvℓ,j​(ℓ,i)=vj​(i)v_{\ell,j}(\ell,i)=v_{j}(i)for eachℓ\ell,
andvℓ,j​(ℓ′,i)=0v_{\ell,j}(\ell^{\prime},i)=0wheneverℓ≠ℓ′\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}, the entries ofyyarey​(ℓ,i)=∑j=1nx′​(ℓ,j)​vℓ,j​(ℓ,i)=∑j=1nx′​(ℓ,j)​vj​(i).y(\ell,i)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x^{\prime}(\ell,j)v_{\ell,j}(\ell,i)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x^{\prime}(\ell,j)v_{j}(i).

This gives us that∑ℓ=1d(∑j=1nx′​(ℓ,j)​vj​(i))2=∑ℓ=1dy​(ℓ,i)2≤50​d.\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x^{\prime}(\ell,j)v_{j}(i)\Big)^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}y(\ell,i)^{2}\leq 50d.(5.3)

Consider the vector coloringx:[n]→ℝdx:[n]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}with vectorsx​(j):=1d​(x′​(1,j),…,x′​(d,j))forj∈[n].x(j):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}(x^{\prime}(1,j),\ldots,x^{\prime}(d,j))\qquad\text{ for $j\in[n]$}.

Clearly,‖x​(j)‖2=1\|x(j)\|_{2}=1as eachx′​(ℓ,j)x^{\prime}(\ell,j)is in{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}.
Moreover, for eachi∈[m]i\in[m], the vector discrepancy is at most‖∑j=1nx​(j)​vj​(i)‖22\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}(i)\bigg\|_{2}^{2}=1d​∑ℓ=1d(∑j=1nx′​(ℓ,j)​vj​(i))2=1d​∑ℓ=1dy​(ℓ,i)2≤10,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x^{\prime}(\ell,j)v_{j}(i)\Big)^{2}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}y(\ell,i)^{2}\leq 10,

where the last two steps follow from (5.3).
∎

Let us mention another equivalent view of
Theorem5.6. Instead of defining the vectorsx​(j)=1d​(x′​(1,j),…,x′​(d,j))x(j)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}(x^{\prime}(1,j),\ldots,x^{\prime}(d,j)), we think ofx′x^{\prime}as defining a random coloring. In particular, we can setx​(j):=x​(ℓ,j)x(j):=x(\ell,j), whereℓ∈[d]\ell\in[d]is chosen uniformly at
random. Then the proof of Theorem5.6shows thatmaxi=1m⁡𝔼​(∑jx​(j)​vj​(i))2≲1.\max_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{j}x(j)v_{j}(i)\Big)^{2}\lesssim 1.(5.4)

Theorem5.6in fact holds with the implied constant on the right hand side of5.2equal to11, which is clearly best possible. We give a different proof of a more general version of this better upper bound in Chapter10.

## 5.3The Main Geometric Core

The original proof of Theorem5.1by Banaszczyk was non-algorithmic and based on clever convex geometric argument and properties of Gaussian measures.
The main technical core of his proof is the following geometric fact. See also Figure5.2.

## Theorem 5.8.

For any convex bodyK∈ℝmK\in\mathbb{R}^{m}withγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2and any vectoru∈ℝmu\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖u‖2≤1/5\|u\|_{2}\leq 1/5, there is a convex bodyK∗uK*ucontained in(K−u)∪(K+u)(K-u)\cup(K+u)such thatγm​(K∗u)≥γm​(K)\gamma^{m}(K*u)\geq\gamma^{m}(K).Figure 5.2:The bodyK∗uK*uobtained fromKK.

Chapter6will be devoted entirely to the proof of Theorem5.8.
For now, it does not matter how the bodyK∗uK*uis obtained, and let us see how Theorem5.1follows directly from Theorem5.8.

## Proof.

(Theorem5.1).
We apply induction on the number of vectorsnn.

The base case ofn=0n=0holds as the origin𝟎\mathbf{0}is
inKK. Indeed, if𝟎∉K\mathbf{0}\notin K, then, becauseKKis convex
and closed,
there must be a hyperplanehhseparating𝟎\mathbf{0}andKK,i.e.,
there is somey∈ℝmy\in\mathbb{R}^{m}andα<0\alpha<0such that⟨x,y⟩≤α\langle x,y\rangle\leq\alphafor allx∈Kx\in K(this is the hyperplane separator theorem, see[105, Corollary 11.4.2]). However,γm​(K)≤γm​({x∈ℝm:⟨x,y⟩≤α})=ℙ​[⟨g,y⟩≤α]<12,\gamma^{m}(K)\leq\gamma^{m}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\langle x,y\rangle\leq\alpha\})=\mathbb{P}[\langle g,y\rangle\leq\alpha]<\frac{1}{2},

for a standard Gaussian random vectorg∈ℝmg\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, contradicting
the assumptionγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2.

Suppose the result holds forn−1n-1.
Consider the convex bodyK′=K∗vnK^{\prime}=K*v_{n}. Thenγm​(K′)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K^{\prime})\geq 1/2by Theorem5.8, and
by the inductive hypothesis there exist signsx1,…,xn−1∈{−1,1}x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}\in\{-1,1\}such thatu:=x1​v1+⋯+xn−1​vn−1∈K′u:=x_{1}v_{1}+\cdots+x_{n-1}v_{n-1}\in K^{\prime}. Now asK′⊆(K−vn)∪(K+vn)K^{\prime}\subseteq(K-v_{n})\cup(K+v_{n}),
eitheru∈K−vnu\in K-v_{n}in which case we setxn=1x_{n}=1so thatx1​v1+…+xn−1​vn−1+xn​vn=u+vn∈Kx_{1}v_{1}+\ldots+x_{n-1}v_{n-1}+x_{n}v_{n}=u+v_{n}\in K, or, otherwiseu∈K+vnu\in K+v_{n}and we can setxn=−1x_{n}=-1.
∎

Despite the simplicity of this inductive argument, it is unclear
how to use it to obtain an efficient algorithm to find the
coloringxx. In general, a convex bodyKKcan be very complicated
and require exponential size to describe it in any reasonably accurate
way. Even if the initial bodyKKis simple (like the unit
cube or the Euclidean ball), a few applications of the∗*operator
above might, potentially, make it very complicated. In particular,
there is no known way to construct an efficient membership oracle for
the intermediate bodies in the inductive proof of
Theorem5.1above using a membership oracle for the initialKK.

## 5.4Equivalence to Sub-Gaussianity

Recall that a Gaussian random variableggwith mean0and
varianceσ2\sigma^{2}has a moment generating function𝔼​[exp⁡(λ​g)]=exp⁡(λ2​σ2/2)\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda g)]=\exp(\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}/2)for allλ∈ℝ\lambda\in\mathbb{R}. This property motivates the following (standard) definition.

## Definition 5.9(sub-Gaussian Random Variable).

We say that a mean0real-valued random variableXXisσ\sigma-sub-Gaussianif𝔼​[exp⁡(λ​X)]≤exp⁡(σ2​λ2/2)for allλ∈ℝ.\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda X)]\leq\exp(\sigma^{2}\lambda^{2}/2)\quad\text{for all $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$}.

As the name suggests, ifXXisσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian then it has sub-Gaussian tails.333Sometimes in the literature, this property is referred toσ2\sigma^{2}-sub-Gaussian instead, but we useσ\sigma-subguassian throughout the text.Indeed, settingλ=t/σ2\lambda=t/\sigma^{2}above and applying Markov’s inequality gives that for anyt>0t>0,ℙ​[X≥t]\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[X\geq t]≤infλ≥0ℙ​[exp⁡(λ​X)≥exp⁡(λ​t)]\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\lambda\geq 0}\mathbb{P}[\exp(\lambda X)\geq\exp(\lambda t)]≤infλ≥0exp⁡(λ2​σ2/2−λ​t)=exp⁡(−t2/2​σ2).\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\lambda\geq 0}\exp(\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}/2-\lambda t)=\exp(-t^{2}/2\sigma^{2}).

Notice that ifX1,…,XnX_{1},\ldots,X_{n}are independent andXiX_{i}isσi\sigma_{i}-sub-Gaussian with mean-zero, the sumX=∑iXiX=\sum_{i}X_{i}is(∑iσi2)1/2\left(\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right)^{1/2}-sub-Gaussian as𝔼​exp⁡(λ​X)=∏i𝔼​[exp⁡(λ​Xi)]≤∏iexp⁡(σi2​λ2/2).\mathbb{E}\exp(\lambda X)=\prod_{i}\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda X_{i})]\leq\prod_{i}\exp(\sigma_{i}^{2}\lambda^{2}/2).

Also, anyXXdistributed on[−c,c][-c,c]with mean-zero iscc-sub-Gaussian: this result is also known as Hoeffding’s lemma.

The definition above is extended to random vectors by asking that each
one-dimensional marginal is sub-Gaussian.

## Definition 5.10(sub-Gaussian Random Vector).

A vector valued random
variableX∈ℝmX\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with mean0isσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian if for all vectorsx∈ℝmx\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, we have that𝔼exp(⟨X,x⟩)]≤exp(σ2∥x∥2/2).\mathbb{E}\exp(\langle X,x\rangle)]\leq\exp(\sigma^{2}\|x\|^{2}/2).

Equivalently, the projection⟨X,θ⟩\langle X,\theta\rangleofXXin any directionθ\theta,‖θ‖2=1\|\theta\|_{2}=1is aσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian real-valued
random variable.

## An equivalent version of Banaszczyk’s Theorem.

Surprisingly, Theorem5.1is equivalent (up to variousO​(1)O(1)factors) to a statement that does not involve mentioning any convex bodyKK.

Consider the following statement.

## Theorem 5.11.

Given arbitrary vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1for everyj∈[n]j\in[n], there is a probability distributionDDover coloringsx∈{−1,1}nx\in\{-1,1\}^{n}such that the discrepancy
vectorY:=∑j=1nx​(j)​vjY:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}isO​(1)O(1)-sub-Gaussian
whenxxis sampled according toDD.

It turns out that Theorems5.1and5.11are equivalent up to constant factors. We sketch the argument below.

To show that Theorem5.1implies Theorem5.11one can
use a cloning trick similar to that in Section5.2.
The idea is to make multiple copies of the instance and apply Theorem5.1with a suitably defined convex bodyKK, and view the
uniform distribution on the colorings for these copies as the
distributionDD. This is similar to the alternative view of
Theorem5.6above, but, instead of asking for
the second moment bound (5.4), we ask that
the random vector∑j=1nx​(j)​vj\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}isO​(1)O(1)-sub-Gaussian.
The details of showing this are analogous to the proof of
Theorem5.6, but are more involved, as one needs
to make exponentially many copies. We refer to[67]for details.

The reverse implication will be more relevant for us, since we will
use it to give an algorithm for Theorem5.1in Chapter9. In particular, we will show that a
distributionDDsatisfying Theorem5.11can be
sampled efficiently.

Intuitively, Theorem5.11should imply Theorem5.1because
the random vectorYYin Theorem5.11isO​(1)O(1)sub-Gaussian, and asγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2, a standard Gaussiang∈ℝmg\in\mathbb{R}^{m}lies inKKwith probability at least1/21/2.
SoYY, being “more concentrated” than a Gaussian with varianceO​(1)O(1)in every direction, should also lie in someO​(1)O(1)-factor scaling ofKKwith
constant probability.

While this intuition can be made rigorous, a
significant hurdle is that the definition of a sub-Gaussian random
vector only requires that the one-dimensional marginals are
sub-Gaussian, and does not make any requirements about
higher-dimensional marginals. Nevertheless, the following comparison
theorem, which is a deep result of Talagrand, allows us to get around
this obstacle. We refer to Talagrand’s book[121]for
a proof; see also[126, Section 8.6].

## Theorem 5.12(Talagrand’s Comparison Theorem).

For any bounded setT⊆ℝmT\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}, and anyσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian
random vector inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}, we have𝔼​supt∈T⟨X,t⟩≲σ​𝔼​supt∈T⟨g,t⟩,\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}\langle X,t\rangle\lesssim\sigma\,\,\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}\langle g,t\rangle,

whereggis a standard Gaussian random vector.

To use Theorem5.12, we need the notion of a polar set, defined for a setK⊆ℝmK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}byK∘:={y∈ℝm:⟨x,y⟩≤1​∀x∈K}.K^{\circ}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\langle x,y\rangle\leq 1\,\,\,\,\forall x\in K\}.

As a consequence of the hyperplane separator theorem, whenKKis convex, closed, and
contains0,K∘∘=KK^{\circ\circ}=K[89, Chapter 5].

We give the main implication next. To avoid some technicalities, we
consider only the case whereγm​(K)≥3/4\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 3/4. This does not make
a difference for most applications of Theorem5.1.

## Theorem 5.13.

Suppose that the random vectorYYin Theorem5.11isσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian.
There is a universal constantc>0c>0such that, for any closed convex
setK⊆ℝmK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}containing0with Gaussian measureγm​(K)≥3/4\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 3/4, we haveℙ​[Y∈c​σ​K]≥1/2.\mathbb{P}[Y\in c\sigma K]\geq 1/2.

## Remark 5.14.

Notice that if we could sample efficiently from the distributionDD, then this also give an efficient algorithm to find a coloringxxwith∑jxj​vj∈2​c​c′​K\sum_{j}x_{j}v_{j}\in 2cc^{\prime}K, by sampling a couple of times fromDD.

## Proof.

LetBℓ2mB_{\ell_{2}^{m}}denote the unitℓ2m\ell_{2}^{m}-ball. We claim that12​Bℓ2m⊆K\frac{1}{2}B_{\ell_{2}^{m}}\subseteq K. Otherwise, there is a pointuu,‖u‖2=12\|u\|_{2}=\frac{1}{2},
that is not inKK, and, sinceKKis closed and convex, by the
hyperplane separator theorem there is a vectory∈ℝmy\in\mathbb{R}^{m}such that⟨x,y⟩≤1\langle x,y\rangle\leq 1but⟨u,y⟩>1\langle u,y\rangle>1. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,⟨u,y⟩>1\langle u,y\rangle>1implies‖y‖2>2\|y\|_{2}>2. Then, for the halfspaceH:={x∈ℝm:⟨x,y⟩≤1}H:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\langle x,y\rangle\leq 1\}we haveγm​(K)≤γm​(H)=ℙ​[⟨g,y⟩≤1]<Φ​(1/‖y‖2)≤Φ​(1/2)<3/4.\gamma^{m}(K)\leq\gamma^{m}(H)=\mathbb{P}[\langle g,y\rangle\leq 1]<\Phi(1/\|y\|_{2})\leq\Phi(1/2)<3/4.

Above,ggis a standard Gaussian vector inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m},Φ\Phiis the
CDF of the one-dimensional standard Gaussian, and we used the
fact that⟨g,y⟩\langle g,y\rangleis a one-dimensional Gaussian with mean0and standard deviation‖y‖2>2\|y\|_{2}>2. This inequality contradictsγm​(K)≥3/4\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 3/4.

Using polarity, we have thatt​K={x∈ℝm:⟨x,y⟩≤t​∀y∈K∘}tK=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\langle x,y\rangle\leq t\ \forall y\in K^{\circ}\}, for anyt≥0t\geq 0. Theorem5.12gives us𝔼​supz∈K∘⟨Y,z⟩≤c1​σ​𝔼​supz∈K∘⟨g,z⟩\mathbb{E}\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle Y,z\rangle\leq c_{1}\sigma\mathbb{E}\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle g,z\rangle(5.5)

for a standard Gaussian random vectorg∈ℝmg\in\mathbb{R}^{m}and some
absolute constantc1>0c_{1}>0.

We want to show that𝔼​supz∈K∘⟨g,z⟩≲1\mathbb{E}\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle g,z\rangle\lesssim 1. Since12​Bℓ2m⊆K\frac{1}{2}B_{\ell_{2}^{m}}\subseteq K, the Cauchy-Schwarz theorem
implies thatK∘⊆2​Bℓ2mK^{\circ}\subseteq 2B_{\ell_{2}^{m}}, and the functionfK​(x):=supz∈K∘⟨x,z⟩f_{K}(x):=\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle x,z\rangleis22-Lipschitz with respect to theℓ2m\ell_{2}^{m}norm. (Note thatfKf_{K}is just the norm with unit ballKKwhenK=−KK=-KandKKis bounded
and has nonempty interior.) Suppose, for contradiction, that𝔼​[fK​(g)]>C\mathbb{E}[f_{K}(g)]>Cfor some sufficiently large constantC>1C>1. Then, the
Gaussian concentration inequality[126, Corollary 8.6.2]gives us that, for
some absolute constantc2>0c_{2}>0,γm​(K)=ℙ​[fK​(g)≤1]≤ℙ​[fK​(g)≤𝔼​[fK​(g)]−(C−1)]≤e−c2​(C−1)2.\gamma^{m}(K)=\mathbb{P}[f_{K}(g)\leq 1]\leq\mathbb{P}[f_{K}(g)\leq\mathbb{E}[f_{K}(g)]-(C-1)]\leq e^{-c_{2}(C-1)^{2}}.

ChoosingCCsufficiently large contradictsγm​(K)≥34\gamma^{m}(K)\geq\frac{3}{4}, so𝔼​supz∈K∘⟨g,z⟩=𝔼​[fK​(g)]≤C\mathbb{E}\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle g,z\rangle=\mathbb{E}[f_{K}(g)]\leq C. Together with (5.5), this gives us𝔼​supz∈K∘⟨Y,z⟩≤c1​C​σ,\mathbb{E}\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle Y,z\rangle\leq c_{1}C\sigma,as well.
Now, by Markov’s inequality,supz∈K∘⟨Y,z⟩≤2​c1​C​σ\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle Y,z\rangle\leq 2c_{1}C\sigmawith probability at
least12\frac{1}{2}, and
therefore,Y∈(2​c1​C)​KY\in(2c_{1}C)Kwith the same probability.
∎

It is not hard to extend Theorem5.13tosymmetricclosed
convex setsKKwithγm​(K)≥12\gamma^{m}(K)\geq\frac{1}{2}. The main observation
is that such a setKKstill must contain a Euclidean ball of some
fixed constant radiusrr, since, if it did not, it would be
sandwiched between two hyperplanes of distance less than2​r2rapart. Extending the theorem to non-symmetricKKis, however,
non-trivial. For example, ifKKis the halfspace{x∈ℝm:x​(1)≤0}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:x(1)\leq 0\}, thenγm​(K)=12\gamma^{m}(K)=\frac{1}{2}, butKKdoes not contain a
Euclidean ball of any positive radius around the origin, and its polar
setK∘K^{\circ}is unbounded, so𝔼​supz∈K∘⟨Y,z⟩=∞\mathbb{E}\sup_{z\in K^{\circ}}\langle Y,z\rangle=\infty. Nevertheless, it is possible to algorithmically reduce the
case of non-symmetricKKwithγm​(K)≥12\gamma^{m}(K)\geq\frac{1}{2}to the
symmetric case: we refer to[44]for the details.

## 5.5Prefix discrepancy

In many applications of discrepancy, one might actually want to bound the discrepancy of every prefix sum, instead of just the overall sum∑ix​(i)​vi\sum_{i}x(i)v_{i}. One example is the application to the Steinitz Problem in Section1.4, which we revisit later in this section.
The following result, also due to Banaszczyk, controls the discrepancy of every prefix sum, at the expense of a slight increase in the Gaussian measure ofKK.

## Theorem 5.15.

Given arbitrary vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vi‖2≤1/5\|v_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1/5and any
convex bodyK⊆ℝmK\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}withγm​(K)≥1−1/(2​n)\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1-1/(2n),
there exists a±1\pm 1coloringxxsuch that∑j=1kx​(j)​vj∈K\sum_{j=1}^{k}x(j)v_{j}\in Kfor eachk∈[n]k\in[n].

Similarly to Theorem5.1, this result also follows from Theorem5.8based on a clever induction argument.

## Proof.

Consider the convex bodiesKn,Kn−1,…,K1K_{n},K_{n-1},\ldots,K_{1}defined
iteratively as follows:Kn:=KK_{n}:=KandKj:=(Kj+1∗vj+1)∩KK_{j}:=(K_{j+1}*v_{j+1})\cap K, forj=n−1,…,1j=n-1,\ldots,1.

We first show by backwards induction thatγm​(Kj)≥1/2+(j−1)/2​n\gamma^{m}(K_{j})\geq 1/2+(j-1)/2n. This implies thatγm​(Kj)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K_{j})\geq 1/2for allj≥1j\geq 1.

The base case ofj=nj=nholds asγm​(Kn)=γm​(K)≥1−1/2​n\gamma^{m}(K_{n})=\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1-1/2n.
Suppose the claim holds for somej≤nj\leq n. Thenγm​(Kj−1)\displaystyle\gamma^{m}(K_{j-1})=γm​((Kj∗vj)∩K)≥γm​(Kj∗vj)−γm​(ℝm∖K)\displaystyle=\gamma^{m}((K_{j}*v_{j})\cap K)\geq\gamma^{m}(K_{j}*v_{j})-\gamma^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus{K})≥γm​(Kj)−γm​(ℝm∖K)≥12+j−12​n−12​n=12+j−22​n,\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{m}(K_{j})-\gamma^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus{K})\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{j-1}{2n}-\frac{1}{2n}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{j-2}{2n},

where we use thatγm​(Kj∗vj)≥γm​(Kj)\gamma^{m}(K_{j}*v_{j})\geq\gamma^{m}(K_{j})asγm​(Kj)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K_{j})\geq 1/2by Theorem5.8, and thatγm​(ℝm∖K)≤1/(2​n)\gamma^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus{K})\leq 1/(2n).

We now show that any sequencex​(1),…,x​(k)∈{−1,+1}x(1),\ldots,x(k)\in\{-1,+1\}satisfying∑j=1ℓx​(j)​vj∈Kℓ\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}x(j)v_{j}\in K_{\ell}for allℓ∈[k]\ell\in[k], can be extended tox​(1),…,x​(k+1)∈{−1,+1}x(1),\ldots,x(k+1)\in\{-1,+1\}such that∑j=1k+1x​(j)​vj∈Kk+1\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}x(j)v_{j}\in K_{k+1}. This will imply the result as the final sequencex​(1),…,x​(n)x(1),\ldots,x(n)will satisfy∑j=1kx​(j)​vj∈Kk⊆K\sum_{j=1}^{k}x(j)v_{j}\in K_{k}\subseteq Kfor allk∈[n]k\in[n].

To initialize the sequence, we pick somex​(1)x(1)such thatx​(1)​v1∈K1x(1)v_{1}\in K_{1}. Such anx​(1)x(1)exists by Theorem5.1asγm​(K1)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K_{1})\geq 1/2.
Then, fork≥1k\geq 1, letx​(1),…,x​(k)x(1),\ldots,x(k)be the sequence chosen so
far, and defineyk:=∑j=1kx​(j)​vjy_{k}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}x(j)v_{j}. Thenyk∈Kk⊆Kk+1∗vk+1⊆(Kk+1+vk+1)∪(Kk+1−vk+1),y_{k}\in K_{k}\subseteq K_{k+1}*v_{k+1}\subseteq(K_{k+1}+v_{k+1})\cup(K_{k+1}-v_{k+1}),

and at least one ofyk+vk+1y_{k}+v_{k+1}oryk−vk+1y_{k}-v_{k+1}must lie inKk+1K_{k+1}. We setx​(k+1)x(k+1)accordingly and extend the sequence.
∎

It is possible to use the proof of Theorem5.15and the
cloning trick in order to formulate a statement similar to
Theorem5.11for prefix sums. We state this
theorem next, and refer to the work of Kulkarni, Reis, and Rothvoss[67]for the
proof.

## Theorem 5.16.

Given arbitrary vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vi‖2≤1\|v_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1, there is a distributionDDover coloringsx∈{−1,1}nx\in\{-1,1\}^{n}such that, for eachk∈[n]k\in[n], the prefix discrepancy
vectorYk:=∑j=1kx​(j)​vjY_{k}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}x(j)v_{j}isO​(1)O(1)-sub-Gaussian whenxxis sampled fromDD.

Unlike Theorem5.11, there is no known efficient
algorithm to sample from the distributionDD. More generally, it is
an intriguing open problem how to make Theorem5.15algorithmic.

## 5.5.1Prefix Komlós Problem and the Steinitz Lemma

Theorem5.15directly implies the following bound for the prefix version of the Komlós problem.

## Theorem 5.17(Prefix Komlós).

Given vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1,
there exists a coloringx:[n]→{−1,+1}x:[n]\to\{-1,+1\}such that‖∑j=1kx​(j)​vj‖2≲log⁡(2​n)\|\sum_{j=1}^{k}x(j)v_{j}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(2n)}for allk∈[n]k\in[n]. I.e., the signed series constant satisfiesss⁡(Bℓ2m,ℓ∞m,n)≲log⁡(2​n).\operatorname{ss}(B_{\ell_{2}^{m}},\ell_{\infty}^{m},n)\lesssim\sqrt{\log(2n)}.

## Proof.

We apply Theorem5.15withK:=[−t,t]mK:=[-t,t]^{m}andt>0t>0such
thatγm​(K)≥1−1/2​n.\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1-1/2n.

As in the proof of
Theorem5.3, we see that settingt≲log⁡(2​m​n)t\lesssim\sqrt{\log(2mn)}suffices.
By Remark5.4, we can also assume thatm≤n2m\leq n^{2}.
∎

A similar proof shows the following bound, which gets tantalizingly
close to resolving Problem3for the case of the
Euclidean norm.

## Theorem 5.18.

Letp≥2p\geq 2and suppose that the vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}each satisfy‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1. There exists a coloringx:[n]→{−1,+1}x:[n]\to\{-1,+1\}such that‖∑j=1kx​(j)​vk‖p≲p​m1/p+log⁡n\|\sum_{j=1}^{k}x(j)v_{k}\|_{p}\lesssim\sqrt{p}m^{1/p}+\sqrt{\log n}. I.e., the signed series constant satisfiesss⁡(Bℓ2m,ℓpm,n)≲p​m1/p+log⁡n.\operatorname{ss}(B_{\ell_{2}^{m}},\ell_{p}^{m},n)\lesssim\sqrt{p}m^{1/p}+\sqrt{\log n}.

## Proof.

We apply Theorem5.15withK=t​BℓpmK=tB_{\ell_{p}^{m}}, whereBℓpmB_{\ell_{p}^{m}}is the unit ball of theℓpm\ell_{p}^{m}norm, andttis
chosen so thatγm​(K)≥1−1/2​n\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1-1/2n. By Jensen’s inequality
and standard bounds on thepp-th moment of a
Gaussian[126, Proposition 2.5.2], we have𝔼​[‖g‖p]≤𝔼​[‖g‖pp]1/p=𝔼​[∑i=1m|g​(i)|p]1/p≲p​m1/p.\mathbb{E}[\|g\|_{p}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|g\|_{p}^{p}]^{1/p}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}|g(i)|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\lesssim\sqrt{p}m^{1/p}.

By the Gaussian concentration inequality, since∥⋅∥p\|\cdot\|_{p}is
1-Lipschitz with respect to the Euclidean norm whenp≥2p\geq 2,ℙ​[‖g‖p>𝔼​[‖g‖p]+s]≤e−c​s2\mathbb{P}[\|g\|_{p}>\mathbb{E}[\|g\|_{p}]+s]\leq e^{-cs^{2}}

for an absolute constantc>0c>0. Choosings:=c′​log⁡(2​n)s:=c^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(2n)}for a large enough constantc′>0c^{\prime}>0makes the
right hand side at most12​n\frac{1}{2n}. Since the left hand side
equalsγm​(ℝm∖K)\gamma^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus K)fort=𝔼​[‖g‖p]+s≲p​m1/p+log⁡(2​n)t=\mathbb{E}[\|g\|_{p}]+s\lesssim\sqrt{p}m^{1/p}+\sqrt{\log(2n)}, this proves the lemma.
∎

If Theorem5.18could be improved by removing the
additivelog⁡2​n\sqrt{\log 2n}term, then it would give tight bounds for
Problem3for any constantp≥2p\geq 2. In particular, via
Chobanyan’s transference theorem (Theorem1.8), it would implyst⁡(Bℓpm,ℓpm)≤m12−1p​st⁡(Bℓ2m,ℓpm)≤m12−1p​ss⁡(Bℓ2m,ℓpm)≲p​m,\operatorname{st}(B_{\ell_{p}^{m}},\ell_{p}^{m})\leq m^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\operatorname{st}(B_{\ell_{2}^{m}},\ell_{p}^{m})\leq m^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\operatorname{ss}(B_{\ell_{2}^{m}},\ell_{p}^{m})\lesssim\sqrt{pm},

which is tight for constantp≥2p\geq 2.

Another open
problem is to make Theorem5.18or Theorem5.17,
algorithmic. For both theorems, there is no known efficient algorithm to find
the coloringxxgiven the vectorsv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}.

## 5.5.2Prefix Discrepancy and theγ2\gamma_{2}Norm

Theorem5.15also immediately implies that
Lemma5.5can be extended to bound prefix discrepancy, at
the cost of a small increase in the logarithmic factor. Here we recall
that the prefix discrepancy of anm×nm\times nreal matrixM=(wi)i=1nM=(w_{i})_{i=1}^{n}is
the quantitypdisc⁡((wi)i=1n):=minx:[n]→{−1,+1}⁡maxj=1n⁡‖∑i=1jx​(i)​wi‖∞.\operatorname{pdisc}((w_{i})_{i=1}^{n}):=\min_{x:[n]\to\{-1,+1\}}\max_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}x(i)w_{i}\right\|_{\infty}.

This definition coincides with our definition ofpdiscℓ∞m\operatorname{pdisc}_{\ell_{\infty}^{m}}from Chapter1.

## Lemma 5.19.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMwe havepdisc⁡(M)≲γ2​(M)​ln⁡(2​m​n).\operatorname{pdisc}(M)\lesssim\gamma_{2}(M)\sqrt{\ln(2mn)}.

## Proof.

By the definition ofγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M), we need to show that ifMMcan be written as the matrix
productM=U​VM=UVof two matricesU∈ℝm×kU\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}andV∈ℝk×nV\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}, where each rowuiu_{i}ofUUsatisfies‖ui‖2≤r\|u_{i}\|_{2}\leq r, and each columnvjv_{j}ofVVsatisfies‖vj‖2≤c\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq c, thenpdisc⁡(M)≲r​c​ln⁡(2​m​n)\operatorname{pdisc}(M)\lesssim rc\sqrt{\ln(2mn)}.
Similarly to the proof of Lemma5.5, we define the convex bodyK={y∈ℝk:‖U​y‖∞≤r​2​ln⁡(2​m​n)}.K=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\|Uy\|_{\infty}\leq r\sqrt{2\ln{(2mn)}}\}.

Arguing as in the proof of Lemma5.5,
we haveγm​(K)≥1−12​n\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1-\frac{1}{2n}.
Now Theorem5.15applied to the columns15​c​v1,…,15​c​vn\frac{1}{5c}v_{1},\ldots,\frac{1}{5c}v_{n}of15​c​V\frac{1}{5c}Vimplies the lemma.
∎

In Chapter7we will use Lemma5.19to prove the best known upper bound for Tusnády’s problem (Problem1).

## 5.6Bibliographic Notes

Theorems5.1and5.3are due to Banaszczyk[10]. The
original proof was non-algorithmic and based on deep results from convex geometry. We will describe Banaszczyk’s proof in detail in
Chapter6.

Lemma5.5is implicit in Larsen’s work on data structure
lower bounds[69], and was made explicit by
Matoušek, Nikolov, and Talwar[91].

The cloning trick in Section5.2is due to
unpublished work by Shachar Lovett, Raghu Meka, and Oded
Regev. Nikolov[97]gave a different and direct proof of
Theorem5.6where the implied constant on the
right hand side of (5.2) is equal to11. Yet another proof of Theorem5.6, also with the constant11on the right hand side, was given by Dadush, Garg, Lovett, and Nikolov[43].
Interestingly, it is unclear how to show this tight
bound using the cloning trick, as the bodyKKin
Theorem5.1needs to be scaled by55if the vectorsvjv_{j}have unit length. We give another (previously unpublished) proof of the tight upper bound in Chapter10.

The equivalence between
Theorems5.1and5.11was shown by
Dadush, Garg, Lovett and Nikolov[43], motivated by the
question of finding an efficient algorithm for Theorem5.1. A direct proof of Theorem5.11,
and an efficient sampling algorithm for the distributionDD, were given
later by Bansal, Dadush, Garg and Lovett[15]using an algorithm called the Gram-Schmidt Walk. We will
describe this algorithm and its analysis in detail in Section9.

The argument we present here that Theorem5.1implies
Theorem5.11via the cloning trick is
different from the Dadush, Garg, Lovett, and Nikolov argument, which
was based on the minimax theorem. The cloning trick based argument
for
Theorems5.11and5.16appears in unpublished work of Nikolov. Kulkarni, Rothvoss and Reis
apply a similar argument to prove that the sequence of signs can even
be computed online, albeit via an inefficient
algorithm[67]. We consider online algorithms for
discrepancy minimization in more detail in Chapter10.

Theorem5.15for prefix discrepancy, and
Theorem5.17for the prefix Komlós problem are
also due to Banaszczyk, in a more recent work[11]. An intriguing open problem is to
find an efficient algorithmic version of either theorem. The
best known algorithmic bound in the prefix setting isO​(log⁡m​n)O(\log mn), which
remarkably also works in the online
setting[6], and is based on a beautiful algorithm called the Self-Balancing Walk, that we will describe in detail
in Chapter10. See also[67,40,81]for related progress.
Finally, we remark that an algorithmicO​((log⁡m​n)1/2)O((\log mn)^{1/2})discrepancy bound for the prefix Komlós
problem would already be interesting to obtain in the special case
where each vectorviv_{i}has only two non-zero entries, as it would
have implications for approximation algorithms for scheduling
problems[19].

## Chapter 6Proof of Banaszczyk’s Theorem

We now describe the original non-constructive proof of Theorem5.1due to Banaszczyk[10].
The proof uses several non-trivial and interesting properties of convex bodies and Gaussian measures, that are not widely used in theoretical computer science. So we give a detailed exposition of these ideas.

## Proof Overview.

Recall from Section5.3that the key technical core underlying Banaszczyk’s theorem was the following geometric result.

## Theorem 6.1.

Given any convex bodyK⊂ℝmK\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}with Gaussian measureγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2and vectoru∈ℝmu\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖u‖2≤1/5\|u\|_{2}\leq 1/5,111Let us denoter=‖u‖2r=\|u\|_{2}. The only property ofrrwe need is thatγ1​([−r,r])≤γ1​((−∞,−1])\gamma^{1}([-r,r])\leq\gamma^{1}((-\infty,-1]).
In particular,γ1​([−1/5,1/5])≈0.1585\gamma^{1}([-1/5,1/5])\approx 0.1585andγ1​((−∞,−1])≈0.1586\gamma^{1}((-\infty,-1])\approx 0.1586.there is a convex bodyK∗uK*ucontained in(K−u)∪(K+u)(K-u)\cup(K+u)such thatγm​(K∗u)≥γm​(K)\gamma^{m}(K*u)\geq\gamma^{m}(K).

The bodyK∗uK*uin Theorem6.1is obtained in a natural way fromKKanduu, see Figure6.1and Section6.1for a formal description.
The transformation ofKKtoK∗uK*uloses some points fromKKand gains some new points, and the proof is based on showing that the Gaussian measure gained is at least the measure lost.

A key difficulty however is that unlike the standard Lebesgue measure, the Gaussian measure changes in complicated ways upon moving points. E.g., while shiftingKKtoK+uK+udoes not change the Lebesgue measure, it is much harder to relateγm​(K+u)\gamma^{m}(K+u)toγm​(K)\gamma^{m}(K),
as the Gaussian density of each pointx∈Kx\in Kchanges fromexp⁡(−‖x‖2/2)\exp(-\|x\|^{2}/2)toexp⁡(−‖x+u‖2/2)\exp(-\|x+u\|^{2}/2)depending onxx.

The key idea is to apply several interesting transformations toKKandK∗uK*u, while preserving various properties, and eventually reduce them to very simple two dimensional bodies. In fact, our final task reduces to comparing the Gaussian measures of11-dimensional intervals!
These final computations however are still quite delicate and use the log-concavity of Gaussian measure in non-trivial ways.

Organization.The rest of the chapter is organized as follows. We describe the bodyK∗uK*uin Section6.1and describe some facts about Gaussians including the notion of Ehrhard symmetrization. In Section6.2, we apply the symmetrization alonguuto transformKKandK∗uK*uinto simpler bodies, but still lying inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}. In Section6.3we transform them further to22-dimensional bodies and finally in Section6.4we compare their volumes and discuss some facts about log-concave measures.

In Section6.5we discuss Ehrhard’s inequality, which is used in the transformations in Sections6.2and6.3.

First, by rotational symmetry of the Gaussian, we can assume thatu=r​e1u=re_{1}, wherer≤1/5r\leq 1/5.
Asu=r​e1u=re_{1}, let us view a pointx=(x1,…,xm)∈ℝmx=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}as(x1,y)∈ℝ×ℝm−1(x_{1},y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}wherey=(x2,…,xm)∈ℝm−1y=(x_{2},\ldots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}.

For eachy∈ℝm−1y\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}, letKy:={t:(t,y)∈K}K_{y}:=\{t:(t,y)\in K\}denote the segment obtained by intersectingKKwith the horizontal line{(t,y):t∈ℝ}\{(t,y):t\in\mathbb{R}\}.

consisting of segments ofKKalonguuthat are at least2​r2rwide.

(in factKy+[−r,r]K_{y}+[-r,r]when non-empty), and the set ofyywith non-empty fibers,
together with the endpoints of these fibers, vary affinely inyybecauseKKis convex.

To prove Theorem6.1, we need to show that the Gaussian measure we lose consisting of the segmentsKyK_{y}with|Ky|<2​r|K_{y}|<2r, is no more that the amount we gain by extending the intervalsKyK_{y}toKy+[−r,r]K_{y}+[-r,r]whenever|Ky|≥2​r|K_{y}|\geq 2r.

## Some Gaussian Facts.

Letϕ​(x)=(2​π)−1/2​exp⁡(−x2/2)\phi(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{2}/2)denote the standard Gaussian density.
Indd-dimensions the Gaussian density is a product distribution withϕ​(x)=∏i=1dϕ​(xi)=(2​π)−d/2​exp⁡(−‖x‖2/2)​forx=(x1,…,xd).\phi(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\phi(x_{i})=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(-\|x\|^{2}/2)\text{ for $x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})$}.

For a setA⊂ℝA\subset\mathbb{R}, letγ1​(A)=∫Aϕ​(x)​𝑑x\gamma^{1}(A)=\int_{A}\phi(x)dxdenote its measure, and letΦ​(x)=γ1​(−∞,x)=(2​π)−1/2​∫−∞xexp⁡(−t2/2)​𝑑t,\Phi(x)=\gamma^{1}(-\infty,x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\exp(-t^{2}/2)dt,

denote the cumulative distribution function ofϕ\phi.

The functionΦ−1:[0,1]→ℝ∪{−∞,∞}\Phi^{-1}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}will play an important role.
For a setA⊂ℝA\subset\mathbb{R}, we define the left-infinite intervalI​(A):=(−∞,Φ−1​(γ1​(A))].I(A):=(-\infty,\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(A))].

Note thatI​(A)I(A)same Gaussian measure asAA, asγ1​((−∞,x])=Φ​(x)\gamma^{1}((-\infty,x])=\Phi(x)for allxxand thusγ1​(I​(A))=γ1​(A)\gamma^{1}(I(A))=\gamma^{1}(A).

When working with Gaussian measures, it is often convenient to replace a setAAbyI​(A)I(A), as it makes it more extremal while preserving the Gaussian measure.
In particular forA⊂ℝA\subset\mathbb{R}, letA+[−r,r]:={x+y:x∈A,y∈[−r,r]},A+[-r,r]:=\{x+y:x\in A,y\in[-r,r]\},

denote the expansion ofAAbyrr.
Then, by the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality (inℝ\mathbb{R}) we have thatγ1​(I​(A)+[−r,r])≤γ1​(A+[−r,r])\gamma^{1}(I(A)+[-r,r])\leq\gamma^{1}(A+[-r,r])(6.2)

for allr≥0r\geq 0.
Also notice thatΦ−1​(γ1​(I​(A)+[−r,r]))\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(I(A)+[-r,r]))is simplyΦ−1​(γ1​(A))+r\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(A))+r. So we can write (6.2) asΦ−1​(γ1​(A+[−r,r]))≥Φ−1​(γ1​(I​(A)))+r.\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(A+[-r,r]))\geq\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(I(A)))+r.(6.3)

## Gaussian symmetrization along a direction.

For a setA⊂ℝdA\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}, and sayu=e1u=e_{1}, the Gaussian symmetrizationIu​(A)I_{u}(A)ofAAalonguuis obtained by replacing each horizontal segmentAy={t:(t,y)∈A}A_{y}=\{t:(t,y)\in A\}by the segmentI​(Ay)I(A_{y}).
Formally,Iu​(A):={(t,y):y∈ℝm−1,t≤Φ−1​(γ1​(Ay))}.I_{u}(A):=\{(t,y):y\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1},t\leq\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(A_{y}))\}.

For example, Figure6.3shows the bodyIu​(K)I_{u}(K)obtained fromKK.

Notice thatγd​(Iu​(A))=γd​(A)\gamma^{d}(I_{u}(A))=\gamma^{d}(A)asγ1​(I​(Ay))=γ1​(Ay)\gamma^{1}(I(A_{y}))=\gamma^{1}(A_{y})for each segmentAyA_{y}(and the coordinates other thanx1x_{1}do not change).

A very interesting fact proved by Ehrhard[47]is the following.

## Theorem 6.2.

IfA⊂ℝdA\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}is convex, then its Gaussian symmetrizationIu​(A)I_{u}(A)along any directionuuis also convex.

We will discuss this in more detail in Section6.5.
In Section6.3, we will also consider a more general notion called Ehrhard symmetrization, where symmetrizingA∈ℝdA\in\mathbb{R}^{d}along a subspaceUUgives ad−dim​(U)+1d-\text{dim}(U)+1dimensional body.
This will be used to reduce our problem to two dimensions.

For allyy,gK​(y)≤fK∗u​(y)g_{K}(y)\leq f_{K*u}(y)and thusK′⊂Iu​(K∗u)K^{\prime}\subset I_{u}(K*u).

## Proof.

Fix someyy. We first show that iffK∗u​(y)=−∞f_{K*u}(y)=-\infty, thengK​(y)g_{K}(y)is also−∞-\infty. Indeed, iffK∗u​(y)=−∞f_{K*u}(y)=-\inftythis means that|Ky|<2​r|K_{y}|<2rby (6.4). But thenγ1​(Ky)<γ1​([−r,r])\gamma^{1}(K_{y})<\gamma^{1}([-r,r])as[−r,r][-r,r]has the largest Gaussian measure among all2​r2rlength intervals. SofK​(y)=Φ−1​(γ1​(Ky))<Φ−1​(γ1​([−r,r]))=−p,f_{K}(y)=\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(K_{y}))<\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}([-r,r]))=-p,

and thusgK​(y)=−∞g_{K}(y)=-\inftyby definition (6.7).

Now, suppose|Ky|≥2​r|K_{y}|\geq 2r. ThenfK∗u​(y)=Φ−1​(γ1​(Ky+[−r,r]))f_{K*u}(y)=\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(K_{y}+[-r,r]))by (6.4), and
by the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality (6.3),fK∗u​(y)≥Φ−1​(γ1​(Ky))+r=fK​(y)+r≥gK​(y).∎f_{K*u}(y)\geq\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{1}(K_{y}))+r=f_{K}(y)+r\geq g_{K}(y).\qed

Abusing notation a bit, let us useKKto denoteIu​(K)I_{u}(K)(as we will not need the original bodyKKanymore).

K_{x-r}&\text{for $x\geq-p+r$}.\end{cases}(6.9)

We now apply another symmetrization, but this time along the subspaceW=span​(e2,…,em)W=\text{span}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}). This will reduceKKandK′K^{\prime}to two-dimensional convex bodies.

The bodiesKWK_{W}andKW′K^{\prime}_{W}have the following properties. First, it is easily checked thatγ2​(KW)=γm​(K)\gamma^{2}(K_{W})=\gamma^{m}(K)andγ2​(KW′)=γm​(K′)\gamma^{2}(K^{\prime}_{W})=\gamma^{m}(K^{\prime})as symmetrization is measure-preserving.
The second remarkable property which will follow from Ehrhard’s inequality in Section6.5is that this symmetrization preserves convexity.

## Proposition 6.4.

AsK,K′K,K^{\prime}are convex,KW,KW′K_{W},K^{\prime}_{W}are also convex.

## The structure ofKWK_{W}andKW′K^{\prime}_{W}.

It is useful to understand the structure ofKWK_{W}andKW′K^{\prime}_{W}more closely.

h_{K}(x-r)&\text{for $x\geq-p+r$}.\end{cases}

## The final simplification: ReducinghKh_{K}to a line.

At this stage, the only complicated quantity left is the functionhKh_{K}. We now show how to replacehKh_{K}by a line such thatγ2​(A)\gamma^{2}(A)can only increase whileγ2​(B)\gamma^{2}(B)can only decrease.

LetL​(x)L(x)denote the line through the pointsP=(−p,hK​(−p))P=(-p,h_{K}(-p))andQ=(−r/2,hK​(−r/2))Q=(-r/2,h_{K}(-r/2))on the curvehK​(x)h_{K}(x), and let us replace the curvehK​(x)h_{K}(x)byL​(x)L(x), see Figure6.7.

## Lemma 6.5.

ReplacinghK​(x)h_{K}(x)byL​(x)L(x)only increasesγ2​(A)\gamma^{2}(A)and only decreasesγ2​(B)\gamma^{2}(B). More precisely,γ2​(C)≥γ2​(A)\gamma^{2}(C)\geq\gamma^{2}(A)andγ2​(D)≤γ2​(B)\gamma^{2}(D)\leq\gamma^{2}(B).

## Proof.

We show that the measure of each horizontal segment inBBonly decreases and for those inAAit only increases.

Recall thathKh_{K}is concave and decreasing.
The horizontal segments(−∞,hK−1​(y))(-\infty,h_{K}^{-1}(y))that comprise the red regionAAcan only get larger ashk−1​(y)≤L−1​(y)h_{k}^{-1}(y)\leq L^{-1}(y)fory≥hK​(−p)y\geq h_{K}(-p). So this only increasesγ2​(A)\gamma^{2}(A).

The blue regionBBconsists of segmentsIx=(x,x+r)I_{x}=(x,x+r)forx≥−px\geq-p.
Notice that for any segmentIa=[a,a+r]I_{a}=[a,a+r]of lengthrr, ifa<−r/2a<-r/2its Gaussian measureγ1​(Ia)\gamma^{1}(I_{a})decreases
upon shiftingIaI_{a}to the left, and ifa≥−r/2a\geq-r/2thenγ1​(Ia)\gamma^{1}(I_{a})decreases upon shiftingIaI_{a}to the right.

The proof of Lemma6.8and later arguments use log-concavity, so let us recall this briefly, focusing only on the properties we need.

## Log-concavity.

A non-negative functionf:ℝd→ℝf:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}is called log-concave iflog⁡f\log fis concave.
Equivalently, for allx,yx,yand0≤λ≤10\leq\lambda\leq 1,f​(λ​x+(1−λ)​y)≥f​(x)λ​f​(y)1−λ.f(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)\geq f(x)^{\lambda}f(y)^{1-\lambda}.(6.11)

Here we will only be interested in functionsf:ℝ→ℝf:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}. We have the following equivalent characterization for such functions.

## Proposition 6.9.

A smooth functionf:ℝ→ℝf:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}is log-concave ifff​(x)/f​(x+a)f(x)/f(x+a)is non-decreasing inxxfor alla≥0a\geq 0.

## Proof.

Ifffis log-concave thenlog⁡f\log fis concave and hence its derivative(log⁡f)′(\log f)^{\prime}is non-increasing. So for any fixeda≥0a\geq 0,(log⁡f​(x)f​(x+a))′=(log⁡f​(x))′−(log⁡f​(x+a))′≥0.\left(\log\frac{f(x)}{f(x+a)}\right)^{\prime}=(\log f(x))^{\prime}-(\log f(x+a))^{\prime}\geq 0.

Solog⁡(f​(x)/f​(x+a))\log(f(x)/f(x+a))is non-decreasing, which implies thatf​(x)/f​(x+a)f(x)/f(x+a)is also non-decreasing. The converse also follows similarly.
∎

Clearly the Gaussian densityϕ​(x)\phi(x)is log-concave. We also have the following.

## Proposition 6.10.

BothΦ\PhiandΦ¯\overline{\Phi}are log-concave.

## Proof.

It is an interesting exercise to show directly that(log⁡Φ)′(\log\Phi)^{\prime}and(log⁡Φ¯)′(\log\overline{\Phi})^{\prime}are non-increasing
(this requires the useful factϕ′​(x)=−x​ϕ​(x)\phi^{\prime}(x)=-x\phi(x)about the Gaussian density).

A more slick way is using the Prekopa-Leindler inequality. In particular, the log-concavity ofΦ¯\overline{\Phi}follows by applying Lemma3.17withA=[x,∞)A=[x,\infty)andB=[y,∞)B=[y,\infty)and using the definition (6.11).
∎

Lemma6.8now follows by writingf​(d)f(d)in (6.10) asf​(d)=(1−Φ¯​(d+r)Φ¯​(d))​(Φ¯​(d)Φ¯​(d+p))f(d)=\left(1-\frac{\overline{\Phi}(d+r)}{\overline{\Phi}(d)}\right)\left(\frac{\overline{\Phi}(d)}{\overline{\Phi}(d+p)}\right)

## 6.5Ehrhard’s inequality

We now describe perhaps the most important Gaussian isoperimetric-type inequality.

LetΦ\Phidenote the Gaussian distribution functionΦ​(x)=γ1​((−∞,x])=(2​π)−1/2​∫−∞xexp⁡(−t2/2)​𝑑t.\Phi(x)=\gamma^{1}((-\infty,x])=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\exp(-t^{2}/2)dt.

The functionΦ−1:[0,1]→ℝ∪{−∞,∞}\Phi^{-1}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}will play an important role.

## Theorem 6.13.

[Ehrhard-Borell inequality][47,36]For any measurable setsA,B⊂ℝnA,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}and0≤λ≤10\leq\lambda\leq 1,Φ−1​(γn​(λ​A+(1−λ)​B))≥λ​Φ−1​(γn​(A))+(1−λ)​Φ−1​(γn​(B)).\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{n}(\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B))\geq\lambda\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{n}(A))+(1-\lambda)\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{n}(B)).(6.13)

More pictorially, imagine associating the setAAwith the intervalIA=(−∞,xA)I_{A}=(-\infty,x_{A})with the “same” Gaussian measure, i.e.,γn​(A)=γ1​((−∞,xA])\gamma^{n}(A)=\gamma^{1}((-\infty,x_{A}]).
Notice thatΦ−1​(γn​(A))=xA\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{n}(A))=x_{A}.
Similarly, consider the intervalIB=(−∞,xB)I_{B}=(-\infty,x_{B})for setBB. Theorem6.13says that pointxλx_{\lambda}corresponding to the setλ​A+(1−λ)​xB\lambda A+(1-\lambda)x_{B}satisfiesxλ≥λ​xA+(1−λ)​xBx_{\lambda}\geq\lambda x_{A}+(1-\lambda)x_{B}.

Let us also contrast this with the Gaussian Brunn-Minkowski inequality in Lemma3.17, which states thatlog⁡γn​(λ​A+(1−λ)​B)≥λ​log⁡γn​(A)+(1−λ)​log⁡γn​(B).\log\gamma^{n}(\lambda A+(1-\lambda)B)\geq\lambda\log\gamma^{n}(A)+(1-\lambda)\log\gamma^{n}(B).(6.14)

Then (6.13) says thatΦ−1∘γn\Phi^{-1}\circ\gamma^{n}is concave, while (6.14) shows thatlog∘γn\log\circ\,\gamma^{n}is concave.
It turns out that Ehrhard’s inequality is stronger than Brunn-Minkowski for the Gaussian measure.

## Gaussian symmetrization.

We discuss Gaussian symmetrization more generally. LetWWbe a subspace ofℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}of dimensionn−kn-k, and let us decomposeℝn=W⟂⊕W\mathbb{R}^{n}=W^{\perp}\oplus Wand view the pointx∈ℝnx\in\mathbb{R}^{n}asx=(y,w)x=(y,w)wherew=ΠW​(x)w=\Pi_{W}(x)andy=ΠW⟂​(x)y=\Pi_{W^{\perp}}(x).

LetA⊂ℝnA\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}be a measurable set.
Fory∈W⟂y\in{W}^{\perp}, consider the affine subspacey+Wy+Wand letAy=A∩(y+W)A_{y}=A\cap(y+W)be then−kn-kdimensional slice obtained by intersectingAAwith this affine subspace.
Consider the functionf:W⟂→ℝf:W^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}defined asf​(y)=Φ−1​(γn−k​(Ay))f(y)=\Phi^{-1}(\gamma^{n-k}(A_{y})).
The Gaussian symmetrization ofAAalongWWis thek+1k+1-dimensional bodyA′={(t,y):y∈W⟂,t≤f​(y)}.A^{\prime}=\{(t,y):y\in W^{\perp},t\leq f(y)\}.

In Sections6.2and6.3, we applied this for subspacesW=span​(e1)W=\text{span}(e_{1})andW=span​(e2,…,em)W=\text{span}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}).

Observe that by constructionγk+1​(A′)=γn​(A)\gamma^{k+1}(A^{\prime})=\gamma^{n}(A).
Moreover importantly, ifAAis convex then Theorem6.13implies that the functionf​(y)f(y)is concave and hence thatA′A^{\prime}is also convex.

## 6.6Bibliographic Notes

The proof here is due to Banaszczyk[10], but our exposition is more detailed.

Theorem6.13was proved by
Ehrhard[47]for the case whenAAandBBare convex, and the inequality for general case was proved by Borell[36].
Several other proofs are also known[124,95].
Interestingly, it follows from[47], that proving Theorem6.13for the case ofn=1n=1implies the result for generalnn.

Ehrhard’s inequality also implies various other inequalities such as the Gaussian Isoperimetric inequality, Bobkov’s inequality and Gross logarithmic Sobolev inequality. A nice survey is in[71].

## Chapter 7Hereditary Discrepancy and Factorization Norms

We have now seen several tools for proving upper bounds on the
discrepancy of set systems and matrices, some of which also give
efficient algorithms for computing a coloring achieving the
discrepancy bound. In this chapter we address the question of the
complexity of approximating discrepancy and hereditary discrepancy. In
addition to showing some hardness results, we will show that
hereditary discrepancy is approximated well by theγ2\gamma_{2}matrix factorization norm. Beyond giving an approximation
algorithm for hereditary discrepancy, this result has a number of
applications to discrepancy theory. It gives a powerful tool for
giving short proofs of upper and lower bounds on discrepancy, and for
proving various stability properties of discrepancy.

## 7.1Complexity of Computing Discrepancy

Perhaps the most basic computational question about discrepancy is
whetherdisc⁡(A)\operatorname{disc}(A)can be computed efficiently given a matrixAAas
input. An easier question is whether the promise thatdisc⁡(A)\operatorname{disc}(A)is
small,e.g., at most a universal constant, or even0, is enough to
efficiently find a coloring whose discrepancy is smaller than
Spencer’s worst-case boundO​(n​log⁡(2​m/n))O(\sqrt{n\log(2m/n)}). Unfortunately, the
answer to both these questions is negative, as long as𝖯≠𝖭𝖯\mathsf{P}\neq\mathsf{NP}, as shown in the following theorem.

## Theorem 7.1.

For a set system(SS,[n])(\SS,[n])with|SS|=O​(n)|\SS|=O(n)sets, it is𝖭𝖯\mathsf{NP}-hard to distinguish between the cases: (i)disc⁡(SS)=0\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=0,
or (ii)disc⁡(SS)=Ω​(n)\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=\Omega(\sqrt{n}).

We will establish Theorem7.1by reduction from
the22-22Set Splitting problem, whose definition and hardness are given below.

## Theorem 7.2.

(Guruswami[55])
For a set system(𝒯,[n])(\mathcal{T},[n])withm=O​(n)m=O(n)sets, where|T|=4|T|=4for allT∈𝒯T\in\mathcal{T}, and eachj∈[n]j\in[n]appears in at most44sets, it is𝖭𝖯\mathsf{NP}-hard to distinguish between the cases:
- 1.

disc⁡(𝒯)=0\operatorname{disc}{(\mathcal{T})}=0.
- 2.

for allx∈{±1}nx\in\{\pm 1\}^{n},|{T∈𝒯:x​(T)≠0}|≥α​m|\{T\in\mathcal{T}:x(T)\not=0\}|\geq\alpha m, whereα≈1/22\alpha\approx 1/22.

Note that Theorem7.2already shows that it is𝖭𝖯\mathsf{NP}-hard to decide if a set system has discrepancy0, or
discrepancy at least22. By composing a22-22Set Splitting
instance, in an appropriate way, with a set system satisfying a robust
discrepancy lower bound ofΩ​(n)\Omega(\sqrt{n}), we can amplify this gap and prove Theorem7.1. The incidence matrix
of the set system used in this composition is given by the following lemma. In it, we
use the notion of a Hadamard matrix, i.e., anm×mm\times mmatrix with
entries in{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}such thatHT​H=m​IH^{T}H=mI. Such matrices are known
to exist, and can be efficiently constructed, for allmmthat are
powers of22(among other values ofmm).

## Lemma 7.3.

LetHHbe anm×mm\times mHadamard matrix such that
one of its rows is the all-ones vector. Then the matrixAAobtained by replacing each−1-1entry ofHHwith0satisfies‖A​x‖∞≥13​‖x‖2\|Ax\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{3}\|x\|_{2}for anyx∈ℝmx\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

## Proof.

Letx∈ℝmx\in\mathbb{R}^{m}be arbitrary. We first observe that, by
comparing theℓ∞\ell_{\infty}andℓ2\ell_{2}norms,‖H​x‖∞2≥1m​‖H​x‖22=1m​xT​HT​H​x=xT​x=‖x‖22.\displaystyle\|Hx\|_{\infty}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{m}\|Hx\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{m}x^{T}H^{T}Hx=x^{T}x=\|x\|_{2}^{2}.(7.1)

Notice thatA=12​(H+J)A=\frac{1}{2}(H+J)whereJJis them×mm\times mall-ones matrix. Suppose, without loss of generality, that the first
row ofHHis the all-ones vector, and, therefore, the first row ofAAis also the all-ones vector. Then‖A​x‖∞=12​‖H​x+J​x‖∞=12​maxi=1m⁡|(H​x)i+(A​x)1|.\|Ax\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\|Hx+Jx\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\max_{i=1}^{m}|(Hx)_{i}+(Ax)_{1}|.

By (7.1), we know that|(H​x)i∗|≥‖x‖2|(Hx)_{i^{*}}|\geq\|x\|_{2}for somei∗i^{*}. If|(A​x)1|≤13​‖x‖2|(Ax)_{1}|\leq\frac{1}{3}\|x\|_{2}, then, by the
triangle inequality,‖A​x‖∞≥12​(|(H​x)i∗|−|(A​x)1|)≥13​‖x‖2.\|Ax\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}(|(Hx)_{i^{*}}|-|(Ax)_{1}|)\geq\frac{1}{3}\|x\|_{2}.

Otherwise, if|(A​x)1|>13​‖x‖2|(Ax)_{1}|>\frac{1}{3}\|x\|_{2}, then‖A​x‖∞≥|(A​x)1|>13​‖x‖2.\|Ax\|_{\infty}\geq|(Ax)_{1}|>\frac{1}{3}\|x\|_{2}.∎

Notice that the requirement that the Hadamard matrixHHhas an all-ones
row is not restrictive. We can replace every entryH​(i,j)H(i,j)inHHbyH​(i,j)​H​(1,j)H(i,j)H(1,j), and still get a Hadamard matrix, but now all entries
in the first row are equal to11.

Theorem7.2and Lemma7.3allow for a
very simple hardness reduction that shows a statement analogous to
Theorem7.1, but for matrices with entries in{0,4}\{0,4\}, as opposed to set systems. The reduction simply takes them×nm\times nincidence matrixBBof the set system𝒯\mathcal{T}in
Theorem7.2, pads it with0’s if necessary to make sure
thatmmis a power of22, and outputs a matrixM:=A​BM:=ABfor
them×mm\times mmatrixAAdefined in Lemma7.3.

## Exercise 7.4.

Analyze the reduction described in the paragraph above.

In order to get a reduction that, instead, outputs a set
system, we need a simple decomposition lemma, stated next.

## Lemma 7.5.

Suppose that(𝒯,[n])(\mathcal{T},[n])is a set system such that each set in𝒯\mathcal{T}has at mostsselements, and each elementj∈[n]j\in[n]appears in at mostttsets in𝒯\mathcal{T}. Then𝒯\mathcal{T}can be partitioned
in polynomial time
intok≤s​t+1k\leq st+1disjoint set systems𝒯1,…,𝒯k\mathcal{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{T}_{k}, where
each𝒯i\mathcal{T}_{i}consists of disjoint subsets of[n][n].

## Proof.

We partition𝒯\mathcal{T}greedily, similarly to the greedy coloring
algorithm for bounded degree graphs. We list the sets in𝒯\mathcal{T}in some
order asT1,…,TmT_{1},\ldots,T_{m}, and, for eachii, starting
fromi=1i=1up toi=mi=m, we addTiT_{i}into𝒯ℓ\mathcal{T}_{\ell}, for the smallestℓ\ellfor which𝒯ℓ\mathcal{T}_{\ell}does not contain any set intersectingTiT_{i}. Since eachTiT_{i}can intersect at mosts​tstother sets in𝒯\mathcal{T}, there is always someℓ≤s​t+1\ell\leq st+1so thatTiT_{i}can
be added to𝒯ℓ\mathcal{T}_{\ell}.
∎

## Proof of Theorem7.1.

Let𝒯\mathcal{T}be the set system from Theorem7.2, and let
us partition it intok≤17k\leq 17set systems𝒯1,…,𝒯k\mathcal{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{T}_{k},

zeros. Let us takeHHto be anm×mm\times mHadamard matrix, and

sets,BℓB_{\ell}has at most a single11in each column, soA​BℓAB_{\ell}is anm×nm\times nmatrix with entries in{0,1}\{0,1\}. We

To finish the proof, we claim that the following two implications
hold for an absolute constantc>0c>0:disc⁡(𝒯)=0\displaystyle\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{T})=0⟹disc⁡(SS)=0;\displaystyle\implies\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=0;(7.2)minx∈{−1,+1}n⁡|{T∈𝒯:x​(T)≠0}|≥α​m\displaystyle\min_{x\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}}|\{T\in\mathcal{T}:x(T)\not=0\}|\geq\alpha m⟹disc⁡(SS)≥c​α​m.\displaystyle\implies\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\geq c\sqrt{\alpha m}.(7.3)

The theorem then follows from Theorem7.2.

We first prove (7.2). Sincedisc⁡(𝒯)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{T})is0,
there exists a coloringx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}for whichdisc⁡(𝒯,x)=0\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{T},x)=0. ThenBℓ​x=0B_{\ell}x=0for allℓ≤k\ell\leq k, since each
row of eachBℓB_{\ell}is the indicator vector of a set in𝒯\mathcal{T}. Therefore,disc⁡(SS,x)=maxℓ=1k⁡disc⁡(SSℓ,x)=maxℓ=1k⁡‖Mℓ​x‖∞=maxℓ=1k⁡‖A​Bℓ​x‖∞=0.\operatorname{disc}(\SS,x)=\max_{\ell=1}^{k}\operatorname{disc}(\SS_{\ell},x)=\max_{\ell=1}^{k}\|M_{\ell}x\|_{\infty}=\max_{\ell=1}^{k}\|AB_{\ell}x\|_{\infty}=0.

Finally, we prove (7.3). Take anyx∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}, and suppose that|{T∈𝒯:x​(T)≠0}|≥α​m|\{T\in\mathcal{T}:x(T)\not=0\}|\geq\alpha m. This means that for someℓ≤k\ell\leq k,|{T∈𝒯ℓ:x​(T)≠0}|≥α​m/k≥α​m/17.|\{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\ell}:x(T)\not=0\}|\geq\alpha m/k\geq\alpha m/17.

Therefore,Bℓ​xB_{\ell}xhas at leastα​m17\frac{\alpha m}{17}non-zero

Sincex∈{−1,+1}nx\in\{-1,+1\}^{n}was arbitrary, this completes the proof

and,

## 7.2Approximating Hereditary Discrepancy

In contrast with the strong hardness result for discrepancy given by
Theorem7.1, we will see in the rest of this
chapter that hereditary discrepancy can be approximated efficiently up
to multiplicative poly-logarithmic factors. This is one concrete
example of hereditary discrepancy being a “better behaved”
discrepancy notion. We will see other examples later on, also in this
chapter.

## 7.2.1Upper Bounds

Recall that the hereditary discrepancyherdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)of a matrixMMis the maximum discrepancydisc⁡(M​(∗,J))\operatorname{disc}(M(*,J))for any subsetJJof the columns ofMM. Since this is a maximum over an exponential
number of quantities, each of them intractable to approximate, it is
far from obvious that we can find useful tractable upper bounds onherdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M). In this subsection we show that this is, in fact,
possible.

Our starting point is the observation that ifffis a function onm×nm\times nmatrices which is monotonically non-increasing under
taking submatrices,i.e.,f​(M​(∗,J))≤f​(M)f(M(*,J))\leq f(M)for anyJ⊆[n]J\subseteq[n], and that also satisfiesdisc⁡(M)≤f​(M)\operatorname{disc}(M)\leq f(M), then we
automatically get the stronger conclusionherdisc⁡(M)≤f​(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\leq f(M). Indeed, for anyJ⊆[n]J\subseteq[n], we havedisc⁡(M​(∗,J))≤f​(M​(∗,J))≤f​(M).\operatorname{disc}(M(*,J))\leq f(M(*,J))\leq f(M).

We call such a functionffahereditary upper boundon discrepancy.

We use the notation‖M‖1→2\|M\|_{1\to 2}for the maximumℓ2\ell_{2}norm
of a column of the matrixMM, and‖M‖2→∞\|M\|_{2\to\infty}for the
maximumℓ2\ell_{2}norm of a row of the matrixMM.111More
generally, for anm×nm\times nmatrixMM, andp,q∈[1,∞]p,q\in[1,\infty],‖M‖p→q:=supx≠0‖M​x‖q‖x‖p\|M\|_{p\to q}:=\sup_{x\neq 0}\frac{\|Mx\|_{q}}{\|x\|_{p}}.

Notice first that bothc​log⁡(2​m)​‖M‖1→2c\sqrt{\log(2m)}\|M\|_{1\to 2}andc​log⁡(2​m)​‖M‖2→∞c\sqrt{\log(2m)}\|M\|_{2\to\infty}are hereditary upper bounds on
discrepancy (for some constantc>0c>0). The first follows from the Banaszczyk upper bound for
the Komlós problem (Theorem5.3), and the second
from Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound, applied to a uniformly
random coloring.
Both these upper bounds can be, unfortunately, very loose,e.g., consider
the all-ones matrix.

However, we already saw in Lemma5.5that these bounds can be interpolated in a useful way using
Banaszczyk’s theorem. Lemma5.5, in the formulation
in (5.1), shows that, for anym×nm\times nmatrixMM,disc⁡(M)≲γ2​(M)​log⁡(2​m),\operatorname{disc}(M)\lesssim\gamma_{2}(M)\sqrt{\log(2m)},whereγ2(M):=inf{∥U∥2→∞∥V∥1→2:UV=M}.\gamma_{2}(M):=\inf\{\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\|V\|_{1\to 2}:UV=M\}.(7.4)

The notationγ2\gamma_{2}comes from functional analysis,
where this function was first studied in connection with factoring
linear operators between Banach spaces through a Hilbert space.

The following corollary follows from the above and the observation
thatγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)is a hereditary upper bound on discrepancy.

## Corollary 7.6(of Lemma5.5).

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMM, we haveherdisc⁡(M)≲ln⁡(2​m)​γ2​(M).\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\lesssim\sqrt{\ln(2m)}\gamma_{2}(M).

## Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma5.5and the observation thatγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)is monotonically
non-increasing under taking submatrices. Indeed, for anyJ⊆[n]J\subseteq[n], and any factorizationM=U​VM=UV, we have thatM​(∗,J)=U​V​(∗,J)M(*,J)=UV(*,J), and‖V​(∗,J)‖1→2≤‖V‖1→2\|V(*,J)\|_{1\to 2}\leq\|V\|_{1\to 2}. Minimizing over all choices ofUUandVVgives usγ2​(M​(∗,J))≤γ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M(*,J))\leq\gamma_{2}(M). Thus, by
Lemma5.5, we havedisc⁡(M​(∗,J))≲ln⁡(2​m)​γ2​(M​(∗,J))≤ln⁡(2​m)​γ2​(M).\operatorname{disc}(M(*,J))\lesssim\sqrt{\ln(2m)}\gamma_{2}(M(*,J))\leq\sqrt{\ln(2m)}\gamma_{2}(M).

Taking the maximum on the left hand side over all choices ofJ⊆[n]J\subseteq[n]gives the
corollary.
∎

Note that the all-ones matrix is no longer a bad example for
Corollary7.6, since both the hereditary discrepancy
and theγ2\gamma_{2}norm of this matrix are equal to11.
