Give an example in which Corollary7.6is tight up
to constant factors. That is, show that for infinitely many values ofmm, there exists anm×nm\times nmatrixMMfor whichdisc⁡(M)≳ln⁡(2​m)​γ2​(M)\operatorname{disc}(M)\gtrsim\sqrt{\ln(2m)}\gamma_{2}(M).

We now show thatγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)also gives a nearly tight
lower bound on hereditary discrepancy (see Theorem7.16for the formal statement). This will be our goal in the rest of this section.

## 7.2.2Semidefinite Program Formulation

We start by reformulatingγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)as a semidefinite program (SDP). One important
consequence of this formulation is thatγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)is
efficiently computable, and, therefore, so is the upper bound we
proved. By semidefinite programming duality, this formulation also gives a dual formulation ofγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)as a maximization problem. This dual formulation will
be essential in giving a lower bound onherdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)in terms ofγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M).

Recall the definition ofγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)in
(7.4). Note first that we can assume that the
infimum is over factorizationsM=U​VM=UVfor which‖U‖2→∞=‖V‖1→2=γ2​(M)\|U\|_{2\to\infty}=\|V\|_{1\to 2}=\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(M)}. This is because, for anyc>0c>0, we can replaceUUbyc​UcUandVVby1c​V\frac{1}{c}Vto get another
factorization, and choosingc:=‖V‖1→2‖U‖2→∞c:=\sqrt{\frac{\|V\|_{1\to 2}}{\|U\|_{2\to\infty}}}makes
the two matrix norms equal.

Note also that, sinceγ2​(M)≤‖I‖2→∞​‖M‖1→2=‖M‖1→2\gamma_{2}(M)\leq\|I\|_{2\to\infty}\|M\|_{1\to 2}=\|M\|_{1\to 2}, we can
further assume that‖U‖2→∞\|U\|_{2\to\infty}and‖V‖1→2\|V\|_{1\to 2}are both
bounded by‖M‖1→21/2\|M\|_{1\to 2}^{1/2}. Then it follows by a standard
compactness argument that the infimum in the definition ofγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)is achieved.

Let us now consider some factorizationM=U​VM=UVof them×nm\times nmatrixMMinto am×km\times kmatrixUUand ak×nk\times nmatrixVV. The matrixX=(UVT)​(UTV)=(U​UTU​VVT​UTVT​V)=(U​UTMMTVT​V)X=\begin{pmatrix}U\\
V^{T}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U^{T}&V\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}UU^{T}&UV\\
V^{T}U^{T}&V^{T}V\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}UU^{T}&M\\
M^{T}&V^{T}V\end{pmatrix}

is positive semidefinite, since it equalsWT​WW^{T}WforW=(UTV)W=\begin{pmatrix}U^{T}&V\end{pmatrix}. Notice that the entryX​(i,i)X(i,i), for1≤i≤m1\leq i\leq m, equals‖U​(i,∗)‖22\|U(i,*)\|_{2}^{2}, and the entryX​(m+j,m+j)X(m+j,m+j)equals‖V​(∗,j)‖22\|V(*,j)\|_{2}^{2}for1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n.

Conversely, suppose thatXXis a semidefinite(m+n)×(m+n)(m+n)\times(m+n)matrix such that the submatrix
formed by the firstmmrows and lastnncolumns equalsMM. SinceXXis positive semidefinite, we can write it asWT​WW^{T}Wfor some(m+n)×(m+n)(m+n)\times(m+n)matrixWW. DefiningUUto be the transpose of
the submatrix ofWWformed by taking its firstmmcolumns, andVVto be the submatrix ofWWformed by its lastnncolumns, we get a
factorizationM=U​VM=UV. Moreover,maxi=1m⁡X​(i,i)=‖U‖2→∞2\max_{i=1}^{m}X(i,i)=\|U\|_{2\to\infty}^{2}, andmaxj=1n⁡X​(m+j,m+j)=‖V‖1→22\max_{j=1}^{n}X(m+j,m+j)=\|V\|_{1\to 2}^{2}. This equivalence directly gives the following lemma.

## Lemma 7.8.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMwe haveγ2​(M)=\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(M)=min⁡t\displaystyle\min t(7.5)s.t.X​(i,m+j)=X​(m+j,i)=M​(i,j)∀i∈[m],j∈[n]\displaystyle X(i,m+j)=X(m+j,i)=M(i,j)\ \ \forall i\in[m],j\in[n](7.6)X​(i,i)≤t∀i∈[m+n]\displaystyle X(i,i)\leq t\ \ \forall i\in[m+n](7.7)X⪰0\displaystyle X\succeq 0(7.8)

whereXXranges over symmetric(m+n)×(m+n)(m+n)\times(m+n)real matrices.

Semidefinite programs share two important properties with linear
programs: they are efficiently solvable, in the sense that an
approximately optimal solution can be found in polynomial time to any
degree of accuracy; they satisfy a duality theory, i.e., we can
derive a dual maximization semidefinite program to a given
minimization semidefinite program, so that the optimal values of the
two programs are equal.222These statements are imprecise: in
order to be efficiently solvable and satisfy strong duality,
semidefinite programs need to be sufficiently “nice”. This turns
out not to be an issue for our application.Then, using
Lemma7.8, we can show thatγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)is efficiently computable, and has a dual
maximization formulation. These facts, whose proofs we omit, are
captured in the following lemma.

## Lemma 7.9.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMM, and anyε>0\varepsilon>0, we can
compute a factorizationM=U​VM=UVso that‖U‖2→∞​‖V‖1→2≤(1+ε)​γ2​(M)\|U\|_{2\to\infty}\|V\|_{1\to 2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma_{2}(M)in time polynomial
inmm,nn, the maximum bitsize of any entry ofMM, and inlog⁡(1/ε)\log(1/\varepsilon).

Moreover,γ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)satisfies the dual formulaγ2​(M)=max⁡{‖P​M​Q‖tr:P,Q​diagonal,tr⁡(P2)=tr⁡(Q2)=1}.\gamma_{2}(M)=\max\{\|PMQ\|_{\operatorname{tr}}:P,Q\text{ diagonal },\operatorname{tr}(P^{2})=\operatorname{tr}(Q^{2})=1\}.(7.9)

Above,‖P​M​Q‖tr\|PMQ\|_{\operatorname{tr}}is the trace norm of the matrixP​M​QPMQ,
i.e., the sum of its singular values.

## 7.2.3Lower Bound

To lower bound the hereditary discrepancy byγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)up to logarithmic factors, we need a general tool for proving lower bounds on hereditary
discrepancy.
We next define an intermediate quantity called the determinant lower bounddetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M). We use the connection between rounding and hereditary discrepancy, discussed Chapter1, and a covering argument, to show thatdetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)is a lower bound on hereditary discrepancy. Finally, we relateγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)anddetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M).

## The Determinant Lower bound and Hereditary Discrepancy.

The determinant lower bound
of anm×nm\times nmatrixMMis defined asdetLB⁡(M):=maxk⁡maxB∈Sk​(M)⁡|det(B)|1/k,\operatorname{detLB}(M):=\max_{k}\max_{B\in S_{k}(M)}|\det(B)|^{1/k},(7.10)

whereSk​(M)S_{k}(M)is the set ofk×kk\times ksubmatrices ofMM.
We have the following lower bound onherdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M).

## Lemma 7.10.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMwe have the inequalityherdisc⁡(M)≥12​detLB⁡(M).\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\geq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{detLB}(M).

## Proof.

We prove a slightly weaker boundherdisc⁡(M)≥detLB⁡(M)/4\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\geq\operatorname{detLB}(M)/4,
and leave the tighter bound as an exercise.
We show that for anyk×kk\times ksquare matrixBB,herdisc⁡(B)≥(1/4)​|det(B)|1/k.\operatorname{herdisc}(B)\geq(1/4)|\det(B)|^{1/k}.The claimed bound then follows directly asherdisc⁡(M)≥maxk⁡maxB∈Sk​(M)⁡herdisc⁡(B)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\geq\max_{k}\max_{B\in S_{k}(M)}\operatorname{herdisc}(B).

It follows from the transference theorem (Theorem1.4) that∀y∈[0,1]k​∃z∈{0,1}k‖B​(y−z)‖∞≤herdisc⁡(B).\forall y\in[0,1]^{k}\ \exists z\in\{0,1\}^{k}\ \ \|B(y-z)\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(B).

LetK:={x∈ℝk:‖B​x‖∞≤herdisc⁡(B)}K:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\|Bx\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(B)\}. The equation above is equivalent to[0,1]k⊆⋃z∈{0,1}k(K+z).[0,1]^{k}\subseteq\bigcup_{z\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(K+z).Lettingvolk⁡(⋅)\operatorname{vol}^{k}(\cdot)be thekk-dimensional Lebesgue
measure, by its translation invariance we have1\displaystyle 1=volk⁡([0,1]k)≤∑z∈{0,1}kvolk⁡(K+z)=2k​volk⁡(K).\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}^{k}([0,1]^{k})\leq\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\operatorname{vol}^{k}(K+z)=2^{k}\operatorname{vol}^{k}(K).(7.11)

To turn this into a lower bound onherdisc⁡(B)\operatorname{herdisc}(B), we need to
relate it tovolk⁡(K)\operatorname{vol}^{k}(K). Let us assume thatBBis invertible:
otherwise, the lower boundherdisc⁡(B)≥14​|det(B)|1/k=0\operatorname{herdisc}(B)\geq\frac{1}{4}|\det(B)|^{1/k}=0is trivial. Then we can writeK\displaystyle K={x∈ℝk:‖B​x‖∞≤herdisc⁡(B)}\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\|Bx\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(B)\}={B−1​y:y∈ℝk,‖y‖∞≤herdisc⁡(B)}.\displaystyle=\{B^{-1}y:y\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|y\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(B)\}.

The right side is the image of the cube[−herdisc⁡(B),herdisc⁡(B)]k[-\operatorname{herdisc}(B),\operatorname{herdisc}(B)]^{k}under multiplication byB−1B^{-1}, and
has volume(2​herdisc⁡(B))k​|det(B−1)|(2\operatorname{herdisc}(B))^{k}|\det(B^{-1})|=2kherdisc(B)k/|det(B)|=2^{k}\operatorname{herdisc}(B)^{k}/|\det(B)|.

Together with (7.11),
we get that1≤2kvolk(K)=4kherdisc(B)k/|det(B)|.1\leq 2^{k}\operatorname{vol}^{k}(K)=4^{k}\operatorname{herdisc}(B)^{k}/|\det(B)|.

Re-arranging gives us the lower bound.
∎

## Remark 7.11.

The determinant lower bound can be far from tight whenmmis
large. For example, takeMMto be a2n×n2^{n}\times nmatrix whose
rows are all the vectors in{−1,+1}n\{-1,+1\}^{n}. Hadamard’s inequality shows thatdetLB⁡(M)≤n\operatorname{detLB}(M)\leq\sqrt{n}, but it is easy to see thatdisc⁡(M)=n\operatorname{disc}(M)=n. Intuitively, the reason for this gap is that the determinant
lower bound only considers square submatrices ofMM. The proof
above, however, can be modified slightly to establish a
stronger volumetric lower bound on hereditary discrepancy, as we
explain next.

Notice that the proof of the inequality (7.11) did
not use thatBBis a square matrix, and in fact works for any
submatrixBBofMM. Using the inequality for
submatrices that consist of a subset of the columns ofMM, we
get that, for anyS⊆[n]S\subseteq[n], the convex body{x∈ℝS:‖M​(∗,S)​x‖∞≤herdisc⁡(M)}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{S}:\|M(*,S)x\|_{\infty}\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\}has|S||S|-dimensional volume at least2−|S|2^{-|S|}, or, equivalently,{x∈ℝS:‖M​(∗,S)​x‖∞≤1}\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{S}:\|M(*,S)x\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}, has volume at
least(2​herdisc⁡(M))−|S|(2\operatorname{herdisc}(M))^{-|S|}. LettingK¯:={x∈ℝn:‖M​x‖∞≤1}\overline{K}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|Mx\|_{\infty}\leq 1\},
this means thatherdisc(M)≥12volLB(M):=minS⊆[n]volS(K¯∩ℝS)−1/|S|,\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\geq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{volLB}(M):=\min_{S\subseteq[n]}\operatorname{vol}^{S}(\overline{K}\cap\mathbb{R}^{S})^{-1/|S|},(7.12)

wherevolS⁡(⋅)\operatorname{vol}^{S}(\cdot)is the|S||S|-dimensional Lebesgue measure
taken insideℝS\mathbb{R}^{S}, and we used the observation thatK¯∩ℝS={x∈ℝS:‖M​(∗,S)​x‖∞≤1}.\overline{K}\cap\mathbb{R}^{S}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{S}:\|M(*,S)x\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}.This volumetric lower bound takes advantage of
“tall” matrices, and can be stronger than the determinant lower
bound. Returning to the2n×n2^{n}\times nmatrixMMabove, whose rows are
all vectors in{−1,+1}n\{-1,+1\}^{n}, we haveK¯={x∈ℝn:‖x‖1≤1}\overline{K}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|x\|_{1}\leq 1\}, i.e., thenn-dimensionalℓ1\ell_{1}ball, which has Lebesgue measure2n/n!2^{n}/n!. SovolLB⁡(M)=(n!)1/n/2≈n/2​e\operatorname{volLB}(M)=(n!)^{1/n}/2\approx n/2eby
Stirling’s approximation. Unlike the determinant lower bound, this
matches the discrepancy ofMMup to a constant factor.

## Exercise 7.12.

Prove the inequalityherdisc⁡(M)≥(1/2)​detLB⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\geq(1/2)\operatorname{detLB}(M)in
Lemma7.10. To do so, argue that
(7.11) can be strengthened tovolk⁡(K)≥1\operatorname{vol}^{k}(K)\geq 1.

## RelatingdetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)andγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M).

Our goal will be to show thatγ2​(M)≲log⁡(2​n)​detLB⁡(M)\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim\log(2n)\operatorname{detLB}(M).

Let us first see how to use
Lemma7.9to get an inequality in the other
direction. Let us take anyk×kk\times ksubmatrixM​(S,T)M(S,T)ofMMthat
achievesdetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M). DefineP​(i,i)=1kP(i,i)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}fori∈Si\in S,Q​(j,j)=1kQ(j,j)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}forj∈Tj\in T, and define all other entries ofPPandQQto be0. Then, by (7.9),γ2​(M)≥‖P​M​Q‖tr=1k​‖M​(S,T)‖tr,\gamma_{2}(M)\geq\|PMQ\|_{\operatorname{tr}}=\frac{1}{k}\|M(S,T)\|_{\operatorname{tr}},

and notice that1k​‖M​(S,T)‖tr\frac{1}{k}\|M(S,T)\|_{\operatorname{tr}}is the arithmetic mean of
the singular values ofM​(S,T)M(S,T). Since|det(M​(S,T))|1/k|\det(M(S,T))|^{1/k}is
the geometric mean of the singular values of the same matrix, the
AM-GM inequality gives us thatγ2​(M)≥detLB⁡(M)\gamma_{2}(M)\geq\operatorname{detLB}(M).

To reverse this inequality, we
then need an inequality that reverses the AM-GM inequality, in the
appropriate sense. The next lemma is sufficient for us for this
purpose.

## Lemma 7.13.

For any non-negative real numbersσ1≥…≥σr≥0\sigma_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\sigma_{r}\geq 0,maxk=1r⁡k​(σ1​⋯​σk)1/k≥maxk=1r⁡k​σk≥σ1+…+σr1+ln⁡r\max_{k=1}^{r}k(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k})^{1/k}\geq\max_{k=1}^{r}k\sigma_{k}\geq\frac{\sigma_{1}+\ldots+\sigma_{r}}{1+\ln r}

## Proof.

The first inequality is trivial. The second
follows as,∑k=1rσk=∑k=1rk​σk⋅1k\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{r}\sigma_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}k\sigma_{k}\cdot\frac{1}{k}≤(maxk=1r⁡k​σk)​(∑k=1r1k)≤(maxk=1r⁡k​σk)​(1+ln⁡r),\displaystyle\leq\left(\max_{k=1}^{r}k\sigma_{k}\right)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k}\right)\leq\left(\max_{k=1}^{r}k\sigma_{k}\right)(1+\ln r),

as∑k=1r1k≤1+ln⁡r\sum_{k=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k}\leq 1+\ln r.
∎

Let us also recall the Cauchy-Binet formula, which states that for any
twom×nm\times nmatricesA,BA,B, wheren≤mn\leq m, we havedet(AT​B)=∑S⊆[m]:|S|=ndet(A​(S,∗))​det(B​(S,∗)).\det(A^{T}B)=\sum_{S\subseteq[m]:|S|=n}\det(A(S,*))\det(B(S,*)).

For a short proof, see Section 3.2 in Tao’s
book[122]. Note that applying the Cauchy-Schwarz
inequality to the right hand side of the Cauchy-Binet formula, we getdet(AT​B)2\displaystyle\det(A^{T}B)^{2}≤det(AT​A)​det(BT​B).\displaystyle\leq\det(A^{T}A)\det(B^{T}B).(7.13)

We need one final lemma before we can relateγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)anddetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M). This lemma allows us to extract a submatrix ofMMto use as a certificate thatdetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)is large.

## Lemma 7.14.

Letn≤mn\leq mbe positive integers, and letDDbe anm×mm\times mdiagonal matrix with non-negative entries such thattr⁡(D)=1\operatorname{tr}(D)=1. Then for anym×nm\times nmatrixAA, there exists a setS⊆[m]S\subseteq[m]of cardinalitynnfor which|det(A​(S,∗))|≥n!​det(AT​D​A).|\det(A(S,*))|\geq\sqrt{n!\det(A^{T}DA)}.

## Proof.

By the Cauchy-Binet formula and Hölder’s inequality, we havedet(AT​D​A)\displaystyle\det(A^{T}DA)=∑S⊆[m]:|S|=ndet(A​(S,∗))2​(∏i∈SD​(i,i))\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[m]:|S|=n}\det(A(S,*))^{2}\left(\prod_{i\in S}D(i,i)\right)≤(maxS⊆[m]:|S|=n​det(A​(S,∗))2)​(∑S⊆[m]:|S|=n∏i∈SD​(i,i)).\displaystyle\leq\left(\max_{S\subseteq[m]:|S|=n}\det(A(S,*))^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{S\subseteq[m]:|S|=n}\prod_{i\in S}D(i,i)\right).

It remains to bound the sum on the right hand side. One can show that this sum is maximized when allD​(i,i)D(i,i)are equal,
in which case it equals(mn)​1mn<1n!{m\choose n}\frac{1}{m^{n}}<\frac{1}{n!}. For an elementary proof of a similar bound,
notice that, since allD​(i,i)D(i,i)are non-negative,1=(D​(1,1)+…+D​(m,m))n≥n!​∑S⊆[m]:|S|=n∏i∈SD​(i,i),1=(D(1,1)+\ldots+D(m,m))^{n}\geq n!\sum_{S\subseteq[m]:|S|=n}\prod_{i\in S}D(i,i),

because when we expand(D​(1,1)+…+D​(m,m))n(D(1,1)+\ldots+D(m,m))^{n}we get each
multilinear term(∏i∈SD​(i,i))\left(\prod_{i\in S}D(i,i)\right)n!n!times,
in addition to the other terms. ∎

We are, finally, ready to prove thatγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)anddetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)are equal up to a logarithmic factor.

## Lemma 7.15.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMof rankrr, we havedetLB⁡(M)≤γ2​(M)≲(1+log⁡r)​detLB⁡(M).\operatorname{detLB}(M)\leq\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim(1+\log r)\operatorname{detLB}(M).

## Proof.

As we argued above, the first inequality follows by considering thek×kk\times ksubmatrixM​(S,T)M(S,T)ofMMachieving|det(M​(S,T))|1/k=detLB⁡(M)|\det(M(S,T))|^{1/k}=\operatorname{detLB}(M), and settingPPandQQin
(7.9) so thatP​M​Q=1k​M​(S,T)PMQ=\frac{1}{k}M(S,T).
Here we prove the second inequality, again using
Lemma7.9, as well as
Lemmas7.13and7.14.

LetPPandQQachieve equality in (7.9), and
letσ1≥…≥σr≥0\sigma_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\sigma_{r}\geq 0be therrlargest singular
values ofP​M​QPMQ. SinceP​M​QPMQhas rank at mostrr, all
other singular values ofP​M​QPMQare0, andγ2​(M)=σ1+…+σr\gamma_{2}(M)=\sigma_{1}+\ldots+\sigma_{r}. Letk∈[r]k\in[r]be the integer for
which Lemma7.13guarantees thatk​(σ1​⋯​σk)1/k≥σ1+…+σr1+ln⁡r=γ2​(M)1+ln⁡r.k(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k})^{1/k}\geq\frac{\sigma_{1}+\ldots+\sigma_{r}}{1+\ln r}=\frac{\gamma_{2}(M)}{1+\ln r}.(7.14)

The goal in the rest of the proof is to show that there exists ak×kk\times ksubmatrixM​(S,T)M(S,T)ofMMfor which|det(M​(S,T))|1/k|\det(M(S,T))|^{1/k}is not
much smaller thank​(σ1​⋯​σk)1/kk(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k})^{1/k}.

Letv1,…,vkv_{1},\ldots,v_{k}be the right singular vectors ofP​M​QPMQassociated withσ1,…,σk\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}, and letVVbe a
matrix with columnsv1,…,vkv_{1},\ldots,v_{k}. Define them×km\times kmatrixA:=M​Q​VA:=MQV. The matrixP​APAthen has singular valuesσ1,…,σk\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}, or, equivalently,AT​P2​AA^{T}P^{2}Ahas
eigenvaluesσ12,…,σk2\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k}^{2}. Lemma7.14applied toAAandD:=P2D:=P^{2}now tells us that there exists a setS⊆[m]S\subseteq[m]of cardinalitykkso that|det(M​(S,∗)​Q​V)|\displaystyle|\det(M(S,*)QV)|=|det(A​(S,∗))|\displaystyle=|\det(A(S,*))|≥k!​det(AT​P2​A)=k!​σ1​⋯​σk.\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{k!\det(A^{T}P^{2}A)}=\sqrt{k!}\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k}.(7.15)

Next we would like to apply Lemma7.14again, in
order to replaceM​(S,∗)​Q​VM(S,*)QVwithM​(S,T)M(S,T)for some cardinalitykksetT⊆[n]T\subseteq[n]. We first need to deal with the matrixVV, however. To this end, apply (7.13) to the
matricesQ​M​(S,∗)TQM(S,*)^{T}andVV, to conclude thatdet(M​(S,∗)​Q2​M​(S,∗)T)\displaystyle\det(M(S,*)Q^{2}M(S,*)^{T})≥det(M​(S,∗)​Q​V)2det(VT​V)\displaystyle\geq\frac{\det(M(S,*)QV)^{2}}{\det(V^{T}V)}=det(M​(S,∗)​Q​V)2,\displaystyle=\det(M(S,*)QV)^{2},(7.16)

where we used thatVT​V=IV^{T}V=I.
Then
(7.15) and (7.16) together give usdet(M​(S,∗)​Q2​M​(S,∗)T)≥k!​σ1​⋯​σk.\sqrt{\det(M(S,*)Q^{2}M(S,*)^{T})}\geq\sqrt{k!}\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k}.

Finally, we apply Lemma7.14one more time, now
with then×kn\times kmatrixA:=M​(S,∗)TA:=M(S,*)^{T}andD:=Q2D:=Q^{2}, concluding that there exists a setT⊆[n]T\subseteq[n]of
cardinalitykkfor which|M​(S,T)|≥k!​det(M​(S,∗)​Q2​M​(S,∗)T)≥k!​σ1​⋯​σk.|M(S,T)|\geq\sqrt{k!\det(M(S,*)Q^{2}M(S,*)^{T})}\geq k!\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k}.

Together with (7.14), and ask!≥(k/e)kk!\geq(k/e)^{k}, we can conclude thatdetLB⁡(M)≥|det(M​(S,T))|1/k\displaystyle\operatorname{detLB}(M)\geq|\det(M(S,T))|^{1/k}≳k​(σ1​⋯​σk)1/k≥γ2​(M)1+ln⁡r.∎\displaystyle\gtrsim k(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{k})^{1/k}\geq\frac{\gamma_{2}(M)}{1+\ln r}.\qed

Lemma7.15gives an approximation algorithm for the
determinant lower bound: computingγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)gives a factorO​(log⁡r)O(\log r)approximation fordetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)for any rankrrmatrixMM. No better polynomial time computable approximation fordetLB⁡(M)\operatorname{detLB}(M)is known.

Combining Lemmas7.10and7.15, we get
the following lower bound on hereditary discrepancy.

## Theorem 7.16.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMof rankrr, we haveherdisc⁡(M)≳γ2​(M)1+log⁡r.\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\gtrsim\frac{\gamma_{2}(M)}{1+\log r}.

## 7.2.4The Approximation Theorem

We can now state our main result.

## Theorem 7.17.

For anym×nm\times nmatrixMMof rankrr, we haveγ2​(M)1+log⁡r≲herdisc⁡(M)≲ln⁡(2​m)​γ2​(M).\frac{\gamma_{2}(M)}{1+\log r}\lesssim\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\lesssim\sqrt{\ln(2m)}\gamma_{2}(M).

Theorem7.17follows immediately from
Corollary7.6, and Theorem7.16.

In Exercise7.7we showed that the upper bound in
Theorem7.17is tight up to constant factors. Soon
we will see a simple example of a matrix for which the lower bound is
tight.

## 7.3Some Properties

There are many natural questions one can ask about the behavior of
hereditary discrepancy under simple transformations. For example, we
can ask howherdisc⁡(SS∪SS′)\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS\cup\SS^{\prime})compares toherdisc⁡(SS)\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS)andherdisc⁡(SS′)\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS^{\prime})for two set systemsSS\SSandSS′\SS^{\prime}on the same
universe. As another example, we can ask howherdisc⁡(MT)\operatorname{herdisc}(M^{T})compares toherdisc⁡(M)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)for a matrixMM. None of these
questions has an obvious answer, but, nevertheless, we can give nearly tight
answers to them using Theorem7.17, and simple
properties of theγ2\gamma_{2}norm. Let us remark that it is essential
here to work with hereditary discrepancy - discrepancy itself does not
behave nicely with respect to most simple transformations. Two
examples of this are offered in the next exercises.

## Exercise 7.18.

Give an example of two set systemsSS\SSandSS′\SS^{\prime}, both on the ground
set[n][n]for an arbitrarily large integernn, so thatdisc⁡(SS)=disc⁡(SS′)=0\operatorname{disc}(\SS)=\operatorname{disc}(\SS^{\prime})=0butdisc⁡(SS∪SS′)≳n\operatorname{disc}(\SS\cup\SS^{\prime})\gtrsim n.

## Exercise 7.19.

Give an example of an×nn\times nmatrix with{0,1}\{0,1\}entries,
fornnarbitrarily large, so thatdisc⁡(M)=0\operatorname{disc}(M)=0butdisc⁡(MT)≳n\operatorname{disc}(M^{T})\gtrsim\sqrt{n}.

The next lemma summarizes the basic properties of theγ2\gamma_{2}norm
that are useful in reasoning about hereditary
discrepancy. Below, we use⊗\otimesfor the Kronecker product: given
two matricesAAandBB, of dimensions, respectively,m×nm\times nandp×qp\times q,A⊗BA\otimes Bis a(m​p)×(n​q)(mp)\times(nq)matrix whose
rows are indexed by[m]×[p][m]\times[p], whose columns are indexed by[n]×[q][n]\times[q], and whose entries are(A⊗B)​((i1,i2),(j1,j2))=A​(i1,j1)​B​(i2,j2)(A\otimes B)((i_{1},i_{2}),(j_{1},j_{2}))=A(i_{1},j_{1})B(i_{2},j_{2}). The key property of Kronecker products that we
repeatedly use is that(A⊗B)​(A′⊗B′)=(A​A′)⊗(B​B′)(A\otimes B)(A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime})=(AA^{\prime})\otimes(BB^{\prime})for any matricesA,A′,B,B′A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime}for which these matrix
products are well-defined.

## Lemma 7.20.

Theγ2\gamma_{2}function on matrices has the following properties:
- 1.

γ2​(M​(S,T))≤γ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M(S,T))\leq\gamma_{2}(M)for anym×nm\times nmatrixMMand any submatrixM​(S,T)M(S,T)of
it;
- 2.

duplicating rows and columns ofMMkeepsγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M)unchanged;
- 3.

γ2​(M)=γ2​(MT)\gamma_{2}(M)=\gamma_{2}(M^{T})for any matrixMM;
- 4.

γ2​(M)=0⟹M=0\gamma_{2}(M)=0\implies M=0;
- 5.

γ2​(c​M)=|c|​γ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(cM)=|c|\gamma_{2}(M)for any matrixMMand real valuecc;
- 6.

γ2​(M+M′)≤γ2​(M)+γ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M+M^{\prime})\leq\gamma_{2}(M)+\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime});
- 7.

for any two matricesM′M^{\prime}andM′′M^{\prime\prime}, each withnncolumns, and the matrixM=(M′M′′)M=\begin{pmatrix}M^{\prime}\\
M^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix},
we haveγ2​(M)≤γ2​(M′)2+γ2​(M′′)2\gamma_{2}(M)\leq\sqrt{\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime})^{2}+\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime\prime})^{2}};
- 8.

γ2​(M⊗M′)=γ2​(M)​γ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M\otimes M^{\prime})=\gamma_{2}(M)\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime})for any matricesMMandM′M^{\prime}.

## Proof.

Property1was noted previously. Next we
give the proofs of some of the remaining properties, and leave
the rest as exercises.

Property6can be proved in several ways,
including by directly giving a factorization ofM+M′M+M^{\prime}using
optimal factorizations ofMMandM′M^{\prime}. Instead, let us prove
it using the SDP characterization from
Lemma7.8. LetX,tX,tbe a solution to the
semidefinite program (7.5)–(7.8)
forMMthat achieves the optimal objective valueγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M), and letX′,t′X^{\prime},t^{\prime}be a solution forM′M^{\prime}that
achieves objective valueγ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime}). ThenX+X′,t+t′X+X^{\prime},t+t^{\prime}is a
feasible solution forM+M′M+M^{\prime}, and achieves objective valueγ2​(M)+γ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M)+\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime}), which is an upper boundγ2​(M+M′)\gamma_{2}(M+M^{\prime}).

While property7can also be shown using the SDP
characterization, we give a direct
factorization ofM=(M′M′′)M=\begin{pmatrix}M^{\prime}\\
M^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}instead. LetM′=U′​V′M^{\prime}=U^{\prime}V^{\prime}andM′′=U′′​V′′M^{\prime\prime}=U^{\prime\prime}V^{\prime\prime}be
factorizations achieving, respectively,γ2​(M′)=‖U′‖2→∞​‖V′‖1→2\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime})=\|U^{\prime}\|_{2\to\infty}\|V^{\prime}\|_{1\to 2}, andγ2​(M′′)=‖U′′‖2→∞​‖V′′‖1→2\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime\prime})=\|U^{\prime\prime}\|_{2\to\infty}\|V^{\prime\prime}\|_{1\to 2}.
Rescale, so that‖U′‖2→∞=‖U′′‖2→∞=1\|U^{\prime}\|_{2\to\infty}=\|U^{\prime\prime}\|_{2\to\infty}=1,‖V′‖1→2=γ2​(M′)\|V^{\prime}\|_{1\to 2}=\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime}), and‖V′′‖1→2=γ2​(M′′)\|V^{\prime\prime}\|_{1\to 2}=\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime\prime}).
Consider then the factorizationM=U​VM=UVwhereU=(U′00U′′);V=(V′V′′).U=\begin{pmatrix}U^{\prime}&0\\
0&U^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix};\ \ \ \ V=\begin{pmatrix}V^{\prime}\\
V^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

Then, clearly,‖U‖2→∞=1.\|U\|_{2\to\infty}=1.Moreover, as‖V​(∗,i)‖22=‖V′​(∗,i)‖22+‖V′′​(∗,i)‖22\|V(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}=\|V^{\prime}(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}+\|V^{\prime\prime}(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}for anyi∈[n]i\in[n], we have‖V‖1→22\displaystyle\|V\|_{1\to 2}^{2}=maxi=1n⁡(‖V′​(∗,i)‖22+‖V′′​(∗,i)‖22)\displaystyle=\max_{i=1}^{n}\left(\|V^{\prime}(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}+\|V^{\prime\prime}(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}\right)≤maxi=1n⁡‖V′​(∗,i)‖22+maxi=1n⁡‖V′′​(∗,i)‖22\displaystyle\leq\max_{i=1}^{n}\|V^{\prime}(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}+\max_{i=1}^{n}\|V^{\prime\prime}(*,i)\|_{2}^{2}=‖V′‖1→22+‖V′′‖1→22.\displaystyle=\|V^{\prime}\|_{1\to 2}^{2}+\|V^{\prime\prime}\|_{1\to 2}^{2}.

Thus,γ2​(M)2≤‖U‖2→∞2​‖V‖1→22≤γ2​(M′)2+γ2​(M′′)2,\gamma_{2}(M)^{2}\leq\|U\|^{2}_{2\to\infty}\|V\|^{2}_{1\to 2}\leq\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime})^{2}+\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime\prime})^{2},as claimed.

Finally, we prove property8.
To
showγ2​(M⊗M′)≤γ2​(M)​γ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M\otimes M^{\prime})\leq\gamma_{2}(M)\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime})we
can, once again, either use the SDP characterization, or give a
direct factorization. Using the SDP characterization and Lemma7.8letX,tX,tbe
a solution to the semidefinite program
(7.5)–(7.8) forMMachieving
valueγ2​(M)\gamma_{2}(M), and letX′,t′X^{\prime},t^{\prime}be
a solution forM′M^{\prime}achieving valueγ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime}). ThenX⊗X′,t​t′X\otimes X^{\prime},tt^{\prime}is a
feasible solution forM⊗M′M\otimes M^{\prime}achieving valueγ2​(M)​γ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M)\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime}). Here we use the fact that the
Kronecker product of two positive semidefinite matrices is positive
semidefinite.

To show the reverse inequalityγ2​(M⊗M′)≥γ2​(M)​γ2​(M′)\gamma_{2}(M\otimes M^{\prime})\geq\gamma_{2}(M)\gamma_{2}(M^{\prime}), we use
Lemma7.9. LetP,QP,Qachieve equality in
(7.9) forMM, letP′,Q′P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}achieve
equality in (7.9) forM′M^{\prime}. Then,γ2​(M⊗M′)\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(M\otimes M^{\prime})≥‖(P⊗P′)​(M⊗M′)​(Q⊗Q′)‖tr\displaystyle\geq\|(P\otimes P^{\prime})(M\otimes M^{\prime})(Q\otimes Q^{\prime})\|_{\operatorname{tr}}=‖(P​M​Q)⊗(P′​M​Q′)‖tr=‖P​M​Q‖tr​‖P′​M​Q′‖tr.\displaystyle=\|(PMQ)\otimes(P^{\prime}MQ^{\prime})\|_{\operatorname{tr}}=\|PMQ\|_{\operatorname{tr}}\|P^{\prime}MQ^{\prime}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}.

Above we used the fact that, for any matricesAAandBB,‖A⊗B‖tr=‖A‖tr​‖B‖tr\|A\otimes B\|_{\operatorname{tr}}=\|A\|_{\operatorname{tr}}\|B\|_{\operatorname{tr}}, which can be verified
easily using the singular value decomposition.
∎

## Exercise 7.21.

Prove the remaining properties3–5in Lemma7.20.

Note that properties4–6in
Lemma7.20show thatγ2\gamma_{2}is, indeed, a
norm on matrices.

Lemma7.20and Theorem7.17show that properties1–8carry
over from theγ2\gamma_{2}norm to hereditary discrepancy, up to
logarithmic factors. Properties1,4, and5hold trivially for
hereditary discrepancy even without the extra logarithmic factors, but
we know no direct proof for the others. The next theorem rephrases
some of these properties for set systems.

## Theorem 7.22.

The following bounds hold:
- 1.

For a set system(SS,𝒰)(\SS,\mathcal{U}), define the dual set systemSS∗\SS^{*}on the universeSS\SSby{Su:u∈𝒰}\{S_{u}:u\in\mathcal{U}\}whereSu={S∈SS:u∈S}S_{u}=\{S\in\SS:u\in S\}; thenherdisc(SS∗)≲log(2|𝒰|)3/2herdisc(SS),\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS^{*})\lesssim\log(2|\mathcal{U}|)^{3/2}\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS),
- 2.

For any set systemsSS1,…,SSk\SS_{1},\ldots,\SS_{k}defined on the same
universe𝒰\mathcal{U},herdisc(SS1∪⋯∪SSk)≲log(2m)3/2(∑i=1kherdisc(SSi)2)1/2,\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\SS_{k})\lesssim\log(2m)^{3/2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS_{i})^{2}\right)^{1/2},

wherem=∑i=1k|SSi|m=\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\SS_{i}|.
- 3.

for set systems(SS1,𝒰1),…,(SSk,𝒰k)(\SS_{1},\mathcal{U}_{1}),\ldots,(\SS_{k},\mathcal{U}_{k}),
letSS1×⋯×SSk\SS_{1}\times\cdots\times\SS_{k}consist of all product setsS1×⋯×SkS_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{k}withSi∈SSiS_{i}\in\SS_{i}; thenlog(2m)−k2−1≲herdisc⁡(SS1×⋯×SSk)herdisc⁡(SS1)​⋯​herdisc⁡(SSk)≲log(2m)k+12,\log(2m)^{-\frac{k}{2}-1}\lesssim\frac{\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS_{1}\times\cdots\times\SS_{k})}{\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS_{1})\cdots\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS_{k})}\lesssim\log(2m)^{k+\frac{1}{2}},

wherem=|SS1|​⋯​|SSk|m=|\SS_{1}|\cdots|\SS_{k}|.

## Proof.

Each of the bounds follows from Theorem7.17, and
respectively, properties3,7, and8of Lemma7.20.
∎

## 7.4Applications

In this section we outline several applications of
Theorem7.17to proving upper and lower bounds on
the discrepancy of set systems.

## 7.4.1Theγ2\gamma_{2}Norm of Intervals

We start with a set system that, at first sight appears to be quite
uninteresting: the set systemℐn\mathcal{I}_{n}of prefix intervals of[n][n], i.e., sets
of the type{1,…​i}\{1,\ldots i\}. The hereditary discrepancy ofℐn\mathcal{I}_{n}is11: we just need to alternate the signs the elements we color to
achieve this bound. It turns out however, that theγ2\gamma_{2}norm of
the incidence matrix ofℐn\mathcal{I}_{n}is on the order oflog⁡n\log n. This fact, which we prove below, shows that the lower bound in
Theorem7.16is tight up to constants. It is also
useful in the applications we develop below.

We useTnT_{n}for the incidence matrix ofℐn\mathcal{I}_{n}, which we
write as the lower triangular matrixTn​(i,j)={1i≥j0i<j.T_{n}(i,j)=\begin{cases}1&i\geq j\\
0&i<j\end{cases}.

## Lemma 7.23.

For the matrixTnT_{n}above,γ2​(Tn)=Θ​(log⁡n)\gamma_{2}(T_{n})=\Theta(\log n).

## Proof.

The upper bound follows from Theorem7.16, sinceγ2​(Tn)≲(1+log⁡n)​herdisc⁡(Tn)=1+log⁡n.\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\lesssim(1+\log n)\operatorname{herdisc}(T_{n})=1+\log n.

For the lower boundγ2​(Tn)≳1+log⁡n\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\gtrsim 1+\log n, we use
Lemma7.9. In particular, settingP=Q=1nP=Q=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}in (7.9), we see thatγ2​(Tn)≥1n​‖Tn‖tr.\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\geq\frac{1}{n}\|T_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}.To prove a lower bound on‖Tn‖tr\|T_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}, we need to estimate the
singular values ofTnT_{n}. This can be done directly by computing the
eigenvectors ofTnT​TnT_{n}^{T}T_{n}, which have a simple closed
form. Instead, we will consider a matrix that is easier to
diagonalize. Consider the matrixSn=2​Tn−JnS_{n}=2T_{n}-J_{n}, whereJnJ_{n}is
then×nn\times nall-ones matrix. In other words,SnS_{n}is the matrix
we get fromTnT_{n}by replacing all entries equal to0with−1-1. By the triangle inequality,γ2​(Tn)≥1n​‖Tn‖tr≥12​n​(‖Sn‖tr−‖Jn‖tr)=12​n​‖Sn‖tr−12,\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\geq\frac{1}{n}\|T_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}\geq\frac{1}{2n}(\|S_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}-\|J_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}})=\frac{1}{2n}\|S_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}-\frac{1}{2},(7.17)

sinceJnJ_{n}has only one nonzero singular value equal tonn. Notice
thatSnS_{n}is a normal matrix, i.e.,SnT​Sn=Sn​SnTS_{n}^{T}S_{n}=S_{n}S_{n}^{T}, and,
therefore, the singular values ofSnS_{n}are equal to the absolute
values of the eigenvalues ofSnS_{n}. To compute‖Sn‖tr\|S_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}, it
then suffices to compute its eigenvalues. We can diagonalizeSnS_{n}over the complex numbers, using a transformation similar to the
discrete Fourier transform. Letω∈ℂ\omega\in\mathbb{C}be a primitive2​n2n-th
root of unity, and, fork∈{0,…,n−1}k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}, define the
vectoruku_{k}byuk​(i)=ω(2​k+1)​(i−1)u_{k}(i)=\omega^{(2k+1)(i-1)}. What motivates
this choice is thatω(2​k+1)​n=−1\omega^{(2k+1)n}=-1. It is easy to
verify thatuku_{k}anduℓu_{\ell}are orthogonal (with the standard
inner product overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}) ifk≠ℓk\neq\ell. Moreover,
notice that(Sn​uk)​(i)\displaystyle(S_{n}u_{k})(i)=(1+…+ω(2​k+1)​(i−1))−(ω(2​k+1)​i+…+ω(2​k+1)​(n−1))\displaystyle=(1+\ldots+\omega^{(2k+1)(i-1)})-(\omega^{(2k+1)i}+\ldots+\omega^{(2k+1)(n-1)})=ω(2​k+1)​i−1ω2​k+1−1−ω(2​k+1)​n−ω(2​k+1)​iω2​k+1−1\displaystyle=\frac{\omega^{(2k+1)i}-1}{\omega^{2k+1}-1}-\frac{\omega^{(2k+1)n}-\omega^{(2k+1)i}}{\omega^{2k+1}-1}=2​ω(2​k+1)​iω2​k+1−1=2​ω2​k+1ω2​k+1−1​uk​(i).\displaystyle=\frac{2\omega^{(2k+1)i}}{\omega^{2k+1}-1}=\frac{2\omega^{2k+1}}{\omega^{2k+1}-1}u_{k}(i).

Therefore,uku_{k}is an eigenvector ofSnS_{n}with eigenvalue2​ω2​k+1ω2​k+1−1\frac{2\omega^{2k+1}}{\omega^{2k+1}-1}. Thus, the
singular values ofSnS_{n}are2/|ω−1|,…,2/|ω2​n−1−1|2/|\omega-1|,\ldots,2/|\omega^{2n-1}-1|. Using elementary
trigonometry,|ω2​k+1−1|=2​sin⁡(π​(2​k+1)2​n),|\omega^{2k+1}-1|=2\sin\left(\frac{\pi(2k+1)}{2n}\right),and, therefore,‖Sn‖tr=∑k=0n−11sin⁡(π​(2​k+1)2​n)≥2​nπ​∑k=0n−112​k+1,\|S_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\sin\left(\frac{\pi(2k+1)}{2n}\right)}\geq\frac{2n}{\pi}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{2k+1},

assin⁡x≤x\sin x\leq xforx≥0x\geq 0.
Plugging back into (7.17), we get thatγ2​(Tn)≥1π​∑k=0n−112​k+1≳1+log⁡n.∎\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\geq\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{2k+1}\gtrsim 1+\log n.\qed

## Exercise 7.24.

Using canonical intervals (see Section2.4), give a factorization
showing thatγ2​(Tn)≲1+log⁡n\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\lesssim 1+\log n. This gives a more

## 7.4.2The Discrepancy of Boxes

Let us now use what we have proved to show nearly tight bounds on the
discrepancy of boxes indd-dimensions, resolving
Problem1up to a fixed power of log.

Recall thatℛd\mathcal{R}_{d}is the set of all axis aligned boxes inℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}, i.e., sets of
the typeRa,b={u∈ℝd:a≤u≤b}R_{a,b}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:a\leq u\leq b\}fora,b∈ℝda,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. Recall also thatdisc⁡(ℛd,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)is the largest discrepancy
achieved by restrictingℛd\mathcal{R}_{d}to a setPPofnnpoints. Theorem7.17, Lemma7.23,
and the properties of theγ2\gamma_{2}norm allow us to prove the
following bounds ondisc⁡(ℛd,n)\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n).

## Theorem 7.25.

For any integerd≥1d\geq 1,log(2n)d−1≲ddisc(ℛd,n)≲dlog(2n)d+12,\log(2n)^{d-1}\lesssim_{d}\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d+\frac{1}{2}},

where the≲d\lesssim_{d}means that the constant
depends ondd.

## Proof.

As we have done before with boxes, we will use the fact that we only
need to consider anchored boxes, restricted to the grid[n]d[n]^{d}. Recall that𝒜d\mathcal{A}_{d}is the set system of anchored boxes of the type[0,b1]×⋯×[0,bd][0,b_{1}]\times\cdots\times[0,b_{d}]forb∈ℝdb\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, and consider
the set systemSS:=𝒜d|[n]d\SS:=\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{[n]^{d}}of subsets of then×⋯×nn\times\cdots\times ngrid induced by anchored boxes. As we observed in Section2.4,2−d​disc⁡(ℛd,n)≤herdisc⁡(SS)≤disc⁡(ℛd,nd).2^{-d}\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\leq\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS)\leq\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n^{d}).(7.18)

Thus, it suffices to boundherdisc⁡(SS)\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS)from
both sides, and we do so by computing theγ2\gamma_{2}norm of the
incidence matrix ofSS\SS.

Let thenMMbe the incidence matrix ofSS\SS. The main
observation is thatSS=ℐn×d\SS=\mathcal{I}_{n}^{\times d}, i.e., the product ofℐn\mathcal{I}_{n}with itselfddtimes. Therefore,M=Tn⊗dM=T_{n}^{\otimes d}, i.e., the Kronecker
product ofTnT_{n}with itselfddtimes. Lemma7.20then shows thatγ2​(M)=γ2​(Tn⊗d)=γ2​(Tn)d,\gamma_{2}(M)=\gamma_{2}(T_{n}^{\otimes d})=\gamma_{2}(T_{n})^{d},so, by Lemma7.23,log(2n)d≲dγ2(M)≲dlog(2n)d.\log(2n)^{d}\lesssim_{d}\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d}.(7.19)

Theorem7.17now implieslog(2n)d−1≲dherdisc(SS)≲dlog(2n)d+12\log(2n)^{d-1}\lesssim_{d}\operatorname{herdisc}(\SS)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d+\frac{1}{2}}

since|SS|≤nd|\SS|\leq n^{d}. This implies the theorem via
(7.18).
∎

Below we will see that the upper bound in
Theorem7.25can be improved further. The lower bound
is the best one known for Tusnády’s problem.

## 7.4.3Other Applications

Let us note a couple of other discrepancy upper bounds that can be
proved using Lemma7.20,
Theorem7.22, and
Theorem7.17. Neither of the two bounds below is
optimal, but they are both non-trivial and follow easily from the
results proved thus far.

## Permutation families.

The set system induced by a single
permutation of[n][n]clearly has hereditary discrepancy11, as we can list the
elements of[n][n](or a subset of it) in the order given by the permutation, and alternate
the signs of the coloring. Then, by property2. of
Theorem7.22, we have that, for a set systemSS\SSinduced bykkpermutations of[n][n],disc(SS)≲klog(kn)3/2\operatorname{disc}(\SS)\lesssim\sqrt{k}\log(kn)^{3/2}.

## Arithmetic Progressions.

Consider the system𝒜​𝒫\mathcal{AP}consisting of all arithmetic progressions restricted to[n][n]. A
classical result of Roth shows thatdisc⁡(𝒜​𝒫)≳n1/4\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{AP})\gtrsim n^{1/4}. We
can show an upper bound that matches Roth’s lower bound up to
logarithmic factors. Let𝒜​𝒫d\mathcal{AP}_{d}be the subset of𝒜​𝒫\mathcal{AP}consisting of
arithmetic progressions of differencedd, i.e., sets of the forma+d,a+2​d,…,a+ℓ​da+d,a+2d,\ldots,a+\ell dfor some integersaaandℓ\ell. Let𝒜​𝒫≥s:=⋃d≥s𝒜​𝒫d\mathcal{AP}_{\geq s}:=\bigcup_{d\geq s}\mathcal{AP}_{d}for a parametersswe will
choose soon. Since
every arithmetic progression in𝒜​𝒫≥s\mathcal{AP}_{\geq s}has difference at leastss, it can have size at mostns\frac{n}{s}, so, for the incidence
matrixM≥sM_{\geq s}of𝒜​𝒫≥s\mathcal{AP}_{\geq s},γ2​(M≥s)≤ns\gamma_{2}(M_{\geq s})\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{s}}. On the other hand, the hereditary discrepancy of𝒜​𝒫d\mathcal{AP}_{d}for anyddis at most11, since we can separately color each
congruence classmodd\mod d, and alternate signs of the coloring within
a class. Therefore, by Theorem7.17,γ2​(Md)≲log⁡(2​n)\gamma_{2}(M_{d})\lesssim\log(2n)for the incidence matrixMdM_{d}of𝒜​𝒫d\mathcal{AP}_{d}. Using property7of
Lemma7.20we have, for the incidence matrixMMof𝒜​𝒫\mathcal{AP},γ2​(M)\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(M)≤(γ2​(M≥s)2+γ2​(Ms−1)2+⋯+γ2​(M1)2)1/2\displaystyle\leq(\gamma_{2}(M_{\geq s})^{2}+\gamma_{2}(M_{s-1})^{2}+\cdots+\gamma_{2}(M_{1})^{2})^{1/2}≲(ns+slog(2n)2)1/2.\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{s}+s\log(2n)^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Optimizing overss, we haveγ2​(M)≲n1/4​log⁡(2​n)\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim n^{1/4}\sqrt{\log(2n)}, and Theorem7.17gives usdisc⁡(𝒜​𝒫)≲n1/4​log⁡(2​n).\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{AP})\lesssim n^{1/4}\log(2n).

## Exercise 7.26.

Use canonical intervals to show thatγ2​(M)≲n1/4\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim n^{1/4}for the incidence matrixMMof the set system of
arithmetic progressions restricted to[n][n].

## 7.5Prefix Discrepancy

Recall that in Lemma5.19we showed that, for any
matrixMMwith columnsu1,…,un∈ℝmu_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, the prefix discrepancypdisc⁡(M)≲γ2​(M)​log⁡(2​m​n).\operatorname{pdisc}(M)\lesssim\gamma_{2}(M)\sqrt{\log(2mn)}.(7.20)

Theorem7.17and this inequality show that prefix
discrepancy is never much bigger than the hereditary discrepancy,
since, for anym×nm\times nmatrixMM,pdisc(M)≲log(mn)1.5herdisc(M).\operatorname{pdisc}(M)\lesssim\log(mn)^{1.5}\operatorname{herdisc}(M).(7.21)

In addition to this connection between prefix and hereditary
discrepancy, the upper bound (7.20) sometimes allows us to
prove tighter discrepancy upper bounds than
Theorem7.17. We give two such examples below, the
first giving the best known upper bound for Tusnády’s problem.

Axis-aligned boxes.As we will see in the next theorem, the
bound (7.21) allows us to improve the upper
bound in Theorem7.25by effectively getting one
dimension “for free”.

## Theorem 7.27.

For any integerd≥1d\geq 1,disc(ℛd,n)≲dlog(2n)d−12.\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{R}_{d},n)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d-\frac{1}{2}}.

## Proof.

As in Theorem7.25, we only consider anchored
boxes, restricted to sets in[n]d[n]^{d}. Equation
(7.21) allows us to restrict the sets we
consider even more. LetP⊆[n]dP\subseteq[n]^{d}be somenn-point set,
and letSS\SSbe the set system of all subsets ofPPof the formR∩PR\cap PwhereR=[0,b​(1)]×⋯×[0,b​(d−1)]×ℝ,R=[0,b(1)]\times\cdots\times[0,b({d-1})]\times\mathbb{R},forb∈ℝd−1b\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}. That is,SS\SSconsists of sets
induced by anchored boxes that extend to infinity in the last
coordinate. Equivalently, letπd−1\pi_{d-1}be the orthogonal
projection onto the firstd−1d-1coordinates ofℝd\mathbb{R}^{d}; thenSS\SSis
the set system consisting of all sets of the type{p:πd−1​(p)∈R}\{p:\pi_{d-1}(p)\in R\}whereR∈𝒜d−1R\in\mathcal{A}_{d-1}is a(d−1)(d-1)-dimensional anchored
box. SinceSS\SSis isomorphic to𝒜d−1|πd−1​(P)\mathcal{A}_{d-1}|_{\pi_{d-1}(P)}, equation (7.19) in the proof
of Theorem7.25gives us that the incidence matrixMMofSS\SSsatisfiesγ2(M)≲dlog(2n)d−1\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d-1}.

Let us orderPPasp1,…,pnp_{1},\ldots,p_{n}so thatp1​(d)≤…≤pn​(d)p_{1}(d)\leq\ldots\leq p_{n}(d), and also order the columns of the incidence matrixMMofSS\SSso that theii-th column is the indicator vector of sets containingpip_{i}.
The key observation we make is that, for any anchored boxR∈𝒜dR\in\mathcal{A}_{d},R∩P=S∩{p1,…,pi}R\cap P=S\cap\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{i}\}for someS∈SSS\in\SSandi∈[n]i\in[n]. In particular, we can takepip_{i}to be the
point with the largestpi​(d)p_{i}(d)coordinate contained inRR, and then
takeS={p∈P:πd−1​(p)∈πd−1​(R)}S=\{p\in P:\pi_{d-1}(p)\in\pi_{d-1}(R)\}. Therefore, for any
coloringx:P→{−1,+1}x:P\to\{-1,+1\},|x​(R∩P)||x(R\cap P)|is bounded bypdisc⁡(M,x)\operatorname{pdisc}(M,x). The theorem now follows from
(7.20) sincedisc(𝒜d|P)≤pdisc(M)≲dlog⁡(2​n)γ2(M)≲dlog(2n)d−12,\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P})\leq\operatorname{pdisc}(M)\lesssim_{d}\sqrt{\log(2n)}\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d-\frac{1}{2}},

where we used the fact that𝒜d|P\mathcal{A}_{d}|_{P}consists of at mostndn^{d}distinct sets, as well as the estimateγ2(M)≲dlog(2n)d−1\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim_{d}\log(2n)^{d-1}above.
∎

## Arithmetic Progressions.

Recall that𝒜​𝒫\mathcal{AP}is the
set system of arithmetic progressions restricted to[n][n]. We can use
equation (7.21) to improve our upper bound on
its discrepancy todisc⁡(𝒜​𝒫)≲n1/4​log⁡n\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{AP})\lesssim n^{1/4}\sqrt{\log n}. To see this, let us defineSS\SSto be the set ofmaximalarithmetic progressions,i.e., progressions inside[n][n]that cannot be extended further to the left or to the right. We claim
that the incidence matrixMMofSS\SSsatisfiesγ2​(M)≲n1/4\gamma_{2}(M)\lesssim n^{1/4}. To see this, notice thatM=(M≥nM<n)M=\begin{pmatrix}M_{\geq\sqrt{n}}\\
M_{<\sqrt{n}}\end{pmatrix}, whereM≥nM_{\geq\sqrt{n}}andM<nM_{<\sqrt{n}}are the incidence matrix of maximal
arithmetic progressions of difference at leastn\sqrt{n}, and at mostn\sqrt{n}respectively.

Recall that each arithmetic progression in[n][n]of differenceddhas
cardinality at mostn/dn/d, and all maximal arithmetic
progressions of the same difference are disjoint. Thus, it follows thatγ2​(M≥n)≤n1/4\gamma_{2}(M_{\geq\sqrt{n}})\leq n^{1/4}as each of its rows has at
mostn\sqrt{n}ones, andγ2​(M<n)≤n1/4\gamma_{2}(M_{<\sqrt{n}})\leq n^{1/4}as each of its columns has at mostn\sqrt{n}ones, one for each
possible difference. So property7of
Lemma7.20gives us thatγ2​(M)≤2​n1/4\gamma_{2}(M)\leq\sqrt{2}n^{1/4}. Now equation
(7.20) gives us thatpdisc⁡(M)≲n1/4​log⁡n,\operatorname{pdisc}(M)\lesssim n^{1/4}\sqrt{\log n},where the colums ofMMare ordered so that theii-th column is
the indicator of the sets inSS\SScontainingii. This means that
there exists a coloringx:[n]→{−1,+1}x:[n]\to\{-1,+1\}such that|x​(S∩[j])|≲n1/4​log⁡n|x(S\cap[j])|\lesssim n^{1/4}\sqrt{\log n}for eachj∈[n]j\in[n]andS∈SSS\in\SS. Since any arithmetic progression inside[n][n]can be written
as(S∩[k])∖(S∩[j])(S\cap[k])\setminus(S\cap[j]), we also havedisc⁡(𝒜​𝒫,x)≲n1/4​log⁡n\operatorname{disc}(\mathcal{AP},x)\lesssim n^{1/4}\sqrt{\log n}.

## 7.6Bibliographic Notes

The computational complexity of discrepancy was first investigated by
Charikar, Newman and Nikolov, who proved
Theorem7.1, and analogous results for set systems
of bounded degree, and for set systems of bounded VC
dimension[38]. It was shown by Austrin, Guruswami, and
Håstad that hereditary discrepancy is𝖭𝖯\mathsf{NP}-hard to
approximate within factor2−ε2-\varepsilonfor anyε>0\varepsilon>0[7].

Theγ2\gamma_{2}norm was first introduced in Banach space theory as a
way to measure how well a bounded linear operatoru:X→Yu:X\to Ybetween
Banach spacesXXandYYfactors through a Hilbert space. In
particular,γ2​(u)\gamma_{2}(u)is the infimum of the product of operator
norms‖v‖​‖w‖\|v\|\|w\|over all operatorsv:X→Hv:X\to Handw:H→Yw:H\to Ysuch
thatw​v=uwv=uandHHis some Hilbert space. Analogously, if we
replace the Hilbert space with anLpL_{p}space over some measure space(Ω,μ)(\Omega,\mu), we get theγp\gamma_{p}norm. Theγ2\gamma_{2}norm we
define here for matrices corresponds to the special case whenX=ℓ1nX=\ell_{1}^{n}andY=ℓ∞mY=\ell_{\infty}^{m}.
Factorization of linear operators throughLpL_{p}spaces was studied systematically by Kwapien[68],
but has its origins already in the work of
Grothendieck[54]. In particular, the dual formulation
(7.9) can be derived from the results of
Grothendieck (up to a factor of 2) and Kwapien; see also the appendix
of Pisier’s survey on Grothendieck’s inequality[102]. The book by
Tomczak-Jaegermann surveys the basic facts and many
applications of theγp\gamma_{p}norms in Banach space theory[123].

The connection between hereditary discrepancy and theγ2\gamma_{2}norm
is due to Matoušek, Nikolov, and Talwar, who also proved
Theorems7.17and7.25[91]. The
semidefinite formulation in Lemma7.8, and the
computability and duality results in Lemma7.9are
due to Lee, Shraibman, and Špalek[77].

Theγ2\gamma_{2}norm has also found other applications,e.g., to
communication complexity and learning
theory[80](see also the
book[76]), and differential privacy[99]. It is also
implicit in the work of Larsen on lower bounds for oblivious range
searching data structure[69], who also discovered the discrepancy upper
bounds stated in Lemma5.5and
Corollary7.6.

The determinant lower bound in Lemma7.10was first
shown by Lovász, Spencer, and Vesztergombi[82]. They also asked
if hereditary discrepancy can be bounded from above by a function of
the determinant lower bound. This was disproved by Hoffman, and his
counterexample can be found in Chapter 4 of Matoušek’s
book[85]. Nevertheless, it is possible to
show thatherdisc⁡(M)≲detLB⁡(M)​log⁡(2​m)​log⁡(2​n)\operatorname{herdisc}(M)\lesssim\operatorname{detLB}(M)\sqrt{\log(2m)\log(2n)}. The first result of this type
was proved by Matoušek[90], and his (loose)
bound was strengthened by Jiang and
Reis[63]. Li and Nikolov showed this bound is
tight[79].
The volume
lower bound in Remark7.11was observed by
Banaszczyk[9]. It was further investigated by Dadush, Nikolov,
Talwar, and Tomczak-Jaegermann, who showed it matches the hereditary
discrepancy up to logarithmic factors for any finite dimensional norm,
and matches a hereditary notion of partial coloring discrepancy up to
constant factors[45].

The lower bound onγ2​(Tn)\gamma_{2}(T_{n})in Lemma7.23is due to Mathias[84]. He also showed thatγ2​(Sn)=1n​‖Sn‖tr\gamma_{2}(S_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\|S_{n}\|_{\operatorname{tr}}, and, therefore,γ2​(Tn)≤12​n​‖Sn‖+12\gamma_{2}(T_{n})\leq\frac{1}{2n}\|S_{n}\|+\frac{1}{2}. Together with (7.17), this gives us the value ofγ2​(Tn)\gamma_{2}(T_{n})up an additive approximation of11. Determining the exact value ofγ2​(Tn)\gamma_{2}(T_{n})as a function ofnn, and giving an optimal factorization remain intriguing open problems, with applications in differential privacy[65].

The improved upper bound on the discrepancy of boxes in
Theorem7.27is due to
Nikolov[98]. Essentially the same proof, together with a
decomposition theorem of Matoušek[87], gives similar upper bounds for
more general set systems induced by polytopes with prescribed facets.
Moreover, the lower bound in Theorem7.25also
extends to these more general set systems, under some technical conditions. These
results are also due to Nikolov[98].

The discrepancy of arithmetic progressions is bounded byO​(n1/4)O(n^{1/4}),
and this was shown by Matoušek and Spencer using the partial
coloring method[88]: see the notes to
Chapter3for more on the discrepancy of arithmetic
progressions.

## Chapter 8Algorithmic Banaszczyk’s bound for Komlós

LetMMbe an instance of the Komlós problem (Problem2), i.e., a matrix all of whose columns haveℓ2\ell_{2}norm at most11.
Recall that applying Banaszczyk’s Theorem5.1withK=c​[−1,1]mK=c[-1,1]^{m}withc≈log⁡2​mc\approx\sqrt{\log 2m}so thatγm​(K)≥1/2\gamma^{m}(K)\geq 1/2, directly implies thatdisc(M)≲log⁡2​m)\operatorname{disc}(M)\lesssim\sqrt{\log 2m}). However, the proof of Theorem5.1in Chapter6does not give an efficient algorithm to find a coloring satisfying this bound.

We now describe an efficient algorithm to compute such a coloring.
This algorithm is based on using a discrete Brownian motion random walk process, similar to those in Chapter3, but using a stronger semidefinite program to guide the random walk.
Later, in Chapter9, we will see yet another algorithm called the Gram-Schmidt Walk, based on a different approach, that in fact works for general convex bodiesKK, and allows us to make Theorem5.1fully algorithmic.

## 8.1Preliminaries

Recall that in the Komlós problem, we are given anm×nm\times nmatrixMM, where each columnj∈[n]j\in[n]has squaredℓ2\ell_{2}norm∑i=1mM​(i,j)2≤1\sum_{i=1}^{m}M(i,j)^{2}\leq 1. In other words, we have‖M‖1→2≤1\|M\|_{1\to 2}\leq 1.
By Remark5.4, we can assume thatn≤m≤n2n\leq m\leq n^{2}, so thatlog⁡n≈log⁡m\log n\approx\log m.
As usual, we useiiandjjto index the rows and columns, and useviv_{i}to denote theii-th row ofMM.

Our goal is to show the following.

## Theorem 8.1.

There is a randomized polynomial time algorithm such that, for anym×nm\times nmatrixMMall of whose columns haveℓ2\ell_{2}norm at most11, the algorithm finds a coloringx∈{−1,1}nx\in\{-1,1\}^{n}with discrepancy‖M​x‖∞≲log⁡2​n\|Mx\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\log 2n}.

Before describing the algorithm, we give some intuition.

Given the matrixMM, we call a rowviv_{i},i∈[m]i\in[m],largeif‖vi‖22≥4\|v_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\geq 4, andsmallotherwise.
Intuitively, the difficulty of the problem is essentially due to large rows. In particular, if all the rows are small, a random coloring already works, as for any rowiiwe have that𝔼​[⟨vi,x⟩2]=‖vi‖22≲1\mathbb{E}[\langle v_{i},x\rangle^{2}]=\|v_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\lesssim 1, and standard concentration bounds directly give that|⟨vi,x⟩|≲log⁡2​n|\langle v_{i},x\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\log 2n}for all rowsviv_{i}with high probability.

This suggests that a reasonable algorithm should try to carefully cancel out the discrepancy for large rows while ensuring that the coloring looks random for small rows.

## A Natural Algorithm that Fails.

We start by describing a very natural candidate algorithm, and showing a bad example where it fails. This example will be instructive to understand, and it will also suggest a natural fix.

Consider the following algorithm. We start initially with the coloringx0=0nx_{0}=0^{n}. At timet=1,2,…t=1,2,\ldots, (as usual) we update the coloringxt−1x_{t-1}toxt=xt−1+Δ​xtx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\Delta x_{t}, whereΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}is some tiny random increment supported on coordinates that are not yet fixed at timett, and chosen as follows.

Letntn_{t}denote the number of active (not yet fixed) coordinates attt.
By averaging, there are at mostnt/4n_{t}/4large rows (restricted to the active coordinates), and
letWtW_{t}be the subspace orthogonal to these rows. Suppose we pickΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}randomly inWtW_{t}.

Clearly, in this algorithm every row incurs zero discrepancy as long as it is large. Moreover, asdim(Wt)≥3​nt/4\dim(W_{t})\geq 3n_{t}/4, intuitively one would expect that the walk still looks sufficiently random, and thus, with high probability, any row only incursO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n})discrepancy once it becomes small (as under a fully random coloring).

## An Instructive Bad Example.

Unfortunately, a serious problem with this idea is that constraining the walk to lie in the subspaceWtW_{t}can create arbitrary correlations between the active coordinates, leading to high discrepancy.
Consider the following example.

Supposev1v_{1}is a small row supported onk≪nk\ll ncoordinates (for concreteness, say,M​(1,j)=k−1/2M(1,j)=k^{-1/2}forj∈[k]j\in[k]and0otherwise). LetWWbe the subspace orthogonal to the large rows. Now supposeWWis such that every vectorv∈Wv\in Wmust satisfyv​(1)=v​(2)=⋯=v​(k)v(1)=v(2)=\cdots=v(k). But then, any updateΔ​x∈W\Delta x\in Wmust haveΔ​x​(1)=⋯=Δ​x​(k)\Delta x(1)=\cdots=\Delta x(k), and hence updatingΔ​x​(1)\Delta x(1)by±ε\pm\varepsilonwill incur discrepancy⟨v1,Δ​x⟩=±k⋅k−1/2​ε=±k1/2​ε\langle v_{1},\Delta x\rangle=\pm k\cdot k^{-1/2}\varepsilon=\pm k^{1/2}\varepsilon. This should be contrasted with the case where theΔ​x​(i)\Delta x(i)are i.i.d.±ε\pm\varepsilon, where⟨v1,Δ​x⟩≈±k1/2⋅k−1/2​ε=±ε\langle v_{1},\Delta x\rangle\approx\pm k^{1/2}\cdot k^{-1/2}\varepsilon=\pm\varepsilon.

Before reading on,
the reader may find it instructive to think about how one can handle this example, even if the subspaceWWis chosen adversarially as above.

## 8.2Algorithm

In the example above, the correlations for the rowv1v_{1}can be handled by also settingΔ​x​(1)=0\Delta x(1)=0(and thus alsoΔ​x​(2)=⋯=Δ​x​(k)=0\Delta x(2)=\cdots=\Delta x(k)=0).

However, it is not immediately clear how to address this problem in general. First, there could be multiple bad rowsviv_{i}with coordinates correlated in arbitrary ways. Moreover, imposing additional constraints onΔ​x\Delta xto fix the currently bad rows can create further correlations and additional bad rows that need to be fixed again and so on.

Interestingly, it turns out that there always exist
good update directionsΔ​x\Delta xthat are

(i) orthogonal to all large rows, and

(ii) still lookrandom(or better) in every direction.

Moreover, these directions can be computed efficiently using a semidefinite program (SDP).
We now describe this SDP, stated below in a slightly more general setting than we need for our algorithm. We describe the overall algorithm, which will be similar to that in Section4.3. We then analyze it in Section8.3.

## 8.2.1SDP Formulation

LetW⊂ℝhW\subset\mathbb{R}^{h}be any subspace with dimensiondim(W)≤δ​h\dim(W)\leq\delta h.
Consider the following SDP with the variableU∈ℝh×hU\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times h}, and with some scalar parameters0≤κ,η≤10\leq\kappa,\eta\leq 1.(SDP)⟨w​w⊤,U⟩\displaystyle\text{(SDP)}\qquad\langle ww^{\top},U\rangle=0\displaystyle=0for all​w∈W\displaystyle\text{for all }w\in W(8.1)⟨ej​ej⊤,U⟩\displaystyle\langle e_{j}e_{j}^{\top},U\rangle≤1\displaystyle\leq 1for all​j∈[h]\displaystyle\text{for all }j\in[h](8.2)tr⁡(U)\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(U)≥κ​h\displaystyle\geq\kappa h(8.3)U\displaystyle U⪯1η​𝖽𝗂𝖺𝗀​(U)\displaystyle\preceq\frac{1}{\eta}\mathsf{diag}(U)(8.4)U\displaystyle U⪰0\displaystyle\succeq 0(8.5)

Here,e1,…,ehe_{1},\ldots,e_{h}are the standard coordinate directions inℝh\mathbb{R}^{h}, and𝖽𝗂𝖺𝗀​(U)\mathsf{diag}(U)is the diagonal matrix we get from zeroing out all non-diagonal entries ofUU.
It is clear that this SDP can be solved in time polynomial inhh.111In particular, for constraint (8.1), one can pick an arbitrary basisw1,…,wδ​hw_{1},\ldots,w_{\delta h}forWWand set⟨wi​wiT,U⟩=0\langle w_{i}w_{i}^{T},U\rangle=0for eachi∈[δ​h]i\in[\delta h]. This ensures that‖U1/2​wi‖2=0\|U^{1/2}w_{i}\|_{2}=0so thatU1/2​wi=𝟎U^{1/2}w_{i}={\bf 0}for eachii, and thus by linearityU1/2​w=𝟎U^{1/2}w=\bf{0}for allw∈Ww\in W.To understand the constraints in this SDP, fix some feasible solutionUUand consider the random vectoru=U1/2​gu=U^{1/2}gwhereg∼N​(0,Ih)g\sim N(0,I_{h}). Clearly,uuhas mean𝔼​[u]=0\mathbb{E}[u]=0and covariance𝔼​[u​u⊤]=U\mathbb{E}[uu^{\top}]=U. Let us think ofuuas the coloring update (ignoring the scaling).
The constraints(8.1)ensure that⟨u,w⟩=0\langle u,w\rangle=0for all vectorsw∈Ww\in W, as⟨u,w⟩=⟨U1/2​g,w⟩=⟨g,U1/2​w⟩=0.\langle u,w\rangle=\langle U^{1/2}g,w\rangle=\langle g,U^{1/2}w\rangle=0.

constraints (8.2) ensure that𝔼​[u​(j)2]≤1\mathbb{E}[u(j)^{2}]\leq 1for all coordinatesj∈[h]j\in[h], as𝔼​[u​(j)2]=𝔼​[⟨u​uT,ej​ejT⟩]=⟨U,ej​ejT⟩≤1.\mathbb{E}[u(j)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\langle uu^{T},e_{j}e_{j}^{T}\rangle]=\langle U,e_{j}e_{j}^{T}\rangle\leq 1.

Forκ>0\kappa>0, the constraint (8.3) rules out the trivial solutionU=𝟎U={\bf 0}.

Random-Like Coloring Update.The constraint (8.4) is more interesting. Notice that
it is equivalent to saying thatθT​U​θ≤(1/η)​θT​𝖽𝗂𝖺𝗀​(U)​θ\theta^{T}U\theta\leq(1/\eta)\,\theta^{T}\mathsf{diag}(U)\theta

for all vectorsθ=(θ​(1),…,θ​(h))∈ℝh\theta=(\theta(1),\ldots,\theta(h))\in\mathbb{R}^{h}.

AsθT​U​θ=𝔼​[⟨θ,u⟩2]\theta^{T}U\theta=\mathbb{E}[\langle\theta,u\rangle^{2}], this says that𝔼​[⟨θ,u⟩2]=𝔼​[(∑j=1hθ​(j)​u​(j))2]≤1η​∑j=1hθ​(j)2​𝔼​[u​(j)2].\mathbb{E}\left[\langle\theta,u\rangle^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{h}\theta(j)u(j)\Big)^{2}\Big]\leq\frac{1}{\eta}\sum_{j=1}^{h}\theta(j)^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[u(j)^{2}].

Notice that if we hadη=1\eta=1and an equality above, then this is the same as saying that the random variablesu​(1),…,u​(h)u(1),\ldots,u(h)are pairwise independent. In our algorithm, we will setη=1/4\eta=1/4, so intuitively (8.4) should be viewed as saying that the “coloring update”uuis almost pairwise independent.

We need the following result about the feasibility of this SDP.
We will prove this later in
Section8.4.

## Theorem 8.2.

(SDP Feasibility)
For any subspaceWWofℝh\mathbb{R}^{h}of dimension at mostδ​h\delta h, the SDP given by (8.1)-(8.5) is feasible
wheneverδ+κ+η≤1\delta+\kappa+\eta\leq 1.

## 8.2.2The Algorithm

LetMMbe the input matrix.
Fix a small step sizeγ=1/n2\gamma=1/n^{2}and letδ=1/n\delta=1/n.
Letxt−1x_{t-1}be the coloring at the end of time stept−1t-1of the algorithm, and defineFt={j:|xt−1​(j)|>1−δ}F_{t}=\{j:|x_{t-1}(j)|>1-\delta\}to be the set of “fixed” coordinates at the end of timet−1t-1(or equivalently at the beginning of timett). We letAt=[n]∖FtA_{t}=[n]\setminus F_{t}be the set of active, i.e., unfixed coordinates.

Initializex0​(j)=0x_{0}(j)=0forj∈[n]j\in[n]andF0=∅F_{0}=\emptyset.
Letnt:=n−|Ft|=|At|n_{t}:=n-|F_{t}|=|A_{t}|.
At each time steptt, we will only update coordinates inAtA_{t}.

Algorithm:At each time stept=1,2,…t=1,2,\ldots, repeat untilnt=0n_{t}=0.
- 1.

Call a rowiilarge(at timett) if∑j∈AtM​(i,j)2≥4\sum_{j\in A_{t}}M(i,j)^{2}\geq 4. LetWtW_{t}denote the subspace spanned by all the large rows. All rows that are not large aresmall.
- 2.

Solve the SDP (8.1)-(8.5) with variableUt∈ℝAt×AtU_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{A_{t}\times A_{t}}, the subspaceW=Wt⊆ℝAtW=W_{t}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{A_{t}},h=nth=n_{t}andκ=η=1/4\kappa=\eta=1/4.

LetΔ​xt=γ​Ut1/2​gt\Delta x_{t}=\gamma U_{t}^{1/2}g_{t}, wheregt∼N​(0,IAt)g_{t}\sim N(0,I_{A_{t}}).

Updatext=xt−1+Δ​xtx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\Delta x_{t}.

LetTTbe the (random) time at which the algorithm terminates. The final coloring is given byx​(i)=sign⁡(xT​(i))x(i)=\operatorname{sign}(x_{T}(i)).

## 8.3Analysis

We first show that the SDP is always feasible at each timett, and that the algorithm runs in polynomial time. Then we bound the discrepancy.

Feasibility.Fix some timett. As∑iM​(i,j)2≤1\sum_{i}M(i,j)^{2}\leq 1for each columnjj, summing over the active columns gives∑j∈At∑iM​(i,j)2≤|At|=nt\sum_{j\in A_{t}}\sum_{i}M(i,j)^{2}\leq|A_{t}|=n_{t}. So by averaging there can be at mostnt/4n_{t}/4large rows, anddim(Wt)≤nt/4\dim(W_{t})\leq n_{t}/4(and thusδ≤1/4\delta\leq 1/4) in the SDP constraint (8.1). (This argument is analogous to the proof of Lemma2.3.)
As we setκ=η=1/4\kappa=\eta=1/4, we have thatδ+κ+η≤1\delta+\kappa+\eta\leq 1, and thus by Theorem8.2the SDP is feasible.

The algorithm also terminates with high probability in polynomial time, as at each timett, the squared norm𝔼​[‖xt‖2]=𝔼​[‖xt−1‖2]+γ2​tr⁡(Ut)≥𝔼​[‖xt−1‖2]+γ2\mathbb{E}[\|x_{t}\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|x_{t-1}\|^{2}]+\gamma^{2}\operatorname{tr}(U_{t})\geq\mathbb{E}[\|x_{t-1}\|^{2}]+\gamma^{2}

rises in expectation by at leastγ2\gamma^{2}, and is bounded bynnsince, with high probability,|xt​(j)|≤1|x_{t}(j)|\leq 1for all time stepsttand coordinatesjj.

We now bound the discrepancy.

## Theorem 8.3.

With high probability,‖M​x‖∞≲log⁡2​n\|Mx\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\log 2n}.

Notice that we only need to bound the discrepancy from the time a row becomes small.
Indeed, for any rowiithat is large at timett, the vectorviv_{i}always lies in the subspaceWtW_{t}. Thus any row incurs0discrepancy as long as it is large.

Random processes with negative drift.We first state a
useful concentration inequality for super-martingales with negative drift.

## Lemma 8.4.

Let{Zt:t=0,1,⋯}\{Z_{t}:t=0,1,\cdots\}be a real-valued random process with incrementsΔ​Zt:=Zt−Zt−1\Delta Z_{t}:=Z_{t}-Z_{t-1}, where there exists anα>0\alpha>0such that, for alltt, with probability 1,|α​Δ​Zt|≤1|\alpha\Delta Z_{t}|\leq 1, and𝔼t−1​[Δ​Zt]≤−α​𝔼t−1​[(Δ​Zt)2].\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[\Delta Z_{t}]\leq-\alpha\,\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[(\Delta Z_{t})^{2}].

Here𝔼t−1​[⋅]\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[\cdot]denotes the conditional expectation𝔼[⋅|Z1,⋯,Zt−1]\mathbb{E}[\cdot|Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{t-1}]. Then for allξ≥0\xi\geq 0, we have thatℙ​[Zt−Z0>ξ]≤exp⁡(−α​ξ).\mathbb{P}[Z_{t}-Z_{0}>\xi]\leq\exp(-\alpha\xi).

## Proof.

By Markov’s inequality,ℙ​[Zt−Z0≥ξ]=ℙ​[exp⁡(α​(Zt−Z0))≥exp⁡(α​ξ)]≤𝔼​[exp⁡(α​(Zt−Z0))]exp⁡(α​ξ).\mathbb{P}[Z_{t}-Z_{0}\geq\xi]=\mathbb{P}[\exp(\alpha(Z_{t}-Z_{0}))\geq\exp(\alpha\xi)]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\exp(\alpha(Z_{t}-Z_{0}))]}{\exp(\alpha\xi)}.

So, it suffices to show that𝔼​[exp⁡(α​(Zt−Z0))]≤1\mathbb{E}[\exp(\alpha(Z_{t}-Z_{0}))]\leq 1.
Now,𝔼t−1​[exp⁡(α​(Zt−Z0))]=\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t-1}\left[\exp(\alpha(Z_{t}-Z_{0}))\right]=exp⁡(α​(Zt−1−Z0))​𝔼t−1​[exp⁡(α​Δ​Zt)]\displaystyle\exp(\alpha(Z_{t-1}-Z_{0}))\mathbb{E}_{t-1}\left[\exp(\alpha\Delta Z_{t})\right]≤\displaystyle\leqexp⁡(α​(Zt−1−Z0))​𝔼t−1​[1+α​Δ​Zt+(α​Δ​Zt)2]\displaystyle\exp(\alpha(Z_{t-1}-Z_{0}))\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[1+\alpha\Delta Z_{t}+(\alpha\Delta Z_{t})^{2}]≤\displaystyle\leqexp⁡(α​(Zt−1−Z0)).\displaystyle\exp(\alpha(Z_{t-1}-Z_{0})).\qquad

Here the first inequality uses thatα​|Δ​Zt|≤1\alpha|\Delta Z_{t}|\leq 1andey≤1+y+y2e^{y}\leq 1+y+y^{2}for|y|≤1|y|\leq 1, and the second that𝔼t−1​[Δ​Zt]≤−α​𝔼t−1​[(Δ​Zt)2]\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[\Delta Z_{t}]\leq-\alpha\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[(\Delta Z_{t})^{2}].

The result now follows by the law of iterated expectations.
∎

## Proof of Theorem8.3.

Fix some rowii. Lettit_{i}be the time when it first becomes small, and
letθ\thetadenote the rowviv_{i}restricted to the coordinates that are alive attit_{i}(and set to0otherwise). Note that‖θ‖22≤4\|\theta\|^{2}_{2}\leq 4.
Our goal is to show that|⟨θ,xT−xti⟩|≲log⁡2​n|\langle\theta,x_{T}-x_{t_{i}}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\log 2n}, with high probability.

Let us writeθ=b+s\theta=b+s, wherebbcontains the big entries ofθ\thetawith magnitude at least1/log⁡2​n1/\sqrt{\log 2n}, with the other entries set to0,
andssconsists of the rest of the entries, with the big entries set to0.

The discrepancy due tobbis trivial to bound. Asbbhas big entries and norm‖b‖2≤‖θ‖2≤2\|b\|_{2}\leq\|\theta\|_{2}\leq 2, it can have at most4​log⁡2​n4\log 2nnon-zero entries. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,|⟨b,xT−xti⟩|≤‖b‖2⋅(∑j:b​(j)≠0(xT​(j)−xti​(j))2)1/2≲log⁡2​n.\big|\langle b,x_{T}-x_{t_{i}}\rangle\big|\leq\|b\|_{2}\cdot\Big(\sum_{j:b(j)\neq 0}(x_{T}(j)-x_{t_{i}}(j))^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\lesssim\sqrt{\log 2n}.

We now focus on bounding the discrepancy ofss.

Regularized Discrepancy.To do this, we will apply Lemma8.4to theregularized discrepancyof the rowss, defined asZt:=⟨s,xt⟩+β​∑j=1ns​(j)2​(1−xt​(j)2),Z_{t}:=\langle s,x_{t}\rangle+\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}s(j)^{2}(1-x_{t}(j)^{2}),

where we setβ:=log⁡2​n\beta:=\sqrt{\log 2n}.

ZtZ_{t}consists of two components: the discrepancy ofss, and the “energy” termβ​∑j=1ns​(j)2​(1−xt​(j)2)\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}s(j)^{2}(1-x_{t}(j)^{2}), which quantifies how far the colors of coordinates that contribute to the discrepancy are from{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}.
Intuitively,ZtZ_{t}is a good proxy for the discrepancy ofssat timett. Moreover, it has the nice property that one can “charge” the increase in discrepancy to the decrease in energy. We show these properties next.

As∑js​(j)2≤‖θ‖2≤4\sum_{j}s(j)^{2}\leq\|\theta\|^{2}\leq 4and1−xt​(j)2≥01-x_{t}(j)^{2}\geq 0for allj∈[n]j\in[n],⟨s,xt⟩≤Zt≤⟨s,xt⟩+4​β,\langle s,x_{t}\rangle\leq Z_{t}\leq\langle s,x_{t}\rangle+4\beta,

ZtZ_{t}then tracks the actual discrepancy ofssto withinO​(β)=O​(log⁡n)O(\beta)=O(\sqrt{\log n}), by our choice ofβ\beta.

Showing the Negative Drift.Next, we claim thatΔ​Zt\Delta Z_{t}satisfies the negative drift condition in Lemma8.4withα=Ω​(β)\alpha=\Omega(\beta).
Indeed, asxt=xt−1+Δ​xtx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\Delta x_{t}, and writingxt​(j)2=xt−1​(j)2+2​Δ​xt​(j)​xt−1​(j)+Δ​xt​(j)2x_{t}(j)^{2}=x_{t-1}(j)^{2}+2\Delta x_{t}(j)x_{t-1}(j)+\Delta x_{t}(j)^{2}, a direct computation givesΔ​Zt=⟨s−2​β​xt−1∘s2,Δ​xt⟩−β​⟨s2,(Δ​xt)2⟩,\displaystyle\Delta Z_{t}=\langle s-2\beta x_{t-1}\circ s^{2},\Delta x_{t}\rangle-\beta\langle s^{2},(\Delta x_{t})^{2}\rangle,(8.6)

where we uses2s^{2}andΔ​xt2\Delta x_{t}^{2}to denote the vector with entriess​(j)2s(j)^{2}and(Δ​xt​(j))2(\Delta x_{t}(j))^{2}respectively, andxt−1∘s2x_{t-1}\circ s^{2}the vector with entriesxt−1​(j)​s​(j)2x_{t-1}(j)s(j)^{2}.

Let us now consider𝔼​[Δ​Zt]\mathbb{E}[\Delta Z_{t}]and𝔼​[(Δ​Zt)2]\mathbb{E}[(\Delta Z_{t})^{2}].
As𝔼​[Δ​xt]=0\mathbb{E}[\Delta x_{t}]=0, we have𝔼​[Δ​Zt]=−β​𝔼​⟨s2,Δ​xt2⟩.\mathbb{E}[\Delta Z_{t}]=-\beta\,\,\mathbb{E}\langle s^{2},\Delta x_{t}^{2}\rangle.(8.7)

Next asΔ​xt=γ​Ut1/2​g\Delta x_{t}=\gamma U_{t}^{1/2}g, and ignoringO​(γ3)O(\gamma^{3})terms asγ\gammais tiny,222As the algorithm runs forO~​(1/γ2)\tilde{O}(1/\gamma^{2})time steps with high probability, the total contribution of these terms over all time steps can be trivially bounded byo​(1)o(1).we have𝔼​[(Δ​Zt)2]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\Delta Z_{t})^{2}]=𝔼​⟨s−2​β​xt−1∘s2,Δ​xt⟩2\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\langle s-2\beta x_{t-1}\circ s^{2},\Delta x_{t}\rangle^{2}≲𝔼​⟨(s−2​β​xt−1∘s2)2,Δ​xt2⟩\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\langle(s-2\beta x_{t-1}\circ s^{2})^{2},\Delta x_{t}^{2}\rangle(8.8)≲𝔼​⟨s2,Δ​xt2⟩.\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\langle s^{2},\Delta x_{t}^{2}\rangle.(8.9)

The first inequality (8.8) uses that for any vectorvv, we have𝔼​[⟨v,Δ​xt⟩2]≲𝔼​[⟨v2,Δ​xt2⟩].\mathbb{E}[\langle v,\Delta x_{t}\rangle^{2}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\langle v^{2},\Delta x_{t}^{2}\rangle].(8.10)

This follows as𝔼​[Δ​xt​Δ​xt⊤]=γ2​U\mathbb{E}[\Delta x_{t}\Delta x_{t}^{\top}]=\gamma^{2}U, and so the SDP constraint (8.4) implies that𝔼​[Δ​xt​Δ​xt⊤]⪯O​(1)⋅𝖽𝗂𝖺𝗀​(𝔼​[Δ​xt​Δ​xt⊤])\mathbb{E}[\Delta x_{t}\Delta x_{t}^{\top}]\preceq O(1)\cdot\mathsf{diag}(\mathbb{E}[\Delta x_{t}\Delta x_{t}^{\top}]).
This immediately implies (8.10).
The inequality (8.9) uses that|2​β​xt−1​(j)​s​(j)2|≲|s​(j)||2\beta x_{t-1}(j)s(j)^{2}|\lesssim|s(j)|, which follows as|s​(j)|≤1/log⁡2​n|s(j)|\leq 1/\sqrt{\log 2n},β=log⁡2​n\beta=\sqrt{\log 2n}and|xt−1​(j)|≤1|x_{t-1}(j)|\leq 1for each coordinatejj.

Together, (8.7) and (8.9) give that, for someα=Ω​(β)\alpha=\Omega(\beta),𝔼​[Δ​Zt]≤−α​𝔼​[(Δ​Zt)2].\mathbb{E}[\Delta Z_{t}]\leq-\alpha\mathbb{E}[(\Delta Z_{t})^{2}].

Bounding the discrepancy.Applying Lemma8.4withα=Ω​(β)\alpha=\Omega(\beta)andξ=Θ​(β−1​log⁡n)\xi=\Theta(\beta^{-1}\log n)givesℙ​[ZT−Zti≥ξ]=exp⁡(−α​ξ)=1/𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒​(n).\mathbb{P}[Z_{T}-Z_{t_{i}}\geq\xi]=\exp(-\alpha\xi)=1/\mathsf{poly}(n).

Asξ=O​(log⁡2​n)\xi=O(\sqrt{\log 2n})by our choice ofβ\beta, this implies thatZT≤Zti+O​(log⁡n)Z_{T}\leq Z_{t_{i}}+O(\sqrt{\log n})with high probability.
As⟨s,xT⟩≤ZT\langle s,x_{T}\rangle\leq Z_{T}andZti=O​(β)Z_{t_{i}}=O(\beta)(as⟨vi,xti⟩=0\langle v_{i},x_{t_{i}}\rangle=0, sinceviv_{i}was large until timetit_{i}, and hence the updatesΔ​xt\Delta x_{t}were orthogonal to it), this implies that⟨s,xT−xti⟩=O​(log⁡n)\langle s,x_{T}-x_{t_{i}}\rangle=O(\sqrt{\log n}). This proves the result.

## 8.4SDP Feasibility

We now prove Theorem8.2. We first state two useful facts.

## Lemma 8.5.

Given anym×nm\times nmatrixMMwith columnsmjm_{j}satisfying‖mj‖2≤1\|m_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1for allj∈[n]j\in[n]andβ∈(0,1]\beta\in(0,1], there exists a subspaceS⊆ℝnS\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}such that (i)dim(S)≥(1−β)​n\dim(S)\geq(1-\beta)nand (ii)∀y∈S\forall y\in S,‖M​y‖22≤(1/β)​‖y‖22\|My\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1/\beta)\|y\|_{2}^{2}.

## Proof.

LetM=∑i=1nσi​pi​qiTM=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}p_{i}q_{i}^{T}by the singular value decomposition ofMM, with singular values0≤σ1≤⋯≤σn0\leq\sigma_{1}\leq\dots\leq\sigma_{n}and{pi:i∈[n]},{qi:i∈[n]}\{p_{i}:i\in[n]\},\{q_{i}:i\in[n]\}are the two sets of orthonormal vectors.

## 8.5Bibliographic Notes

Theorem8.3was first proved by Bansal, Dadush and Garg in[14]. However, the original proof was more complicated, and our exposition here is substantially simpler and is based on the ideas in[16].

Several alternate ways to prove Theorem8.3have also been discovered since then, using a variety of different algorithmic ideas.
In particular, Levy, Ramadas and Rothvoss[78]gave a deterministic algorithm based on a nice application of the multiplicative updatie method. More recently, Bansal, Laddha and Vempala[18]gave an algorithm using the barrier function methods, and Pesenti and Vladu[100]gave another algorithm based on regularization. Both these algorithms are also deterministic.

## Chapter 9The Gram Schmidt Walk Algorithm

We now give an efficient algorithm for Banaszczyk’s result as stated
in Theorem5.1(assuming access to a membership oracle for
the convex setKK).
As discussed in Section5.3, it is not clear how to
work directly with a generalKKor use the arguments in
Chapter6or Chapter8to give an algorithm.

Instead we will use the following equivalent formulation described in Section5.4.

## Theorem 9.1.

Given arbitrary vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vi‖2≤1\|v_{i}\|_{2}\leq 1, there is a distributionDDover coloringsx∈{−1,1}nx\in\{-1,1\}^{n}such that the resulting discrepancy vector∑j=1nx​(j)​vj\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}, wherexxis sampled fromDD, has a
mean-zeroO​(1)O(1)-sub-Gaussian distribution.

Moreover, givenv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}, a coloringxxcan be sampled fromDDin polynomial time.

We will describe a randomized algorithm called the Gram-Schmidt Walk (GS Walk), that givenv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}produces a random coloringxx(sampled from some implicit distributionDD) such that the resulting discrepancy vectorYYis20\sqrt{20}-sub-Gaussian.

Before describing the algorithm, it is instructive to consider the following two examples to get some intuition for the sub-Gaussianity condition in Theorem9.1.

## Example 9.2.

Suppose thatv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}are orthogonal unit vectors.

We claim that in this case, a uniformly random±1\pm 1coloring suffices. Indeed fix some vectorθ∈ℝm\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}. Then⟨θ,Y⟩=⟨θ,∑j=1nx​(j)​vj⟩=∑j=1nx​(j)​⟨θ,vj⟩.\langle\theta,Y\rangle=\langle\theta,\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)\langle\theta,v_{j}\rangle.

For a uniformly random coloringx∈{−1,1}nx\in\{-1,1\}^{n}, eachx​(j)x(j)is an
independent and uniform sample in{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}, and hencex​(j)​⟨θ,vj⟩x(j)\langle\theta,v_{j}\rangleisO​(|⟨θ,vj⟩|)O(|\langle\theta,v_{j}\rangle|)-sub-Gaussian by Hoeffding’s lemma. By
independence∑j=1nx​(j)​⟨θ,vj⟩\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)\langle\theta,v_{j}\rangleisσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian
withσ≲∑j=1n⟨θ,vj⟩2\sigma\lesssim\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle\theta,v_{j}\rangle^{2}}, and the
right hand side is at most‖θ‖2\|\theta\|_{2}as thevjv_{j}are orthonormal.

## Remark 9.3.

The randomness is crucial; if we just output a fixed coloringxx,
thenY=∑j=1nx​(j)​vjY=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)v_{j}isn\sqrt{n}-sub-Gaussian as⟨Y,θ⟩=‖Y‖2=n\langle Y,\theta\rangle=\|Y\|_{2}=\sqrt{n}for the unit vectorθ=Y/‖Y‖2\theta=Y/\|Y\|_{2}.

## Example 9.4.

On the other extreme, suppose thatv1=⋯=vn=vv_{1}=\cdots=v_{n}=vare all
identical to somevvwith‖v‖2=1\|v\|_{2}=1. Then a uniformly random
coloring is very bad asY=(∑j=1nx​(j))​vY=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)\right)v, and𝔼​[⟨Y,θ⟩2]=𝔼​(∑j=1nx​(j))2=n\mathbb{E}[\langle Y,\theta\rangle^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x(j)\right)^{2}=nforθ=v\theta=v, while we require that𝔼​[⟨Y,θ⟩2]≲1\mathbb{E}[\langle Y,\theta\rangle^{2}]\lesssim 1for anO​(1)O(1)-sub-GaussianYYand a unit vectorθ\theta.

The right thing to do in this example, of course, is to pair up the
signsx​(j)x(j)and cancel them out as much as possible. We can
then make sure that‖Y‖2≲1\|Y\|_{2}\lesssim 1with probability11.

The GS Walk algorithm will handle these two extreme scenarios in a unified way. It will exploit the linear dependencies to cancel things exactly as much as possible, and at the same time have enough randomness so that the resulting distribution isO​(1)O(1)-sub-Gaussian.

We describe this algorithm next in Section9.1.
In Section9.2we show that the covariance of the output discrepancy vectorYYsatisfiesCov​(Y)⪯I\text{Cov}(Y)\preceq Iand finally in Section9.3we prove the sub-Gaussianity.

## 9.1The Gram-Schmidt Walk

We first describe the algorithm informally.

Letv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}be the input vectors. The algorithm begins with the fractional coloringx0=(0,…,0)x_{0}=(0,\ldots,0)and updates it iteratively over time until the final coloringxT∈{−1,1}nx_{T}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}. Once a coordinate reaches±1\pm 1it is frozen and not updated anymore, otherwise it is alive.

To update the coloring at timett, we compute an update directionutu_{t}based on which coordinates are still alive, and setxt=xt−1+δt​utx_{t}=x_{t-1}+\delta_{t}u_{t}where the step sizeδt\delta_{t}is chosen randomly such that𝔼​[δt]=0\mathbb{E}[\delta_{t}]=0and at least one new variable gets frozen.

## Computing the update.

The idea behind the update step (which is the crux of the algorithm) is the following.

Assume for simplicity that no coordinate is frozen.
LetBBdenote the matrix with columnsv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}.
If the coloringxxis updated byuu, the discrepancy vector changes byB​u=u​(1)​v1+u​(2)​v2+…+u​(n)​vnBu=u(1)v_{1}+u(2)v_{2}+\ldots+u(n)v_{n}.

Suppose that we are required to setu​(n)=1u(n)=1(to ensure progress on coordinatenn),
but can freely setu​(1),…,u​(n−1)u(1),\ldots,u(n-1)inℝ\mathbb{R}(not just in[−1,1][-1,1]). Then intuitively, we should try to use this flexibility to cancel things to to makeB​uBuas small as possible.

Concretely, let us try to minimize‖B​u‖2\|Bu\|_{2}subject tou​(n)=1u(n)=1.
Then we explicitly haveminu:u​(n)=1⁡‖B​u‖2\displaystyle\min_{u:u(n)=1}\|Bu\|_{2}=minu​(1),…,u​(n−1)⁡‖vn+∑j=1n−1u​(j)​vj‖2\displaystyle=\min_{u(1),\ldots,u(n-1)}\left\|v_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}u(j)v_{j}\right\|_{2}=‖ΠV⟂​(vn)‖2,\displaystyle=\|\Pi_{V^{\perp}}(v_{n})\|_{2},(9.1)

whereV=span​(v1,…,vn−1)V=\text{span}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})is the subspace spanned byv1,…,vn−1v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1},V⟂V^{\perp}is the subspace orthogonal toVV, and for any subspaceWW,ΠW\Pi_{W}denotes the orthogonal projection operator ontoWW.

To see (9.1), setu​(1),…,u​(n−1)u(1),\ldots,u(n-1)so that∑i=1n−1u​(i)​vi=−ΠV​(vn)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}u(i)v_{i}=-\Pi_{V}(v_{n})and henceB​u=vn−ΠV​(vn)=ΠV⟂​(vn)Bu=v_{n}-\Pi_{V}(v_{n})=\Pi_{V^{\perp}}(v_{n}). This is also
the least possible value of‖B​u‖2\|Bu\|_{2}, because∑j=1n−1u​(j)​vj∈V\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}u(j)v_{j}\in V, so‖B​u‖2≥‖ΠV⟂​(B​u)‖2=‖ΠV⟂​(vn)‖2.\|Bu\|_{2}\geq\|\Pi_{V^{\perp}}(Bu)\|_{2}=\|\Pi_{V^{\perp}}(v_{n})\|_{2}.

## Notation.

The algorithm is described formally below. See also Figure9.1.

Letptp_{t}be the largest-indexed (alive) element inAt−1A_{t-1}at timett.

We compute the vectorutu_{t}to achieve the minimum in (9.1) (restricted to alive coordinates andptp_{t}as the indexnn), and updatextx_{t}randomly alongutu_{t}so thatxtx_{t}stays in[−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}and least one more variable reaches±1\pm 1.
We callptp_{t}thepivotat timett, and it plays a special role at timett.Figure 9.1:The left shows the pivot and discrepancy update direction. The right shows how the coloring updates.

Fort=1,…,nt=1,\ldots,ndo the following:
- 1.

Computeut=argminu​‖B​u‖2u_{t}=\text{argmin}_{u}\|Bu\|_{2}, subject tou​(pt)=1u(p_{t})=1for the pivotptp_{t}, andu​(j)=0u(j)=0for each frozen variablej∉At−1j\notin A_{t-1}.
