## 9.1.1Basic Properties

Let us note some basic properties of the algorithm and define some notation that will be useful later.

## Coloring update.

LetΔ​xt:=xt−xt−1=δt​ut\Delta x_{t}:=x_{t}-x_{t-1}=\delta_{t}u_{t}denote the coloring update at timett, and
letΩt−1\Omega_{t-1}denote all the random choicesδ1,…,δt−1\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{t-1}made by the algorithm by timet−1t-1.
Note that𝔼​[δt|Ωt−1]=0\mathbb{E}[\delta_{t}|\Omega_{t-1}]=0, and thus each colorxt​(i)x_{t}(i)evolves as a martingale.

The step sizesδt−,δt+\delta_{t}^{-},\delta_{t}^{+}ensure that at least one alive variable always reaches±1\pm 1.
We can assume that exactly one variable gets frozen per step (else we can divide the step into several steps). Thus the algorithm terminates in exactlynnsteps.

## Discrepancy update.

Letdt:=B​xtd_{t}:=Bx_{t}denote the discrepancy vector at the end of timett, and letΔ​dt:=dt−dt−1\Delta d_{t}:=d_{t}-d_{t-1}denote its update attt.

At timett, letVtV_{t}denote the subspace spanned by the alive vectors excluding the pivot, that is by{vi:i∈At−1,i≠pt}\{v_{i}:i\in A_{t-1},i\neq p_{t}\}.
Notice thatV0⊇V1⊇…⊇Vn=∅V_{0}\supseteq V_{1}\supseteq\ldots\supseteq V_{n}=\emptyset, is a (random) nested sequence produced by the execution of the algorithm, where we defineV0=span​{v1,…,vn}V_{0}=\text{span}\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}.
Equivalently,V0⟂⊆V1⟂⊆…⊆Vn⟂V_{0}^{\perp}\subseteq V_{1}^{\perp}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq V_{n}^{\perp}.

AsB​ut=ΠVt⟂​(vpt)Bu_{t}=\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})by (9.1), we can explicitly writeΔ​dt=B​Δ​xt=δt​B​ut=δt​ΠVt⟂​(vpt).\Delta d_{t}=B\Delta x_{t}=\delta_{t}Bu_{t}=\delta_{t}\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}}).(9.2)

## Phases.

When the coloring is updated at timett, two things may happen:
- 1.

Some non-pivoti≠pti\neq p_{t}gets frozen. Then we updateAt=At−1∖{i}A_{t}=A_{t-1}\setminus\{i\}and (crucially) keep the pivot unchanged, i.e.,pt+1=ptp_{t+1}=p_{t}.
- 2.

The current pivot gets frozen, i.e.,xt​(pt)x_{t}(p_{t})reaches±1\pm 1. Then we updateAt=At−1∖{pt}A_{t}=A_{t-1}\setminus\{p_{t}\}and choose a new pivotpt+1∈Atp_{t+1}\in A_{t}(as the largest indexed element inAtA_{t}).

For a pivotptp_{t}at timett, letft≤tf_{t}\leq tandℓt≥t\ell_{t}\geq tdenote the first (resp. last) time whenptp_{t}was the pivot. We call
the time interval[ft,ℓt][f_{t},\ell_{t}]aphase. Thus the phases
partition the time steps[n][n]into maximal intervals, where inside
each interval the pivot remains unchanged.

## 9.1.2Examples

It is instructive to see the algorithm on Examples9.2and9.4.

If theviv_{i}are orthogonal, we claim that it produces a uniformly random coloring.
Indeed, asΠVt​(vpt)=0\Pi_{V_{t}}(v_{p_{t}})=0, we haveΠVt⟂​(vpt)=vpt\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})=v_{p_{t}}and thusut​(pt)=1u_{t}(p_{t})=1andut​(i)=0u_{t}(i)=0fori≠pti\neq p_{t}. In other words, the algorithm only updatesx​(pt)x(p_{t})at timettand sets it independently to±1\pm 1. At each step it will choose a new pivot and thus output a uniformly random±1\pm 1coloring.

If theviv_{i}are identical, we claim that the algorithm will cancel out the colors as much as possible as desired.
Indeed, as long as at least two elements are alive, we haveΠVt​(vpt)=vpt\Pi_{V_{t}}(v_{p_{t}})=v_{p_{t}}and thusΠVt⟂​(vpt)=0\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})=0and soΔ​dt=0\Delta d_{t}=0by (9.2). So the final coloring will have an equal number of±1\pm 1(up to the parity ofnn).

## 9.2Bounding the Covariance

Before proving Theorem9.9in Section9.3,
we first bound the covariance ofdnd_{n}.
This already contains most of the conceptual ideas and avoids various technical details.
In fact, we prove the following optimum covariance bound.

## Theorem 9.5.

The final discrepancy vector has covariance𝔼​[dn​dnT]⪯I.\mathbb{E}[d_{n}d_{n}^{T}]\preceq I.

Equivalently,𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]≤‖θ‖22\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}]\leq\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}for allθ∈ℝm\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Let us fix someθ∈ℝm\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
We will track how𝔼​[⟨dt,θ⟩2]\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}]evolves as the algorithm proceeds.

Asdn=∑t=1nΔ​dtd_{n}=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\Delta d_{t}andΔ​dt=δt​B​ut\Delta d_{t}=\delta_{t}Bu_{t}, we have⟨dn,θ⟩=∑t=1n⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩=∑t=1nδt​⟨B​ut,θ⟩.\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{t}\langle Bu_{t},\theta\rangle.

As the incrementsδt\delta_{t}satisfy𝔼​[δt|Ωt−1]=0\mathbb{E}[\delta_{t}|\Omega_{t-1}]=0(and the update directionutu_{t}and the pivotptp_{t}are completely
determined byΩt−1\Omega_{t-1}), the sequence⟨dt,θ⟩\langle d_{t},\theta\ranglehas martingale increments. Thus we have that,𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]=𝔼​(∑t=1nδt​⟨B​ut,θ⟩)2=𝔼​[∑t=1nδt2​⟨B​ut,θ⟩2].\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{t}\langle Bu_{t},\theta\rangle\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{t}^{2}\langle Bu_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}\right].(9.3)

## A naive attempt.

As‖B​ut‖2=‖ΠVt⟂​(vpt)‖2≤‖vpt‖2≤1\|Bu_{t}\|_{2}=\|\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})\|_{2}\leq\|v_{p_{t}}\|_{2}\leq 1, one can bound the right side of (9.3)
by𝔼​[∑tδt2​‖θ‖22]\mathbb{E}[\sum_{t}\delta_{t}^{2}\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}]. However, this can be as large asΩ​(n)​‖θ‖22\Omega(n)\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}in general (instead of the desired bound of‖θ‖22\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}).

The problem is that the bound‖ΠVt⟂​(vpt)‖2≤1\|\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})\|_{2}\leq 1is too crude, and does not exploit the linear algebraic properties of the algorithm; in particular how the subspacesVtV_{t}evolve and how the pivotsptp_{t}are chosen.

To this end, we will decompose the discrepancy incrementΔ​dt=δt​B​ut=δt​ΠVt⟂​(vpt)\Delta d_{t}=\delta_{t}Bu_{t}=\delta_{t}\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})further based on evolution ofVtV_{t}during the phase whereptp_{t}was the pivot.

## Nested subspaces and decomposingΠVt⟂​(vpt)\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})further.

Recall that for any two subspaces nestedA⊆BA\subseteq B, we can
writeBBas the direct sum of the orthogonal subspacesAAandB∩A⟂B\cap A^{\perp}. Equivalently,
the projectionΠB\Pi_{B}ontoBBcan be written asΠB=ΠB∩A⟂+ΠA\Pi_{B}=\Pi_{B\cap A^{\perp}}+\Pi_{A}, the sum of projections ontoB∩A⟂B\cap A^{\perp}andAA. In other words,ΠB−ΠA=ΠB∩A⟂\Pi_{B}-\Pi_{A}=\Pi_{B\cap A^{\perp}}.

Applying this to the sequence of subspacesV0⟂⊆V1⟂⊆…⊆Vn⟂V_{0}^{\perp}\subseteq V_{1}^{\perp}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq V_{n}^{\perp}produced by the algorithm, let us defineΠt:=ΠVt⟂−ΠVt−1⟂=ΠVt⟂∩Vt−1,\Pi_{t}:=\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}-\Pi_{V_{t-1}^{\perp}}=\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}\cap V_{t-1}},(9.4)

fort=1,…,nt=1,\ldots,n. Notice that theΠt\Pi_{t}are mutually orthogonal,i.e.,Πs​Πt=Πt​Πs=0\Pi_{s}\Pi_{t}=\Pi_{t}\Pi_{s}=0whenevers<ts<t. This is the case since
the subspaceVs⟂∩Vs−1⊆Vs⟂⊆Vt−1⟂V_{s}^{\perp}\cap V_{s-1}\subseteq V_{s}^{\perp}\subseteq V_{t-1}^{\perp}is orthogonal toVt−1V_{t-1}.

We have the following useful fact.

## Lemma 9.6.

At any timet∈[n]t\in[n], we have thatB​ut=ΠVt⟂​(vpt)=∑t′=fttΠt′​(vpt)Bu_{t}=\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})=\sum_{t^{\prime}=f_{t}}^{t}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p_{t}}),
whereptp_{t}is the pivot andftf_{t}is the start time of the phase containingtt.

Henceforth, we denotePt:=∑t′=fttΠt′P_{t}:=\sum_{t^{\prime}=f_{t}}^{t}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}, so thatB​ut=Pt​vptBu_{t}=P_{t}v_{p_{t}}.

## Proof.

Asvptv_{p_{t}}was alive at timeft−1f_{t}-1and it was not the pivot at that time it lies in the subspaceVft−1V_{f_{t}-1}. SoΠVft−1⟂​(vpt)=0\Pi_{V_{f_{t}-1}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})=0and thus,ΠVt⟂​(vpt)=ΠVt⟂​(vpt)−ΠVft−1⟂​(vpt)=∑t′∈[ft,t]Πt′​(vpt).∎\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})=\Pi_{V_{t}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})-\Pi_{V_{f_{t}-1}^{\perp}}(v_{p_{t}})=\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[f_{t},t]}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p_{t}}).\qed

We can now show the following refined bound on𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}].

## Lemma 9.7.𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]≤𝔼​[∑t=1n‖Πt​(θ)‖22​∑t′=tℓtδt′2],\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\|\Pi_{t}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{\ell_{t}}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}\right],

whereℓt\ell_{t}is the last time of the phase containingtt.

## Proof.

By (9.3) and Lemma9.6, we have𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]=𝔼​[∑t=1nδt2​⟨B​ut,θ⟩2]=𝔼​[∑t=1nδt2​⟨Pt​vpt,θ⟩2],\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{t}^{2}\langle Bu_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{t}^{2}\langle P_{t}v_{p_{t}},\theta\rangle^{2}\right],(9.5)

AsPtP_{t}is self-adjoint and‖vpt‖2≤1\|v_{p_{t}}\|_{2}\leq 1, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality⟨Pt​vpt,θ⟩2=⟨vpt,Pt​θ⟩2≤‖vpt‖22​‖Pt​θ‖22≤‖Pt​θ‖22.\ \langle P_{t}v_{p_{t}},\theta\rangle^{2}=\langle v_{p_{t}},P_{t}\theta\rangle^{2}\leq\|v_{p_{t}}\|^{2}_{2}\,\|P_{t}\theta\|^{2}_{2}\leq\|P_{t}\theta\|^{2}_{2}.

Further, as theΠt′\Pi_{t^{\prime}}are mutually orthogonal,‖Pt​θ‖22=‖∑t′∈[ft,t]Πt′​(θ)‖22=∑t′∈[ft,t]‖Πt′​(θ)‖22.\|P_{t}\theta\|^{2}_{2}=\left\|\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[f_{t},t]}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(\theta)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[f_{t},t]}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}.

Plugging this into (9.5) and interchanging summations gives𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]≤𝔼​[∑t=1nδt2​∑t′∈[ft,t]‖Πt′​(θ)‖22]=𝔼​[∑t=1n‖Πt​(θ)‖22​∑t′∈[t,ℓt]δt′2].∎\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\delta_{t}^{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[f_{t},t]}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{n}\ \|\Pi_{t}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[t,\ell_{t}]}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}\right].\qed

## A martingale argument.

We can now finish the proof of Theorem9.5.
We only need the following standard fact about martingales.

Consider a real-valued martingaleXtX_{t}starting at some fixedX0X_{0}with incrementsδt=Xt−Xt−1\delta_{t}=X_{t}-X_{t-1}, where|Xt|≤1|X_{t}|\leq 1for
allttwith probability11.

## Lemma 9.8.

Letτ:=min⁡{t:|Xt|=1}\tau:=\min\{t:|X_{t}|=1\}be the first (random) time when
the martingaleXtX_{t}above reaches+1+1or−1-1, and assume that,
for some fixedNN,τ≤N\tau\leq Nwith probability11. For anyt≥1t\geq 1, the expected quadratic variation of future increments
satisfies𝔼​[∑t′=tτδt′2|Ωt−1]=1−Xt−12\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{\tau}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}|\Omega_{t-1}\right]=1-X_{t-1}^{2}, whereΩt−1\Omega_{t-1}denotes all the random choices up
to timet−1t-1.

## Proof.

We assume thatt≤τt\leq\tau, since the lemma is trivial otherwise.
Let us define the auxiliary random variablesYs:=Xt+s−12−∑t′=tt+s−1δt′2Y_{s}:=X_{t+s-1}^{2}-\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{t+s-1}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}, for any integers≥1s\geq 1, andY0:=Xt−12Y_{0}:=X_{t-1}^{2}. We claim that, conditional onΩt−1\Omega_{t-1},Y0,Y1,…Y_{0},Y_{1},\ldotsforms a
martingale sequence. Indeed, for anys≥1s\geq 1we have𝔼​[Ys−Ys−1|Ωt+s−2]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{s}-Y_{s-1}|\Omega_{t+s-2}]=𝔼​[Xt+s−12−Xt+s−22−δt+s−12|Ωt+s−2]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X_{t+s-1}^{2}-X_{t+s-2}^{2}-\delta_{t+s-1}^{2}|\Omega_{t+s-2}]=𝔼​[2​δt+s−1​Xt+s−2|Ωt+s−2]=0,\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[2\delta_{t+s-1}X_{t+s-2}|\Omega_{t+s-2}]=0,

by the martingale property ofXtX_{t}. Thus, by the optional stopping
theorem, and usingXτ2=1X_{\tau}^{2}=1,𝔼​[1−∑t′=tτδt′2|Ωt−1]=𝔼​[Yτ−t+1|Ωt−1]=Y0=Xt−12.\mathbb{E}\left[1-\sum_{t^{\prime}=t}^{\tau}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}|\Omega_{t-1}\right]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{\tau-t+1}|\Omega_{t-1}]=Y_{0}=X_{t-1}^{2}.

The lemma follows after re-arranging.
∎

## Proof of Theorem9.5.

Fix some timett, and condition onΩt−1\Omega_{t-1}. This fixesVtV_{t},Πt\Pi_{t}and the pivotptp_{t}. Asptp_{t}stays unchanged until its color reaches±1\pm 1(which happens at timeℓt\ell_{t}), by Lemma9.8𝔼​[∑t′∈[t,ℓt]δt′2|Ωt−1]=1−xt−1​(pt)2≤1.\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[t,\ell_{t}]}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}\,|\Omega_{t-1}\bigg]=1-x_{t-1}(p_{t})^{2}\leq 1.

Averaging overΩt−1\Omega_{t-1}gives that𝔼​[∑t′∈[t,ℓt]δt′2​‖Πt​(θ)‖22]≤‖Πt​(θ)‖22,\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t^{\prime}\in[t,\ell_{t}]}\delta_{t^{\prime}}^{2}\|\Pi_{t}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\|\Pi_{t}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2},and thus by Lemma9.7𝔼​[⟨dn,θ⟩2]≤∑t=1n‖Πt​(θ)‖22=‖∑t=1nΠt​(θ)‖22≤‖θ‖22,\mathbb{E}[\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle^{2}]\leq\sum_{t=1}^{n}\|\Pi_{t}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{n}\Pi_{t}(\theta)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\theta\|_{2}^{2},

where we use that theΠt\Pi_{t}are mutually orthogonal.

## 9.3Proving Sub-gaussianity

We now prove the sub-Gaussianity property. In particular, we will show the following.

## Theorem 9.9.

For any vectorθ∈ℝm\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}, we have𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dn,θ⟩)]≤exp⁡(10​‖θ‖22).\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle)]\leq\exp(10\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}).(9.6)

The proof will follow a similar structure as the bound on the covariance. We fix someθ∈ℝm\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}and track the evolution of𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt,θ⟩)]\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle)]over time.

## Proof Overview.

Clearlyexp⁡(⟨d0,θ⟩)=1\exp(\langle d_{0},\theta\rangle)=1asx0=𝟎x_{0}=\bf{0}initially. To show that (9.6) holds eventually at the end of the algorithm,
a natural idea to define a suitable potentialΦt\Phi_{t}so that (roughly) the increase indtd_{t}can be charged to the decrease inΦt\Phi_{t}.
More precisely,
suppose we have someΦt\Phi_{t}such thatZt=⟨dt,θ⟩+ΦtZ_{t}=\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle+\Phi_{t}satisfies the following properties:𝔼​[exp⁡(Zt)|Ωt−1]≤exp⁡(Zt−1)(Super-martingale)\mathbb{E}[\exp(Z_{t})|\Omega_{t-1}]\leq\exp(Z_{t-1})\qquad\text{(Super-martingale)}Φ0≤10∥θ∥22andΦn=0.(Boundary conditions)\Phi_{0}\leq{10\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}}\text{ and }\Phi_{n}=0.\qquad\text{(Boundary conditions)}

Then Theorem9.9follows directly as𝔼​[exp⁡(Zn)]≤exp⁡(Z0)\mathbb{E}[\exp(Z_{n})]\leq\exp(Z_{0})by super-martingale condition, andZn=⟨dn,θ⟩Z_{n}=\langle d_{n},\theta\rangleandZ0=Φ0≤10​‖θ‖22Z_{0}=\Phi_{0}\leq 10\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}by the boundary conditions.

DenotingZt=Zt−1+Δ​Zt=Δ​dt+Δ​ΦtZ_{t}=Z_{t-1}+\Delta Z_{t}=\Delta d_{t}+\Delta\Phi_{t}, the first property is same as showing that𝔼​[exp⁡(Δ​Zt)|Ωt−1]≤1\mathbb{E}[\exp(\Delta Z_{t})|\Omega_{t-1}]\leq 1,
or equivalently𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩+Δ​Φt)|Ωt−1]≤1.\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle+\Delta\Phi_{t})|\Omega_{t-1}]\leq 1.(9.7)

At a high level this is what we do, but one complication is that (9.7) does not hold when|⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩|≫1|\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle|\gg 1.
This introduces some technical complications. In particular, we work with a modifiedZtZ_{t}that ignores time steps where|⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩||\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle|can be large, and bound the contribution of such steps to⟨dn,θ⟩\langle d_{n},\theta\rangleseparately.

The motivates the notion ofbadtimes that we define formally below. We then define the potentialΦt\Phi_{t}andZtZ_{t}and give the detailed proof.

## 9.3.1Notation and the Potential

## Bad times.

Recall that by Lemma9.6,Δ​dt=δt​B​ut=δt​Pt​vpt\Delta d_{t}=\delta_{t}Bu_{t}=\delta_{t}P_{t}v_{p_{t}}.
We call a timettbadif‖Pt​θ‖2>1/4\|P_{t}\theta\|_{2}>1/4.
Note that whetherttis bad or not is completely determined byΩt−1\Omega_{t-1}(the random choices beforett), sincePtP_{t}is.

Consider the potentialΦt:={‖ΠVt​(θ)‖22+(1−xt​(pt)2)​‖Pt​θ‖22iftis not bad‖ΠVt​θ‖22iftis bad\displaystyle\Phi_{t}:=\begin{cases}\|\Pi_{V_{t}}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}+(1-x_{t}(p_{t})^{2})\|P_{t}\theta\|_{2}^{2}\quad&\text{if $t$ is not bad }\\
\|\Pi_{V_{t}}\theta\|_{2}^{2}\quad&\text{if $t$ is bad }\end{cases}

The term(1−xt​(pt)2)​‖Pt​θ‖22(1-x_{t}(p_{t})^{2})\|P_{t}\theta\|_{2}^{2}tracks progress toward freezing the pivot.

Let’s compute the boundary conditions.

(i) InitiallyΦ0≤‖θ‖22\Phi_{0}\leq\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}asP0=0P_{0}=0and,

(ii) EventuallyΦn=0\Phi_{n}=0asVn={0}V_{n}=\{0\}andxt∈{−1,1}nx_{t}\in\{-1,1\}^{n}att=nt=n.

## The processZtZ_{t}.

We defineZt:=Yt+2​ΦtZ_{t}:=Y_{t}+2\Phi_{t}, whereYt:=Y0+∑t′=1tΔ​Yt′Y_{t}:=Y_{0}+\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t}\Delta Y_{t^{\prime}}is the process defined byY0=0Y_{0}=0and the following increments fort=1,…,nt=1,\ldots,nΔ​Yt:={⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩if​t​is not bad0iftis bad\displaystyle\Delta Y_{t}:=\begin{cases}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle&\mbox{if }t\text{ is not bad}\\
0&\text{ if $t$ is bad}\end{cases}

Intuitively, one should think ofYtY_{t}is⟨dt,θ⟩\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle, except that we do not updateYtY_{t}at timettifttis a bad time (so whiledtd_{t}updates todt−1+Δ​dt−1d_{t-1}+\Delta d_{t-1}, we keepYtY_{t}unchanged toYt−1Y_{t-1}).

Notice that initiallyZ0=Y0+2​Φ0=2​Φ0≤2​‖θ‖22Z_{0}=Y_{0}+2\Phi_{0}=2\Phi_{0}\leq 2\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}.
Eventually at timet=nt=n, usingΦn=0\Phi_{n}=0,Zn=Yn+2​Φn=Yn=∑t=1nΔ​Yt=∑t∉B⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩Z_{n}=Y_{n}+2\Phi_{n}=Y_{n}=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\Delta Y_{t}=\sum_{t\notin B}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle(9.8)

whereBBdenotes the set of all the bad timestt.

## 9.3.2The Key Lemmas

We will show the following key lemmas, which will directly imply Theorem9.9.

## Lemma 9.10.

(Super-martingale property.)𝔼​[exp⁡(Δ​Zt)|Ωt−1]≤1\mathbb{E}[\exp(\Delta Z_{t})|\Omega_{t-1}]\leq 1for eacht≥1t\geq 1. Thus,𝔼​[exp⁡(Zn)]≤exp⁡(Z0)=exp⁡(2​‖θ‖22).\mathbb{E}[\exp(Z_{n})]\leq\exp(Z_{0})=\exp(2\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}).

## Lemma 9.11.

(Contribution of bad times.)
 LetBBbe the set of bad timesttwith‖Pt​θ‖2>1/4\|P_{t}\theta\|_{2}>1/4. Then|∑t∈B⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩|≤8​‖θ‖22|\sum_{t\in B}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle|\leq 8\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}.

Let us see how these directly give Theorem9.9.

## Proof.

(Theorem9.9.)
Writingdn=∑t∉BΔ​dt+∑t∈BΔ​dtd_{n}=\sum_{t\notin B}\Delta d_{t}+\sum_{t\in B}\Delta d_{t}, as the sum of increments over bad and non-bad times,⟨dn,θ⟩\displaystyle\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle=∑t∉B⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩⏟=Zn​(9.8)+∑t∈B⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩⏟≤8​‖θ‖22​(Lem.9.11)≤Zn+8​‖θ‖22.\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{t\notin B}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle}_{=Z_{n}\,\,\,\eqref{eq:gs-zn}}+\underbrace{\sum_{t\in B}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle}_{\leq 8\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}\text{ (Lem. \ref{lem:gs:bound_badtimes})}}\leq Z_{n}+8\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}.

As𝔼​[exp⁡(Zn)]≤exp⁡(2​‖θ‖22)\mathbb{E}[\exp(Z_{n})]\leq\exp(2\|\theta\|_{2}^{2})by Lemma9.10, we get the desired bound𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dn,θ⟩)]≤𝔼​[exp⁡(Zn)]​exp⁡(8​‖θ‖22)≤exp⁡(10​‖θ‖22).∎\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{n},\theta\rangle)]\leq\mathbb{E}[\exp(Z_{n})]\exp(8\|\theta\|_{2}^{2})\leq\exp(10\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}).\qed

## 9.3.3Proof of Lemma9.11

Our goal here is to bound the contribution of bad time steps. We first bound the discrepancy incurred during any sub-interval of a phase.

## Lemma 9.12.

Letkkbe any phase with start and end timesbkb_{k}andeke_{k}. Then|∑t=fg⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩|≤2​‖Pg​θ‖2\left|\sum_{t=f}^{g}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle\right|\leq 2\|P_{g}\theta\|_{2}for any timesf,g∈[bk,ek]f,g\in[b_{k},e_{k}].

## Proof.

Letppdenote the pivot during phasekk.
ThenΔ​dt=δt​Pt​vp\Delta d_{t}=\delta_{t}P_{t}v_{p}at any timettin the phase, andPt=∑t′=bktΠt′P_{t}=\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{t}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}. So we have∑t=fgΔ​dt\displaystyle\sum_{t=f}^{g}\Delta d_{t}=∑t=fgδt​Pt​(vp)=∑t=fgδt​(∑t′=bktΠt′​(vp))\displaystyle=\sum_{t=f}^{g}\delta_{t}P_{t}(v_{p})=\sum_{t=f}^{g}\delta_{t}\left(\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{t}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p})\right)=∑t′=bkgΠt′(vp)(∑t=max⁡(t′,f)gδt).(interchanging sums)\displaystyle=\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p})\left(\sum_{t=\max(t^{\prime},f)}^{g}\delta_{t}\right).\qquad\text{(interchanging sums)}(9.9)

As the pivotppstays unchanged during the phase andδt=Δ​xt​(p)\delta_{t}=\Delta x_{t}(p)for eachttin the phase, a key observation is that for anyu,v∈[bk,ek]u,v\in[b_{k},e_{k}],|∑t=uvδt|=|xv​(p)−xu−1​(p)|≤2.\left|\sum_{t=u}^{v}\delta_{t}\right|=|x_{v}(p)-x_{u-1}(p)|\leq 2.

Using this bound in (9.9), by the triangle inequality,|∑t=fg⟨Δ​dt,θ⟩|≤2​∑t′=bkg|⟨Πt′​(vp),θ⟩|.\left|\sum_{t=f}^{g}\langle\Delta d_{t},\theta\rangle\right|\leq 2\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}|\langle\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p}),\theta\rangle|.

AsΠt′\Pi_{t^{\prime}}is a projection, it is self-adjoint, andΠt′2=Πt′\Pi_{t^{\prime}}^{2}=\Pi_{t^{\prime}}. Using this, and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice,
gives us that∑t′=bkg|⟨Πt′​(vp),θ⟩|\displaystyle\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}|\langle\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p}),\theta\rangle|≤∑t′=bkg‖Πt′​(vp)‖2​‖Πt′​θ‖2\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p})\|_{2}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}\theta\|_{2}≤(∑t′=bkg‖Πt′​(vp)‖22)1/2⏟=‖Pg​(vp)‖2⁣≤‖vp‖2⁣≤1​(∑t′=bkg‖Πt′​θ‖22)1/2⏟=‖Pg​θ‖2≤‖Pg​θ‖2.\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\Big(\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(v_{p})\|_{2}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}}_{=\,\|P_{g}(v_{p})\|_{2}\leq\|v_{p}\|_{2}\leq 1}\underbrace{\Big(\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}\theta\|_{2}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}}_{=\,\|P_{g}\theta\|_{2}}\leq\|P_{g}\theta\|_{2}.

The last step uses thatPg=∑t′=bkgΠt′P_{g}=\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}\Pi_{t^{\prime}}, and as the projectionsΠt′\Pi_{t^{\prime}}are mutually orthogonal‖Pg​w‖22=∑t′=bkg‖Πt′​(w)‖22\|P_{g}w\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{t^{\prime}=b_{k}}^{g}\|\Pi_{t^{\prime}}(w)\|_{2}^{2}for any vectorww.
∎

We now finish the proof of Lemma9.11.

## 9.4Bibliographic Notes

The Gram-Schmidt Walk (GS Walk) algorithm in Section9.1is due to Bansal, Dadush, Garg and Lovett[15]. They showed that the distributionDDin Theorem9.1produced by the GS Walk is40\sqrt{40}-sub-Gaussian.
Our presentation follows their approach while simplifying some of the arguments.

Remarkably, motivated by applications to the design of randomized controlled trials, Harshaw, Sävje, Peng and Spielman[57]showed that the distributionDDoutput by GS walk algorithm is in fact 1-sub-Gaussian. This is clearly optimal — as already forn=1n=1, the random vector±e1\pm e_{1}is exactly 1-sub-Gaussian. Proving this refined bound requires more care and we do not do this here.
The tight covariance bound in Theorem9.5is also due to[57].

## Chapter 10Online Discrepancy

So far in all the discrepancy problems we considered, the vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}were given to us upfront. Another very natural setting is where the vectors are revealed in an online manner and whenvtv_{t}arrives at timett, its signx​(t)x(t)must be chosen immediately and irrevocably, without the knowledge of future vectors.
The goal is to keep the discrepancy‖dt‖∞\|d_{t}\|_{\infty}small at all timestt, wheredt=x​(1)​v1+…+x​(t)​vtd_{t}=x(1)v_{1}+\ldots+x(t)v_{t}. In a sense, this is very similar
to prefix discrepancy, where we also need to minimizemaxt=1n⁡‖dt‖∞\max_{t=1}^{n}\|d_{t}\|_{\infty}. However, the difference from the online setting is that in (offline) prefix discrepancy problems, we can choose the coloringxxwith full advance knowledge ofv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}. We will see in this
chapter that a simple online discrepancy minimization algorithm also
gives the best algorithmic results for a number of prefix discrepancy
problems we have already considered.

## Online Komlós Problem.

As the primary example of an online discrepancy problem, we focus here on the online Komlós setting where the vectors satisfy‖vt‖2≤1\|v_{t}\|_{2}\leq 1. It is useful to think ofnnas being much larger thanmm(the problem is already non-trivial form=2m=2).

Notice that sampling eachx​(t)x(t)independently and uniformly can be
thought of as an online algorithm, and incurs discrepancyO​(n)O(\sqrt{n})in the Komlós setting.
Unfortunately, no online algorithm can do better in general. In
particular, suppose thatm=2m=2, and each vectorvtv_{t}is picked by an
adversary that knows the discrepancy vectordt−1d_{t-1}. Then the adversary can pickvtv_{t}orthogonal todt−1d_{t-1}so that‖dt‖22=‖dt−1‖22+‖vt‖22\|d_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=\|d_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}irrespective of the signx​(t)x(t)chosen by the online
algorithm. Repeating this fornnsteps gives‖dn‖∞≥n2\|d_{n}\|_{\infty}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}}.

Interestingly, substantially better results can be obtained if the vectorsvtv_{t}are chosen in a less adversarial manner.

## Oblivious Adversary Model.

A standard model in online computation is that of an oblivious adversary. Here the adversary still knows the online algorithm and can pick the worst case sequence of vectors accordingly. However it must do so in advance before the online algorithm begins its execution.

Clearly, oblivious adversaries do not help for deterministic
algorithms — as the adversary knows exactly what the algorithm will do on each input, it can still pick the worst possible sequence in advance.
However, randomization can be very helpful here as the adversary cannot see the specific random choices made by the algorithm (even though it knows the algorithm itself).

## 10.1Self-Balancing Walk

We now describe an elegant algorithm called the Self-Balancing Walk (SW) with the following guarantee.

## Theorem 10.1.

For anyδ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), there is a polynomial time randomized online algorithm against oblivious
adversaries, that, for any input vectorsv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}with‖vt‖2≤1\|v_{t}\|_{2}\leq 1for eachtt, with probability1−δ1-\deltasatisfies that‖dt‖∞=O​(log⁡(m​n/δ))\|d_{t}\|_{\infty}=O(\log(mn/\delta))for allt∈[n]t\in[n].

Theorem10.1is remarkable in many ways.
It givesO​(log⁡m​n)O(\log mn)discrepancy w.h.p. for the online Komlós
problem. This matches the best known algorithmic bound for the offlineprefixKomlós problem that we discussed in
Chapter5, and comes close to the non-constructiveO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n})bound from Theorem5.17.
Finally, the algorithm is very fast to implement and only requires computing a single inner product at each step.

## The Gaussian Walk (GW) Algorithm.

To gain some intuition for the SW algorithm, we first describe another algorithm with weaker requirements. We allow our algorithm to choose, at each time steptt, a random colorx​(t)x(t)which can be any real number (possibly outside the interval[−1,1][-1,1]), but must satisfy𝔼​[x​(t)2]=1\mathbb{E}[x(t)^{2}]=1.

Remarkably, one can ensure that𝔼​[dt​(i)2]≤1\mathbb{E}[d_{t}(i)^{2}]\leq 1holds for each coordinatei∈[m]i\in[m]and each timett.111Finding such a distribution overxtx_{t}is equivalent to constructing a feasible SDP solution with vector discrepancy at most11, as discussed later.Moreover, the (random) colorx​(t)x(t)can be computed online, using the following algorithm.


Fort=1,…,nt=1,\ldots,ndo the following:
- 1.

Given the vectorvtv_{t}at timett, computebt=⟨vt,dt−1⟩.b_{t}=\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangle.
- 2.

Samplex​(t)x(t)from the Gaussian distributionN​(−bt,1−𝔼​[bt2])N(-b_{t},1-\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]).222We will show later that this variance is always non-negative.
- 3.

Setdt=dt−1+x​(t)​vtd_{t}=d_{t-1}+x(t)v_{t}.

Note herebtb_{t}is a random variable asdt−1d_{t-1}is random, and the expectation𝔼​[bt2]\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]in Step 2
is over the randomness up to timet−1t-1.

The choice to makex​(t)x(t)Gaussian is not essential, and we could have chosen any random variable with mean−bt-b_{t}and variance1−𝔼​[bt2]1-\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]. The bias−bt-b_{t}ofx​(t)x(t)plays an important role — it makes sure that, if⟨vt,dt−1⟩\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangleis large, then the discrepancy vectordtd_{t}is pushed towards the origin in the direction ofvtv_{t}. To see this more precisely, we can writex​(t)x(t)asx​(t)=−bt+y​(t)x(t)=-b_{t}+y(t)wherey​(t)∼N​(0,1−𝔼​[bt2])y(t)\sim N(0,1-\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]), independent ofdt−1d_{t-1}. Thendt=dt−1−bt​vt+y​(t)​vtd_{t}=d_{t-1}-b_{t}v_{t}+y(t)v_{t}.

We have the following guarantee.

## Theorem 10.2.

For anyv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}provided by an oblivious adversary such that‖vt‖2≤1\|v_{t}\|_{2}\leq 1for alltt, the GW algorithm above is well-defined, and, at any time steptt, satisfies𝔼​[x​(t)2]=1\mathbb{E}[x(t)^{2}]=1and𝔼​[dt​dtT]⪯I\mathbb{E}[d_{t}d_{t}^{T}]\preceq I.333Recall that the notationA⪯BA\preceq B, for two symmetricm×mm\times mmatricesAAandBB, means thatB−AB-Ais positive semidefinite.

## Proof.

We will show using induction onttthat𝔼​[dt​dtT]⪯I\mathbb{E}[d_{t}d_{t}^{T}]\preceq I.
This holds trivially for the base caset=0t=0sinced0=0d_{0}=0. Consider somet≥1t\geq 1and assume
inductively that𝔼​[dt−1​dt−1T]⪯I\mathbb{E}[d_{t-1}d_{t-1}^{T}]\preceq I.

First note that this implies that the algorithm is well-defined at timett, as𝔼​[bt2]=𝔼​[⟨vt,dt−1⟩2]≤‖vt‖22≤1\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangle^{2}]\leq\|v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1, so the variance ofx​(t)x(t)is non-negative.
As𝔼​[bt2]≤1\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]\leq 1, it is also easily seen that𝔼​[x​(t)2]=1\mathbb{E}[x(t)^{2}]=1.

We now show the inductive step that𝔼​[dt​dtT]⪯I\mathbb{E}[d_{t}d_{t}^{T}]\preceq I. Let us definedt′:=dt−1−bt​vtd^{\prime}_{t}:=d_{t-1}-b_{t}v_{t}, so thatdt=dt′+y​(t)​vtd_{t}=d^{\prime}_{t}+y(t)v_{t}.
A key observation is thatdt′=dt−1−bt​vt=dt−1−⟨dt−1,vt⟩​vt=(I−vt​vtT)​dt−1.d^{\prime}_{t}=d_{t-1}-b_{t}v_{t}=d_{t-1}-\langle d_{t-1},v_{t}\rangle v_{t}=(I-v_{t}v_{t}^{T})d_{t-1}.

Let us further defineσt2:=𝔼​[y​(t)2]=1−𝔼​[bt2]\sigma_{t}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[y(t)^{2}]=1-\mathbb{E}[b_{t}^{2}]. We thus have,𝔼​[dt​dtT]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[d_{t}d_{t}^{T}]=𝔼​[(dt′+y​(t)​vt)​(dt′+y​(t)​vt)T]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(d^{\prime}_{t}+y(t)v_{t})(d^{\prime}_{t}+y(t)v_{t})^{T}]=𝔼​[(dt′)​(dt′)T]+σt2​vt​vtT\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(d^{\prime}_{t})(d^{\prime}_{t})^{T}]+\sigma_{t}^{2}v_{t}v_{t}^{T}=(I−vt​vtT)​𝔼​[dt−1​dt−1T]​(I−vt​vtT)+σt2​vt​vtT\displaystyle=(I-v_{t}v_{t}^{T})\mathbb{E}[d_{t-1}d_{t-1}^{T}](I-v_{t}v_{t}^{T})+\sigma_{t}^{2}v_{t}v_{t}^{T}⪯(I−vt​vtT)2+σt2​vt​vtT\displaystyle\preceq(I-v_{t}v_{t}^{T})^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}v_{t}v_{t}^{T}=I−(2−‖vt‖22−σt2)​vt​vtT⪯I.\displaystyle=I-(2-\|v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}-\sigma_{t}^{2})v_{t}v_{t}^{T}\preceq I.

Here, the second equality uses thaty​(t)y(t)is independent ofdt′d^{\prime}_{t}and has mean0, the fourth line uses the inductive hypothesis𝔼​[dt−1​dt−1T]⪯I\mathbb{E}[d_{t-1}d_{t-1}^{T}]\preceq I, and the last inequality follows as‖vt‖22≤1\|v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1andσt2≤1\sigma_{t}^{2}\leq 1.
∎

## Remark 10.3.

Theorem10.2is similar to Theorem5.16. Indeed, sincedtd_{t}is jointly Gaussian, has mean0, and has variance at most11in every direction, it is also11-subgaussian. However, unlike Theorem5.16, we allow the “colors” to be any real number (instead of just±1\pm 1). On the other hand, we achieve better constants, and have an online algorithm.

An explicit Vector Coloring.As a corollary of Theorem10.2, we get a vector coloring for the prefix Komlós problem achieving vector discrepancy11. This is a strengthening of Theorem5.6.

## Theorem 10.4.

For any vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}with‖vj‖2≤1\|v_{j}\|_{2}\leq 1for allj∈[n]j\in[n], there exist vectorsw1,…​wn∈ℝdw_{1},\ldots w_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}for some positive integerdd, such that‖wj‖2=1\|w_{j}\|_{2}=1for allj∈[n]j\in[n], andmaxj=1n⁡maxi=1m⁡‖∑k=1jwk​vk​(i)‖2≤1.\max_{j=1}^{n}\max_{i=1}^{m}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{j}w_{k}v_{k}(i)\right\|_{2}\leq 1.

## Proof.

Letx:=(x​(1),…,x​(n))x:=(x(1),\ldots,x(n))be the random colors determined by the GW algorithm, and define the positive semidefinite matrixXXwith entriesX​(i,j)=𝔼​[x​(i)​x​(j)]X(i,j)=\mathbb{E}[x(i)x(j)]. Theorem10.2implies thatX​(j,j)=1X(j,j)=1for allj∈[n]j\in[n], and also thatVj​X​VjT⪯IV_{j}XV_{j}^{T}\preceq Ifor allj∈[n]j\in[n], whereVjV_{j}is them×nm\times nmatrix whose firstjjcolumns arev1,…,vjv_{1},\ldots,v_{j}, and the remainingn−jn-jcolumns are0. Now the theorem is implied by takingw1,…,wnw_{1},\ldots,w_{n}to be any vectors such that⟨wi,wj⟩=X​(i,j)\langle w_{i},w_{j}\rangle=X(i,j).
∎

## The Self-Balancing Walk (SW) Algorithm.

We now formulate the Self-Balancing Walk (SW) algorithm that proves Theorem10.1. The SW algorithm is very similar to the GW algorithm, with some key differences. We need to make sure that each colorx​(t)x(t)is in{−1,+1}\{-1,+1\}. Since this forces the bias to be in[−1,1][-1,1], we will make the bias smaller in proportion to⟨vt,dt−1⟩\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangle, and, moreover, the algorithm will fail when the bias is outside the interval[−1,1][-1,1]. Below, we letc=2​log⁡(4​m​n/δ)c=2\log(4mn/\delta), and, as before, letdtd_{t}denote the discrepancy vector at timett. Initially,d0=0d_{0}=0.


Fort=1,…,nt=1,\ldots,ndo the following:
- 1.

Given the vectorvtv_{t}at timett, computebt=⟨vt,dt−1⟩/c.b_{t}=\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangle/c.(10.1)
- 2.

If|bt|>1|b_{t}|>1, abort the algorithm.
Otherwise, setx​(t)={−1​w.p.​(1+bt)/2+1​otherwise.x(t)=\begin{cases}-1\text{ \,\,\, w.p. }(1+b_{t})/2\\
+1\text{ \,\,\,\, otherwise}.\end{cases}(10.2)
- 3.

Setdt=dt−1+x​(t)​vtd_{t}=d_{t-1}+x(t)v_{t}.

To see the parallel between the GW and SW algorithms, notice that,
conditioned ondt−1d_{t-1}, the signx​(t)x(t)satisfies𝔼​[x​(t)|dt−1]=−bt\mathbb{E}[x(t)|d_{t-1}]=-b_{t}, and, asbt=⟨vt,dt−1⟩/cb_{t}=\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangle/c, the discrepancydtd_{t}satisfies𝔼​[dt|dt−1]=dt−1−bt​vt=(I−1c​vt​vtT)​dt−1.\mathbb{E}[d_{t}|d_{t-1}]=d_{t-1}-b_{t}v_{t}=\left(I-\frac{1}{c}v_{t}v_{t}^{T}\right)d_{t-1}.

## 10.2Analysis

We now prove Theorem10.1. The proof follows the proof of Theorem10.2, but is more involved. Instead of just bounding the second moments of the discrepancy vectordtd_{t}, we show thatdtd_{t}is subgaussian. Moreover, we need to account for the possibility that the algorithm aborts. To deal with this second issue, it will be convenient to consider a modified algorithm that never aborts but may sometimes output a non-sense “sign”x​(t)x(t)with|x​(t)|>1|x(t)|>1. Consider the following algorithm.

## Modified Walk (MW).

At each timett, computebt=⟨dt−1,vt⟩/cb_{t}=\langle d_{t-1},v_{t}\rangle/cexactly as before.

(i) If|bt|>1|b_{t}|>1, setx​(t)=−btx(t)=-b_{t}deterministically.

(ii) Else if|bt|≤1|b_{t}|\leq 1, then setx​(t)x(t)randomly exactly as in (10.2).


Notice that MW never aborts, but may set|x​(t)|>1|x(t)|>1in step (i).

## Coupling SW and MW.

Notice that if we use the same random choices in SW in (10.2) and MW in step (ii), then they both behave identically until the first (random) timeτ\tauwhen|bτ|>1|b_{\tau}|>1. In particular, at this time SW will abort, but MW will outputxτx_{\tau}with|xτ|>1|x_{\tau}|>1.

For this reason it will suffice to analyze the MW algorithm.

## Sub-gaussianity ofdtd_{t}.

Recall from Section5.4that
a mean-zero vector-valued random variableX∈ℝmX\in\mathbb{R}^{m}isσ\sigma-sub-Gaussian if𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨X,θ⟩)]≤exp⁡(σ2​‖θ‖2/2)for allθ∈ℝm,\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle X,\theta\rangle)]\leq\exp(\sigma^{2}\|\theta\|^{2}/2)\quad\text{for all $\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$},(10.3)

and that such anXXsatisfiesℙ​[|⟨X,θ⟩|≥x]≤2​exp⁡(−x2/(2​σ2​‖θ‖2))for allθ∈ℝm.\mathbb{P}[|\langle X,\theta\rangle|\geq x]\leq 2\exp(-x^{2}/(2\sigma^{2}\|\theta\|^{2}))\quad\text{for all $\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$}.(10.4)

MW satisfies the following remarkable guarantee.

## Theorem 10.5.

At each timett, the discrepancy vectordtd_{t}produced by MW isc\sqrt{c}-sub-Gaussian.

Interestingly, Theorem10.5directly implies Theorem10.1—by simply settingc=2​ln⁡(4​m​n/δ)c=2\ln(4mn/\delta)to ensure thatτ>n\tau>nwith high probability.

## Proof of Theorem10.1.

As the adversary is oblivious,v1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n}are fixed in advance (and do not depend upon the randomness of the algorithm).

Fix a timett.
By Theorem10.5the vectordtd_{t}produced by MW isc\sqrt{c}-sub-Gaussian. So, applying (10.4) withX=dt−1X=d_{t-1}andθ=vt\theta=v_{t}, and using that‖vt‖2≤1\|v_{t}\|_{2}\leq 1andσ2=c\sigma^{2}=c,ℙ​[|bt|>1]=ℙ​[|⟨dt−1,vt⟩|≥c]≤2​e−c/2=δ2​m​n.\mathbb{P}[|b_{t}|>1]=\mathbb{P}[|\langle d_{t-1},v_{t}\rangle|\geq c]\leq 2e^{-c/2}=\frac{\delta}{2mn}.

By a union bound over thenntime steps, the probability that SW aborts at somet≤nt\leq nis at mostδ2​m≤δ2\frac{\delta}{2m}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}.

Similarly, for a fixed timettand standard coordinate vectoreie_{i}, applying (10.4) withX=dt,θ=eiX=d_{t},\theta=e_{i},x=cx=c, andσ2=c\sigma^{2}=cgivesℙ​[|⟨dt,ei⟩|>c]≤2​e−c/2≤δ4​m​n.\mathbb{P}[|\langle d_{t},e_{i}\rangle|>c]\leq 2e^{-c/2}\leq\frac{\delta}{4mn}.

Thus, by a union bound over allt∈[n],i∈[m]t\in[n],i\in[m], the probability that‖dt‖∞>c\|d_{t}\|_{\infty}>cfor any timettis at mostδ/2\delta/2.
∎

## Remark 10.6.

We can also derive the following more general theorem, using an
argument analogous to the proof of
Theorem5.13. While an even more general statement
is possible, we state the theorem for a symmetric convex bodyKKinℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}with non-empty interior. We use‖y‖K:=inf{t:y∈t​K}\|y\|_{K}:=\inf\{t:y\in tK\}for the norm with unit ballKK. We useBℓ2mB_{\ell_{2}^{m}}for the unit ball of theℓ2m\ell_{2}^{m}norm.

## Theorem 10.7.

Letv1,v2,…,vn∈ℝmv_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}satisfy‖vt‖2≤1\|v_{t}\|_{2}\leq 1for
allt∈[n]t\in[n], and suppose thatKKis a symmetric convex body inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}such thatr​Bℓ2m⊆KrB_{\ell_{2}^{m}}\subseteq K. Then, for anyδ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), and for an appropriate choice ofcc, with probability at
least1−δ1-\deltathe SW algorithm satisfies‖dt‖K≲log⁡(n/δ)​(𝔼​‖g‖K+r​log⁡(n/δ)),\|d_{t}\|_{K}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(n/\delta)}(\mathbb{E}\|g\|_{K}+r\sqrt{\log(n/\delta)}),

whereggis a standard Gaussian random vector inℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}.

The proof of Theorem10.7is similar to the proof of
Theorem10.1above. We pickccon the order oflog⁡(n/δ)\log(n/\delta), which is enough to argue that the probability that
SW aborts is at mostδ2\frac{\delta}{2}. Then we estimatemaxt=1n⁡‖dt‖K\max_{t=1}^{n}\|d_{t}\|_{K}using the fact thatdtd_{t}isc\sqrt{c}-sub-Gaussian,
and the high probability version of Talagrand’s comparison inequality
(see,e.g., Exercise 8.6.5 in[126]). We leave the details
as an exercise.

Theorem10.7can be used to give an efficient (online!)
algorithm that nearly matches other prefix discrepancy bounds we have
already seen, in particular those derived from Theorem5.15. In particular, the bounds on prefix discrepancy in theℓp\ell_{p}norm in Theorem5.18can be matched up to an
additionalO​(log⁡n)O(\sqrt{\log n})multiplicative factor.

## 10.2.1Proof of Theorem10.5

We now prove Theorem10.5.

Fix some timett.
Conditioned ondt−1d_{t-1}, as the “sign”x​(t)x(t)satisfies𝔼​[x​(t)|dt−1]=−bt\mathbb{E}[x(t)|d_{t-1}]=-b_{t}, let us writey​(t):=x​(t)+bty(t):=x(t)+b_{t},
so that𝔼​[y​(t)|dt−1]=0\mathbb{E}[y(t)|d_{t-1}]=0.
In particular
if|bt|>1|b_{t}|>1theny​(t)=0y(t)=0, and otherwisey​(t)={−(1−bt)​w.p.​(1+bt)/2+(1+bt)​w.p.​(1−bt)/2y(t)=\begin{cases}-(1-b_{t})\text{ w.p. }(1+b_{t})/2\\
+(1+b_{t})\text{ w.p. }(1-b_{t})/2\end{cases}(10.5)

Asbt=⟨vt,dt−1⟩/cb_{t}=\langle v_{t},d_{t-1}\rangle/c, we can writedt=dt−1+x​(t)​vt=dt−1−bt​vt+y​(t)​vt=(I−1c​vt​vtT)​dt−1+y​(t)​vt.d_{t}=d_{t-1}+x(t)v_{t}=d_{t-1}-b_{t}v_{t}+y(t)v_{t}=\left(I-\frac{1}{c}v_{t}v_{t}^{T}\right)d_{t-1}+y(t)v_{t}.

Let us denoteAt:=(I−vt​vtT/c)A_{t}:=(I-v_{t}v_{t}^{T}/c), so thatdt=At​dt−1+y​(t)​vtd_{t}=A_{t}d_{t-1}+y(t)v_{t}.
Note thatAtA_{t}only depends onvtv_{t}, and hence it does not depend on the randomness of the algorithm as the adversary is oblivious.

## The inductive argument.

We now prove the sub-Gaussianity ofdtd_{t}by induction ontt.
Fix some test vectorθ∈ℝm\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}. We will show that𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt,θ⟩)]≤exp⁡(c​‖θ‖22/2).\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle)]\leq\exp(c\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2).

This clearly holds fort=0t=0as⟨dt,θ⟩=0\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle=0.
Assume inductively that𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt−1,u⟩)]≤exp⁡(c​‖u‖22/2)for all​u∈ℝm.\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t-1},u\rangle)]\leq\exp(c\|u\|_{2}^{2}/2)\quad\text{ for all }u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.(10.6)

Asdt=At​dt−1+y​(t)​vtd_{t}=A_{t}d_{t-1}+y(t)v_{t}, we have𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt,θ⟩)|dt−1]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle)|d_{t-1}]=𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨At​dt−1+y​(t)​vt,θ⟩)|dt−1]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle A_{t}d_{t-1}+y(t)v_{t},\theta\rangle)|d_{t-1}]=exp⁡(⟨At​dt−1,θ⟩)​𝔼​[exp⁡(y​(t)​⟨vt,θ⟩)|dt−1]\displaystyle=\exp(\langle A_{t}d_{t-1},\theta\rangle)\,{\mathbb{E}[\exp(y(t)\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle)|d_{t-1}]}≤exp⁡(⟨At​dt−1,θ⟩)​exp⁡(⟨vt,θ⟩2/2).\displaystyle\leq\exp(\langle A_{t}d_{t-1},\theta\rangle)\exp(\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}/2).(10.7)

The last step follows from Hoeffding’s lemma,
applied toy​(t)y(t)conditioned ondt−1d_{t-1}, and noting that𝔼​[y​(t)|dt−1]=0\mathbb{E}[y(t)|d_{t-1}]=0, and thaty​(t)y(t)takes values in an interval of
length22.

Taking expectation overdt−1d_{t-1}in (10.7) gives𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt,θ⟩)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle)]≤exp⁡(⟨vt,θ⟩2/2)​𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨At​dt−1,θ⟩)]\displaystyle\leq\exp(\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}/2)\,\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle A_{t}d_{t-1},\theta\rangle)]=exp⁡(⟨vt,θ⟩2/2)​𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt−1,At​θ⟩)]\displaystyle=\exp(\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}/2)\,\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t-1},A_{t}\theta\rangle)]≤exp⁡(⟨vt,θ⟩2/2)​exp⁡(c​‖At​θ‖22/2)\displaystyle\leq\exp(\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}/2)\,\exp(c\|A_{t}\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2)(10.8)

where the second line uses thatAtA_{t}is self-adjoint and the last step uses the inductive hypothesis (10.6) fordt−1d_{t-1}withu=At​θu=A_{t}\theta.

Now, asAt​θ=θ−⟨vt,θ⟩c​vtA_{t}\theta=\theta-\frac{\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle}{c}v_{t},‖At​θ‖22\displaystyle\|A_{t}\theta\|_{2}^{2}=‖θ−⟨vt,θ⟩c​vt‖22\displaystyle=\left\|\theta-\frac{\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle}{c}v_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}=‖θ‖22−2​⟨vt,θ⟩2c+⟨vt,θ⟩2​‖vt‖22c2\displaystyle=\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}}{c}+\frac{\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}\|v_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{c^{2}}≤‖θ‖22−⟨vt,θ⟩2c.\displaystyle\leq\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{\langle v_{t},\theta\rangle^{2}}{c}.(asc≥1c\geq 1,‖vt‖2≤1\|v_{t}\|^{2}\leq 1)

Plugging this in (10.8) gives the desired bound𝔼​[exp⁡(⟨dt,θ⟩)]≤exp⁡(c​‖θ‖22/2).\mathbb{E}[\exp(\langle d_{t},\theta\rangle)]\leq\exp(c\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}/2).

## 10.3Bibliographic Notes

Online discrepancy was first studied by Spencer[112].
Besides the online Komlós problem we consider here, several other discrepancy problems have also been studied in the online setting.
Bárány[22]considered the general setting where the vectorsvtv_{t}are chosen from some fixed setSS, and showed almost matching upper and lower bounds on the online discrepancy achievable by any deterministic algorithm.

Spencer[114]considered the setting where the vectorsvtv_{t}satisfy‖vt‖∞≤1\|v_{t}\|_{\infty}\leq 1and showed that forn=mn=many online algorithm must incur discrepancyΩ​(n​log⁡n)\Omega(\sqrt{n\log n}). In contrast, the offline prefix discrepancy isO​(n)O(\sqrt{n}).
Bansal and Spencer[20]considered the setting where the vectorsvtv_{t}are chosen uniformly and independently from{−1,1}m\{-1,1\}^{m}and gave an online strategy that at any timettensures that‖dt‖∞=O​(m)\|d_{t}\|_{\infty}=O(\sqrt{m})with high probability.

Another very interesting question is the online carpooling problem[3]. Here there arennnodes
and edgeset=(ut,vt)e_{t}=(u_{t},v_{t})arrive over time that must be oriented fromut→vtu_{t}\rightarrow v_{t}orvt→utv_{t}\rightarrow u_{t}. The goal is to minimize
the discrepancy at each vertex, equal to the absolute value of the
difference between in-degree and out-degree.
If the edges arrive randomly from the complete graph the discrepancy isO​(log⁡log⁡n)O(\log\log n)at any fixed time[3].
For arbitrary graphs and oblivious adversaries Theorem10.1implies anO​(log⁡(n​T))O(\log(nT))discrepancy, whereTTis the time horizon.
A prominent open question is whether apolylog​(n)\text{polylog}(n)bound (independent ofTT) exists. To this end,[3]gave a
randomized algorithm withO​(n​log⁡n)O(\sqrt{n\log n})discrepancy at any fixed time, and also showed a lower bound ofΩ​(log1/3⁡n)\Omega(\log^{1/3}n)for any randomized algorithm.

For the online Komlós problem, the Self-Balancing Walk algorithm in Section10.1is due to Alweiss, Liu and Sawhney[6]. Their original proof
follows a different approach and is based on showing that the distribution of the discrepancy vector is stochastically dominated by a suitably scaled Gaussian distribution. The exposition here gives better constants and is simpler, and is based on a personal communication by Arun Jambulapati. The weaker Gaussian Walk algorithm and the corresponding vector coloring in Theorem10.4are unpublished results of Nikolov.

Very recently, Kulkarni, Reis, and Rothvoss[67]showed theexistenceof an online algorithm withO​(log⁡m​n)O(\sqrt{\log mn})discrepancy. Moreover, this algorithm matches
the guarantees of Theorem5.16while being online.
The boundO​(log⁡m​n)O(\sqrt{\log mn})is also the best achievable
by any online algorithm for the Komlós problem, in contrast to offline prefix Komlós whereO​(1)O(1)discrepancy may be possible.
The algorithm of Kulkarni, Reis, and Rothvoss is highly non-constructive444Roughly speaking, given a time horizonnn, it computes a distribution over exponentially many decision trees, where each decision tree specifies a deterministic online coloring strategy for every possible sequence of incoming vectors.,
but it strongly suggests that a simpler and more efficient algorithm might exist.
Finding an efficient algorithm withO​(log⁡m​n)O(\sqrt{\log mn})discrepancy for the online Komlós problem is an outstanding open problem.

As further evidence, Liu, Sah and Sawhney[81]gave an efficient Gaussian Fixed Point Walk algorithm that gives a partial{−1,0,1}\{-1,0,1\}coloring with discrepancyO​(log⁡m​n)O(\sqrt{\log mn})where the color0is used at most4%4\%of the time.
In fact, their algorithm ensures that the discrepancy vector is distributed exactly
as the standard GaussianN​(0,Im)N(0,I_{m}).
In another remarkable result, Chewi, Gerber, Rigollet and Turner[40]showed that if one relaxes the colors to be two-dimensional unit vectors, then there is an efficient algorithm that ensures that the discrepancy is exactly distributed as the standard Gaussian.
